<meta>This page serves as a modern digital disclaimer and introductory guide for the digitized volume, explaining its status as a public domain work and the terms for its use.</meta>

<image-desc>The multicolored Google logo is positioned at the top of the page.</image-desc>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the **public domain** <term>The status of a work that is no longer protected by copyright law and is free for the public to use.</term>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other **notes in the margin** <note>original: "marginalia"; Latin for things written in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public, and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive; therefore, in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on **automated querying** <term>The use of software or scripts to automatically send requests or search terms to a computer system.</term>.

We also ask that you:

+ **Make non-commercial use of the files:** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ **Refrain from automated querying:** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, **optical character recognition** <note>original: "OCR"; the electronic conversion of images of typed or handwritten text into machine-encoded text.</note>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ **Maintain attribution:** The Google **watermark** <term>A faint identifying image or pattern in paper or a digital file that identifies its origin.</term> you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ **Keep it legal:** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. **Copyright infringement** <term>The unauthorized use of works under copyright, which violates the owner's exclusive rights.</term> liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

## INTRODUCTION.

**Porphyry**, the celebrated author of the treatises translated in this volume, was honored by his contemporaries and by succeeding Platonists with the title of *the philosopher* because of his extraordinary philosophical achievements. He is likewise called by Simplicius "the most learned of the philosophers" and is praised by Proclus for his <term>thoughts suited to sacred things</term> <note>original Greek: ιεροπρεπη νοηματα (hieroprepē noēmata)</note>, or conceptions adapted to holiness. The truth of all these titles is clearly and abundantly confirmed by the following treatises.

Only a few biographical details about this great man have been preserved for us, and they are as follows. He was born at Tyre <note>An ancient Phoenician city in modern-day Lebanon</note> in the twelfth year of the reign of the Emperor Alexander Severus, in the year 233 AD. He died at Rome when he was more than seventy years old, during the latter part of the Emperor Diocletian's reign. He was also a student first of Longinus and later of the great Plotinus, whom he met in the thirtieth year of his...

<summary>This page introduces the 3rd-century philosopher Porphyry, noting his high reputation among fellow Platonists and providing basic biographical data regarding his birth in Tyre and his studies under Longinus and Plotinus.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Platonism, Simplicius, Proclus, Alexander Severus, Diocletian, Plotinus, biography, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Porphyry, focusing on his relationship with his teacher Plotinus and his role in editing Plotinus's writings. It picks up from the previous page's discussion of Porphyry's age when he met Plotinus.</meta>

## INTRODUCTION.

age; and we owe the publication of the priceless and exceptionally deep works of that most extraordinary man to Porphyry. As I noted in my *History of the Restoration of the Platonic Theology*, it took a long time for Plotinus to write down his thoughts and share a record of his unique mind with the world. That light, which was meant to enlighten the philosophical world, had previously shone only in solitude or for a few close followers. It was through Porphyry alone that it finally emerged from its private sanctuary and shone in full perfection, spreading everywhere. According to Eunapius, Porphyry was "like a chain of Hermes <note>original: "Mercurial chain." In Neoplatonism, this refers to a series of divine messengers or a flow of knowledge from the divine to the human realm.</note> lowered for the benefit of mortals, explaining everything with accuracy and clarity through his vast learning."

Eunapius also tells us that when Porphyry first joined Plotinus, he left all his other teachers behind and devoted himself entirely to his friendship with that remarkable man. There, he filled his mind with knowledge <note>original: "science," used here in the classical sense of structured, certain knowledge</note> as if from an eternal and never-ending fountain. However, after becoming overwhelmed by the greatness of these teachings, he developed a dislike for the physical body and could no longer tolerate the chains of mortal life.

<summary>This page describes Porphyry's essential role in publishing the works of Plotinus and his intense devotion to his teacher's philosophy, which led him toward a rejection of physical existence.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Plotinus, Eunapius, Neoplatonism, Mercurial chain, Platonic Theology, philosophical publishing, asceticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Porphyry, focusing on a period of personal crisis where Plotinus intervened in his life, and transitions into his intellectual contributions to the restoration of Platonic philosophy.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

<margin>a In the *Life of Plotinus* <note>original: "In Vit. Plotin."</note></margin>
> "Therefore," he says<gloss>a</gloss>, "I formed an intention to take my own life, which Plotinus remarkably perceived. As I was walking home, he stood before me and said, '*Your current plan, Porphyry, is not the result of a sound mind, but rather of a soul raging with a melancholy <note>original: "atrabilarious," a term from the ancient medical theory of the four humors, suggesting a madness caused by an excess of black bile.</note> fury.*' As a result, he ordered me to leave Rome; and accordingly I went to Sicily, having heard that a certain worthy and refined man lived at that time near Lilybæum <note>An ancient city on the western coast of Sicily, modern-day Marsala.</note>. And so, indeed, I was freed from this mental distress; but in the meantime, I was prevented from being with Plotinus until his death."

Porphyry also holds a highly distinguished place among those great geniuses who helped explain the original teachings <note>original: "dogmas"</note> of Plato after they had been lost for more than five hundred years—as I have shown in my previously mentioned *History of the Restoration of the Platonic Theology*. Among these teachings, the one that is supremely important is this: that the indescribable principle of all things, which Plato calls *the Good* and *the One*, is something higher than both the intellect and existence itself. As we are told by Proclus, this was proven by Porphyry using many powerful and beautiful arguments in his book *Concerning Principles*.

<summary>Porphyry describes how his teacher Plotinus recognized his suicidal depression and sent him to Sicily to recover, and the text highlights Porphyry’s role in re-establishing the Platonic doctrine that the ultimate source of reality transcends intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Plotinus, Plato, Proclus, Neoplatonism, the One, the Good, Sicily, mental health, Platonic Theology</keywords>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
# INTRODUCTION.

...which is unfortunately lost. This teaching <note>original: "dogma"</note>, which was derived mainly from the sixth book of the *Republic* and the *Parmenides* of Plato, and was adopted by all later Platonists, is extensively explained. Its truth is supported by reasoning filled with what Plato calls "geometrical necessities" <note>Arguments so logical and precise that they are as certain as mathematics.</note> by those two great philosophical luminaries, Proclus and Damascius^b. Proclus was the <term>Coryphæus</term> <note>original: "Coryphæus," the leader or conductor of a Greek choir; used here to mean the head or most prominent member of the school.</note> of the Platonists, and Damascius possessed a profoundly investigative mind.

Among the students of Porphyry, the most famous was Iamblichus, a man of uncommonly sharp intelligence. Because of the greatness of his ideas and his impressive mastery of theological learning, he—like his master Plato—was given the title "*the divine*." This extraordinary man, though deeply devoted to Platonic philosophy, also explored the wisdom of other schools, particularly the Pythagoreans, Egyptians, and Chaldeans. From their harmonious combination, he formed one beautiful system of hidden <note>original: "recondite"</note> knowledge^c.

The Emperor Julian says of Iamblichus "that he lived later in time than Plato, but was not inferior to him in genius" <note>original: "posterior in time, but not in genius, to Plato himself."</note>.
</note>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page transitions from discussing Porphyry's general influence to his specific work on vegetarianism and the spiritual hierarchy of men according to Plotinus.</meta>

<page-num>ix</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

Regarding the works of Porphyry that are translated in this volume, the first is *On Abstinence from Animal Food*. This is a treatise not only filled with deep learning <note>original: "replete with great erudition"</note> but is also notable for the pure lifestyle it encourages <note>original: "inculcates"</note> and the sacredness of thought <note>original: "sanctity of conception"</note> that fills it. At the same time, it must be remembered that Porphyry wrote this—as he himself informs us—specifically for the person who wishes, in this present life, to free themselves as much as possible from the chains of physical existence <note>original: "fetters of the corporeal nature"</note>. Such a person seeks to raise their mind’s eye to the contemplation of **truly-existing being** <note>original: το οντως ον (*to ontōs on*)</note> and to find their home in the divine, as if returning to their true fatherland^d. However, such a person, as—

---

^d Plotinus places such a man in the category of **divine men** in the following extract from my translation of his treatise *On Intellect, Ideas, and Real Being* (Ennead 5.9). This extract, which is exceptionally beautiful in the original Greek, forms the beginning of the treatise: “Since all people, from birth, use their physical senses before their intellect, they are naturally first preoccupied with sensible objects <note>original: "sensibles"; things perceived by the senses rather than the mind</note>. Some people, going no further than this, pass through life considering these physical things to be the beginning and end of everything. They believe that whatever is painful among these is evil, and whatever is pleasant is good, thinking it sufficient to pursue one and avoid the other. Even those among them who claim to have a greater share of reason than others think this is wisdom. They are affected in a way similar to heavy birds: by collecting many things from the earth and being weighed down by that weight, they are unable to fly high,”

...he beautifully observes, a person must strip away everything of a mortal nature that they have taken on. They must withdraw from the senses and imagination, along with the irrationality that accompanies them, and from any clinging affection or passion toward those things. They must enter the arena <note>original: "stadium"</note> naked and unburdened, striving for the most glorious of all prizes: the **Olympia of the soul**<gloss>e</gloss>. Because of this, he says, “My discourse is not aimed at those occupied in menial trades <note>original: "sordid mechanical arts"</note>, nor those engaged in athletic exercises. It is not for soldiers, sailors, public speakers <note>original: "rhetoricians"</note>, nor those who lead a purely active [life]...

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;...even though they have naturally received wings for this purpose. But others are raised slightly above lower things, as the better part of their soul calls them away from pleasure toward a more worthy pursuit. However, because they are unable to look upward and find nothing else to give them rest, they turn back toward worldly actions and the choice of inferior things, though they still use the name of 'virtue.' Thus, they return to the very things they first tried to escape, though their efforts were in vain. *In the third class is the race of divine men*. Through a superior power and with piercing eyes, they clearly perceive heavenly <note>original: "supernal"</note> light. They raise themselves to this vision, rising above the clouds and darkness of this lower world. Staying there, they look down on everything in these realms of the senses. They take no delight in any place other than the one that is truly and properly their own—like a person who, after many wanderings, is finally restored to his rightful country.”

<summary>Plotinus describes the spiritual journey as stripping away the physical self to achieve the "Olympia of the soul," categorizing humanity into those focused on the world, those who try but fail to rise, and "divine men" who successfully reach the spiritual realm.</summary>

I write for the one who, in matters of diet and other essential concerns, differs from those who choose other ways of living; *for I direct my words to none but people like these*<note>original: page 19</note>." This treatise is also highly valuable for the historical information it contains, quite apart from the abundant philosophical beauty found within it.

The *Explanation of the Homeric Cave of the Nymphs*, which follows next, is remarkable not only for the great learning <note>original: erudition</note> it displays, but also for containing profound secrets <note>original: arcana</note> of the mythology and symbolic theology of the Greeks.

<note>f. The translator of this work and the other treatises in this volume has faced circumstances where he was forced to combine an active lifestyle with a contemplative one <note>original Greek: μετα θεωρητικου νου πολιτευομενος, meaning "conducting oneself with a contemplative mind"</note> while learning the philosophy of Plato and sharing it for the benefit of others. Because of this, he has also found it necessary to eat meat <note>original: "a fleshy diet"</note>. However, nothing but absolute necessity—due to reasons that are not necessary to detail here—could have led him to choose animal food instead of vegetable nourishment. Even though he was raised on Eleatic <note>A pre-Socratic school of philosophy focused on the nature of "Being"</note> and Academic <note>The school founded by Plato</note> studies, he did not study in the idyllic "Academic groves" of leisure.</note>

<page-num>xii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

*Auxiliaries to the Perception of Intelligibles* <note>Intelligibles are things that can only be understood by the mind or intellect, rather than the physical senses.</note> may be considered an excellent introduction to the works of Plotinus in general, from which a great part of it is extracted. In particular, it serves as an introduction to the following books by that most sublime genius: *On the Virtues*<gloss>h</gloss>; *On the Impassivity of Incorporeal Natures*<gloss>i</gloss> <note>"Impassivity" here refers to the state of being unaffected by external or physical changes.</note>; and *On Truly-Existing Being*, in which it is demonstrated that such being is everywhere one and the same whole<gloss>k</gloss>. This Porphyrian treatise is also admirably designed to assist the student of the *Theological Elements* of Proclus, a work that can never be praised enough for its scientific accuracy, profound concepts, and the clear <note>original: luminous</note> development of the most important principles <note>original: dogmas</note> it displays.

In the fourth place, Porphyry, in his treatise *On the Cave of the Nymphs*, informs us that Numenius the Pythagorean considered the character of Ulysses in the *Odyssey* as the image of a man who passes in an orderly manner over the stormy sea of "generation"—or a life governed by the senses—and thus at length arrives at a region where tempests and seas are unknown, and finds a nation

> “Who never knew salt, or heard the billows roar:”

I have endeavored, with the assistance of this

### INTRODUCTION.

suggestion, to reveal <note>original: unfold</note>, in the Appendix that concludes this work, the secret meaning of the allegory; and I trust it will be done in a way that the intelligent reader will not find either imaginary <note>original: visionary</note> or pointless.

Regarding the translation of these treatises, I have endeavored to faithfully preserve both the content <note>original: matter</note> and style of the author. I have used the best editions of these works, along with all the information that seemed most important and appropriate from the remarks of critics and philologists <note>A scholar who studies the history of language and literature.</note>, but especially from the explanations of philosophers. This, I trust, will be clear from reading the notes that accompany the translation.

Of all the other writings of Porphyry, besides those translated in this volume, unfortunately few have been preserved in their entirety <note>original: entire</note>¹, the greater part of what remains of them being fragments. Among these fragments, however, there is one very important piece recently found by Angelo Mai <note>original: Angelus Maius</note> and published by him in Milan <note>original: Mediol.</note> in 1816, as an octavo <note>original: 8vo, a book size where each sheet is folded into eight leaves</note> volume. It consists of nearly the whole of the *Epistle of Porphyry to his wife Marcella*, in which I have discovered the—

---

¹ For even regarding the treatise *On Abstinence from Animal Food*, there is every reason to believe that something is missing <note>original: wanting</note> at the end of it.

<meta>This page continues the introduction, specifically detailing the author's discovery of the Greek origins for the "Sentences of Sextus the Pythagorean" within Porphyry’s "Epistle to Marcella."</meta>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

original source of many of the *Sentences* of the celebrated Sextus the Pythagorean<gloss>m</gloss>, which have been

<gloss>m</gloss> See the Latin translation of these *Sentences* by Tyrannius Rufinus <note>original: Ruffinus</note>, in the *Small Mythological Works* <note>original: Opuscula Mythologica</note> by Thomas Gale. The *Sentences* found in this *Epistle of Porphyry* were published by me, along with some criticisms <note>original: animadversions</note>, in the *Classical Journal* about two years ago. However, due to the great importance of these *Sentences*, and for the benefit of those who may not own that journal, I will repeat here what I wrote there on this subject.

After stating that the editor deserves great praise for publishing this *Epistle*, I noted that he failed to mention the sources for many of the beautiful moral sayings that fill this work. Therefore, it is necessary to reveal <note>original: unfold</note> them to the reader, especially since this allows several of the *Sentences* of Sextus the Pythagorean to be recovered in their original Greek. I then remarked:

> “However, before revealing these sources, I will provide the reader with a correction <note>original: emendation</note> of the following faulty sentence on page 19: <term>Education consists not in the collection of much learning [but in the release] from the passions of the soul.</term> <note>original Greek: Το δε πεπαιδευσθαι ουκ εν πολυμαθειας αναληψει * * * * παλαξει δε των ψυχικων παθων εθεωρειτο.</note> The editor, not being an expert <note>original: adept</note> in the philosophy of Pythagoras and Plato, thought that *palaxei* <note>original Greek: παλαξει</note> was a real word; he notes, ‘Note the word *palaxis* <note>original Latin: Nota vocabulum παλαξις</note>,’ when in fact it is only part of a word—specifically, a fragment of *apallagei* <note>original Greek: απαλλαξει; meaning 'release' or 'liberation'</note>. Therefore, if after the word *analepsei* <note>original Greek: αναληψει; meaning 'acquisition'</note> the words *en apallagei* <note>original Greek: εν απαλλαξει; meaning 'in release'</note> are inserted, Porphyry's sentence will be perfect in both its structure and meaning. In English, it would read: ‘True education <note>original: Erudition</note> does not consist in the gathering of many facts <note>original: resumption of polymathy</note>, but is seen in a liberation from the passions belonging to the soul.’ The editor, failing to see the need for this correction, completely misunderstood the meaning of the sentence in his translation: ‘But a good education is never—’ <note>original Latin: Bonam autem institutionem nun-</note>

<vocab>Sextus Pythagoricus, Ruffinus, Porphyry, Classical Journal, Pythagoras, Plato, polymathy</vocab>

<page-num>xv</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION.

...previously supposed to exist only in the fraudulent Latin version by the priest Rufinus <note>original: Presbyter Ruffinus</note>.

> "...than I should value [an education] which, despite its abundance of learning, is stained by the contamination of the animal passions." <note>original Latin: "quam æstimem, quæ cum eruditionis copia, animalium quoque passionum contaminatione sordescat."</note>

The first sentence of which I have discovered the source is from Sextus, found on page 23: "For God is not in want of anything; but the wise man alone is in want of God." <note>original Greek: θεὸς μὲν γὰρ δεῖται οὐδενός· σοφὸς δὲ μόνου θεοῦ</note> In the version by Rufinus, this is: "God indeed lacks nothing, but the faithful man lacks God alone." <note>original Latin: "Deus quidem nullius eget, fidelis autem Dei solius." See Small Mythological Works (Opuscula Mythologica), 8vo. 1688, p. 646.</note>

2: "In every action, and in every deed and word, let God be present as the overseer and inspector" (p. 24). <note>original Greek: Πάσης πράξεως καὶ παντὸς ἔργου καὶ λόγου θεὸς ἐπόπτης παρέστω καὶ ἔφορος</note> This is evidently derived from the following sentence by Demophilus (*Small Mythological Works*, p. 621): "If you always remember that wherever your soul or your body performs any deed, God is present as an inspector in all your prayers and actions, you will reverence the nature of an inspector from whom nothing can be concealed, and you will have God for a housemate." <note>original Greek: Ἐὰν ἀεὶ μνημονεύης, ὅτι ὅπου ἂν ᾖ ἡ ψυχὴ σού, καὶ τὸ σῶμα ἔργον ἀποτελεῖ, θεὸς ἐφέστηκεν ἔφορος, ἐν πάσαις σου ταῖς εὐχαῖς καὶ πράξεσιν, αἰδεσθήση μὲν τοῦ θεωροῦ τὸ ἄληστον, ἕξεις δὲ τὸν θεὸν σύνοικον</note> What immediately follows in this paragraph is from Sextus, namely: "We should consider God as the cause of all the good things that we do." <note>original Greek: καὶ πάντων ὧν πράττομεν ἀγαθῶν τὸν θεὸν αἴτιον ἡγώμεθα</note> And Sextus says (p. 648), "God is the guide for men in good actions." <note>original Latin: "Deus in bonis actibus hominibus dux est."</note> Porphyry adds: "But we ourselves who chose them are the causes of evils; God is blameless." <note>original Greek: Τῶν δὲ κακῶν αἴτιοι ἡμεῖς ἐσμεν οἱ ἑλόμενοι, θεὸς δὲ ἀναίτιος.</note> The latter part is evidently from Sextus, who says (p. 648), "God is the author of no evil." <note>original Latin: "Mali nullius autor est Deus."</note> Porphyry further adds: "Hence we should ask of God things which are worthy of him, and which we cannot receive from another; and we pray that those things which are achieved through virtuous labors may be fulfilled after those labors." <note>original Greek: Ὅθεν καὶ εὐκταῖον τὰ ἃξια θεοῦ· καὶ αἰτώμεθα ἃ μὴ λάβοιμεν ἂν παρ' ἑτέρου· καὶ ὧν ἡγεμόνες οἱ μετ' ἀρετῆς πόνοι, ταῦτα εὐχόμεθα γενέσθαι μετὰ τοὺς πόνους.</note>

<summary>This page identifies Porphyry's "Epistle to Marcella" as the original Greek source for several famous moral maxims previously attributed to the "Sentences of Sextus" and Demophilus. It provides comparative translations to prove that these teachings on God's nature and human virtue were shared across Pythagorean and Platonic traditions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction, specifically focusing on tracing the origins of Porphyry's moral philosophy to earlier sources such as the Sentences of Sextus, Demophilus, and Pythagoras.</meta>

<page-num>xvi</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION.

...Rufinus <note>original: Ruffinus</note>. Regarding the other complete works and fragments that still exist, and also what we receive from any other: "We should pray to obtain those goods that are achieved through effort combined with virtue after the work is finished." All of this comes from Sextus. On page 648, he says: "Ask God for those things which it is fitting for God to provide. Seek from God what you cannot receive from a human. In matters where work must come first, wish for these to happen for you after the work [is done]." <note>original Latin: "Hæc posce à Deo, quæ dignum est præstare Deum. Ea pete à Deo, quæ accipere ab homine non potes. In quibus præcedere debet labor, hæc tibi opta evenire post laborem."</note> However, in this last sentence, Rufinus omitted the words "with virtue" after "work."

What Porphyry says almost immediately after this is exactly the first of the *Sentences of Demophilus* (*Small Mythological Works* <note>original: Opusc. Mythol.</note>, p. 626): "Do not ask God for what you cannot keep once you have it. For every gift of God is permanent; therefore, he will not give what you cannot retain." <note>original Greek: "Ἃ δὲ κτησάμενος οὐ καθέξεις, μὴ αἴτου παρὰ θεοῦ· δῶρον γὰρ θεοῦ πᾶν ἀναφαίρετον· ὥστε οὐ δώσει ὃ μὴ καθέξεις."</note>

The sentence following this is attributed to Pythagoras and can be found in the *Sentences of Stobaeus* (1609 edition, p. 65): "Despise those things you will not need once you are freed from the body. Instead, practice those things you will need when you are free from the body, and call upon the Gods to be your helpers." <note>original Greek: "Ὧν δὲ τοῦ σώματος ἀπαλλαγεῖσα οὐ δεηθήσῃ, ἐκείνων καταφρόνει· καὶ ὧν ἂν ἀπαλλαγεῖσα δέῃ, εἰς ταῦτα σὺ ἀσκουμένη τὸν θεὸν παρεκάλει γενέσθαι συλληπτορα."</note> In Stobaeus, however, there are some differences that make the sentence more complete. For example, "all" is added after "despise"; the word for "need" is slightly different; "the Gods" is used instead of "God"; and the phrasing for "become your helper" is more specific. Therefore, the full translation would be: "Despise all those things which, when liberated from the body, you will not want; and by training yourself in those things you will need when liberated from the body, invoke the Gods to become your helpers."

On pages 27 and 28, Porphyry says, "Since it is more desirable for you [money] to be lost in vain..." <note>original Greek: "αἱρετωτέρου σοι ὄντος [χρήματα] εἰκῇ"</note>

<page-num>xvii</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION.

Regarding the lost writings of Porphyry, I refer the reader to the *Greek Library* <note>original Latin: Bibliotheca Græca</note> by Fabricius. 

Porphyry also writes: "It should be more desirable for you to carelessly throw away riches than reason; and to be defeated when speaking the truth than to win through deception." <note>original Greek: βαλειν ἢ λογον· και το ηττασθαι τ' αληθη λεγοντα, ἢ νικαν απατωντα</note> The latter part of this sentence is also found in Sextus; for on page 649 he says, "It is better to be defeated while speaking the truth than to win while lying." <note>original Latin: Melius est vinci vera dicentem, quam vincere, mentientem</note> 

Almost immediately after this, Porphyry adds: "It is impossible for the same person to be a lover of God, a lover of pleasure, and a lover of the body. For a lover of pleasure is also a lover of the body; but a lover of the body is entirely a lover of riches; and the lover of riches is, of necessity, unjust. The unjust man is unholy toward both God and his parents, and lawless toward everyone else. Therefore, even if he sacrifices hecatombs <note>term: hecatomb|A great public sacrifice, traditionally of one hundred oxen</note> and adorns temples with ten thousand offerings, he is still impious, godless, and a thief of sacred things in his personal intentions. For this reason, one must avoid every lover of the body as if they were godless and polluted." <note>original Greek: Αδυνατον τον αυτον φιλοθεον τε ειναι και φιληδονον και φιλοσωματον· ο γαρ φιληδονος και φιλοσωματος παντως και φιλοχρηματος· ο δε φιλοχρηματος, εξ αναγκης αδικος· ο δε αδικος, και εις θεον και εις πατερας ανοσιος, και εις τους αλλους παρανομος· ωστε καν εκατομβας θυη, και μυριοις αναθημασι νεως αγαλλῃ, ασεβης εστι και αθεος και τῇ προαιρεσει ιεροσυλος· διο και παντα φιλοσωματον ως αθεον και μιαρον εκτρεπεσθαι χρη.</note> 

This sentence is the last of the *Sentences of Demophilus* (*Small Mythological Works* <note>original Latin: Opusc. Mythol.</note>, p. 625); however, in Porphyry's version, it is missing some parts and contains more than what is found in Demophilus in others. For in the first clause, "lover of riches" <note>original Greek: φιλοχρηματον</note> is missing. In the second clause, after the words "for a lover of pleasure and a lover of the body," the words "the lover of the body" are missing. 

In Demophilus, instead of the phrase "the unjust man is unholy toward both God and his parents, and lawless toward everyone else," there is only "the unjust man is unholy toward God and lawless toward men." Also, in Demophilus, after the phrase "even if he sacrifices hecatombs," the words "and adorns temples with ten thousand offerings" are missing. Conversely, in Porphyry's text, after "adorns temples," the words "he is much more unholy, and" are missing. 

This sentence, therefore, once corrected by comparing these sources, will read in English: "It is impossible for the same person to be a lover of God, a lover of pleasure, a lover of the body, and a lover of riches. For a lover of pleasure is also a lover of the body; but a lover of the body is entirely a lover of riches; and a..."

> "A lover of riches is necessarily unjust. But he who is unjust is impious towards God and his parents, and lawless towards others. So that, though he should sacrifice hecatombs <note>term: hecatombs|A great public sacrifice, traditionally of one hundred oxen</note>, and adorn temples with ten thousand gifts, he will be much more unholy, impious, atheistical, and sacrilegious in his deliberate choice. Hence it is necessary to avoid every lover of the body as one who is without God and is defiled."

3. The following passages in the epistle of Porphyry are from Sextus: "The man who is worthy of God, will be himself a god," <note>original Greek: Ο δε αξιος ανθρωπος θεου, θεος αν ειη</note> (page 30). And Sextus says, "A man worthy of God is a god even among men" <note>original Latin: Dignus Deo homo, deus est et in hominibus</note> (page 654). 

Porphyry says, "And you will honor God in the best manner when you make your own reasoning power similar to God," <note>original Greek: Και τιμησεις μεν αριστα τον θεον, οταν τη θεω την σαυτης διανοιαν ομοιωσης</note> (page 30). Thus also Sextus says, "He honors God best who makes his mind, as much as possible, similar to God" <note>original Latin: Optime honorat Deum ille, qui mentem suam, quantum fieri potest, similem Deo facit</note> (page 655). 

Again, Porphyry says, "God strengthens a man when he performs beautiful deeds; but an evil daemon <note>term: daemon|In Greek philosophy, a 'daemon' is a tutelary spirit or guiding divinity, not necessarily evil like the modern 'demon'</note> is the leader of bad actions," <note>original Greek: Θεος δε ανθρωπον βεβαιοι πρασσοντα καλα· κακων δε πραξεων κακος δαιμων ηγεμων</note> (page 31). And Sextus says, "God confirms the good acts of men. Of bad acts, an evil daemon is the leader" <note>original Latin: Deus bonos actus hominum confirmat. Malorum actuum, malus dæmon dux est</note> (page 653). 

Porphyry adds, "The soul of the wise man is adapted to God; it always beholds God, and is always present with God," <note>original Greek: Ψυχη δε σοφου αρμοζεται προς θεον, αει θεον ορα, συνεστιν αει θεω</note> (page 31). Thus, too, Sextus says, "The soul of the wise man hears God, the soul of the wise man is adapted by God, and the soul of the wise man is always with God" <note>original Latin: Sapientis anima audit Deum, sapientis anima aptatur à Deo, sapientis anima semper est cum Deo</note> (page 655). There is, however, some difference between the original Greek and the Latin version, which is most probably owing to the deception of Rufinus <note>note: Rufinus of Aquileia (c. 345–410 AD) was a monk and translator known for taking liberties with his translations of Greek texts into Latin</note>. # INTRODUCTION.

work <note>original: "work" refers back to the *History of the Restoration of the Platonic Theology* mentioned on the previous page</note>, in speaking of Porphyry's lost treatise on the *Reascent of the Soul*, I have given a [missing text].

In the last place, Porphyry says, "Philanthropy should be considered by you as the foundation of piety," <note>original Greek: Αλλα κρηπις ευσεβειας σοι νομιζεσθω η φιλανθρωπια</note> (page 58). And Sextus says, "The foundation and beginning of the worship of God is to love God's men" <note>original Latin: Fundamentum et initium est cultûs Dei, amare Dei homines</note> (page 654). Ruffinus <note>A 4th-century monk and translator known for adapting Greek works for Latin-speaking Christians</note>, however, in his version, dishonestly translates philanthropy as *loving the men of God*, so that this sentence, as well as the others, might appear to be written by Sixtus the Bishop! <note>Sixtus II was a 3rd-century Pope; Taylor suggests Ruffinus altered the text to make a pagan Pythagorean work appear Christian.</note>

4. The learned reader will find the following passages in the *Epistle of Porphyry* to be sentences originally from Demophilus, namely: 

*   "For to speak the word of God to those corrupted by opinion..." up to "...carries an equal weight," <note>original Greek: Λογον γαρ θεου τοις υπο δοξης διεφθαρμενοις λεγειν... ισον φερει</note> (page 29). 
*   "It is not the tongue of the wise man that is precious to God..." up to "...only he knows how to pray," <note>original Greek: Ουχ η γλωττα του σοφου τιμιον παρα θεω... μονος ειδως ευξασθαι</note> (page 32). 
*   "The gods do not harm us out of anger..." up to "...to God, nothing is unintended," <note>original Greek: Ου χολωθεντες ουν οι θεοι βλαπτουσι... θεω δε ουδεν αβουλητον</note> (page 35). 
*   "Neither tears nor supplications turn God, nor do animal sacrifices honor God, nor does a multitude of offerings adorn God..." up to "...provision for the sacrilegious," <note>original Greek: Ουτε δακρυα και ικετειαι θεον επιστρεφουσι, ουτε θυηπολια θεον τιμωσιν, ουτε αναθηματων πληθος κοσμουσι θεον... ιεροσυλοις χορηγια</note> (page 36). In this passage, however, there is a remarkable difference, as the learned reader will find, between the text of Porphyry and that of Demophilus. 
*   "If you always remember that wherever your soul walks, and your body completes a task..." <note>original Greek: Εαν ουν αει μνημονευης, οτι οπου αν η ψυχη σου περιπατη, και το σωμα ενεργον αποτελη; Taylor notes to <term>read as</term> (lege) εργον (ergon/work) rather than ενεργον (energon/active)</note> up to "...God as a companion," <note>original Greek: τον θεον συνοικον</note> (page 37). 
*   "The intelligent and God-loving man..." up to "...acts with diligence," <note>original Greek: Ο συνετος ανηρ και θεοφιλης... σπουδαζεται ποιησας</note> (page 54). 
*   "And the wise man, sent forth naked..." up to "...God who listens," <note>original Greek: Γυμνος δε αποσταλεις [σοφος]... επηκοος ο θεος</note> (page 54). 
*   "It is more difficult to be a slave to passions than to tyrants" <note>original Greek: Χαλεπωτερον δουλευειν παθεσιν ἢ τυραννοις</note>. 
*   And "for as many passions as there are of the soul, so many are its cruel masters," <note>original Greek: οσα γαρ παθη ψυχης, τοσουτοι και ωμοι δεσποται</note> (page 57). 
*   And lastly, "it is far better to die than to darken the soul through lack of self-control" <note>original Greek: πολλω γαρ κρειττον τεθναναι ἢ δι' ακρασιαν την ψυχην αμαυρωσαι</note> (page 58). 

In all these passages, it will be found by comparing them with Porphyry that they occasionally differ from the text of Demophilus, yet not in a way that alters the sense.

<page-num>xx</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION.

long and most interesting extract regarding that treatise, taken from Synesius' work *On Dreams* <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD) was a philosopher-bishop who integrated Neoplatonic thought with Christianity; his work *On Dreams* discusses the nature of the soul.</note>.

I will only add that many of the *Sentences* <note>original: "Sentences"; here meaning a collection of moral aphorisms or short philosophical maxims (from the Latin *sententiae*)</note> of Demophilus can be found among those attributed to Sextus. This is not at all surprising, as it was a common practice among the Pythagoreans—due to their high ideals of friendship—to consider the work of any single member as the collective production of them all <note>The Pythagoreans lived by the motto "friends have all things in common," which extended even to their writings and discoveries.</note>.

# THE
## SELECT WORKS OF PORPHYRY.

---

->ON<-

## ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.

---

### BOOK THE FIRST.

<margin><sup>a</sup> Porphyry elsewhere calls this Firmus Castricius his friend and fellow disciple. See more concerning him in Porphyry’s *Life of Plotinus*.</margin>

I. Hearing from some of our acquaintances, Firmus<sup>a</sup>, that you have rejected a meatless <note>original: "fleshless"</note> diet and have returned to eating animal food, I did not believe the report at first. I considered your self-control <note>original: "temperance"</note> and the respect you were taught to pay to those ancient and holy men from whom we received the teachings of philosophy. 

But when others who followed confirmed this report, it seemed to me that it would be too blunt <note>original: "rustic"; meaning unrefined or lacking in philosophical grace</note> and far removed from the rational method of persuasion to simply reproach you. You have neither "fled from evil and found something better" (as the proverb says), nor have you, according to Empedocles <note>Empedocles (c. 490–430 BC) was a Greek philosopher who taught that the soul is an exile from the divine realm and must abstain from meat to return there</note>, regretted your former way of living and converted yourself to one that is more excellent. I have therefore thought it worthy of the friendship that exists between us—and also appropriate for those who have dedicated their lives to—

<summary>Porphyry addresses his friend Firmus Castricius, expressing surprise and concern over reports that Firmus has abandoned his vegetarian lifestyle. He decides to write a persuasive defense of abstinence rather than a simple rebuke.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s introduction to his treatise, where he transitions from expressing surprise at Firmus’s change of heart to investigating the possible reasons for it.</meta>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

conformably to truth, to disclose your errors through a refutation derived from rational discussion.

2. For when I considered with myself what could be the cause of this change in your diet, I could by no means suppose that it was for the sake of health and strength—as the uneducated <note>original: "vulgar and idiots"; in this context, "idiots" refers to the Greek *idiotes*, meaning private citizens or those without specialized philosophical training</note> would say. On the contrary, you yourself, when you were with us, confessed that a meatless <note>original: "fleshless"</note> diet contributed both to health and to the proper endurance of philosophical labors; and experience testifies that in saying this you spoke the truth. It appears, therefore, that you have returned to your former unlawful conduct <margin>Porphyry calls Firmus’s conduct "unlawful" <note>original: *paranomēmata*</note> because the eating of meat is, for the most part, contrary to the laws of genuine philosophy.</margin> either through some deception <margin>The original Greek text here is: "either through deception, or through thinking it makes no difference to wisdom," etc. <note>original: ἢ δι᾿ ἀπάτην οὖν, ἢ τὸ μηδὲν διαφέρειν ἡγεῖσθαι πρὸς φρόνησιν, κ.τ.λ.</note>. I have followed the reading of the scholar Felicianus, who suggests the change was driven by the deceptive idea that diet does not matter.</margin>, because you think it makes no difference to the attainment of wisdom whether you use this or that diet; or perhaps through some other cause of which I am ignorant, which excited in you a greater fear than that which could be produced by the impiety of your offense. For I would not say that you have rejected the philosophical laws which we inherited from our ancestors, and which you have so much admired, through a lack of self-control or for the sake of greedy gluttony. Nor would I say that you are naturally inferior to some of the common people, who, when they have committed themselves to certain laws—even those contrary to the ones under which they formerly lived—will suffer mutilation <note>original: "amputation"</note> rather than violate them, and will abstain from certain animals on which they previously fed more strictly than they would from human flesh.

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s address to Firmus Castricius. Porphyry expresses his disappointment that Firmus has abandoned his vegetarian lifestyle and outlines his plan to refute the common philosophical arguments used to justify eating meat.</meta>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK I</header>

...I am indignant with you because, having been persuaded by certain cold and very corrupt arguments <note>original: "sophisms"; these are clever but false arguments intended to deceive</note>, you have deceived yourself and have tried to undermine a belief <note>original: "dogma"</note> that is both ancient and beloved by the Gods. Therefore, I felt it was necessary not only to show you our own opinion on this matter but also to gather and dismantle the arguments of our opponents. Their arguments are much stronger than those you mentioned—both in their number, their power, and their overall presentation. By doing this, I will demonstrate that the truth cannot be defeated even by those arguments that seem heavy and important, let alone by superficial fallacies.

For you are perhaps unaware that many philosophers are opposed to abstaining from animal food. This is the case with those of the Peripatetic and Stoic schools, and with most of the Epicureans. The Epicureans, in particular, have written in opposition to the philosophy of Pythagoras and Empedocles, which you once studied with such great passion. Many scholars <note>original: "philologists"</note> are also opposed to this abstinence; among them, Clodius the Neapolitan wrote a treatise against those who refrain from eating meat. I will present the investigations and common arguments these men use against our belief, while omitting the specific reasons they use against the scientific demonstrations of Empedocles.

### The Arguments of the Peripatetics and Stoics, from Heraclides Ponticus <note>original: "Heraclides Ponticus"; this philosopher was a student <note>original: "auditor"</note> of Plato and Speusippus.</note>

4. Our opponents argue, first of all, that justice will be thrown into confusion—and that things which should be fixed will be disrupted—if we extend the concept of justice to irrational creatures instead of just to rational ones. They believe that only Gods and humans have a relationship with us, and that there is no real alliance between us and the animals, who [in reality] have no connection to us. If we accepted such an alliance, we could no longer use some of them for hard labor or others for food, based on the conviction that such an association...

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of arguments against vegetarianism, specifically from the perspective of the Stoics and Peripatetics, who argue that justice only applies to rational beings and that human life depends on the use of animals.</meta>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK I</header>

The distinction between us and them <note>animals</note> is similar to that of a foreign state; they belong to a different tribe than ours, and giving them the same rights is dishonorable. For someone who treats animals as if they were men—by sparing them and not harming them—tries to apply the concept of justice to something that cannot support it. In doing so, he both destroys the power of justice and corrupts what is appropriate by introducing something foreign. For it necessarily follows that either we act unjustly by sparing them, or if we spare them and do not utilize them, it will be impossible for us to live. We would also, in a way, live the lives of beasts ourselves if we rejected the utility they are capable of providing.

5. I will not bother to mention the countless multitudes of Nomads and Troglodytes <note>original: "Troglodytæ"; ancient terms for nomadic tribes and cave-dwellers</note> who know no other food than flesh. But for those of us who appear to live gently and with a love for humanity, what work would be left for us on the earth or in the sea? What illustrious art or variety in our food would remain if we conducted ourselves harmlessly and reverently toward animals, as if they were of a kindred nature with us? It would be impossible to designate any work, any medicine, or any remedy for the needs that threaten our lives; we cannot act justly unless we preserve the ancient boundaries and laws.

> To fishes, savage beasts, and birds, devoid
> Of justice, Jove <note>the Roman name for Zeus, the king of the gods</note> granted that they devour each other;
> But justice to mankind he gave. <note>Hesiod, *Works and Days*, Book I, line 275.</note>

<gloss>That is, justice toward each other.</gloss>

6. However, it is not possible for us to act unjustly toward those to whom we have no obligation to act justly. Therefore, for those who reject this reasoning, no other path of justice is left—neither broad nor narrow—that they can enter. As we have already observed, our nature is not self-sufficient but is in need of many things. If we did not use animals, our nature would be entirely destroyed and trapped in a life of extreme difficulty, lacking tools <note>original: "unorganic"</note> and deprived of necessities—

<meta>This page continues the summary of arguments against vegetarianism, concluding the views of the Stoics and Peripatetics before transitioning to the social-contract theories of the Epicureans.</meta>

...necessities, if they were excluded from the assistance derived from animals. It is also said that those first humans did not live prosperously; for this superstition <note>referring to the refusal to kill living things</note> did not stop with animals but compelled its followers even to spare plants. For indeed, what greater injury does a person commit by cutting the throat of an ox or a sheep than by cutting down a fir tree or an oak? After all, according to the doctrine of transmigration <note>original: "transmigration"; the belief that souls move from one body to another, including into plants</note>, a soul is also implanted in these trees. These, therefore, are the principal arguments of the Stoics <note>a school of philosophy emphasizing reason and the idea that only rational beings have rights</note> and the Peripatetics <note>the followers of Aristotle’s school of thought</note>.

### *The Arguments of the Epicureans, from Hermachus* <note>Hermachus was a philosopher from Mitylene, a student and the successor of Epicurus</note>.

7. The Epicureans <note>followers of the philosopher Epicurus, who focused on seeking modest pleasure and avoiding pain through rational social contracts</note>, however, recounting a long history of human development, say that the ancient lawmakers—considering the social nature of life and the way humans interact—proclaimed that manslaughter <note>the killing of another human being</note> was unholy and punished it with significant disgrace. Perhaps, indeed, a certain natural alliance exists among humans due to their similarity in form and soul. This may be why they do not as readily kill a fellow human as they do other animals that are granted for our use. 

Nevertheless, the primary reason why manslaughter was considered a grievous and impious act was the belief that it did not benefit the overall nature and condition of human life. Based on this principle, those who are capable of perceiving the advantages of this law require no other reason to be restrained from such a terrible deed. However, those who cannot fully grasp this benefit will abstain from killing each other only because they are terrified by the severity of the punishment. Those who recognize the utility of the aforementioned law will observe it willingly, while those who cannot see the benefit it provides will obey the command only out of fear of the consequences...

<summary>This page concludes the Stoic argument that vegetarianism logically leads to the absurdity of not harming plants, then introduces the Epicurean view that laws against killing humans are based on social utility and mutual benefit rather than inherent rights for all living things.</summary>
<keywords>Stoics, Peripatetics, Epicureans, Hermachus, transmigration of souls, manslaughter, social contract, ethics of killing</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the Epicurean argument regarding the origin of laws and the social contract. It explains why laws against killing were established and why punishment is necessary for those who lack the reason to see the benefits of social cooperation.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

...because they feared the threats of the laws. These threats were established by certain people for the sake of those who could not use reason to understand why the decree was beneficial, even though most people would find its benefits obvious.

8. None of the legal institutions established from the beginning—whether written or unwritten—which still exist and are passed down from one generation to another, became law through violence. Instead, they became law through the consent of those who followed them. The people who introduced these laws to the public excelled in wisdom, rather than in physical strength or the kind of power used to suppress the masses. As a result, some people were led to a rational understanding of the law's usefulness <note>original: "utility"</note>—something they previously only felt instinctively or frequently forgot. Others, however, were simply terrified by the severity of the punishments. 

There was no other way to remedy the ignorance of what is beneficial than the fear of legal punishment. Even today, this fear alone keeps the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"; referring to the general population who do not live by philosophical reason</note> in check and prevents them from doing anything—publicly or privately—that would harm the community. If all people were equally capable of observing and remembering what is advantageous, there would be no need for laws. People would naturally avoid forbidden things and do what they were expected to do. Understanding what is useful versus what is harmful is enough motivation to choose the right path and avoid the wrong one. 

The imposition of punishment, however, is meant for those who do not foresee what is beneficial. The threat of punishment <note>original: "impendent punishment"</note> forces these people to suppress the impulses that lead to harmful actions and compels them to do what is right.

9. For this reason, legislators also decreed that even involuntary manslaughter should not go entirely unpunished. This was done so that people would have no excuse to intentionally mimic such deeds—which...

<meta>This page continues the Epicurean argument regarding the role of early legislators. ...were involuntarily performed, but also that they might prevent many things of this kind from taking place, which happen, in reality, involuntarily. For this is not beneficial for the same reasons that men were forbidden from voluntarily destroying each other. Because some involuntary deeds proceed from an unstable cause that human nature cannot guard against, while others are produced by our negligence and inattention to circumstances, legislators—wishing to restrain the laziness that is harmful to our neighbors—did not leave even an involuntary harmful deed without punishment. Instead, through the fear of penalties, they prevented many offenses of this kind. 

I am also of the opinion that the killings <note>original: "slaughters"</note> which are allowed by law, and which receive their customary atonements <note>original: "expiations"</note> through certain purification rituals, were introduced by those ancient legislators. They very properly instituted these things for no other reason than their desire to prevent men as much as possible from voluntary murder. For the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> everywhere require some obstacle to stop them from quickly doing what is not beneficial to the community. 

Hence, those who first recognized this to be the case did not only ordain the punishment of fines. They also stirred up a certain other irrational dread by proclaiming that anyone who had killed a person in any way whatever was not "pure," unless they used purification rituals after the deed. In this way, the part of the soul that lacks intellect was disciplined and acquired a proper mildness. Those who governed the people invented certain "taming arts" from the very beginning to subdue the irrational impulses of desire. One of the rules <note>original: "precepts"</note> promoted on this occasion was that men should not destroy each other indiscriminately.

10. Those, however, who first defined what we ought and ought not to do, very properly did not forbid us to kill other animals. For the advantage...

This result is achieved through the opposite practice, since it is impossible for humans to be preserved unless they strive to defend those raised alongside them from the attacks of other animals. At that time, therefore, some of those with the most refined <note>original: "elegant"</note> manners remembered that they avoided killing <note>original: "abstained from slaughter"</note> because it served public safety. They also reminded the rest of the people in their social circles of the consequences of this restraint. By refraining from killing their own kind, they could maintain the community <note>original: "communion"</note> that greatly contributes to the personal safety of every individual. 

It was found useful for people not to separate from each other and to avoid harming those living in the same place. This was necessary not only to repel attacks from other species of animals, but also to defend against humans who intended to act wickedly <note>original: "nefariously"</note>. Therefore, for these reasons, they avoided killing other humans to a certain extent—specifically to ensure cooperation in life's necessities and to provide a practical solution to each of the hardships mentioned above. Over time, however, the human population grew through social interaction and became more widely spread. As other animal species were driven away, certain individuals began to think rationally about what was useful for human sustenance <note>original: "nutriment"</note>. They no longer relied on mere instinct to remember these benefits.

11. Consequently, they worked even harder to restrain those who were quick to kill one another and who, by forgetting past events, left themselves with a weaker <note>original: "imbecile"</note> defense. To achieve this, they introduced those legal systems <note>original: "institutes"</note> that still exist in cities and nations today. The general population voluntarily agreed to these laws because they now better understood the advantages of living together in society with...

<meta>This page continues the argument that laws regarding the killing of animals are based on human utility and self-preservation. It explains that both the extermination of dangerous predators and the controlled slaughter of domestic livestock serve to protect human resources and safety.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>

...each other. For the destruction of everything harmful <note>original: "noxious"</note>, and the preservation of that which helps to eliminate such harm, both contribute to a life without fear. Therefore, it is reasonable to suppose that one of the practices mentioned above was forbidden, while the other was not. 

Nor should it be said that the law allows us to destroy animals that do not harm human nature and are not in any other way injurious to our lives. For, practically speaking, none of the animals the law permits us to kill fall into that category; if we allowed them to increase too much, they would eventually become harmful to us. 

However, because of the number of them currently kept, certain advantages are granted to human life. Sheep and cattle, and every similar animal, are beneficial to our necessary needs when their numbers are moderate. But if they become extremely numerous <note>original: "redundant in the extreme"</note> and far exceed the number that is sufficient, they then become detrimental to our lives. The latter (cattle) would use their strength against us—which they possess as an innate power of nature—and the former (sheep) would consume the food <note>original: "nutriment"</note> that grows from the earth intended for our benefit alone. 

Because of this, the slaughter of these kinds of animals is not prohibited, so that we may keep only as many as are sufficient for our use and can be easily managed <note>original: "subdue"</note>. Horses, cattle, and sheep, and all tame animals, are not like lions and wolves or any of the so-called wild <note>original: "savage"</note> animals. With wild animals, whether their number is small or great, there is no amount of them that could be kept to help ease the necessities of our life. For this reason, we utterly destroy some of them; but of the others, we take only as many as are found to be more than what is useful for us.

...established the laws from the beginning; and that advantage and disadvantage were the reasons why some animals were permitted to be eaten and others were not. Therefore, those who claim that everything beautiful and just exists only according to the specific opinions of the people who established the laws are incredibly foolish <note>original: "full of a certain most profound stupidity"</note>. For it is impossible for justice to work differently than any other useful thing in life, such as things that are healthy <note>original: "salubrious"</note> or a countless multitude of other benefits. 

However, mistaken opinions are held on many specific points, regarding both public and private matters. Some people do not recognize those legal principles <note>original: "institutes"</note> that are suited to all people alike. Instead, some treat them as if they are matters of indifference and ignore them, while others—holding the opposite view—mistakenly believe that things that are not universally profitable are beneficial everywhere. Because of this, they cling to practices that are inappropriate, even though in some cases they do discover what is beneficial for themselves and what contributes to the general good <note>original: "utility"</note>. 

Included among these practices are the eating of animals and the legally required killings <note>original: "destructions"</note> of them that are established by most nations because of the specific nature of their region. However, it is not necessary for us to keep these rules <note>original: "institutes"</note>, because we do not live in the same place as the people who originally created them. 

Therefore, if it were possible to make a kind of contract <note>original: "compact"</note> with other animals just as we do with humans—agreeing that we should not kill them, they should not kill us, and we should not destroy them indiscriminately—it would be good to extend justice to include them. For expanding justice in this way would provide us with safety. But since it is impossible for animals, who do not possess reason, to share in the law with us, we cannot gain any more benefit from a sense of security with animals than we can from inanimate objects. We can only...

### *The Arguments of Claudius the Neapolitan, who published a Treatise against Abstinence from Animal Food.*

<margin>
Homer, *Iliad*, Book 11, line 479.
Homer, *Iliad*, Book 4, line 35.
Homer, *Iliad*, Book 22, line 347.
</margin>

13. It now remains for us to bring forward what the common people and the general public <note>original: "plebeians and the vulgar"</note> are accustomed to say on this subject. For they say that the ancients abstained from eating animals not out of religious devotion <note>original: "piety"</note>, but because they did not yet know how to use fire. However, they claim that as soon as humans became acquainted with the usefulness of fire, they then considered it to be most honorable and sacred. They likewise called fire Vesta <note>Vesta was the Roman goddess of the hearth and the sacred fire.</note>, and from this the name *convestals* <term>convestals: those who share a hearth or home; companions</term> was derived; after this, they began to use animals for food. For it is natural for man to eat meat, but contrary to his nature to eat it raw. Therefore, once fire was discovered, they embraced what is natural and allowed the eating of boiled and roasted meat. 

Because of this, lynxes are said by Homer to be *raw-flesh eaters* <term>original: "crudivorous," from the Latin *crudus* (raw) and *vorare* (to devour)</term>; and of King Priam, also, Homer says—as a mark of disgrace—

-> "You, O Priam, devour raw meat." <-

And, 

-> "Tearing it apart, he devoured the raw meat." <note>original: "Raw flesh, dilacerating, he devoured."</note> <-

This is said as if the eating of raw meat belonged only to the unholy <note>original: "impious"</note>. Telemachus, also, when the goddess Minerva <note>In the *Odyssey*, the goddess Athena often appears to Odysseus's son, Telemachus, in disguise; the translator uses her Roman name, Minerva.</note> was his guest, placed before her not raw meat, but roasted. At first, therefore, men did not eat animals because man is not naturally a devourer of raw meat. But when the use of fire was discovered, fire was employed not only for the cooking of meat, but also for most other foods. For man is not naturally adapted to eat...

<summary>This page presents the arguments of Claudius the Neapolitan, who suggests that meat-eating is natural to humans but required the discovery of fire to become a civilized practice. He uses Homeric epics to argue that eating raw meat is a sign of savagery, whereas roasting meat is a mark of human culture.</summary>

<keywords>Epicureans, Claudius the Neapolitan, Vesta, fire, Homer, Iliad, Priam, Telemachus, meat-eating, raw meat, nature vs. culture</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the arguments against abstinence from animal food, specifically addressing the idea that meat-eating is natural to humans and that killing animals is a form of "just war" for human preservation.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

eating raw flesh is evident from certain nations that feed on fish. For these people roast them—some upon stones that are intensely heated by the sun, and others roast them in the sand. That man is naturally adapted to feed on flesh is evident from the fact that no nation entirely abstains from animal food. Nor is this a practice adopted by the Greeks through moral corruption, since the same custom is accepted by the foreigners <note>original: "barbarians"</note>.

14. Anyone who forbids humans from feeding on animals and considers it unjust will also argue that it is not just to kill them or deprive them of life. Nevertheless, a natural and just war is ingrained in us against beasts <note>original: "brutes"</note>. For some of them voluntarily attack humans, such as wolves and lions. Others do so involuntarily, like serpents, which do not bite unless they are stepped on. Some animals attack people directly, while others destroy the fruits of the earth. 

For all these reasons, we do not spare the lives of beasts. We destroy those who initiate hostilities against us, as well as those who do not, to prevent ourselves from suffering any harm from them. There is no one who, if he sees a serpent, will not destroy it if he is able, so that neither it nor any other serpent may bite a person. This arises not only from our hatred of those creatures that destroy our race, but also from the natural kindness that exists between one human and another. 

But even though the war against beasts is just, we still abstain from eating many that live alongside humans. For this reason, the Greeks do not feed on dogs, horses, or donkeys, because the tame versions of these animals belong to the same species as the wild ones. Nevertheless, they do eat pigs <note>original: "swine"</note> and birds. They argue that a pig is useful for nothing but food. The Phoenicians and Jews, however, abstain from it because, essentially, it is not native to those places. Indeed, it is said that this animal is not seen in Ethiopia even today. Just as no Greek sacrifices a camel or an elephant to the Gods because Greece does not produce these animals, so a pig is not sacrificed to the Gods in Cyprus or Phoenicia because it is not—

<summary>This page argues that meat-eating is a universal human trait and justifies the killing of animals as a "just war" for human safety and crop protection. It also attempts to explain regional dietary taboos, such as the Jewish avoidance of pork, as being based on the local availability of animals rather than moral or religious reasons.</summary>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's arguments against vegetarianism, specifically addressing why certain animals are not eaten and arguing that meat-eating is beneficial for health and necessary for ecological balance.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD—BOOK I</header>

...native <note>original: "indigenous"</note> to those places. For the same reason, the Egyptians do not sacrifice this animal to the gods. In short, the fact that some nations abstain from eating pigs is similar to our own unwillingness to eat the flesh of camels.

15. But why should anyone abstain from eating animals? Is it because eating them makes the soul or the body worse? It is evident, however, that neither is harmed by it. Animals that feed on flesh are more intelligent than others, as they are <term>venatic</term> <note>skilled in hunting</note> and possess the skill to supply themselves with food and gain power and strength; this is clearly seen in lions and wolves. Therefore, eating flesh injures neither the soul nor the body. 

This is also obvious from the example of <term>athletæ</term> <note>athletes</note>, whose bodies become stronger by eating meat, and from physicians, who restore patients to health by prescribing animal food. Indeed, it is a strong sign that Pythagoras was not thinking rationally that none of the other wise men shared his opinion. Neither any of the Seven Wise Men <note>A group of seven 6th-century BCE Greek philosophers, statesmen, and lawgivers renowned for their wisdom.</note>, nor any of the <term>physiologists</term> <note>natural philosophers who studied the physical world</note> who lived after them, nor even the incredibly wise Socrates or his followers, ever adopted his views.

16. However, let us assume for a moment that everyone was convinced of this doctrine regarding abstinence from animals. What would then limit the breeding of animals? No one is ignorant of how many offspring pigs and hares produce. Add all other animals to that list. Where would they find enough pasture? And what would farmers <note>original: "husbandmen"</note> do? They would not be allowed to destroy the animals that destroy their crops. 

The earth would not be able to support such a multitude of animals. Decay would also be caused by the rotting bodies of those that die naturally. Thus, once a plague <note>original: "pestilence"</note> broke out, there would be no refuge left. The seas, rivers, and marshes would be filled with fish, and the air with birds, while the earth would be overrun with every kind of creeping creature.

17. Likewise, how many people would be prevented from having their diseases cured if they abstained from using animals in medicine?

<summary>The author argues that eating meat does not harm the soul or body, citing the strength of athletes and the wisdom of Socrates. He also warns that universal vegetarianism would lead to an ecological catastrophe of overpopulation and disease.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Socrates, Seven Wise Men, animal overpopulation, medical use of animals, health and meat-eating, venatic, athletes</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the arguments against vegetarianism, focusing on the medicinal benefits of eating animals and a philosophical argument regarding the reincarnation of souls.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

17. For we see that those who are blind recover their sight by eating a viper. A servant of Craterus <note>Craterus was a famous physician, likely the one who served Alexander the Great.</note>, the physician, happened to be seized with a new kind of disease in which the flesh fell away from the bones. He derived no benefit from medicines; but by eating a viper prepared in the same way as a fish, the flesh became fused <note>original: "conglutinated"</note> to the bones, and he was restored to health. Many other animals and their various parts also cure diseases when they are properly used for that purpose; anyone who rejects animal food will be deprived of all these remedies.

18. But if, as they say, plants also have a soul, what will become of our lives if we destroy neither animals nor plants? If, however, a person is not considered impious <note>disrespectful to the gods or natural law</note> for cutting down plants, then neither is the person who kills animals.

19. But someone may, perhaps, say that it is not proper to destroy that which belongs to the same group as ourselves, if the souls of animals are of the same nature as our own. If, however, it should be granted that souls enter into bodies voluntarily, it must be said that they do so through a love of youthful vitality <note>original: "juvenility"</note>; for in the season of youth, there is an enjoyment of all things. Why, therefore, do they not enter into the nature of man again? 

If they enter bodies voluntarily for the sake of youth, and pass through every species of animal, they would actually be very happy to be killed. For in this way, their return to the human form will be more rapid. The bodies that are eaten will not produce any pain in the souls of those bodies, because the souls have been liberated from them; indeed, those souls will want to be placed back into the nature of man. Hence, just as much as they suffer when leaving the human form, they will rejoice when they leave other animal bodies. By doing so, they will more swiftly become human again—the species which rules over all irrational animals <note>animals lacking the human capacity for reason</note> in the same way that God rules over men. There is, therefore, a sufficient reason for destroying other animals: namely, that they act unjustly by destroying men. But if the souls of men are immortal, while those of irrational animals are mortal, men...

<summary>This page argues that animal products are necessary for medicine and suggests that if souls reincarnate, killing animals actually benefits them by speeding their return to human form.</summary>
<keywords>Craterus, medicinal vipers, transmigration of souls, animal souls, hierarchy of nature, reincarnation, juvenility</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a series of arguments against vegetarianism, specifically addressing the ideas that killing animals might benefit their souls and that using animal products like wool and milk would be equally "unjust" if killing were forbidden.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

...will not act unjustly by destroying irrational animals. And if the souls of animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> are immortal, we shall benefit them by liberating them from their bodies. For, by killing them, we shall cause them to return to human nature.

20. If, however, we only defend ourselves by putting animals to death, we do not act unjustly, but we take vengeance on those that injure us. Hence, if the souls of animals are indeed immortal, we benefit them by destroying them. But if their souls are mortal, we do nothing impious <note>disrespectful to divine law</note> in putting them to death. And if we defend ourselves against them, how is it possible that in so doing we should not act justly? For we destroy, indeed, a serpent and a scorpion, even though they do not attack us, in order that some other person may not be injured by them; and in so doing we defend the human race in general. But shall we not act justly in putting those animals to death which either attack men, or those that associate with men, or injure the fruits of the earth?

21. If, however, someone should, nevertheless, think it is unjust to destroy animals, such a person should neither use milk, nor wool, nor sheep, nor honey. For, just as you injure a man by taking from him his garments, you also injure a sheep by shearing it. For the wool which you take from it is its clothing <note>original: "vestment"</note>. Milk, likewise, was not produced for you, but for the young of the animal that has it. The bee also collects honey as food for itself; which you, by taking away, use for your own pleasure. I pass over in silence the opinion of the Egyptians, that we act unjustly by meddling with plants. But if these things were produced for our sake, then the bee, being a servant to us, produces <note>original: "elaborates"</note> honey, and the wool grows on the back of sheep so that it may be an ornament to us and provide us with a gentle warmth <note>original: "bland heat"</note>.

22. Cooperating also with the Gods themselves in what contributes to piety <note>religious devotion</note>, we sacrifice animals: for, of the Gods, Apollo, indeed, is called the *Slayer of Wolves* <note>original: "λυκοκτονος" (lykoktonos)</note>; and Diana <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Artemis.</note> is called the *Destroyer of Wild Beasts* <note>original: "θηροκτονος" (thēroktonos)</note>.

<summary>This page presents arguments that killing animals can be seen as a benefit to their souls or a matter of human self-defense, while also suggesting that if killing animals were unjust, then using any animal products like wool or milk would also be forbidden.</summary>
