<meta>This page continues the presentation of arguments against vegetarianism, specifically countering the Pythagorean tradition by citing the behavior of heroes and the practical dangers of overpopulation by wild animals.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
->ON ABSTINENCE FROM<-

Demi-gods likewise, and all the heroes who excel us in both ancestry and virtue, have so thoroughly approved of the slaughter of animals that they have sacrificed to the Gods *Dodeceïdes* <gloss>sacrifices of twelve animals</gloss> and *Hecatombs* <note>original: "Hecatombs"; a grand sacrifice of one hundred oxen</note>. Furthermore, Hercules is celebrated for being an *ox-devourer*.

23. It is, however, stupid to say that Pythagoras exhorted men to abstain from animals in order to prevent them from eating each other as much as possible. For if all men at the time of Pythagoras were <term>anthropophagites</term> <note>original: "anthropophagites"; cannibals</note>, he must have been out of his mind to lead men away from eating other animals so that they might abstain from devouring each other. In that case, he should have instead encouraged them to become cannibals, by showing them that it was an equal crime to devour each other as it was to eat the flesh of oxen and swine. But if men at that time did not eat each other, what reason was there for this teaching? And if he established this law only for himself and his associates, the suggestion that he did so is disgraceful; for it implies that those who lived with Pythagoras were cannibals.

24. Indeed, we argue that the exact opposite of what he predicted would happen. For if we abstained from animals, we would not only be deprived of pleasure and the wealth derived from them, but we would also lose our fields, which would be destroyed by wild beasts. The whole earth would be occupied by serpents and birds, making it difficult to plow the land; the scattered seeds would immediately be gathered up by the birds; and all fruits that had reached maturity would be consumed by four-legged animals. Men, being oppressed by such a lack of food, would be compelled by harsh necessity to attack each other.

25. Moreover, the Gods themselves, as a remedy, have delivered commands to many people concerning the sacrifice of animals. History is full of instances of...

<meta>This page continues the list of arguments used by opponents of vegetarianism. After arguing that wild animals would overrun the earth, the text now claims that even the Gods have commanded animal sacrifice and consumption in times of necessity or through divine miracles.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

...the Gods having ordered certain persons to sacrifice animals, and, when sacrificed, to eat them. For example, during the return of the Heraclidae <note>original: "Heraclidæ"; the legendary descendants of Hercules who returned to conquer the Peloponnese</note>, those who engaged in war against Lacedaemon <note>original: "Lacedæmon"; the city-state of Sparta</note>, in conjunction with Eurysthenes and Procles <note>original: "Proscles"</note>, were compelled by a lack of supplies to eat serpents, which the land at that time provided for the nourishment of the army. In Libya, also, a swarm of locusts fell to provide relief for another army that was suffering from hunger. The same thing happened at Gades <note>modern-day Cadiz, Spain</note>.

Bogus was a king of the Mauritanians who was killed by Agrippa at Mothone. In that place, he attacked the temple of Hercules, which was extremely wealthy. It was the custom of the priests to sprinkle the altar with blood daily. However, the fact that this was not done by human choice, but by divine will, was demonstrated by the situation at that time. Because the siege continued for a long time, there were no victims <term>victims</term> <note>animals intended for sacrifice</note> available. 

The priest, being unsure of how he should act, had the following vision in a dream. He seemed to find himself standing in the middle of the pillars of the temple of Hercules; he then saw a bird sitting opposite the altar, attempting to fly toward it, but which finally flew into his own hands. He also saw the altar sprinkled with its blood. Seeing this, he rose as soon as it was day and went to the altar. Standing on the tower <note>original: "turret"</note> just as he thought he did in his dream, he looked around and saw the very same bird he had seen in his sleep. Hoping, therefore, that his dream would be fulfilled, he stood still. He saw the bird fly to the altar, sit upon it, and surrender itself into the hands of the high priest. Thus the bird was sacrificed, and the altar sprinkled with blood. 

That which happened at Cyzicus, however, is even more famous than this event. While Mithridates was besieging this city, the festival of Proserpina <note>original: "Proserpine"; the Roman name for Persephone, Goddess of the Underworld</note> was being celebrated, during which it was required to sacrifice an ox. However, the sacred herds from which it was necessary the victim should be chosen...

When, therefore, the hour demanded the sacrifice, the ox lowed and swam across the sea, and the guards of the city opened the gates for it. Then the ox directly ran into the city and stood at the altar, where it was sacrificed to the Goddess. It was not unreasonable, therefore, to think it most pious to sacrifice many animals, since it appeared that the sacrifice of them was pleasing to the Gods.

26. But what would be the condition of a city if all the citizens were of this opinion [namely, that they should abstain from destroying animals]? For how would they repel their enemies when they were attacked by them, if they were extremely careful not to kill any of them? In such a case, indeed, they would be immediately destroyed. And it would be too tedious <note>original: "prolix"</note> to narrate other difficulties and inconveniences that would necessarily occur. Furthermore, that it is not impious to kill and feed on animals is evident from the fact that Pythagoras himself changed the standard diet for athletes. Though those before him permitted the athletes to drink milk and to eat cheese soaked <note>original: "irrigated"</note> in water, and others after him rejected this diet and fed them with dried figs, he abolished <note>original: "abrogating"</note> the ancient custom and allowed them to feed on flesh, finding that such a diet greatly increased their strength. Some also relate that the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, who were traditionally famous for their vegetarianism</note> themselves did not spare animals when they sacrificed to the gods. Such, therefore, are the arguments of Clodius, Heraclides Ponticus, Hermachus the Epicurean, and the Stoics and Peripatetics <note>The Stoics and Peripatetics (followers of Aristotle) were major philosophical schools that generally argued against giving moral consideration to animals</note> [against abstinence from animal food]. Among these are also included the arguments which were sent to us by you, O Castricius. As, however, I intend to oppose these opinions and those of the general public, I may reasonably preface my remarks with what follows.

27. In the first place, therefore, it must be known—

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s introduction to his defense of vegetarianism. Having previously addressed the arguments of his opponents, he now begins to define his specific audience: the philosopher seeking spiritual wakefulness.</meta>

...that my argument does not bring with it an encouragement <note>original: "exhortation"</note> for every type of person. For it is not directed to those who are occupied in menial <term>mechanical arts</term> <note>In ancient philosophy, "mechanical arts" referred to manual labor or trades, which were thought to leave little time for spiritual contemplation.</note>, nor to those who are engaged in athletic exercises; neither to soldiers, nor sailors, nor public speakers <term>rhetoricians</term>, nor to those who lead a life of public action. But I write to the person who considers what he is, where he came from, and where he ought to go, and who—in what pertains to food <note>original: "nutriment"</note> and other necessary concerns—is different from those who choose other ways of life; for I direct my discourse to no one but such as these. 

For, even in this common life, the same advice cannot be given to a sleeper—who tries to sleep through his whole life and, for this purpose, gathers sleep-inducing <term>soporiferous</term> things from all places—as is given to the person who is anxious to drive away sleep and to keep everything around him in a state of alertness <note>original: "vigilant condition"</note>. To the former, it is necessary to recommend intoxication, overeating <note>original: "surfeiting"</note>, and indulgence <note>original: "satiety"</note>, and to urge him to choose a dark house, and

->A bed luxurious, wide, and soft,—<-

as the poets say; and he should procure for himself all such things as cause drowsiness, and which produce sluggishness and forgetfulness <note>original: "oblivion"</note>, whether they are scents, ointments, or liquid or solid medicines. But to the latter, it is necessary to advise the use of a sober drink without wine, a light <term>attenuated</term> diet almost approaching to fasting, and a bright house that enjoys fresh air and wind. One must urge him to be intensely active through care <note>original: "solicitude"</note> and thought, and to prepare for himself a small and hard bed. 

But whether we are naturally suited to this—I mean to a life of wakefulness, giving as little as possible to sleep, since we do not live among those who are perpetually awake—or whether we are designed to be in a drowsy state of existence, is a matter for another dis-

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that the philosopher must choose a diet that supports spiritual wakefulness. He argues that most human laws are designed for the average person, not the person seeking a higher, contemplative life.</meta>

28. However, for the person who once suspects the enchantments that accompany our journey through this present life and those belonging to the place where we live; who also realizes that he is naturally alert and considers the sleep-inducing <note>original: "somniferous"</note> nature of the region he inhabits—it is to this person we speak. We prescribe food that is consistent <note>original: "consentaneous"</note> with his suspicions and his knowledge of this earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> home, and we urge him to let those who are drowsy stay stretched out on their beds, lost in sleep. 

For it is necessary to be cautious; just as those who look at people with sore eyes catch an eye infection <term>ophthalmy</term> <note>original: "ophthalmy"; an ancient term for various inflammatory eye conditions.</note>, and just as we yawn <note>original: "gape"</note> when we are with those who are yawning, we too might be filled with drowsiness and sleep. This is especially true since the region we inhabit is "cold" and likely to fill the eyes with discharge <term>rheum</term> <note>original: "rheum"; a watery discharge from the eyes, nose, or mouth.</note>; it is marshy in nature and drags down all its inhabitants into a state of sleepiness and forgetfulness <note>original: "oblivious condition"</note>. 

If, therefore, lawmakers had established laws for cities with a view toward a contemplative and intellectual life, it would certainly be necessary to obey those laws and comply with what they decreed regarding food. But if they established their laws with an eye toward a life lived merely according to basic nature—which is considered a middle ground between an animal-like <note>original: "irrational"</note> and an intellectual life—and if they looked only to what the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> accept (who believe that external things and things pertaining to the body are the only "goods" or "evils"), then why should anyone, by citing these laws, try to undermine a way of life that is more excellent than any law written for the masses? Such a life is especially in harmony with an unwritten and divine law. For that is the truth of the matter.

29. The contemplation that brings us happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note> does not consist, as some might think, in a vast number of discussions and academic subjects <note>original: "disciplines"</note>; nor is it increased by a great quantity of words. For if this were so, nothing would prevent those from being...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's discussion on the necessity of a lifestyle that matches one's intellectual pursuits. He moves from criticizing laws made for the masses to defining the ultimate goal of the philosopher: the contemplation of true reality and the union of the mind with that reality.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>->ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.<-</header>

Happy is the person by whom all branches of study <note>original: "disciplines"</note> are collected and understood. However, at present, no single branch of study completes this contemplation—not even those studies concerning truly existing beings—unless our own nature <gloss>m</gloss> and life are in harmony with them. For since it is said that every purpose has three goals <gloss>n</gloss>, our ultimate goal is to achieve the contemplation of true reality <note>original: "real being"</note>. Attaining this goal brings about, as much as possible, a union between the person contemplating and the object being contemplated. For the soul's ascent is to nothing other than true reality itself, nor is its union with anything else. Since the intellect <term>intellect</term> <note>In Platonic philosophy, "intellect" (Nous) refers to the highest part of the soul, capable of direct spiritual perception of eternal truths.</note> is true reality, our goal is to live according to the intellect.

Therefore, any discussions or external studies <note>original: "exoteric disciplines"</note> that hinder our purification do not contribute to our true happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note>. If happiness consisted only in academic achievements, then those who ignore their diet and their actions could reach this goal. But since this purpose requires us to trade the common life of the masses for a different one, and to become purified in both word and deed, let us consider which reasonings and which actions will help us achieve this end.

30. Should we say, then, that these actions are the ones that separate us from the objects of the senses <term>sensibles</term> <note>original: "sensibles"; things perceived through the five senses rather than the mind.</note> and the passions associated with them? Do they elevate us as much as possible toward a life that is intellectual, free from distracting mental images <note>original: "unimaginative"</note>, and undisturbed by emotion <note>original: "impassive"</note>? Should we also say that the opposites of these things are foreign to us and deserve to be rejected? This is especially true if they separate us from a life lived according to the intellect. I believe we must admit that we should follow whatever the intellect draws us toward. For we are like people who are either entering or leaving a foreign land,

<gloss>m</gloss> In the original Greek: "if there is not also a nature and life in accordance with these" <note>original: εαν μη προση και η κατ’ αυτα φυσιωσις και ζωη</note>; but it is obviously necessary to read "nature" <note>original: φυσις</note> instead of "production of nature" <note>original: φυσιωσις</note>.

<gloss>n</gloss> Namely: As it appears to me, a pleasurable, a profitable, and a virtuous goal. This last one is a truly beautiful and good end.

<summary>Porphyry argues that true happiness and the soul's ascent depend on a life lived in accordance with the intellect, rather than mere academic study. He defines the philosopher's goal as the union of the soul with true reality through purification and the rejection of sensory distractions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument regarding the soul's purification. He uses the metaphor of a person returning from exile in a foreign land to describe the soul's journey back to its true, intellectual home, away from the distractions of the material world.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

It is not only because we are banished from our close companions, but because we live in a foreign land, that we are filled with primitive <note>original: "barbaric"</note> passions, customs, and laws, and have a great natural tendency toward them all. Therefore, anyone who wishes to return to their true family and friends should not only start the journey with enthusiasm <note>original: "alacrity"</note> but, in order to be properly received, should reflect on how to strip away <note>original: "divest himself of"</note> everything foreign they have taken on. They must recall to their memory the things they have forgotten; without these memories, they cannot be welcomed back by their kin and friends. 

In the same way, if we intend to return to what is truly our own, it is necessary to strip ourselves of every mortal thing we have acquired, along with our attachment to them—for this attachment is the cause of our descent into this earthly <note>original: "terrestrial"</note> region. We must awaken our memory of that blessed and eternal essence and hurry our return to that nature which has no color and no physical qualities. We must earnestly strive to accomplish two things: first, to cast aside everything material and mortal; and second, to return and once again live among our true kindred, ascending to them by the opposite path we took when we descended here. 

For we were originally intellectual beings <note>original: "natures"</note>, and we still are, in our core <note>original: "essences"</note>, purified from all sense-perception and irrationality. However, we have become tangled <note>original: "complicated"</note> with physical objects <term>sensibles</term> <note>In philosophy, "sensibles" refers to things perceived by the five senses, as opposed to "intelligibles," which are perceived by the mind.</note> because we were unable to remain eternally with the intellectual world <term>the intelligible</term> and because we have the capacity to engage with earthly concerns. 

All the powers of the soul that work in connection with the senses and the body are damaged because the soul does not remain in the intellectual realm. It is like land in poor condition: even though it frequently receives the seed of wheat, it produces nothing but weeds <note>original: "tares"</note>. This is due to a certain corruption <note>original: "depravity"</note> of the soul, which—though it does not destroy the soul's fundamental nature through the creation of...

<summary>Porphyry compares the soul's life in the material world to a person living in a foreign land and argues that we must strip away physical attachments to return to our true intellectual nature. He explains that our involvement with the senses has corrupted our higher powers, much like poor soil turns good seed into weeds.</summary>
<keywords>soul's descent, purification, intellectual nature, the intelligible, material world, allegory of exile, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument regarding the soul's purification and its need to detach from the physical world. He transitions from the metaphor of a returning exile to the metaphor of an athlete stripping for a race, emphasizing that physical abstinence (like not eating meat) is only the first, most visible step in a deeper internal process of shedding irrational passions.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

irrationality; but through this, the soul is joined with a mortal nature and is drawn down from its own proper state into a foreign condition of being.

31. If, therefore, we desire to return to those natures with which we were once associated, we must endeavor to the utmost of our power to withdraw ourselves from sense-perception and imagination, and the irrationality that accompanies them, as well as from the passions that exist around them, as far as the necessities of our life permit. We should clearly organize those things pertaining to the intellect, securing for it peace and quiet from the conflict with the irrational part of ourselves. In this way, we will not only be listeners <note>original: "auditors"</note> to the intellect and to "intelligibles" <note>Intelligibles are truths or realities that can only be understood by the mind, not perceived by the senses.</note>, but may, as much as possible, enjoy the contemplation of them. By being established in a non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note> nature, we may truly live through the intellect, rather than living falsely in connection with things allied to physical bodies. 

We must, therefore, strip off <note>original: "divest ourselves of"</note> our many garments—both this visible, fleshy clothing and those internal layers with which we are inwardly clothed, and which are close to our skin <note>original: "proximate to our cutaneous habiliments"</note>. We must enter the arena <note>original: "stadium"</note> naked and unclothed, striving for the most glorious of all prizes: the Olympia of the soul <note>The "Olympia" refers to the highest spiritual achievement, likened to winning the crown at the Olympic games.</note>. 

The first thing, however, and that without which we cannot compete, is to strip ourselves of our garments. But since some of these are external and others internal, the process of stripping them away <note>original: "denudation"</note> is also twofold: one kind involves obvious things, while the other involves things that are less apparent. For instance, refusing to eat or not accepting what is offered are obvious actions; but not *desiring* them is more hidden. Therefore, along with our deeds, we must also withdraw from the lingering affection and passion we feel for them. For what benefit do we gain by abstaining from the actions themselves if we still hold tightly to the internal causes from which those actions proceed?

32. But this departure [from sense-perception, imagination, and...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's discussion on the soul's separation from the body. Having established that the soul must "strip off" its physical attachments, he now explains how to achieve this through the quiet death of the passions rather than through force.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

irrationality] can be achieved by force, but also by persuasion and reason, through the fading away—and one might say, the forgetting and death—of the passions. This is indeed the best kind of departure, since it is accomplished without harming <note>original: "oppressing"</note> that from which we are separated <note>original: "divulsed"</note>. For, in the world of physical objects <note>original: "sensibles"</note>, a separation by force cannot happen without either tearing a part or leaving a scar of the pulling away <note>original: "vestige of avulsion"</note>. But this spiritual separation is brought about by a steady disregard <note>original: "negligence"</note> of the passions. This disregard is produced by abstaining from those physical perceptions <note>original: "sensible perceptions"</note> that stir up the passions, and by keeping a steady focus on intellectual realities <note>original: "intelligibles"</note>. Among these passions or disturbances <note>original: "perturbations"</note>, we must include those that arise from food.

33. We should therefore abstain, just as we do from other things, from certain foods—namely, those that are naturally suited to stir up the emotional or "passive" part of our soul. Regarding this, we should consider the following: There are two fountains whose streams water the bond that ties the soul to the body. When the soul is filled with these, as if by deadly poisons, she forgets the proper objects of her contemplation. These fountains are pleasure and pain. The senses prepare the way for these through physical perception, along with the imagination, opinions, and memories that accompany them. When the passions are stirred by these things, and the entire irrational nature becomes strengthened or "fattened," the soul is dragged downward and abandons its natural love for true reality <note>original: "true being"</note>.

Therefore, we must separate ourselves from these as much as possible. This separation must be achieved by avoiding the passions that live through the senses and the irrational part of ourselves. The senses are used for objects of sight, hearing, taste, smell, or touch; for the senses are, in a way, the capital city <note>original: "metropolis"</note> of that foreign colony of passions within us. Let us, therefore, consider how much "fuel" for the passions enters us through each of...

<summary>Porphyry argues that the soul's detachment from the body is best achieved by ignoring the passions rather than by force. He identifies pleasure and pain as the primary sources that bind the soul to physical life, fueled by the five senses.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that the soul must be liberated from the "bonds" of the body. He is detailing how the five senses act as gateways that stir up the irrational passions, thereby trapping the soul in a state of physical distraction.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

...the senses. This is brought about partly by watching horse races and athletes <note>original: "athletæ"</note>, or those whose bodies are twisted in dance; and partly from looking at beautiful women. These things ensnare our irrational nature, attacking and overpowering it through many different kinds of deceptions.

34. For the soul, being stirred into a Bacchic frenzy <note>Bacchic fury: a state of wild, uncontrollable excitement associated with the Greek god Bacchus (Dionysus)</note> by the irrational part of ourselves, is made to leap, shout, and cry out. This outward chaos is fueled by an internal disturbance that was first sparked by the senses. 

Excitement also enters through the ears, affecting our emotions <note>original: "of a passive nature"</note>. This is produced by certain noises, sounds, indecent language, and slander. Because of these things, many people are driven away from reason and become wildly agitated; some even lose their self-control <note>original: "become effeminate"</note> and twist their bodies in all sorts of ways. And who doesn't know how much the use of incense <note>original: "fumigations"</note> and the scent of sweet perfumes—which lovers use to attract the objects of their affection—strengthens <note>original: "fattens"</note> the irrational part of the soul? 

But what need is there to speak of the passions produced through taste? Here, especially, we find a double-layered bond. One layer is fed by the emotions excited by taste. The other layer is made heavy and powerful by taking in "foreign bodies" <insert>that is, the bodies of other creatures, different from our own</insert>. For, as a certain physician once said, the only poisons are not those prepared by medical skill. There are also those which we take in every day as food—both in what we eat and what we drink. A far more deadly poison is passed to the soul through these than from the poisons specifically mixed to destroy the physical body. 

As for the sense of touch, it nearly transforms the soul into the body itself. It creates in the soul certain mindless reactions <note>original: "inarticulate sounds"</note>, similar to those that occur in lifeless objects. From all these things, memories, imaginations, and opinions are gathered together, stirring up a swarm of passions—namely, of...

<summary>Porphyry explains how the five senses—sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch—overpower reason and "fatten" the irrational soul. He specifically warns that eating animal flesh introduces "foreign bodies" that act as a spiritual poison more deadly than physical toxins.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s description of the negative psychological effects caused by the senses and physical appetites. He argues that sensory indulgence feeds passions like fear and anger, leading to a need for total withdrawal from society to achieve spiritual purity.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.</header>

...fear, desire, anger, love, pleasure-seeking <gloss>For the Greek word *philtron* (charms/potions) here, I read *philedonon* (lovers of pleasure).</gloss>, pain, envy <note>original: "emulation"</note>, anxiety <note>original: "solicitude"</note>, and disease; they cause the soul to be filled with similar disturbances <note>original: "perturbations"</note>.

**35.** Therefore, to be purified from all of these is extremely difficult and requires a great struggle. We must work hard both day and night to be freed from paying attention to them, especially because we are inevitably entangled <note>original: "complicated"</note> with our physical senses. For this reason, we should withdraw ourselves as much as possible from those places where we might, even unintentionally, encounter this hostile crowd of distractions. Based on experience, we should avoid even entering into a contest with these urges—or even trying to win a victory over them—and avoid the risk of failing due to a lack of practice <note>original: "want of exercise from inexperience"</note>.

**36.** We learn that this way of life was adopted by some of the famous ancient Pythagoreans <note>Pythagoreans: followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, known for their disciplined lifestyle and vegetarianism</note> and other wise men. Some of them lived in the most isolated places, while others lived in temples and sacred groves where, even though they were located in cities, all noise and crowds were kept away. Plato, for example, chose to live in the Academy, a place that was not only secluded and far from the city, but was also said to be unhealthy <note>original: "insalubrious"</note>. Others have even sacrificed <note>original: "not spared"</note> their own eyes out of a desire not to be pulled away <note>original: "divulged"</note> from the internal contemplation of reality. 

If someone, however, thinks they can interact with other men and fill their senses with the passions that come with such interactions, while simultaneously imagining they can remain unaffected <note>original: "impassive"</note>, they are ignorant. They deceive both themselves and those who are convinced by them. They do not see that we are enslaved to many passions simply because we do not distance ourselves from the crowd. For the author did not speak pointlessly, nor did he misrepresent the nature of the leading <note>original: "Coryphæan"</note> philosophers, when he said of them: "From their youth, these men do not even know the way to the marketplace <note>original: "forum"</note>, nor do they know where the courthouse, the senate house, or any other public assembly of the city is located. They also neither hear nor..."

<summary>Porphyry argues that sensory experiences feed destructive passions, necessitating a life of solitude and withdrawal. He cites the examples of the Pythagoreans and Plato, who sought isolated or even unhealthy environments to protect their philosophical contemplation from the distractions of the public.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the philosophical life of withdrawal, transitioning from the description of the Pythagoreans' secluded lifestyle to Plato’s description of the "leading philosopher" who is mentally detached from worldly affairs and social gossip.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

<margin><sup>P</sup> The common people <note>original: "multitude"</note> are ignorant that they are ignorant regarding objects that are the most brilliant and real. However, the leading <term>Coryphæan</term> <note>Coryphæan: from *koryphaios*, referring to the leader of a chorus; here it means the "highest" or "ideal" philosopher</note> philosopher is ignorant that he is ignorant regarding objects that are the most unsubstantial and obscure. The ignorance of the masses is the result of a lack of knowledge, but the philosopher's ignorance is the result of a superior level of intellectual focus <note>original: "transcendency of gnostic energy"</note>. What Porphyry says here of the leading philosopher is derived from the *Theaetetus* of Plato.</margin>

He does not care to see laws or decrees, whether they are spoken aloud or written down. As for the eager efforts of his associates to obtain political offices <note>original: "magistracies"</note>, their political clubs, their banquets and decadent feasts accompanied by flute-players—he does not even dream of taking part in them. Whether something in the city has happened well or ill, or what misfortune has befallen anyone from their ancestors <note>original: "progenitors"</note>, whether male or female, these things are more hidden from such a person than (as the saying goes) how many gallons <note>original: "choes"—a Greek liquid measure of about three quarts</note> the sea contains. Furthermore, he is even unaware that he is unaware<sup>P</sup> of all these details. For he does not abstain from them to gain a reputation; rather, in reality, only his body lives and moves within the city. His reasoning power, considering all these worldly matters as trivial and worthless, "is carried away," according to Pindar, "on all sides, and does not apply itself to anything that is nearby."

**37.** In this passage, Plato asserts that the leading <term>Coryphæan</term> philosopher remains unaffected <note>original: "impassive"</note> by these matters by not involving himself in them at all. Consequently, he knows the way neither to the court of justice nor the senate-house, nor anything else previously mentioned. Plato does not say that the philosopher is familiar with these things and experiences them with his senses while simply ignoring them; on the contrary, he says that by abstaining from them, he is even unaware that he is ignorant of them. He also adds that this philosopher does not even dream of attending banquets. Much less, therefore, would he be angry if he were deprived of soup or pieces of meat <note>original: "broth, or pieces of flesh"</note>; nor, in short, will he allow...

<sig>A</sig>

matters of this kind. Would he not rather consider abstaining from all these things as trivial and of no consequence, while considering the consumption <note>original: "assumption"</note> of them to be a matter of great importance and harmful <note>original: "noxious"</note>? For since there are two patterns <note>original: "paradigms"</note> in the order of things—one of a divine nature, which is most happy, and the other devoid of divinity, which is most miserable<sup>q</sup>—the <term>Coryphaean</term> <note>Coryphaean: from the Greek *koryphaios*, the leader of a chorus; here it refers to the highest or most exemplary philosopher</note> philosopher will make himself like the first, but will make himself unlike the other. He will lead a life that conforms to the pattern he resembles—namely, a life satisfied with simple <note>original: "slender"</note> food, sufficient to itself, and filled as little as possible with mortal things.

38. Therefore, as long as anyone argues about food and insists that this or that thing should be eaten—failing to realize that, if it were possible, we should abstain from all food—and by this argument follows his passions, such a person holds a foolish opinion, as if the subjects of his disagreement were of no importance. The philosopher, therefore, will not use violence to separate himself from his earthly <note>original: "terrestrial"</note> bonds <note>This likely refers to a prohibition against suicide; the philosopher must let the soul depart naturally rather than through a forced, "violent" act</note>; for anyone forced to do this remains, in spirit, in the place from which he was forced to depart. Nor should it be thought that strengthening these bonds is a matter of small importance. 

So, by granting to nature only what is necessary—and this of a light quality through minimal food—he will reject whatever exceeds this as merely contributing to pleasure. For he will be convinced of the truth of what Plato says: that sensory perception is a nail by which the soul is fastened to bodies<sup>r</sup>, through the sticking together <note>original: "agglutination"</note> of the passions and the enjoyment of physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> delight. For if sensory perceptions were not an obstacle to the pure activity <note>original: "energy"</note> of the soul, why would it be a terrible thing to be in a body, while...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument against the idea that a philosopher can indulge in worldly pleasures or various types of food while claiming their soul remains detached and unaffected. He specifically references Plato's description of the philosopher from the "Theaetetus."</meta>

at the same time the soul remained unaffected <note>original: "impassive"</note> by the motions of the body?

39. How is it, also, that you have decided and said that you are not influenced by the things which you suffer, and that you are not present with the things by which you are passively affected? For intellect, indeed, is present with itself, even though we are not always present with it. But he who departs from the intellect is in that place to which he departs; and when, by active reasoning <note>original: "discursive energies"</note>, he applies himself upwards and downwards by his apprehension of things, he is present wherever his focus <note>original: "apprehension"</note> is. But it is one thing to ignore sensory objects <note>original: "sensibles"</note> because one is focused on other things, and another for a man to think that even though he attends to sensory objects, he is not truly present with them. Nor can anyone show that Plato admits this without simultaneously proving himself to be deceived.

Therefore, he who submits to the consumption <note>original: "assumption"</note> of every kind of food, and voluntarily gives himself over to alluring spectacles, to conversation with the crowd, and to laughter—such a person, by acting this way, is mentally present where the passion is which he experiences. But he who abstains from these things because he is focused on other [higher] things is the one who, through his lack of worldly experience <note>original: "unskilfulness"</note>, excites laughter not only in Thracian maidservants <note>A reference to Plato’s *Theaetetus*, where a Thracian maid laughs at the philosopher Thales for falling into a well while looking at the stars.</note> but in the rest of the common people <note>original: "vulgar"</note>. When such a man sits at a banquet, he falls into the greatest confusion—not from any defect of his senses, nor from a superior accuracy of sensory perception while only using the irrational part of the soul (for Plato does not venture to assert this); rather, he is confused because in slanderous conversation he has nothing reproachful to say of anyone. This is because he knows no evil of anyone, having never made individuals the subject of his meditation. Being in such a state of confusion, therefore, Plato says he appears to be ridiculous. And when others are praising and boasting, he is clearly seen to laugh—not mockingly, but in reality—and so he appears to be delusional.

40. So that, through ignorance of, and abstaining...

<summary>Porphyry argues that a person's mind is present wherever their attention is directed; therefore, one cannot indulge in worldly pleasures while claiming spiritual detachment. He uses Plato's "Theaetetus" to illustrate how a true philosopher's focus on higher truths makes them appear foolish or "delusional" to the common world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that a philosopher cannot truly focus on the divine while simultaneously indulging in physical pleasures. He refutes the idea that the soul can be split, attending to higher truths while the body indulges in "sensible" or physical gratifications.</meta>

from physical <note>original: "sensible"</note> concerns, he is unacquainted with them. But it is by no means to be admitted that—even if he were familiar with sensory things and were acting through his irrational part—it would still be possible for him at the same time to genuinely contemplate the objects of pure reason <note>original: "intellect"</note>. For even those who claim that we have two souls <note>A reference to the Platonic idea of a rational and an irrational soul</note> do not admit that we can pay attention to two different things at the exact same time. For if that were true, they would be creating a union of two separate animals where, because they are engaged in different activities <note>original: "energies"</note>, one would not be able to perceive the actions of the other.

41. Furthermore, why would it be necessary for the passions to waste away, for us to "die" to them, and for this to be our daily meditation, if it were possible (as some claim) to act according to the higher mind <note>original: "intellect"</note> while we are simultaneously intimately connected with mortal concerns, and this without the oversight of the mind? For, as the poet Epicharmus says, "It is the mind that sees, and the mind that hears." <note>original: "intellect sees, and intellect hears"</note> But if it were possible to be present with immaterial things while eating luxuriously and drinking the sweetest wine, why could this not also be achieved while you are performing things that are not even appropriate to mention? For all these passions everywhere arise from the "boy"* who is within us. And you will certainly admit that the more vulgar <note>original: "baser"</note> these passions are, the more we are dragged down toward them. What distinction should be made here if you admit that it is impossible to experience some passions without being mentally present with them, yet claim you can do other things at the same time that you are contemplating divine realities <note>original: "intelligibles"</note>? It is not simply because some things are considered shameful <note>original: "base"</note> by the general public while others are not. All the passions mentioned above are shameful. Therefore, to achieve a life according to the higher mind...

* Sensory perception, and that which is beautiful in the activities of the senses, are referred to this way by Plato. <note>In Plato's "Phaedrus," the lower, desiring part of the soul is sometimes compared to a child or a "boy" that needs discipline.</note>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that the human soul cannot focus on both the physical world and the divine realm of pure reason at the same time. He specifically addresses the need to limit physical intake to the bare minimum required for survival to avoid mental distraction.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

It is necessary to abstain from all these things, in the same way as one abstains from sexual <note>original: "venereal"</note> concerns. Therefore, we must allow only a small amount of food for nature's sake, out of the necessity of physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note> [or because of our connection with a changing and transient state of being]. For where there is physical sensation and sensory perception, there is a departure and separation from the world of the mind <note>original: "intelligible"</note>. The stronger the stimulation of our irrational side <note>The part of the soul governed by appetites and emotions rather than reason</note>, the further we depart from intellectual contemplation <note>original: "intellection"</note>. It is not possible for us to be pulled back and forth between this place and that; we cannot be *here* [in the physical world] and yet simultaneously be *there* [present with the pure essence of the mind]. Our attention does not function with just a part of us, but with our entire self.

42. However, imagining that someone who is influenced by their senses can still focus on mental truths <note>original: "energize about intelligibles"</note> has led many "Barbarians" <note>This term was used by Greeks like Porphyry to refer to non-Greeks, but here likely refers to specific religious groups like the Gnostics</note> to ruin. These people arrogantly claim that although they indulge in every kind of pleasure, they can still turn themselves toward things of a higher nature than physical sensations, even while they are acting out of their irrational side. I have heard some people defending their unfortunate habits <note>original: "patronizing their infelicity"</note> in the following way:

> “We are not,” they say, “defiled by food, just as the sea is not defiled by the filth of rivers. We have power over everything we eat, just as the sea has power over all moisture <note>original: "humidity"</note>. If the sea were to close its mouth so as not to receive the streams that now flow into it, it would be great in its own eyes, but small in relation to the rest of the world, because it would be unable to absorb dirt and corruption. If it were afraid of being stained, it would not accept these streams; but because it knows its own greatness, it receives everything and is not disgusted by anything that enters it. In the same way,” they say, “if we were afraid of food, we would be enslaved by the very concept of fear. It is necessary that all things should be obedient to us. For if we collect a small amount of water that has become dirty, it is immediately stained and overwhelmed—

<summary>Porphyry argues that sensory indulgence distracts the soul from higher contemplation and refutes the "Barbarian" claim that a strong soul can indulge in any food or pleasure without being corrupted.</summary>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s rebuttal of those who believe that physical indulgence does not affect the purity of the soul. He specifically addresses the arguments of certain "Barbarians" (Gnostics) and Cynic philosophers who claim a "liberty" to eat anything without spiritual consequence.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

by filth; but this is not the case with the deep sea. In the same way, food overcomes those who are weak-willed <note>original: "pusillanimous"</note>; but for those who claim an absolute freedom regarding food, everything is eaten as nourishment, and no defilement is produced.” Therefore, these men, deceiving themselves with such arguments, act in a way that matches their own delusion. But instead of gaining freedom, they are thrown into an abyss of misery and are overwhelmed. This also led some of the Cynics to desire every kind of food because they stubbornly held onto a mistaken principle which we usually call "matters of indifference" <note>original: "a thing of an indifferent nature"; in Stoic and Cynic philosophy, "indifferents" (adiaphora) are things that are neither morally good nor bad in themselves, such as wealth or what one eats.</note>.

43. However, the person who is cautious and suspicious of nature's charms—who has examined the physical properties of the body and knows it was designed as a tool for the soul's powers—will also know how easily passion aligns itself with the body. This happens whether we want it to or not, whenever something external affects the body and that sensation finally reaches our conscious perception. For perception is essentially a response [to whatever causes that sensation]. But the soul cannot respond unless she turns herself completely toward the sound and focuses her attentive eye <note>original: "animadversive eye"</note> on the sensation. In short, the irrational part of the soul is unable to judge how, when, or what it should pay attention to. On its own, it is reckless, like horses without a charioteer <gloss>†</gloss>. Wherever it leans downward, it is carried along without any ability to control itself regarding external things. It does not know the proper time or the correct amount of food to take unless the eye of the charioteer is watching over it. The charioteer regulates and governs the impulses of the irrational part, which is essentially blind.

---

<note>† In the *Phaedrus*, Plato compares the rational part of the soul to a charioteer and the two irrational parts—*desire* and *anger*—to two horses. See my translation of that dialogue.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues against the idea that the soul is unaffected by what the body consumes, using Plato's allegory of the charioteer to show that the rational mind must actively govern the "blind" irrational appetites.</summary>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that a virtuous person must maintain the soul's dominance over the body. He uses the metaphor of a "charioteer" to describe how reason should manage the irrational impulses of the soul.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

But he who removes reason's dominion over the irrational part of the soul, and allows it to be carried along according to its own nature, will give in to desire and anger and suffer them to go as far as they please. On the contrary, the virtuous man will act in such a way that his deeds are aligned with presiding reason, even during the activities of his irrational side.

44. And in this, the virtuous man appears to differ from the wicked man: the former always has reason present—governing and guiding the irrational part like a charioteer <note>This is a famous metaphor from Plato’s *Phaedrus*, where the soul is compared to a charioteer driving two horses—one representing noble spirit and the other representing unruly physical appetite.</note>—but the latter does many things without reason as his guide. Hence, the wicked man is said to be completely irrational and is carried along in a disorderly way by his lack of reason. However, the virtuous man is obedient to reason and is superior to every irrational desire. This is the reason why the common crowd errs in words and deeds, in desire and anger, and why, conversely, good men act with rectitude <note>original: "rectitude"; meaning moral uprightness or correctness.</note>. It is because the crowd allows the "boy" within them to do whatever he pleases, while good men give themselves up to the guidance of the "boy's tutor" (that is, to reason) and govern their affairs in partnership with it. 

Therefore, in matters of food and other physical activities and enjoyments, when the charioteer is present, he defines what is appropriate <note>original: "commensurate"</note> and well-timed. But when the charioteer is absent—and, as some say, is occupied with his own higher concerns—then, if he still has our attention with him, he does not permit it to be disturbed or to act at all alongside the irrational power. If, however, he should allow our attention to be directed toward the "boy" without his own supervision, he would destroy the man, who would be recklessly carried away by the folly of the irrational part.

45. Hence, for virtuous men, abstinence in food and in physical enjoyments and actions is more appropriate than abstinence in what pertains to the sense of touch. This is because, while we touch physical objects, it is necessary that we descend from our proper conduct to the instruction of—

He explains that while the masses are led by their desires, the virtuous person uses reason to determine the appropriate amount of food and physical pleasure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument about the necessity of reason governing the physical appetite. He uses the metaphor of the "boy" (the irrational desire) and the "tutor" (reason) to explain how a philosopher should approach eating.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

...that which is most irrational in us; yet this control is even more necessary when it comes to taking in food. For our irrational nature is incapable of considering what the effects will be, because this part of the soul is essentially ignorant of anything that is not immediately present. If it were possible to be freed from food in the same way we can turn away from visible objects—for we can focus on other things once the imagination is withdrawn from them—it would be a simple task to be immediately liberated from the necessities of our mortal nature by yielding to it only slightly. 

Since, however, food requires time for cooking and digesting, along with the cooperation of sleep and rest, followed by a certain physical state resulting from digestion and the removal of waste, it is necessary that the "tutor of the boy" <note>Reason, acting as the guardian of the impulsive physical appetite.</note> within us should be present. This tutor, by selecting things of a light nature that will not be a hindrance, grants these to the body because he foresees the future. He prevents the burden that would be produced if he allowed our desires to weigh us down for the sake of the trifling pleasure arising from the swallowing <note>original: "deglutition"</note> of food.

46. Reason, therefore, rightly rejecting excess and the superfluous, will limit what is necessary to narrow boundaries. It does this so that it is not troubled by the effort of procuring the many things the body demands. By not being concerned with elegance, reason will not need a multitude of servants; it will not seek to receive great pleasure in eating, nor will it allow itself to become lazy through over-fullness <note>original: "satiety"</note>. It will not make its burden (the body) heavier and thus become sleepy <note>original: "somnolent"</note>. Nor will it allow the body to be filled with fattening things, which only strengthens the bond <note>The Neoplatonic concept of the "bond" refers to the attachment that ties the soul to the physical body and the material world.</note> between soul and body, making the person more sluggish and weak <note>original: "imbecile"</note> in the performance of his proper work. For, let any man show us one who endeavors as much as possible to live according...

<summary>Porphyry argues that reason must act as a tutor to the body's irrational desires, especially regarding food. He advocates for a simple, light diet to prevent the body from becoming a heavy "burden" that hinders the soul's higher work.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument from page 34, concluding his rhetorical point that no rational person would claim a meat-based diet is more practical or healthful for the mind than a plant-based one.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

[...and focused on] intellect, and not to be attracted by the passions of the body, that animal food is more easily obtained than food from fruits and herbs; or that the preparation of meat is more simple than that of plants; and, in short, that it does not require cooks, but—when compared with plant-based <note>original: "inanimate"</note> nourishment—is enjoyed without pleasure, is lighter in digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note>, and is more rapidly processed, excites the desires to a lesser degree, and contributes less to the strength of the body than a vegetable diet.

47. If, however, neither any physician, nor philosopher, nor athlete <note>original: "wrestler"</note>, nor any ordinary person <note>original: "one of the vulgar"</note> has dared to assert this, why should we not willingly abstain from this physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> burden? Why should we not, at the same time, liberate ourselves from many inconveniences by abandoning a meat-based <note>original: "fleshly"</note> diet? For we would not be liberated from one only, but from a myriad of evils by accustoming ourselves to be satisfied with things of the simplest <note>original: "smallest"</note> nature. Specifically, we would be freed from: an obsession with wealth, the need for numerous servants, a multitude of kitchen utensils, a state of constant sleepiness <note>original: "somnolent condition"</note>, many severe diseases, the constant need for medical assistance, incentives for sexual lust <note>original: "venery"</note>, heavy bodily odors <note>original: "gross exhalations"</note>, an abundance of bodily waste <note>original: "excrements"</note>, the coarseness <note>original: "crassitude"</note> of the body's bond to the soul, the kind of physical strength that incites one to [base] actions, and, in short, a vast epic of troubles <note>original: "an Iliad of evils," a reference to Homer's Iliad meaning a long and disastrous series of misfortunes</note>. 

But from all these, plant-based and light <note>original: "slender"</note> food—which is easily obtained—will liberate us. It will procure peace for us by preserving our power of reason. For, as Diogenes says, thieves and enemies are not found among those who feed on barley-cake <gloss>u</gloss>, but sycophants and tyrants are produced from those who feed on flesh. Thus, the cause of our being in want of many things is taken away, along with the massive amount of nourishment introduced into the body. As the weight of things to be digested is lightened, the eye of the soul <note>The Platonic concept of the mind's ability to perceive spiritual and intellectual truths.</note> will become free and will be established as in—

---

<gloss>u</gloss> A kind of bread made of milk and flour. <note>In the original Greek, this likely refers to *maza*, a simple barley cake or porridge.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that a meatless diet frees the philosopher from physical burdens, diseases, and the social vices (like greed and tyranny) associated with luxury. He emphasizes that simple food clarifies the "eye of the soul" for intellectual pursuits.</summary>

<keywords>abstinence, vegetarianism, Diogenes, digestion, eye of the soul, health, simplicity, vice vs virtue</keywords>

<page-num>36</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

-> a harbor beyond the smoke and the waves of physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> nature. <-

48. And this needs neither warning nor formal proof, because of the obviousness that immediately accompanies it. Hence, it is not only those who strive to live according to the mind <note>original: "intellect," the higher rational soul</note>, and who establish a life of the mind as the goal of their pursuits, who have perceived that this abstinence was necessary to reach that goal. Rather, as it appears to me, nearly every philosopher—preferring simple living <note>original: "frugality"</note> to luxury—has chosen a life that is satisfied with a little, rather than one that requires a multitude of things. And, though it will seem contradictory <note>original: "paradoxical"</note> to many, we shall find that this is asserted and praised even by men who thought that pleasure is the ultimate goal of those who practice philosophy. For most of the Epicureans, beginning from the leader <note>original: "Corypheus," the head of a Greek chorus</note> of their sect, appear to have been satisfied with barley-cake <note>original: "maize," likely referring to the Greek "maza" or simple grain meal</note> and fruits. They have filled their writings with demonstrations of how little nature actually requires, and how its necessities may be sufficiently satisfied by meager and easily obtained food.

49. For the wealth of nature, they say, is limited and easily obtained; but the wealth that proceeds from empty <note>original: "vain"</note> opinions is unlimited and difficult to acquire. For things that may be easily obtained beautifully and more than adequately remove the physical pain caused by lack <note>original: "indigence"</note>. These are things with the simple nature of liquid and solid food <note>original: "moist and dry aliments"</note>. But everything else, they say, which results in luxury, is not accompanied by a necessary craving <note>original: "appetition"</note>, nor is it necessarily produced by an actual pain. Instead, it arises partly from the irritation and sharp longing caused simply because something is absent; partly from a desire for joy; and partly from empty and false beliefs <note>original: "dogmas"</note>. These beliefs relate neither to any natural physical need, nor to the actual health <note>original: "dissolution," here meaning the breakdown or preservation of the body</note> of the human frame, when those luxuries are missing. For things that may be obtained everywhere are sufficient for those natural purposes.

<summary>Porphyry argues that simple living is universally recognized by philosophers as the path to a higher life, noting that even the pleasure-seeking Epicureans championed a diet of simple grains and fruits over luxury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that a simple, plant-based diet is easier to maintain than one involving meat, and that philosophy should guide how we meet our basic needs.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

which nature necessarily requires. But these things, because of their simplicity and scarcity <note>original: "paucity"</note>, are easy to obtain. Indeed, he who feeds on flesh also requires inanimate things <note>original: "inanimate natures," referring to plants</note>; but he who is satisfied with inanimate things is easily supplied with only half of what the meat-eater requires, and needs very little expense for the preparation of his food.

50. They likewise say that it is necessary for someone preparing the requirements of life not to use philosophy merely as a later addition <note>original: "accession"</note>. Instead, once he has embraced philosophy, he should, with a confident mind, truly endure <gloss>x</gloss> the daily events of life. For if we calculate and obtain what is necessary for our nature without the guidance of philosophy, we commit our own well-being to a bad counselor. Therefore, it is necessary that those who practice philosophy should provide for these needs and attend to them as diligently as possible. However, to the extent that there is a departure from philosophy—which is unable to achieve a perfect purification <gloss>y</gloss>—we should not strive to obtain either riches or nourishment. Consequently, we should engage in these practical matters in conjunction with philosophy, and be immediately convinced that it is much better to pursue that which is minimal, simple, and light in nourishment. For that which is minimal and free from trouble <note>original: "molestation"</note> is derived from that which is minimal <gloss>z</gloss>.

51. The preparation of these things also carries with it...

---

<gloss>x</gloss> In the original Greek: *alla paraskeuasamenon to tharrein te psyche gnesios outos antechesthai ton kath’ hemeran* (meaning: "but having prepared oneself to be confident in the soul, thus genuinely to endure the daily events"). The editor of the 1767 quarto edition, who appears to have been nothing more than a mere verbal critic, says in a note on this passage that the word *antechesthai* signifies *pertinacissime illis inhærere, nihil ultra studere* <note>Latin: "to cling to those things most stubbornly, to study nothing further"</note>. However, it must be obvious to anyone who understands the context that in this place it signifies *to endure*.

<gloss>y</gloss> In the original Greek: *ho me kyrieuei tes teleias ektharreseos* (meaning: "that which does not master the perfect confidence"). But for *ektharreseos* (confidence), I follow the reading of Felicianus: *ekkatharseos* (purification).

<gloss>z</gloss> In the original Greek: *elachiston gar kai to ochleron ek tou elachistou* (meaning: "for the least and the troublesome is from the least"). But it is obviously necessary to read *anochleron* (free from trouble) instead of *ochleron* (troublesome). Even so, this error was not noticed by the German editor of this work, Jacob Rhoer.

<summary>Porphyry argues that a simple, plant-based diet is more economical and easier to prepare than a meat-based one, and stresses that philosophy must guide our physical needs to achieve spiritual purification.</summary>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that luxurious foods (especially meat) create physical and mental obstacles for the philosopher, while simple food satisfies natural needs without causing distress.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM [ANIMAL FOOD]</header>

[The preparation of these things also carries] with it many obstacles <note>original: "impediments"</note>, whether from the weight of the body [which such foods tend to increase], the difficulty of obtaining them, or because they interrupt the continuous activity of our highest reasoning processes <note>original: "energy of our most principal reasonings"</note> <sup>a</sup>, or for some other reason. For this mental activity then immediately becomes useless and is changed by the accompanying disturbances <note>original: "concomitant perturbations"</note>.

A philosopher, however, should hope that he will not lack anything throughout his life. This hope is well maintained by things that are easy to get; on the other hand, this hope is frustrated by things of a luxurious nature <note>original: "sumptuous nature"</note>. For this reason, the masses—even if they have plenty—constantly struggle to get more, as if they were in poverty. But remembering that even the greatest possible wealth has no real power to calm the anxieties <note>original: "dissolving the perturbations"</note> of the soul will make us satisfied with simple things that are easy to find.

Simple and obvious things, which can be obtained with great ease, remove the physical cravings <note>original: "tumult occasioned by the flesh"</note>. Furthermore, lacking luxury items will not bother someone who meditates on death. Moreover, the pain of poverty <note>original: "indigence"</note> is much milder than the pain caused by overeating <note>original: "repletion"</note>. Anyone who does not deceive himself with empty opinions will recognize this.

A variety of food does not calm the disturbances of the soul; it does not even increase the physical pleasure felt by the body. For pleasure ends as soon as the pain [of hunger] is removed <sup>b</sup>. Therefore, eating meat does not remove anything truly harmful to our nature, nor does it achieve anything that would lead to pain if left undone.

<note><sup>a</sup> That is, our reasonings regarding intellectual or spiritual matters <note>original: "intelligible objects"</note>.</note>

<note><sup>b</sup> Consistent with this, Aristotle beautifully observes in his *Nicomachean Ethics* that physical pleasures are cures for pain and that they satisfy nature's needs <note>original: "fill up the indigence of nature"</note>, but they do not perfect any activity <note>original: "energy"</note> of the rational soul.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that meat-eating and food variety hinder philosophical reasoning and create anxiety. He asserts that true physical pleasure comes simply from removing the pain of hunger, making luxurious diets unnecessary for the soul's peace.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, abstinence, animal food, philosophy, Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, soul perturbations, physical pleasure, simplicity</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument regarding the necessity of a simple diet for the philosopher, specifically addressing why meat is neither necessary nor healthy for the soul’s pursuit of wisdom.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK 1.</header>

But the pleasure that comes with eating meat is intense <note>original: "violent"</note>, and perhaps even mixed with its opposite [pain]. For it does not contribute to the length of one's life, but only to a variety of pleasures; in this respect, it is similar to sexual indulgence <note>original: "venereal enjoyments"</note> and the drinking of imported wines—things which nature is perfectly able to do without. The things that nature requires to survive are actually very few; they can be obtained easily and in harmony with justice, liberty, peace, and abundant leisure.

52. Furthermore, animal food does not contribute to health; instead, it is an obstacle to it. Health is maintained by the same methods through which it is recovered. Since health is recovered through a very minimal and meatless <note>original: "fleshless"</note> diet, it is also preserved by that same diet. 

If, however, a vegetable-based diet does not grant someone the strength of Milo <note>Milo of Croton was a famous 6th-century BCE Greek wrestler known for his incredible physical strength and meat-heavy diet</note>, or lead to a general increase in power, it does not matter. A philosopher does not require such strength if he intends to devote himself to contemplation rather than an active and self-indulgent <note>original: "intemperate"</note> life. 

It is not at all surprising that the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> imagine that eating animals contributes to health. They also believe that sensual pleasures and sexual indulgence <note>original: "venery"</note> preserve health, though none of these things actually benefit anyone; those who engage in them should be thankful if they are not actively harmed by them. If many people disagree with this opinion, it is of no concern to us. Among the common people, one finds no true loyalty or constancy in friendship and goodwill. They are neither capable of receiving these virtues nor of participating in any wisdom worth mentioning. They do not understand what is beneficial for the individual or for the public, nor are they capable of judging between corrupt and refined manners so as to distinguish one from the other. In addition to these failings, they are full of arrogance and a lack of self-control. For this reason, there is no need to fear that there will be a shortage of people who feed on animals.

53. If all men thought correctly, there would be no need for bird-catchers <note>original: "fowlers"</note>, or hunters, or fishermen, or swine-herds...

<summary>Porphyry argues that meat is a luxury rather than a necessity for health or longevity, and while it might provide physical strength, such strength is unnecessary for the contemplative life of a philosopher.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that a simple, meatless diet is essential for the philosopher. He transitions from discussing physical strength to the relationship between a calm soul, a healthy body, and the pursuit of divine contemplation.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

herds. But animals that govern themselves, having no guardian or ruler, would quickly perish. They would be destroyed by others who would attack them and reduce their numbers <note>original: "diminish their multitude"</note>, as is seen to be the case with countless <note>original: "myriads"</note> animals that humans do not eat. However, since many kinds of folly constantly live within mankind, there will always be an enormous number of people who greedily eat meat. 

It is necessary, however, to preserve health—not out of a fear of death, but so that we are not hindered from achieving the good that comes from contemplation. The best way to preserve health is to maintain a calm soul and to direct one's reasoning toward truly existing being <note>a Platonic concept referring to the eternal, unchanging reality of the divine</note>. This provides great benefits to the body, as our associates have proven through experience. 

For example, some people who were afflicted with gout in their feet and hands—to such a degree that they suffered from it for eight full years—were cured after they gave up their wealth and devoted themselves to the contemplation of the divine<sup>c</sup>. At the same moment they abandoned their riches and their worries about human affairs, they were also freed from their physical disease. Thus, a specific state of the soul greatly contributes to health and the well-being of the entire body. Reducing one's food <note>original: "nutriment"</note> intake mostly contributes to this as well. 

In short, as Epicurus also rightly said: we should avoid food that we desire and pursue for pleasure but, after eating it, consider unpleasant <note>original: "of an unacceptable nature"</note>. All luxurious and heavy <note>original: "gross"</note> foods are like this. People who strongly desire such food are affected in this way; they end up facing great expense, disease, overindulgence <note>original: "repletion"</note>, or a loss of leisure<sup>d</sup>.

---

<sup>c</sup> Porphyry mentions in his *Life of Plotinus* that this was the case with the senator Rogatianus. <note>Rogatianus was a Roman senator who became a disciple of Plotinus and famously gave up his possessions and office to live as an ascetic.</note>

<sup>d</sup> And leisure, for those who know how to use it correctly, is, as Socrates said, "the most beautiful of possessions" <note>original Greek: καλλιστον κτηματων (kallistōn ktēmatōn)</note>.

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD—BOOK I.</header>

54. Therefore, even when eating simple and light food, overeating <note>original: "repletion"</note> should be avoided. In every situation, we should consider the consequences of possessing or enjoying something. We must ask how significant that thing is and whether it can actually relieve the distress of the body or the soul. We should never act without limits; instead, we should apply boundaries and measurements to these matters. Through reasoning, we can conclude that anyone who fears giving up animal food—and continues to eat meat for pleasure—is actually afraid of death. This is because as soon as they are deprived of such food, they imagine that some vague, terrible thing will happen, ultimately resulting in death. 

From these and similar fears, an insatiable desire arises for wealth, possessions, and fame. People begin to believe that every good thing increases as they acquire these over time, and they come to dread death as if it were an infinite evil. However, the pleasure produced by luxury does not even come close to the pleasure experienced by those who live frugally. Such a person finds great joy in thinking about how little they actually need. 

If we set aside luxury, an obsession with sexual pursuits <note>original: "venereal occupations"</note>, and ambition for status, what use is there for idle wealth? It would offer us no advantage and would only become a burden, much like overeating. On the other hand, the pleasure that comes from frugality is genuine and pure. It is also necessary to train the body to distance itself, as much as possible, from the pleasure of feeling stuffed <note>original: "satiety"</note> with luxurious food. We should not, however, avoid the feeling of fullness produced by a simple diet. This ensures that moderation governs everything, and that what is necessary—or what is most excellent—sets the limits for what we eat. 

For someone who subdues <note>original: "mortifies"</note> his bodily desires in this way will receive every possible good by becoming self-sufficient and more like the divine. In this state, he will also no longer desire a longer life as if more time would bring an increase in goodness. He will

<summary>Porphyry argues that overeating and luxury stem from a fear of death and a desire for external status, whereas a frugal, meatless diet leads to self-sufficiency and likeness to the divine.</summary>

In this way, he will be truly rich, measuring his wealth by natural limits rather than by vanity or public opinion. He will not rely on the hope of achieving some extreme, "perfect" pleasure—the existence of which is unbelievable—because pursuing such a thing would only cause great trouble. Instead, he will remain satisfied with his current situation and will not feel anxious about needing to live for a longer period of time just to acquire more.

55. Furthermore, is it not absurd that a person in great physical pain, or facing a terrible external disaster <note>original: "calamity"</note>, or bound in actual chains, does not even think of food? If food is placed before such a person, he often refuses even what is necessary for survival. Yet, the person who is truly "imprisoned" by their passions and tormented by internal disasters tries to acquire a huge variety of foods. By doing so, they focus on the very things that strengthen their spiritual chains. 

How could this be the behavior of people who actually understand their own suffering? Rather, it seems to be the behavior of those who delight in their own misery and are ignorant of the evils they are enduring. These people are affected in the opposite way of those who are physically imprisoned and aware of their misery. Because they find no real satisfaction in their current life and are filled with immense agitation <note>original: "perturbation"</note>, they greedily long for a different kind of life. 

No one who is able to free himself from these internal disturbances will desire silver tables, couches, ointments, or professional cooks. They will not want expensive dishes and clothes, or dinners famous for being extravagant and varied. Such desires arise from being completely useless to any real purpose in life, from a vague and misguided pursuit of "the good," and from massive internal unrest. Consequently, some people do not remember the past because the current moment overwhelms their memory; others ignore the present because they are unhappy with their current circumstances.

56. The contemplative philosopher, however, will always adopt a simple diet <note>original: "slender diet"</note>. He understands the specific nature of the bond that ties his soul to his body, so he is not...

<summary>Porphyry argues that a true philosopher finds wealth in simple living and avoids the spiritual "chains" of luxury. He compares those obsessed with fine dining to prisoners who are unaware of their own captivity, noting that internal agitation drives the desire for excess.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that a philosopher’s physical needs are easily satisfied, and that spiritual health requires prioritizing the intellect over the body's desires.</meta>
