<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK I.</header>

...capable of desiring luxuries. Therefore, being delighted with simple food, he will not seek out animal food <note>original: "nutriment"</note>, as if he were not satisfied with a vegetable diet. But even if the nature of a philosopher’s body were not as we have supposed it to be—if it were not so manageable and so easily satisfied by things that are simple to obtain—and if it were necessary to endure some pain and annoyance for the sake of true salvation, should we not willingly endure them? 

After all, when it is necessary for us to be freed from disease, do we not voluntarily suffer many pains? For example, we allow ourselves to be cut, covered in blood, or burned <note>Referring to ancient surgical practices like bloodletting and cauterization</note>; we drink bitter medicines and are purged through the bowels, through emetics <note>substances used to induce vomiting</note>, and through the nostrils. We even reward those who cause us to suffer in this way. 

This being the case, should we not endure everything, even the most painful circumstances, with composure? We should do this for the sake of being purified from internal disease, since our struggle <note>original: "contest"</note> is for immortality and a connection with the divine, from which we are held back by our attachment to the body. By no means, therefore, should we follow the laws of the body, which are violent and contrary to the laws of the intellect and the paths that lead to salvation. However, since we are not currently discussing the endurance of pain, but rather the rejection of unnecessary pleasures, what excuse is left for those who shamelessly <note>original: "impudently"</note> try to defend their own lack of self-control?

57. If it is necessary not to hide anything out of fear, but to speak freely, there is no other way to achieve the goal [of a contemplative life] than by sticking to God as if fastened by a nail. We must be separated <note>original: "divulged," likely from the Latin *divulsus*, meaning torn away or separated</note> from the body and from those pleasures of the soul that exist because of it; for our salvation is achieved through our actions, and not through mere attention to words. But just as it is impossible with any kind of diet—and especially by feeding on flesh—to become fit for a union with even a lesser <note>original: "partial"</note> deity, it is even less possible with...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s discussion on the necessity of purification for the philosopher, arguing that because the Divine is perfectly pure, the person who seeks to approach God must also be pure in both body and soul.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE, &c.</header>

...that God who is beyond all things and exists above a nature that is simply incorporeal <note>original: "incorporeal," meaning non-physical or purely spiritual</note>. Only after many various purifications of both soul and body will a person who is naturally of an excellent character, and who lives with piety and purity, be considered worthy to perceive him—and even then, only with difficulty. Therefore, just as the Father of all things excels in simplicity, purity, and self-sufficiency—being established far beyond any material representation—it is even more necessary that the person who approaches him be pure and holy in every respect. This holiness must begin with the body and end internally, applying to every part of the person, and indeed to everything they possess, a purity suited to the nature of each part. 

Perhaps no one will disagree with these points. However, one might doubt why we claim that abstaining from animal food relates to purity, given that in religious sacrifices we slaughter sheep and oxen and believe these offerings <note>original: "immolations," referring to the ritual killing of a victim for sacrifice</note> are pure and acceptable to the Gods. Because resolving this doubt requires a long discussion, we must begin our consideration of sacrifices from a different starting point.

<summary>Porphyry argues that because the supreme God is perfectly pure and beyond the material world, those seeking him must purify every aspect of themselves. He then raises the central problem of how animal sacrifice can be considered "pure" if the philosopher is meant to abstain from animal food.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s transition into the second book of his treatise. Having established that a philosopher seeking the divine must remain pure, he now addresses the specific objection that animal sacrifice is a standard religious practice, which would seem to conflict with a vegetarian diet.</meta>

### ON
# ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.
## BOOK THE SECOND.

1. Continuing our investigation into the simplicity and purity of diet, we have now come, Castricius <note>Castricius Firmus was a friend of Porphyry and a fellow philosopher to whom this work is addressed.</note>, to the discussion of sacrifices. This is a difficult topic that requires a thorough explanation if we want to reach a conclusion that is acceptable to the Gods. Since this is the right place for such a discussion, we will now reveal what we believe to be the truth and what can be explained, while also correcting any points that were overlooked in the premise we proposed at the beginning.

2. First, we argue that just because animals are killed, it does not mean it is necessary to eat them. Even if someone agrees that they should be killed, they have not proven that they should be eaten. For example, laws allow us to defend ourselves against enemies who attack us by killing them; however, these same laws do not permit us to eat our enemies, as that would be contrary to human nature. Second, it does not follow that because it may be appropriate to sacrifice certain animals to spirits <note>original: "dæmons," referring to intermediary beings between gods and humans, not necessarily evil entities</note>, Gods, or specific powers—for reasons that are either understood or hidden from us—it is therefore necessary to feed on those animals. It can be shown that people have included animals in sacrifices that no one, even among those who...

<summary>Porphyry begins Book 2 by addressing the practice of animal sacrifice. He argues that the ritual killing of an animal for the Gods does not justify eating its meat, comparing it to how killing an enemy in war does not justify cannibalism.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, sacrifice, Castricius, diet, ethics, animal slaughter, ritual, dæmons, purity</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s historical analysis of sacrifice, specifically drawing from the work of Theophrastus to show that the earliest offerings to the Gods were simple plants and grains, not animals.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

introduced, we do not rightly interpret; since we call the worship of the Gods through the slaughter <note>original: "immolation"</note> of animals *thusia* <note>original: θυσία (*thusia*). Porphyry is arguing that the word originally referred to the burning of plants, not the killing of animals.</note>. But so careful were the ancient people not to violate this custom, that they used curses <note>original: "execrations"</note> against those who, neglecting the original practices, introduced new ways of sacrificing. Because of this, they now call the substances used for burning as incense *aromata* <note>original: ἀρώματα (*aromata*)</note>—that is, aromatics—because they were once considered things of a cursed or "execrable" nature. 

The antiquity of the previously mentioned incense offerings can be understood by anyone who considers that many people today still sacrifice certain types of fragrant wood. In earlier times, after the earth produced grass, it produced trees. When humans first fed on the fruits of the oak, they offered only a few of those fruits to the Gods because they were rare, but in their sacrifices, they burned many of the oak's leaves. Later, however, when human life progressed to a milder form of nourishment and sacrifices of nuts were introduced, they used the proverb, "enough of the oak" <note>This was a common Greek proverb used when someone moved from a primitive or poor state to a better one, referring to the transition from eating acorns to eating grain.</note>.

6. But as barley appeared after legumes, the human race used it in primitive sacrifices, first moistening it with water for this purpose. Afterward, when they had learned to break and grind it to make it edible, they treated the tools used for this—which had provided such divine assistance to human life—as sacred objects, hiding them away in a secret place. 

Believing the food produced from ground grain to be blessed compared to their former diet, they eventually offered the first fruits of it to the Gods. For this reason, even today at the conclusion of sacrifices, we use fruits that are bruised or ground. This practice testifies to how far modern incense offerings have drifted from their ancient simplicity, even though we often do not perceive the original reason why we perform these rituals. Proceeding from this point, and being more abundantly supplied—

<meta>This page continues the argument that early human sacrifices were bloodless and consisted of the first-fruits of the earth. It moves from describing grain offerings to the inclusion of liquid offerings like wine and oil, before detailing the tragic transition to animal slaughter.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK II.</header>

Along with other fruits and wheat, the first-fruits of cakes—made of fine wheat flour and every other kind of grain—were offered in sacrifices to the Gods. Many flowers were collected for this purpose, mingled with everything that was considered beautiful and suited to a divine sense because of its fragrance. Some of these flowers were used for garlands, while others were committed to the sacrificial fire. But when they had discovered the use of the "divine drops" of wine, honey, and also oil for the purposes of human life, they then sacrificed these to their causes, the Gods.

7. These things appear to be evidenced by the splendid procession in honor of the Sun and the Hours <note>The Hours (Greek: *Horae*) were the goddesses of the seasons and the natural portions of time.</note>, which is still performed at Athens today. In this procession, there are other herbs besides grass, along with acorns, the fruit of the crabapple tree <note>original: "crab tree"</note>, barley, wheat, a heap of dried figs, cakes made of wheat and barley flour, and, finally, an earthen pot. 

This method of offering first-fruits in sacrifices eventually degenerated into lawlessness, and the practice of animal sacrifice <note>original: "immolations"</note>—dire and full of cruelty—was introduced. It seems that the curses <note>original: "execrations"</note> which were formerly uttered against us have now reached their full realization, as men slaughter animals and defile altars with blood. This began during the period in which mankind first tasted blood, having experienced the evils of famine and war. 

As *Theophrastus* says, the Divine, being indignant, appears to have inflicted a punishment suited to the crime. Consequently, some men became atheists; but others, because they formed erroneous conceptions of a divine nature, may be more justly called *malicious-minded* <note>original: κακόφρονες (*kakophrones*)</note> than *god-forsaken* <note>original: κακόθεοι (*kakotheoi*)</note>^c. This is because they think that the Gods are corrupt and are in no respect naturally more excellent than we are. Thus, some were seen to live without—

---

* ^c *That is, they may be called malevolent rather than simply unhappy.* <note>The Greek term *kakotheoi* can mean "hated by the gods" or "miserable," while *kakophrones* implies a malicious or perverted intellect.</note>

<sig>E</sig>

<summary>Porphyry describes how ancient bloodless sacrifices included flowers, wine, honey, and oil, citing Athenian traditions as evidence. He argues that the shift to animal sacrifice was a degenerate act born of war and famine, leading to atheism and a low opinion of the Gods.</summary>

8. Hence the Thoes <gloss>d</gloss>, who lived on the borders of Thrace, were suddenly wiped out from among the rest of humanity because they neither offered any first-fruits nor sacrificed to the Gods. Consequently, neither the inhabitants, nor the city, nor even the foundations of the houses could be found by anyone.

> "Men prone to evil denied the Gods their due,
> And by their follies made their days but few.
> The altars of the blessed ones neglected stand,
> Without the offerings which the laws demand;
> But angry Zeus <note>original: "Jove"</note> laid this people in the dust,
> Because no honors to the Gods they paid."
> ->HESIOD, *Works and Days* <note>original: "Op. et Di. lib. i. v. 133." (Opera et Dies)</note>.<-

Nor did they offer first-fruits to the Gods, as was only right that they should. But regarding the Bassarians <note>A tribe in Thrace often associated with the ecstatic and violent worship of Dionysus.</note>, who formerly were not only eager to sacrifice bulls but also ate the flesh of slaughtered men (in the same manner as we now do with other animals, for we offer to the Gods some parts of them as first-fruits and eat the rest)—concerning these men, who has not heard that they rushed at each other in a frenzy and bit one another, literally feeding on blood? They did not cease to act in this manner until the entire race of those who practiced sacrifices of this kind was destroyed.

9. Therefore, animal sacrifice is a later and more recent practice. It originated from a cause that was not of a pleasing nature—unlike the sacrifice of fruits—but began instead from famine or some other unfortunate circumstance. The causes, indeed, of the specific ritual slaughters <term>mactations</term> among the...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of sacrifice, moving from the general impiety of tribes like the Thoes and Bassarians to the specific legends of Athens. It argues that the shift from plant-based offerings to animal sacrifice was not a natural progression but the result of accidental errors, fear, or desperate necessity such as famine.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK II.</header>

[The practices of the] Athenians had their beginning either in ignorance, anger, or fear. For the slaughter of pigs <note>original: "swine"</note> is attributed to an accidental mistake by Clymene, who killed the animal by unintentionally striking it. Consequently, her husband, terrified as if he had committed an illegal act, consulted the oracle of the Pythian God <note>Apollo</note> about the matter. But as the God did not condemn what had happened, the slaughter of animals was afterwards considered a morally neutral matter <note>original: "a thing of an indifferent nature"</note>. The inspector of sacred rites, however, who was a descendant of prophets, wished to make an offering of first-fruits from sheep; it is said he was permitted to do so by an oracle, but only with great caution and fear. For the oracle was as follows:

> “Descendant of prophets, the Gods permit you not
> To slay sheep by force; but with washed hands
> It is lawful for you to kill any sheep
> That dies a voluntary death.”

10. But a goat was first slain in Icarus, a mountain in Attica, because it had chewed on a vine. And Diomus, who was a priest of Zeus Polieus <note>original: "Jupiter Polieus," the protector of the city</note>, was the first to slay an ox. He did so because during the festival sacred to Zeus called the Diipolia <note>An ancient Athenian festival involving the symbolic trial of the axe used to kill the ox.</note>, while fruits were being prepared in the ancient manner, an ox approached and tasted the sacred cake. Aided by others who were present, the priest killed the ox. These are the causes assigned by the Athenians for this deed; however, others tell different stories. All of them, though, are full of explanations that are not holy. Most people assign famine, and the injustice that comes with it, as the cause. Therefore, once men had tasted animal flesh, they offered the animals as first-fruits to the Gods; but before this, they were accustomed to abstain from animal food. From this, since animal sacrifice is no older than the period when meat became "necessary" food, we can determine what the proper diet <note>original: "nutriment"</note> of humanity ought to be. But it does not follow, simply because men have tasted of and offered animals in...

<summary>Porphyry recounts Athenian legends explaining the origins of animal sacrifice as the result of accidental killings or responses to "crimes" by animals (like eating sacred cakes), arguing that meat-eating only began out of necessity or famine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that animal sacrifice arose from accidental circumstances or dire necessity rather than true piety. It specifically concludes the anecdote about the first ox killed in Athens and transitions into the arguments of the philosopher Theophrastus against the slaughter of living beings.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

sacrifices as first-fruits, it must necessarily be admitted that it is not pious to eat that which was not piously offered to the Gods. <note>The argument here is that if the original animal "sacrifice" was actually an accidental or forced act of slaughter rather than a holy ritual, then consuming the meat cannot be considered a religious act.</note>

11. But what especially proves that everything of this kind originated from injustice is this: the same animals are neither sacrificed nor eaten by every nation. Instead, people decide what is appropriate for them to do based on what they find to be useful to themselves. Among the Egyptians and Phoenicians, for instance, a person would sooner taste human flesh than the flesh of a cow. The reason for this is that this animal is useful, but also rare among them. Hence, though they eat bulls and offer them in sacrifice as first-fruits, they spare cows for the sake of their offspring <note>original: "progeny"</note> and ordain that if anyone kills them, it shall be considered a crime requiring atonement <note>original: "expiation"</note>. Thus, for the sake of utility within one and the same species <note>original: "genus"</note> of animals, they distinguish between what is pious and what is impious. Since these details are established in this way, Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and student of Aristotle who wrote a famous treatise "On Piety."</note> reasonably forbids those who wish to be truly pious from sacrificing animals, employing these and other similar arguments, such as the following.

12. In the first place, we only sacrificed animals because of a "greater necessity," as we have said. For pestilence and war were the causes that introduced the necessity of eating them. Since we are now well-supplied with fruits, what reason is there to continue using the sacrifice of necessity? In the next place, rewards and thanks for benefits should be given differently to different persons, according to the value of the benefit conferred. Therefore, the greatest rewards, and those consisting of the most honorable things, should be given to those who have benefited us in the greatest degree—especially if they are the source of these gifts. But the most beautiful and honorable of those things by which the Gods benefit us are the fruits of the earth. For through these they preserve us and enable us to live according to the law <note>original: "legitimately"</note>; so that, from...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal sacrifice is based on human utility rather than divine law, citing Egyptian taboos against killing cows. He presents Theophrastus's view that since we have an abundance of crops, we should offer the fruits of the earth as the most honorable thanks to the Gods.</summary>

<keywords>Theophrastus, Egypt, Phoenicians, animal sacrifice, piety, utility, fruits of the earth, cows, religious law</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosopher Theophrastus’s argument against animal sacrifice, focusing on the idea that true sacrifice must be harmless and that the life (soul) of an animal is far more valuable than plant life.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

[In the first place, we] ought to venerate them. Besides, it is necessary to sacrifice only those things that do not cause injury to anyone. For nothing should be as harmless <note>original: "innoxious"</note> to all things as a sacrifice. Some might argue that God gave animals for our use just as much as He gave the fruits of the earth; however, it does not follow that they should be sacrificed. In doing so, they are harmed because they are deprived of their life. For sacrifice is, as the name implies, something holy <note>The author is highlighting a Greek wordplay: the word for sacrifice (*thysia*) was thought to be related to the word for holy (*hosia*). Original Greek: *η γαρ θυσια, οσια τις εστιν κατα τουνομα*.</note>. But no one is holy who seeks to repay a favor using things that belong to another, whether he takes fruits or plants from someone who is unwilling to lose them. For how can an act be holy when those from whom the items are taken are harmed? If someone who takes fruits from others cannot sacrifice with holiness <note>original: "sanctity"</note>, then it certainly cannot be holy to sacrifice animals taken from others, which are in every respect more precious than the fruits of the earth. Such an act is an even more terrible deed. A soul is much more precious than the vegetable products of the earth, and it is not right that we should take that life away by sacrificing animals.

13. Someone, however, might say that we also take something away from plants when we eat them and sacrifice them to the Gods. But this taking <note>original: "ablation"</note> is not the same, since we do not take from those who are unwilling. If we did not gather the fruits, they would eventually drop from the plants on their own. Furthermore, gathering fruit does not destroy the plant itself, whereas animals are destroyed when they lose their life-giving spirit <note>original: "animating principle"</note>. Regarding the honey we receive from bees: since we obtain this through our own labor, it is appropriate that we should both benefit from it. The bees collect their honey from plants, but we carefully tend to the bees. For this reason, it is right that a fair exchange should be made—our care for their labor—so that they suffer no harm.

<summary>Porphyry argues that true sacrifice must be harmless and holy, asserting that killing animals is an act of injustice because it deprives a living being of its soul, unlike the gathering of plants or honey.</summary>
<keywords>sacrifice, ethics of eating, animal souls, Theophrastus, non-violence, bees, vegetable productions</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that sacrifices should be inexpensive and accessible to everyone to encourage constant devotion. It shifts from discussing the nature of property to the practical availability of plants versus animals.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM [ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.]</header>

However, what is useless to the plants but beneficial to us—such as their fruit—will be the reward we receive from them in exchange for our care for them. Therefore, in our sacrifices, we should refrain from using animals. For while everything in reality belongs to the Gods, plants seem more like our own property because we sow, cultivate, and nurture them with our own labor.

We should offer sacrifices from our own property, not from the property of others. Offerings that are inexpensive and easy to obtain are more holy, more pleasing to the Gods, and better suited for the purpose of sacrifice and the practice of constant devotion <note>original: "piety"</note>. Therefore, anything that is not holy, or that cannot be obtained at a small expense, should not be offered as a sacrifice, even if it is available.

14. We can see that animals are not among the things that are easy or cheap to obtain by looking at the majority of the human race; we should not only consider the few wealthy men who have plenty of sheep and oxen. First, there are many nations that do not even possess the types of animals usually offered in sacrifice, except perhaps for some lowly <note>original: "ignoble"</note> species. Second, most people who live in cities rarely own such animals.

Someone might argue that city-dwellers do not have an abundance of cultivated <note>original: "mild"</note> fruits. Even if that were true, they do not lack other plant products from the earth; it is never as difficult to find fruits as it is to find animals. Thus, an abundance of fruits and other vegetables is much easier to obtain than an abundance of animals. Since these items are easy and inexpensive to get, they encourage a lifestyle of constant and universal devotion.

15. Experience also shows that the Gods take more pleasure in these simple gifts than in lavish <note>original: "sumptuous"</note> offerings. For instance, when a certain man from Thessaly <note>Thessaly was a region in ancient Greece.</note> sacrificed oxen with gilded horns to the Pythian deity <note>The God Apollo at the oracle of Delphi.</note>...

<summary>Porphyry argues that sacrifices should consist of plants rather than animals because plants are the product of human labor and are inexpensive, making constant devotion possible for everyone. He suggests that the Gods prefer these simple, accessible offerings over the costly and rare sacrifice of animals.</summary>

Therefore, a sacrifice involving small expense is pleasing to the Gods; divinity looks more to the character and behavior of those who sacrifice than to the quantity of things offered.

16. Theopompus <note>A Greek historian of the 4th century BCE</note> also tells similar stories. He says that a certain Magnesian <note>A resident of Magnesia, a region in Greece or Asia Minor</note> came from Asia to Delphi. He was a very wealthy man with many cattle, and every year he made many magnificent sacrifices to the Gods, both because of his great wealth and his sincere desire to please the Gods. Having this mindset, he went to the temple at Delphi, bringing a hecatomb for the God. While honoring Apollo so magnificently, he consulted the oracle. Believing that he worshiped the Gods more beautifully than any other man, he asked the Pythian deity <note>The Pythia was the priestess of Apollo at Delphi who delivered his oracles</note> who the man was that worshiped the divine most willingly and in the best manner, making the most acceptable sacrifices. He assumed that on this occasion, the God would judge him to be the best.

However, the Pythian deity answered that Clearchus, who lived in Methydrium <note>A small, remote mountain town in Arcadia</note>, worshiped the Gods in a way that surpassed all other men. The Magnesian was astonished and wanted to see Clearchus to learn how he performed his sacrifices. Traveling quickly to Methydrium, he initially looked down on how small and humble <note>original: "vile"</note> the town was, believing that neither a private citizen nor even the whole city could...

He likewise said that he performed public sacrifices annually, omitting no festive day; and that in these festivals he worshiped the gods, not by slaying oxen, nor by cutting victims into fragments, but that he sacrificed whatever he might happen to have. He was diligent in offering the first-fruits <note>The first gathered crops of a season, traditionally dedicated to a deity</note> to the gods from all the vegetable productions of the seasons, and from all the fruits with which he was supplied. He added that some of these he placed before the statues of the gods$^f$, but that he burnt others on their altars; and that, being a student of frugality <note>original: "studious of frugality"; living simply and avoiding waste</note>, he avoided the sacrificing of oxen.

It is also related by some writers that certain tyrants—after the Carthaginians were conquered—engaged in a great competition to place hecatombs <note>original: "hecatombs"; a massive public sacrifice, traditionally of 100 oxen</note> before Apollo. Afterward, they inquired of the god which of the offerings delighted him most. Contrary to all their expectations, he answered that he was most pleased with the cakes of Docimus. 

This Docimus was an inhabitant of Delphi who cultivated some rugged and stony land. On that day, Docimus came from the place he farmed, took a few handfuls of meal from a bag tied around him, and sacrificed them to the god. The god was more delighted with his offering than with the magnificent...

18. For this reason, vessels made of earthenware, wood, and wicker were used in the past, especially in public sacrifices, because the ancients were convinced that the Divine is pleased with such things. Consequently, even now, the oldest vessels—those made of wood—are considered more sacred, both because of their material and the simplicity of the craftsmanship by which they were shaped. It is said, therefore, that when his brother asked him to write a <term>Pæan</term> <note>original: "Pæan"; a song of praise or triumph, specifically a hymn to Apollo</note> in honor of Apollo, Aeschylus replied that the best hymn had already been written by Tynnichus <note>original: "Tynnichus"; a Chalcidensian poet mentioned by Plato in his *Ion*</note>. He said that if his own composition were compared to that of Tynnichus, the result would be the same as if new statues were compared with ancient ones. For the latter, though they are simple in their design, are perceived to be divine; but the former, though they are crafted with the greatest precision, produce admiration but are not believed to possess so much of a divine [quality]...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the superiority of simple, bloodless sacrifices over animal slaughter, citing ancient poets and religious traditions to argue that the gods value the purity of the worshiper's soul more than expensive offerings.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD: BOOK II</header>

nature. Hence Hesiod, praising the law of ancient sacrifices, very properly says,

> Observe your country's sacrificial rites;
> [In pious deeds] the ancient law is best¹.

19. Those who have written about sacred rituals and sacrifices advise us to be careful to preserve the traditions regarding the <term>popana</term> <note>original: "popana"; thin, round cakes or wafers offered to the gods in bloodless sacrifice</note>, because these are more acceptable to the gods than the sacrifice performed through the <term>slaughter</term> <note>original: "mactation"; the ritual killing of an animal</note> of animals. Sophocles also, describing a sacrifice that is pleasing to the divine, says in his *Polyidus*:

> Sheepskins were used in sacrifice,
> Along with libations of wine and well-preserved grapes,
> And fruits of every kind collected in a heap;
> The olive's <term>oily</term> <note>original: "pinguid"; rich, fat, or oily</note> juice, and the most
> Diverse wax-work of the yellow bee.

In the past, there were also venerable monuments in Delos <note>Delos was a sacred island in the Aegean Sea, the mythological birthplace of Apollo and Artemis</note> belonging to the Hyperboreans <note>A mythical people who lived in a land of eternal sunshine in the far north, "beyond the North Wind"</note>, who brought handfuls of fruit as offerings. It is necessary, therefore, that we should make our offerings with purified character, presenting to the gods those sacrifices that please them, rather than those involving great expense.

Now, however, if a man's body is not clean and dressed in a magnificent garment, he does not think he is fit for the sanctity of sacrifice. But once he has made his body and his clothing look splendid—even if his soul is not simultaneously purified from vice—he proceeds to sacrifice and thinks it is of no consequence. He acts as if the divine did not especially rejoice in that which is most divine in our own nature when it is in a pure condition, being allied to its own essence. In Epidaurus <note>A major Greek religious site famous for its healing temple of Asclepius</note>, therefore, the following inscription was placed on the doors of the temple:

> Whoever enters the fragrant temple
> Should be pure and holy; and to be wise
> In matters of sanctity, is to be pure.

¹ See Hesiod, Fragment verse 169.

# ANIMAL FOOD—BOOK II

20. But that God is not delighted with the grandeur <note>original: "amplitude"</note> of sacrifices, but with any simple offering, is evident from this: that of our daily food, whatever it may be that is placed before us, we all make an offering to the gods before we have tasted it ourselves. This offering is small indeed, but it is the greatest testimony of honor to the divine. Moreover, Theophrastus shows—by listing many of the rituals of different countries—that the sacrifices of the ancients consisted of fruits. He describes the history of <term>libations</term> <note>original: "libations"; the ritual pouring out of a liquid as an offering to a deity</note> in the following manner: “Ancient sacrifices were for the most part performed with sobriety. Those sacrifices are 'sober' in which the libations are made with water. Afterward, however, libations were made with honey, for we first received this liquid fruit prepared for us by the bees. In the third place, libations were made with oil; and in the fourth and last place, with wine.”

21. These things, however, are evidenced not only by the pillars which are preserved in *Cyrbe* <note>An ancient city in Crete</note>, and which contain what are essentially true descriptions of the Cretan sacred rites of the *Corybantes* <note>The armed and dancing priests of the goddess Cybele</note>; but also by Empedocles, who, in discussing sacrifices and <term>theogony</term> <note>original: "theogony"; the study of the origin and genealogy of the gods</note>, says:

> Among them, neither Mars <note>The god of war</note> nor dreadful war was found,
> Nor Saturn, Neptune, or supreme Jove,
> But Venus, the queen of beauty.

And Venus represents friendship. Afterward he adds:

> With painted animals, and statues once
> Of sacred form, with sweet-smelling ointments,
> The smoke of frankincense and pure myrrh,
> And with libations of yellow honey poured upon the ground,
> Venus was made favorable <note>original: "propitious"</note>.

This ancient custom is even now still preserved by some people as a certain remnant of the truth. And finally, Empedocles says:

> Nor were the altars then wet with the blood of bulls
> Senselessly <note>original: "irrationally"</note> slain.

<summary>Porphyry argues that the gods prefer simple offerings like fruit and water over animal sacrifice, citing Theophrastus's history of libations and Empedocles's description of an ancient, bloodless age of peace.</summary>
<keywords>Theophrastus, Empedocles, libations, animal sacrifice, Cyrbe, Corybantes, theogony, Venus, Golden Age, bloodless sacrifice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument against animal sacrifice, logicizing that if we do not kill harmless humans, we should not kill harmless animals, and that harmful animals are unfit for sacrifice.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>

# ON ABSTINENCE FROM <note>The full title is "On Abstinence from Animal Food"</note>

22. For, as it appears to me, when friendship and a proper sense of the duties pertaining to kindred natures <note>original: "kindred natures"; the idea that humans and animals share a common biological or spiritual origin</note> was possessed by all men, no one slaughtered any living being, because they thought that other animals were related to them. But when strife and turmoil, every kind of contention, and the principles of war invaded mankind, then, for the first time, no one truly spared any of their fellow creatures. 

The following points should also be considered: just as we feel a connection between ourselves and harmful men—who are driven by a certain impulse of their own nature and wickedness to injure anyone they meet, and yet we think it necessary that all of them should be punished and destroyed—the same logic applies to irrational animals. Regarding those animals that are naturally harmful <note>original: "malefic"</note> and unjust, and who are driven to injure those who approach them, it is perhaps appropriate that they should be destroyed. 

However, with respect to other animals who do not act unjustly at all, and are not naturally driven to injure us, it is certainly unjust to destroy and murder them. Doing so is no different than slaying men who are not wicked <note>original: "iniquitous"</note>. This seems to demonstrate that the justice between us and other animals arises from the fact that some are naturally harmful and dangerous, while others are not—just as is the case among human beings.

23. Should those animals which are thought to deserve death, then, be sacrificed to the Gods? But how can this be possible if they are naturally corrupt? For it is no more proper to sacrifice animals like these than it would be to sacrifice mutilated animals. If we did so, we would be offering the "first-fruits" of things that have an evil nature, rather than sacrificing for the sake of honoring the Gods. 

Hence, if animals are to be sacrificed to the Gods, we should sacrifice those that are perfectly harmless <note>original: "innoxious"</note>. However, it is generally agreed that those animals who do not injure us at all should not be destroyed; it follows then that they should not be sacrificed to the Gods either. If, therefore, neither these harmless animals, nor those that are harmful,

<summary>Porphyry argues that justice applies to animals just as it does to humans: only harmful beings deserve death, while harmless ones should be left in peace. He further argues that neither type is fit for religious sacrifice, as harmful animals are too corrupt for the Gods and harmless ones do not deserve to die.</summary>

<keywords>animal ethics, justice, sacrifice, kinship, harmlessness, Porphyry, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument regarding which animals, if any, are appropriate for sacrifice. Having previously discussed that harmful animals should be destroyed but not sacrificed, he now argues that harmless animals should be spared from both slaughter and sacrifice.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>

# ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK II

...are to be sacrificed, is it not evident that we should abstain from them more than from anything else? Is it not also clear that we should not sacrifice any one of them, even if it is appropriate that some of them should be destroyed?

24. To this, we may add that we should sacrifice to the Gods for three reasons: to honor them, to show our gratitude, or because we are in need of some good. Just as we offer the "first-fruits" <note>original: "first-fruits"; the first and best portion of a harvest or labor offered as a gift to a deity or superior</note> to honorable men, we likewise believe it is necessary to offer them to the Gods. We honor the Gods either by seeking ways to avoid misfortune and obtain what is good, or after we have already been helped, or in order to obtain some immediate benefit and assistance, or simply to worship the goodness of their divine nature. 

Therefore, if the first-fruits of animals are to be offered to the Gods, some of them would have to be sacrificed for one of these reasons. For whatever we sacrifice, we do so for the sake of one of the purposes mentioned above. 

Is it then reasonable to think that God is honored by us when we are clearly seen to act unjustly through the very offerings we present to him? Or will he not instead think that he is dishonored by such a sacrifice? In such an act—by sacrificing <note>original: "immolating"</note> animals that have not harmed us at all—we effectively admit that we have acted unjustly. Thus, no animal should be sacrificed for the sake of honoring the divine. 

Nor should they be sacrificed to show our gratitude to the Gods. For he who makes a just repayment <note>original: "retribution"; here meaning a return-gift or payment for a favor received</note> for the benefits he has received ought not to do so by injuring other animals. He would no more appear to be making a proper repayment than a man who, after robbing his neighbor of his property, gives it to another person for the sake of gaining honor. 

Finally, animals are not to be sacrificed to obtain a certain good that we lack. For someone who tries to benefit himself by acting unjustly is to be suspected as the kind of person who would not be grateful even after he has received the benefit. Consequently, animals are not to be...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal sacrifice cannot fulfill the three primary purposes of worship—honor, gratitude, or petition—because sacrificing an innocent creature is inherently unjust and therefore offensive to the Gods.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, animal sacrifice, justice, first-fruits, gratitude, divine honor, ethics, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument from the previous page, specifically addressing why animals should not be sacrificed to the Gods to gain a personal advantage, as such an act is inherently unjust and cannot be hidden from divine awareness.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK II</header>

...sacrificed to the Gods with the expectation of gaining some advantage from the sacrifice. For a person who does this might escape the notice of men, but it is impossible to hide his true intentions from the divine <note>original: "divinity"</note>. Therefore, if we are to sacrifice for a specific reason, but none of the previously mentioned reasons justify it, it is clear that animals should not be sacrificed.

25. By trying to hide the truth of these things behind the pleasure we get from these sacrifices, we only deceive ourselves; we cannot deceive the divine. We do not sacrifice any animals that are lowly in nature, offer no great benefit to our lives, or provide us with no pleasure. After all, who has ever sacrificed snakes, scorpions, apes, or any similar creatures? Yet we do not hold back from killing any animal that is useful to us or provides us with enjoyment. In reality, we slit their throats and skin <note>original: "excoriate"; to strip the hide or skin from a carcass</note> them while claiming the gods approve of it¹. For we sacrifice oxen and sheep to the Gods, as well as deer, birds, and fat pigs—none of which are particularly pure, but all of which provide us with delight. Among these animals, some assist us in our daily work, while others provide us with food or satisfy our other needs. Even those that provide neither benefit are still killed by men in sacrifice, just like the ones that are useful, simply because we enjoy eating them. However, we do not sacrifice donkeys or elephants or any other animal that works with us but does not serve our pleasure. Yet, even outside of sacrifice, we do not hold back from killing such animals if we find the act of eating <note>original: "deglutition"; the act of swallowing</note> them to be pleasurable. And of those which are fit to be sacrificed, we do not sacrifice such...

---

¹ That is, under the excuse that we are being supported by the divine in doing so.

<summary>Porphyry exposes the hypocrisy of animal sacrifice, arguing that humans only sacrifice animals they find tasty or useful, proving that the ritual is driven by human appetite rather than true religious devotion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that animal sacrifice is often practiced for human pleasure rather than divine service. He begins here by contrasting common Greek practices with those of the Jews (specifically the Essenes) and the Egyptians.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

...that are acceptable to the Gods, but rather those that greatly satisfy human desires. In doing this, we testify against ourselves: we continue to sacrifice to the Gods for our own pleasure, rather than to please the Gods themselves.

26. Regarding the Syrians, Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371 – c. 287 BC) was a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle who wrote extensively on nature and religion.</note> says that the Jews continue to sacrifice animals based on their original customs. However, if we were convinced to sacrifice exactly as they do, we would likely stop the practice altogether. This is because they do not feast on the meat of the sacrificed animals. Instead, they throw the entire victim into the fire and pour a great deal of honey and wine over it during the night. They burn the sacrifice quickly so that the all-seeing sun will not witness the act. They perform these rites while fasting during the days in between. Throughout this time, acting as a class of philosophers, they discuss the nature of the divine <note>original: "divinity"</note>. At night, they devote themselves to the study of the stars, observing them and invoking God through prayer <note>Porphyry is referring here to the Jewish sect known as the Essenes (or Essæans); he discusses them further in the fourth book of this work.</note>. They offer both animals and themselves out of necessity rather than their own free will. 

Anyone can see the truth of this by looking at the Egyptians, the most learned of all people. They are so far from killing animals that they create images of them as representations of the Gods; they believe animals are so closely connected and allied to both Gods and humans.

27. In the beginning, sacrifices to the Gods consisted of fruits. Over time, however, people became less holy <note>original: "negligent of sanctity"</note> because fruits became scarce. Lacking proper nourishment, they were driven to eat one another. After begging the divine with many prayers, they first began to offer themselves as sacrifices to the Gods, not only conse— <note>The text likely continues "consecrating" or "consecrating themselves."</note>

<summary>Porphyry contrasts Greek animal sacrifice with the more austere, non-consuming rituals of the Essenes and the animal-revering customs of the Egyptians. He also argues that animal and human sacrifice only began as a desperate response to famine and a decline in holiness.</summary>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s history of sacrifice. He transitions from discussing the Egyptians to explaining how early humans moved from offering beautiful possessions to sacrificing humans, then animals, and finally to eating those animals out of greed.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

...consecrating <note>original: "crating"</note> to the divinities whatever among their possessions was most beautiful. But, going beyond this, they eventually sacrificed members of their own species. Hence, even to the present time—not only in Arcadia during the Lupercal festivals <note>The Lupercalia was an ancient, pastoral Roman festival; Porphyry links it here to older Greek traditions in Arcadia</note> and in Carthage—men are sacrificed in common to Saturn <note>The Roman name for the Greek Titan Kronos, often associated with human sacrifice in Phoenician and Carthaginian contexts</note>. Periodically, also, to remember the ancient legal custom <note>original: "legal institute"</note>, they sprinkle the altars of those of the same tribe with blood, even though the sacrificial rites officially exclude anyone from participating who is accused of murder <note>original: "human slaughter"</note> through the voice of the crier.

Proceeding from this practice, they began to make the bodies of other animals take the place of their own in sacrifices. Then, through a boredom with lawful food and a loss of piety, they were driven by greed <note>original: "voracity"</note> to leave nothing untasted and nothing undevoured. This is exactly what now happens to all people regarding the food they get from fruits. For when they have satisfied their necessary hunger by eating them, they then search for a surplus of luxury, laboring to obtain many types of food that exceed the limits of moderation <note>original: "temperance"</note>.

Thus, as if they had made grand sacrifices to the Gods, they also proceeded to taste the animals which they sacrificed <note>original: "immolated"</note>. From this initial act, the eating of animals became a human addition to the nutrition derived from fruits. Just as ancient people offered the first produce of their fruits to the Gods and then gladly tasted what they had offered after their pious sacrifice, they thought the same thing should be done when they sacrificed the firstborn animals to the divinities. However, ancient piety did not establish these details in this way; instead, it honored each of the Gods with fruits. For both nature and every sense of the human soul are delighted by such offerings <note>original: "oblations"</note>.

> ->No altar then was wet with blood of bulls<-
> ->Irrationally slain; but this was thought<-
> ->To be of every impious deed the worst:<-
> ->To devour the limbs of brutes deprived of life.<-
<note>These lines are attributed to the Pre-Socratic philosopher Empedocles, describing a legendary "Golden Age" where no blood was shed.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal sacrifice and meat-eating are corruptions of ancient piety, evolving from human sacrifice and a gluttonous desire to eat more than just the fruits of the earth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument for vegetarianism by citing historical and religious precedents. Having previously discussed the shift from human to animal sacrifice, he now focuses on specific traditions in Delos and Athens that originally emphasized bloodless offerings.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

28. The truth of this can also be seen from the altar still preserved on the island of Delos. Because no animal is brought to it or sacrificed upon it, it is called the "altar of the pious." This shows that the inhabitants not only avoid sacrificing animals themselves, but they also believe that the founders of this altar were just as pious as those who continue to use it today. Therefore, the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, who were famous for their vegetarianism and belief in the transmigration of souls</note>, having adopted this method of sacrifice, abstained from animal food throughout their entire lives. Even on the rare occasions when they offered an animal to the Gods as a substitute for themselves, they only tasted a tiny portion of it, truly living their lives without consuming other animals. We, however, do not act this way. Instead, by gorging ourselves on a meat-heavy diet, we have fallen into a widespread lawlessness <note>original: "manifold illegality"</note> by slaughtering animals and using them for food. It is neither appropriate for the altars of the Gods to be defiled with murder, nor for such food to be touched by men—just as it is unfit for men to eat one another. Instead, the ancient rule still preserved at Athens should be obeyed throughout one's life.

29. As we have observed before, in the past men sacrificed fruits to the Gods rather than animals and did not use animals for food. It is said that during a public sacrifice held at Athens, a man named Diomus (or Sopater)—who was not a native citizen but a farmer in the surrounding region of Attica—saw an ox approaching a table. The ox, coming from its work in the fields, began to eat the cakes and other offerings that were placed there to be burnt as a sacrifice to the Gods, and it trampled on the rest. Seizing a sharp axe that was nearby, Diomus became so angry that he struck and killed the ox. Once his anger cooled, however, he realized the gravity of what he had done. He buried the ox and, treating the act as a religious crime <note>original: "impiety"</note>, went into voluntary exile and fled to Crete. Shortly after, a severe drought <note>original: "dryness"</note> struck the land of Attica due to extreme heat, resulting in a terrible crop failure. When the Pythian oracle <note>original: "Pythian deity"; the Oracle of Apollo at Delphi</note> was consulted regarding this disaster...

<page-num>66</page-num>

As a result of this, they consulted the oracle by common consent. The God <note>Likely referring to the Oracle of Apollo at Delphi</note> answered that the Cretan exile must make amends for the crime. He declared that if the murderer was punished, and a statue of the slaughtered ox was erected on the spot where it fell, it would benefit everyone—both those who had eaten its flesh and those who had not. 

Therefore, an inquiry was made into the matter. When Sopater was found and his deed discovered, he sought a way to escape the charge of impiety <note>original: "impiety," meaning a lack of respect for the gods or religious law</note>. He believed he would be freed from this trouble if the entire community took part in the act. He told those who came to him that it was necessary for the whole city to sacrifice an ox. However, since they were hesitant about who should actually strike the animal, Sopater said he would take the task upon himself, provided they granted him citizenship and shared the responsibility for the slaughter with him. This request was granted. They returned to the city and arranged for the ritual to be carried out exactly as it is performed today:

30. They chose young women to be water-carriers <note>original: "drawers of water"</note>, whose job was to bring water to sharpen an axe and a knife. Once the tools were sharpened, one person handed over the axe, another struck the ox with it, and a third person cut the animal's throat. After this, they skinned <note>original: "excoriated"</note> the animal, and everyone present ate some of its meat. 

Once these tasks were finished, they sewed the ox’s hide back together and stuffed it with straw. They stood it upright so it looked just as it did when it was alive and yoked it to a plow, as if it were about to work. They then began a legal trial regarding the killing of the ox, summoning everyone who took part in the act to defend their actions. The water-carriers accused those who sharpened the axe and the knife, claiming they were more to blame. Those who sharpened the tools accused the person who handed over the axe. That person accused the man who cut the ox's throat, and he, in turn, blamed the knife itself—hence,

<summary>This page describes the origin of the Athenian ritual of the Dipolieia, where an ox is sacrificed and a mock trial is held to shift the blame for the animal's death from the people to the weapon itself.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the description of the Athenian ritual of the "Dipolieia" (the sacrifice of the ox) and transitions into a philosophical argument against killing animals for food.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD—BOOK II</header>

Since the knife could not defend itself, they condemned it as the cause of the slaughter. From that time until now, during the festival sacred to Jupiter <note>original: "Jupiter," the Roman name for the Greek god Zeus</note> on the Acropolis in Athens, the sacrifice of an ox is performed in this same way. 

They place cakes on a bronze table and drive oxen around it; the ox that tastes the cakes distributed on the table is the one chosen to be killed. The families of those who originally performed this ritual still exist today. All those who are descended from Sopater are called "ox-slayers" <note>original: *boutupoi*</note>. Those descended from the man who drove the ox around the table are called "goaders" <note>original: *kentriadai*, meaning those who stimulate or prod the animal</note>. Finally, those descended from the one who cut the ox's throat are called "carvers" <note>original: *daitroi*, or dividers</note>, so named because of the banquet that follows the distribution of the meat. Once they have stuffed the hide <note>to make it appear as if the ox were standing again</note> and the legal trial is finished, they throw the knife into the sea.

31. Therefore, the ancients did not believe it was holy to kill animals that worked alongside us in tasks that benefit our lives, and we should avoid doing this even now. Just as it was once considered impious <note>original: "not pious," meaning disrespectful to the gods or natural law</note> for men to harm these animals, it should now be considered unholy to kill them for the sake of food. 

Even if someone claims this must be done out of religious reverence for the Gods, we should still reject any craving or impulse that arises from the body. We must ensure we do not obtain food from improper sources, which would make violence a constant companion in our lives. By rejecting such food, we will at least all benefit in terms of our mutual safety, if nothing else. Those who are naturally repulsed by the destruction of animals of a different species will obviously refrain from harming those of their own kind. 

Consequently, it would perhaps have been best if people in later times had immediately stopped slaughtering these animals. However, since no one is free from error, it remains for future generations to take...

<meta>This page continues the argument that humans should seek to purify themselves from the "crime" of eating meat, transitioning into a discussion on why fruit is the only appropriate sacrifice for the "visible" gods of the heavens.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

[...to wash] away through purifications the crime of their ancestors regarding food <note>original: "nutriment"</note>. This, however, will be achieved if we place the horrific nature of such conduct before our eyes and exclaim with Empedocles <note>Empedocles (c. 490–430 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that eating animal flesh was a form of spiritual pollution and murder</note>:

> Ah me! Why did cruel Time not destroy me 
> Before I committed such a crime—
> Before my lips began the sinful deed,
> To feed on the terrible food of flesh?

For the natural conscience only feels sympathetic grief for past errors in those who actively try to find a remedy for the evils that afflict them. In this way, by offering pure and holy sacrifices to the divine, everyone may obtain the greatest benefits from the Gods through their own sanctity.

32. But the benefit we receive from fruits is the first and greatest of all. As soon as fruits are matured, they alone should be offered to the Gods and to the Earth, which produces them. For Earth is the common Vesta <note>original: "Vesta," the Roman name for Hestia, the goddess of the hearth; here she represents the stable center of the world and the source of life</note> of both Gods and men. It is necessary that all of us, resting on her surface as if on the breast of our mother and nurse, should celebrate her divinity and love her with a child's affection, as she is the source of our existence. For by doing this, when we eventually exchange this life for another, we shall again be thought worthy of a home in the heavens and of living among all the celestial Gods. Since we see them now<note>n</note>, we ought to honor them with those fruits of which they are the cause, sacrificing indeed to—

---

<note>ⁿ In the original Greek, the text reads, "to honor these as now seeing them" [original: *ὡς νυν ὁρῶντας τιμᾶν τούτους*]. Instead of this, the scholar Reiske proposes to read, "those whom we do *not* now see" [original: *οὐς νυν οὐχ ὁρῶντας τιμᾶν δεῖ*]. But the insertion of "not" [original: *οὐχ*] is completely absurd, because the celestial beings (the stars and planets) are called the *visible Gods*. For instance, in the *Timaeus*, Plato records the speech of the Demiurge <note>The 'Demiurge' is the divine craftsman who shapes the universe in Platonic thought</note> to the "junior" or "mundane" Gods—who consist of the celestial and atmospheric deities—calling the celestial Gods "those that revolve visibly" and the others "those that become visible whenever they please." The passage says: "When all the gods, both those who move visibly in circles and those who appear as far as they wish, had come into being..." [original: *Ἐπεὶ οὖν πάντες ὅσοι τε περιπολοῦσι φανερῶς, καὶ ὅσοι φαίνονται καθ’ ὅσον ἂν ἐθέλωσι θεοὶ, γένεσιν ἔσχον*]. Therefore, following the original meaning, I read the text as: "since we see them now, we ought to honor them." To this may be added that our author, Porphyry, explicitly calls the stars "visible Gods" in paragraph 37.</note>

<summary>Porphyry quotes Empedocles to lament the "crime" of eating meat and argues that humans should instead offer fruits to the Earth and the celestial (visible) Gods.</summary>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page concludes the summary of Theophrastus’s arguments against animal sacrifice and begins Porphyry's own instructions on how a philosopher should offer sacrifices according to the law while maintaining personal purity.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

[...honor] them for all these things once they have ripened, without assuming that every one of us is worthy enough to offer sacrifices to the Gods. For just as all things are not meant to be sacrificed to the Gods, neither, perhaps, are the Gods pleased by the sacrifice of just anyone. This, therefore, is the core of the arguments presented by Theophrastus to show that animals ought not to be sacrificed; this does not include the mythical stories scattered throughout his work, nor a few points which I have added to what he said.

33. I, however, shall not attempt to overturn the legal institutions that various nations have established. It is not my intention at present to discuss a political system <note>original: "polity"</note>. But since the laws by which we are governed allow us to worship the divine with very simple, inanimate things, let us choose what is least expensive and offer sacrifice according to the law of the city. We should strive to offer a fitting sacrifice, approaching the Gods with absolute purity. In short, if the offering of first-fruits <note>original: "oblation"</note> has any value as an expression of gratitude for the benefits we receive, it would be completely illogical for us to avoid eating animals ourselves while still offering them as first-fruits to the Gods. For the Gods are not inferior to us, nor do they need things that we ourselves do not require <note>original: "indigent"</note>; nor is it sacred to offer the first-fruits of food that we ourselves do not eat. We find it is common among men that when they refrain from animal food, they do not make offerings of animals; instead, they offer the Gods the first-fruits of what they themselves eat. Therefore, it is appropriate that he who abstains from animals should offer the first-fruits of the things he consumes for food.

34. Let us, therefore, also sacrifice, but let us sacrifice in an appropriate manner, offering different sacrifices to different divine powers; to the God who is above all things...

---

Valentinus erroneously translates this as follows: "We will therefore also sacrifice..." (original: “Sacrificabimus igitur etiam et nos,”)</note>

things, as a certain wise man said, neither ~~sacrificing with~~
incense, nor consecrating anything perceptible to the senses <term>original: "sensible"; referring to physical objects that can be seen or felt</term>. For there is nothing material which is not immediately impure to an immaterial nature. Hence, neither spoken language nor internal speech is suited to the highest God when it is defiled by any passion of the soul; instead, we should worship him in profound silence with a pure soul and with pure thoughts about him. It is necessary, therefore, that being joined with and made like him, we should offer to him—as a sacred sacrifice—the elevation of our intellect <term>the higher, intuitive part of the mind</term>, and this offering will be both a hymn and our salvation. Therefore, in a calm, detached contemplation of this divinity by the soul, the sacrifice to him is perfectly achieved. 

However, to his offspring—the intelligible Gods <note>non-material deities that can only be understood by the mind</note>—hymns spoken aloud are to be offered. For to each of the divinities, a sacrifice is to be made of the first-fruits of the things which he bestows, and through which he nourishes and preserves us. Therefore, just as the farmer offers handfuls of the fruits and berries which the season first produces, we should also offer to the divinities the first-fruits of our thoughts regarding their transcendent excellence. We should give them thanks for the contemplation which they grant us, and for truly nourishing us through the vision of themselves which they provide—associating with us, appearing to us, and shining upon us for our salvation.

35. Now, however, many of those who apply themselves to philosophy are unwilling to do this; and, pursuing fame <note>original: "renown"</note> rather than honoring divinity, they are busily occupied with statues. They neither consider whether these statues are to be revered or not, nor do they endeavor to learn from those who are divinely wise how far and to what degree it is necessary to proceed in this matter. We, however, shall by no means argue with...

...these, nor are we very desirous of being instructed in matters of this kind. Instead, imitating holy and ancient men, we offer to the Gods, above all else, the first-fruits of contemplation which they have shared with us; it is through the use of this contemplation that we become participants in true salvation.

36. The Pythagoreans <note>followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, known for their focus on mathematics and vegetarianism</note>, therefore, diligently applied themselves to the study of numbers and geometry. For the most part, they "sacrificed" these intellectual concepts to the Gods, naming a certain number Minerva, another Diana, and another Apollo. They also called one number "justice" and another "temperance." <margin>p</margin> They adopted a similar method with geometric diagrams. By offerings of this kind, they made the Gods favorable toward them, so as to obtain the objects of their wishes through the things they dedicated and the names by which they invoked them. 

They also frequently employed the aid of the Gods in divination <note>predicting the future or discovering hidden knowledge</note>, and when they required help with a particular investigation. To achieve this, they made use of the celestial Gods—both the "erratic" <term>the planets, or "wandering stars"</term> and the "non-erratic" <term>the fixed stars</term>. Among all of these, one must consider the sun as the leader, with the moon ranked in the second place. To these we should add fire in the third place, because of its alliance to them, as "the theologian" <margin>q</margin> says. This theologian also says that no animal is to be sacrificed, but that first-fruits are to be offered from meal, honey, and the vegetable productions of the earth. He adds that fire is not to be lit on a hearth defiled with gore, and asserts other things of the same kind. For what reason is there to transcribe everything he says? Anyone who is devoted to piety knows that to the Gods, no...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchy of sacrifice. The previous page established that intellectual contemplation is the highest form of offering to the celestial Gods. This page clarifies that animal sacrifice is only appropriate for lower, material deities and spirits (daemons).</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>

<margin>
r Although Porphyry excelled in all philosophical knowledge—for which reason he was also called *kat' exochen* <note>original: κατ' εξοχην; meaning "pre-eminently" or "by excellence"</note>, the philosopher—he was nevertheless inferior to his student Iamblichus in theological information. For this reason, Iamblichus was called by all the Platonists who came after him "the divine" and "the great priest." I shall therefore present the reader with an extract from my translation of his treatise *On the Mysteries*, which seems to me to be an excellent supplement to what Porphyry has said in this book about sacrificing animals; it provides a satisfactory answer to the question of whether they should be sacrificed or not.

In Chapter 14 of Section 5, Iamblichus observes the following: "We shall begin the explanation of this subject in the best possible way if we demonstrate that the sacred law of sacrifices is connected with the hierarchy of the Gods. In the first place, we say that among the Gods, some are material while others are immaterial. The material Gods are those who encompass matter within themselves and organize it; the immaterial Gods are those who are perfectly exempt from and transcend matter. According to the art of sacrifice, it is necessary to begin sacred rituals with the material Gods, for the ascent to the immaterial Gods cannot be achieved otherwise. 

The material Gods, therefore, have a certain communication with matter insofar as they preside over it. Consequently, they have authority over things that happen to matter, such as the division, striking, rebounding, transformation, birth, and decay of all material bodies. Therefore, anyone who wishes to worship these beings through theurgy <term>the practice of rituals intended to invoke the presence of or cooperate with the gods</term> in a manner suited to them and the authority they are assigned, should use a material mode of worship, since they are material. In this way, we will be led toward a familiarity with them and worship them in an appropriate and related manner. *Therefore, dead bodies and things deprived of life, the slaying of animals and the consumption of victims—and, in short, the transformation of the matter which is offered—pertain to these Gods. This is not because of the Gods themselves, but because of the matter over which they preside.* For though they are, in the highest degree, separate from matter, they are simultaneously present with it; and although they encompass matter through an immaterial power, they coexist with it. Things that are governed are not foreign to their governors, and instruments are not unsuited to those who use them. Therefore, it is inappropriate to offer matter to the immaterial Gods through sacrifices, but this practice is perfectly suited to all the material Gods."
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the excerpt from Iamblichus’s "On the Mysteries," which argues that while the highest form of worship is purely intellectual and immaterial, material sacrifices (like animals) are necessary for most people who are still bound to their physical bodies.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

[These] are the people who should sacrifice to these deities, and the extent to which they should carry out their sacrifices.

"Just as there is a time when we become entirely soul, exist outside of the body, and soar sublimely on high in union with all the immaterial Gods; so, too, there is a two-fold mode of worship. One of these is simple, incorporeal, and free from all physical change <note>original: "generation"; referring to the cycle of birth and death in the material world</note>; this mode belongs to pure souls. But the other is full of material things and is suited to souls that are neither pure nor free from the physical world." He adds, "We must admit, therefore, that there are two types of sacrifice. One kind belongs to men who are not entirely purified—which, as Heraclitus says, rarely happens to even one man, or to a very small, easily-counted few. The other kind is material and consists of change <note>original: "mutation"</note>; it is suited to souls still held by the body. Therefore, for cities and peoples not yet freed from earthly fate <note>original: "sublunary fate"</note> and the inevitable connection to physical bodies, if this latter mode of sacrifice is not allowed, they will lose both immaterial and material benefits. For they will not be able to receive the former, and they will not offer what is appropriate to the latter."

He further informs us, in Chapter 22, that although the peak of the sacrificial art leads back to the most primary of all the Gods [that is, to the unspeakable <note>original: "ineffable"</note> cause of all] and worships the many essences and principles that are rooted and concentrated in it all at once; yet this only happens at the very end of life, and to a very few people. We must be satisfied if it happens when the sun of life is setting. "But," he says, "our current discussion does not establish laws for a man of this kind; for he is above all law. Instead, it issues a law like the one we are discussing now for those who need divine legislation." In the passage above, when using the phrase "*a man of this kind*," Iamblichus most likely refers to Plotinus. Both Plotinus's own works and the biography of him written by Porphyry show that he was a man capable of returning to and becoming united with the highest God, and thus worshipping all the divine powers rooted in Him at the same time.

To these excellent observations by Iamblichus, we may add what the philosopher Sallust says in his "golden treatise" *On the Gods and the World*: "Since life primarily exists in the Gods, and there is also a certain human life, and because the latter desires to be united to the former, a bridge <note>original: "medium"</note> is required. Natures that are far apart from each other cannot be joined without a bridge; and it is necessary that this bridge be similar to the things it connects. Life, therefore, must necessarily be the bridge for life. For this reason, happy men of the present day and all the ancients have sacrificed animals. They did this not randomly, but in a way suited to every God,"

It cites Iamblichus and Sallust to argue that animal sacrifice serves as a necessary "bridge" between human life and divine life for most people.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of divine worship, transitioning from the material sacrifices required by most people to a philosophical breakdown of the hierarchy of divine beings—ranging from the supreme, indivisible God to the various classes of "daemons" or spirits.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>

I will pass over other matters in silence. However, I will present what some Platonists have revealed, so that the topics we are discussing may become clear to the intelligent reader. What they have explained is as follows:

37. The first God is incorporeal <note>without a physical body</note>, unchanging, and indivisible. He does not exist within anything else, nor are his energies limited. As previously mentioned, he does not need anything outside of himself. The same is true for the "soul of the world." This world-soul contains the principle of that which is divided into three parts and is naturally capable of moving itself. It is designed to move in a beautiful and orderly way, and to move the physical body of the world according to the most excellent rational principles <note>original: "reasons"; referring to the *logoi*, or the productive powers and creative blueprints of the universe</note>. Although the world-soul is itself incorporeal and free from any suffering or physical sensation <note>original: "participation of any passion"</note>, it still connects with and surrounds the physical body of the universe.

To the remaining Gods—to the world itself, and to the fixed and wandering stars <note>original: "inerratic and erratic stars"; the planets</note>—thanks are to be returned in the manner mentioned above, through sacrifices of non-living things <note>original: "inanimate natures"; such as incense or plants rather than animals</note>. These are visible Gods, consisting of both soul and body. 

There remains, however, a vast number of invisible beings whom Plato generally calls "daemons" <note>original: "dæmons"; in Neoplatonism, these are not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits or messengers between the high Gods and humanity</note>. Some of these have been named by men and receive honors and religious rituals similar to those paid to the Gods. Others, who for the most part are not specifically named, receive secret religious worship and titles from certain people in villages and cities. The rest of this multitude is known by the common name of "daemons."

...along with many other ceremonies regarding the worship of the divine.” However, the truly intellectual and pious person should never forget that prayer—as Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a celebrated Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on prayer and ritual.</note> divinely observes—possesses in itself a supernatural perfection and power.

• For a more theological account of daemons, I refer the reader to my translation of the previously mentioned, excellent treatise by Iamblichus, *On the Mysteries*.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of spiritual beings, moving from the higher gods to the nature and function of "daemons" (spirits) and how they interact with human life and the natural world.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
