The general belief regarding all these invisible beings is that if they become angry because they are neglected or denied the religious respect they deserve, they become harmful to those who ignore them. Conversely, they become kind again if they are calmed through prayers, petitions, sacrifices, and similar rituals.

38. But because people have such confused ideas about these beings—which has led to serious accusations—it is necessary to distinguish their true natures using logic. For perhaps it will be asked how human error about these spirits first began. We should make the distinction in the following way:

Those souls who are the offspring of the universal soul, and who govern the vast regions below the moon, inhabit a spiritual substance <note>original: "pneumatic substance"; a refined, air-like matter believed to be the "body" of a spirit</note>. They rule over this substance according to reason and are considered "good spirits" <term>daemons</term> <note>original: "dæmons"; in Greek philosophy, these are intermediate spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily evil "demons"</note>. They are diligently employed in making everything beneficial to the subjects under their government, whether they oversee certain animals or the crops placed under their care. They also preside over the elements that support these things, such as rainfall, moderate winds, calm weather, and other factors that work together, like the healthy balance of the seasons.

They are also our guides in the pursuit of music and all forms of learning <note>original: "erudition"</note>, as well as medicine, athletics <note>original: "gymnastic"</note>, and everything else of that nature. It is impossible for these spirits to provide such benefits and yet also be the cause of harm in those very same areas. Among these, we must also include the "messengers" <note>original: "transporters"</note>, as Plato calls them in his *Symposium* <note>original: "Banquet"</note>. These are the beings who report human affairs to the Gods and the will of the Gods to humans. They carry our prayers to the Gods as judges and prophetically reveal to us the encouragements and warnings of the Gods. But those souls who do not rule over the...

<summary>Porphyry describes the nature of "good daemons"—benevolent spirits who govern the natural world, weather, and human arts like medicine and music, while acting as messengers between humanity and the divine.</summary>

spiritual <term>pneumatic</term> substance <note>original: "pneumatic substance"; a refined, air-like matter believed to be the "body" of a spirit</note> with which they are connected, but for the most part, they are overwhelmed by it. These souls are violently agitated and carried along in a disorderly way whenever the angry impulses and the desires of this spiritual substance receive a sudden push. These souls are indeed spirits <term>daemons</term>, but they are rightly called harmful <note>original: "malefic"</note> spirits.

**39.** All these beings—including those who possess the opposite power—are invisible and completely undetectable by human senses. This is because they are not enclosed in a solid body, and they do not all have a single shape; instead, they take many different forms. The shapes that define their spiritual substance are sometimes visible and sometimes hidden. Sometimes the harmful ones even change their shapes. 

However, this spiritual substance—insofar as it is a type of physical matter—is susceptible to outside influence and can decay <note>original: "passive and corruptible"</note>. Although the soul that rests upon it keeps its form intact for a long time, it is not eternal. It is likely that this substance constantly loses some of its matter and must be nourished. 

The spiritual substance of good spirits <term>daemons</term> possesses symmetry, just like the bodies of the visible Gods <note>The "visible Gods" refers to the stars and planets</note>. In contrast, the spirit of harmful daemons lacks symmetry. Because they are so dominated by their reactions and emotions <note>original: "passivity"; the state of being acted upon by external forces rather than acting through reason</note>, they are confined to the region around the Earth <note>original: "terrestrial region"</note>. 

Consequently, there is no evil that they do not try to commit. Because they are violent and deceitful by nature, and lack the guidance of more virtuous spirits, they usually make sudden and forceful attacks. Sometimes they try to hide their raids, but other times they assault openly. For this reason, the harm they cause happens quickly, while the help and corrections from better spirits seem to take longer. This is because everything good is manageable and steady; it acts in an orderly way and never exceeds what is appropriate. By forming this...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s description of "malefic dæmons" (harmful spirits), specifically explaining how they deceive humanity by shifting the blame for their own destructive actions onto the benevolent Gods.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

By holding this opinion, you will never fall into that most absurd notion that evil may be expected from the good, or good from the evil. For this notion is not only ridiculous, but when the common people adopt these erroneous ideas about the Gods, they spread them to the rest of mankind.

**40.** It must be admitted, therefore, that one of the greatest harms caused by harmful spirits <term>malefic dæmons</term> is this: although they are the actual causes of the disasters that happen on earth—such as plagues <note>original: "pestilence"</note>, famine <note>original: "sterility"</note>, earthquakes, excessive droughts, and similar things—they try to persuade us that they are actually the causes of the exact opposite, such as fertility, health <note>original: "salubrity"</note>, and peace in nature. 

By doing this, they clear themselves of blame. In the first place, they try to avoid being caught as the sources of the damage; in the second place, they turn us toward making prayers <note>original: "supplications"</note> and sacrifices to the good Gods, making it seem as if those Gods were the ones who were angry. 

They do these things, and others like them, because they wish to turn us away from a correct understanding of the Gods and redirect our attention to themselves. They delight in everyone who acts in such inconsistent and clashing ways. By pretending to be other Gods <note>original: "assuming the persons of other Gods"</note>, they enjoy the results of our lack of wisdom and our foolishness. They win the favor of the common people by inflaming human minds with a love for riches, power, and pleasure, and by filling them with a desire for empty fame <note>original: "vain glory"</note>. From these desires, rebellion <note>original: "sedition"</note>, war, and other related evils are produced. 

But the most terrible thing of all is that they go even further. They persuade men that these same evils are caused by the highest Gods. They do not stop until they have even subjected the most excellent divinities to these slanders <note>original: "calumnies"</note>, claiming that it is through the Gods that everything is in a state of total confusion. It is not only the common people who are affected in this way; even many of those who are familiar with philosophy are misled. The cause of this confusion, however, applies equally to philosophers and the common people; for of...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how harmful spirits (malefic demons) deceive humanity. It explains that even philosophers can fall into popular errors and highlights the role of poetry and the true nature of divinity and benevolent spirits.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

Even philosophers—at least those who do not distance themselves from popular misconceptions—fall into the same error as the masses. Furthermore, when the common people hear the opinions of famous men that happen to match their own, they become even more certain in their false ideas about the Gods.

**41.** Poetry also inflames the opinions of men by using language designed to cause wonder and enchantment; it not only charms the ears but is also capable of making people believe in things that are utterly impossible. At the same time, however, it is necessary to be firmly convinced that what is good can never cause harm, and what is evil can never be beneficial. As Plato says, it is not the nature of heat to make things cold, but rather the nature of its opposite; in the same way, it is not the nature of justice to cause injury. 

Now, divinity is naturally the most just of all things; if it were not, it would not be divine. Therefore, the power and capacity to do good cannot be separated from the benevolent spirits <term>beneficent dæmons</term> <note>original: "dæmons" — In this context, these are "daimones," spirits that act as intermediaries between gods and humans, which could be either good or bad.</note>. The power that is naturally inclined and wishes to cause harm is the opposite of the power that does good, and opposites can never exist within the same nature. 

Therefore, just as harmful spirits <term>malefic dæmons</term> injure the human race in many ways—sometimes in the most serious matters—good spirits never stop performing their duty. In fact, as much as possible, they warn us in advance <note>original: "presignify"</note> of the dangers looming from the harmful spirits. They reveal these dangers through dreams, through a divinely inspired soul, and through many other signs. Thus, a person who is capable of interpreting what is being signaled may recognize and avoid all the perils that threaten them. 

These spirits indicate future events to all people, but not everyone can understand what they are indicating, nor is everyone able to "read" the messages they write. Only the person who has learned their "alphabet" <note>original: "letters" — referring to the symbolic language of omens and divine signs</note> is able to do this. All enchantment <note>meaning: magic or sorcery</note> and witchcraft, however, are carried out through spirits of the opposite nature; for those who perform...

<summary>Porphyry argues that divinity and good spirits can only do good, while harmful spirits cause disasters. He explains that good spirits provide warnings of danger through dreams and omens, though only the wise can interpret them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of harmful spirits (malefic demons) and why they crave animal sacrifices. It emphasizes the importance of soul purity for the philosopher.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

...evil through enchantments, especially worship these harmful <term>malefic</term> beings and the power that presides over them.

**42.** For they are full of every kind of illusion and are well-equipped to deceive through extraordinary and miraculous effects. Through these spirits, unhappy people obtain love potions <note>original: "philtres"</note> and charms to incite desire. All lack of self-control, all hope for wealth and fame, and especially all deception exist because of them, since falsehood is naturally allied to these malevolent beings. *For they wish to be considered as Gods, and the power which rules over them is ambitious to appear as the greatest God.* These are the ones who rejoice in drink offerings <note>original: "libations"</note> and the aroma of sacrifices, which "fatten" their spirit-body <term>pneumatic vehicle</term> <note>original: "pneumatic vehicle" — In Neoplatonic thought, this is a subtle, airy body that carries the soul and can be nourished or weighed down by physical vapors.</note>. This spirit-body lives on vapors and exhalations, and its life takes various forms depending on the different types of vapors it consumes. It is also strengthened by the smell of blood and flesh.

**43.** For this reason, a wise and temperate person will be solemnly cautious about using sacrifices of this kind, because through them he will attract such spirits to himself; instead, he will strive in every possible way to purify his soul. These harmful beings do not attack a pure soul because it is unlike them. If cities find it necessary to make these spirits favorable <note>original: "propitious"</note> to them, that is of no concern to us. Such spirits believe that riches and external or physical things are good, and their opposites are evil; they pay the smallest amount of attention to the well-being of the soul.

We, however, to the best of our ability, strive not to be in need of the things they provide. Our entire goal is to become like God and the divine powers that surround Him—both in what relates to our soul and our external life. *This is achieved through a total liberation from the rule of the passions, a clear perception of truly existing beings, and a vital focus toward them.* On the other hand, we strive to be unlike depraved men and evil spirits—and, in short, unlike every being that delights in a mortal and material nature.

<summary>Porphyry explains that malevolent spirits deceive humans and feed on the vapors of animal sacrifices, whereas the philosopher must purify the soul and avoid such rituals to become more like the divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that philosophers should avoid animal sacrifices because such rites attract lower, malevolent spirits. It transitions into a discussion on why sacrificing an animal does not necessitate eating it.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

In accordance with what Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC) was a Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle.</note> says, we should also offer sacrifices from those things that theologians permit us to use for this purpose. We should do this knowing that the more we fail to free ourselves from the passions of the soul, the more we connect ourselves with a depraved power. This makes it necessary to make that power favorable <note>original: "propitious"</note> to us. For, as theologians say, it is necessary for those who are bound<sup>t</sup> to external things and have not yet mastered their passions to turn away the anger of this harmful <term>malefic</term> power; if they do not, there will be no end to their troubles.

**44.** So far, the nature of sacrifices has been explained. However, as we said at the beginning, even if animals are sacrificed, it is not strictly necessary that they also be eaten. We shall now show that it is necessary to abstain from eating them, even though it may sometimes be necessary to sacrifice them. All theologians agree that in sacrifices made to turn away some evil, the sacrificed <note>original: "immolated"</note> animals are not to be tasted but are to be used as offerings of atonement <note>original: "expiations"</note>. They say that no one should enter the city or their own house until they have first purified their clothes and their body in rivers or a fountain. 

Consequently, they order those whom they permit to sacrifice to abstain from the victims and to purify themselves before the sacrifice through fasting, and especially by abstaining from animal flesh. They add *that purity is the guardian of piety; it is like a symbol or divine seal that protects its possessor from the attacks and temptations of evil spirits.* For such a person, being naturally opposed to these spirits and having a more divine nature in his...

---

<sup>t</sup> In the original Greek text, the word is *deomenois* (those in need), but it is necessary to read it as *dedemenois* (those who are bound). It is evident that both Latin translators of this work found "bound" in their manuscripts. Felicianus translated it as "those who are bound <note>original: "devincti"</note> to external things," and Valentinus as "those who are entangled <note>original: "illigantur"</note> in external things." Reisk, however, took no notice of this error in the printed text.

<summary>Porphyry argues that while rituals to appease harmful spirits may be necessary for those ruled by their passions, a philosopher must remain pure. He distinguishes between the act of sacrifice and the act of eating, noting that many sacred rites strictly forbid consuming the victim.</summary>

<sig>G</sig>

[performing higher] operations than those by whom he is attacked. Because he is more pure in both his body and in the passions of his soul, he remains uninjured, for he is surrounded by purity as if by a fortress <note>original: "bulwark"</note>.

**45.** Therefore, a defense of this kind has appeared to be necessary even to sorcerers <note>original: "enchanters"</note>, though it is not effective for them on every occasion. This is because they summon evil spirits <note>original: "dæmons"</note> for lustful <note>original: "lascivious"</note> purposes. Consequently, true purity does not belong to sorcerers, but to divine men and those who are divinely wise. Purity everywhere acts as a guard to those who practice it and brings them into harmony <note>original: "conciliates them"</note> with the divine nature. I wish, therefore, that sorcerers would practice purity continually, for then they would not engage in spells <note>original: "incantations"</note>; through such purity, they would be deprived of the enjoyment of those very things for the sake of which they act so irreverently <note>original: "impiously"</note>. 

Instead, because they are full of passions and abstain from impure food for only a short time, they remain filled with impurity. They suffer the punishment of their illegal conduct toward the universe <note>original: "the whole of things"</note>—partly from the spirits they provoke, and partly from Justice, who perceives all mortal deeds and thoughts. Therefore, both internal and external purity belong to a divine man. He is one who earnestly endeavors to be liberated from the passions of the soul, who abstains from food that excites those passions, and who is nourished with divine wisdom. By maintaining right thoughts, he becomes like God <note>original: "is assimilated to divinity"</note> himself. For such a man, being consecrated by a sacrifice of the mind <note>original: "intellectual sacrifice"</note>, approaches God in a white garment and with a truly pure **freedom from emotion** <note>original: "impassivity"; referring to the philosophical state of *apatheia*, where the soul is not disturbed by worldly desires</note> of soul and **lightness** <note>original: "levity"</note> of body. He is not burdened by foreign and external fluids <note>original: "juices"; referring to the bodily humors produced by heavy foods</note> or the passions of the soul.

**46.** Indeed, if it is accepted as necessary that those who enter temples built by men for the gods should have pure feet and shoes free from every stain, then in the temple of the Father of All—which is this world—is it not even more proper to preserve our outermost skin-like clothing <note>original: "cutaneous vestment"</note>— [pure]...

<summary>Porphyry argues that purity protects the "divine man" from evil spirits and aligns him with the divine. He contrasts the true philosopher's lasting purity with the temporary and hypocritical abstinence practiced by sorcerers.</summary>

[nourish]ment pure, and to dwell in this temple with an undefiled garment. For if the danger consisted only in the defilement of the body, it might, perhaps, be lawful to neglect it. But now, since every physical body <note>original: "sensible body," meaning a body perceptible by the senses</note> is attended by an outflow <note>original: "efflux"</note> of material spirits <note>original: "dæmons"</note>, the power that is friendly toward and familiar with this impurity is simultaneously present through similarity and alliance with the impurity produced from flesh and blood.

47. Hence, scholars of the divine <note>original: "theologians"</note> have rightly paid attention to abstinence. These things were indicated to us by a certain Egyptian <sup>u</sup>, who also assigned a most natural cause for them, which was verified by experience. For since a corrupt and irrational soul is still compelled to cling to the body when it leaves it, and since the souls of those who die by violence are also detained near the body, this circumstance should prevent a man from forcibly expelling his own soul from his body. The violent slaughter of animals, therefore, forces their souls to remain attached to <note>original: "be delighted with"</note> the bodies which they have left. The soul is by no means prevented from being there, as it is attracted by a kindred nature. This is why many souls are seen to lament, and some remain near unburied bodies. These souls are improperly used by sorcerers <note>original: "enchanters"</note> as servants to their designs, being compelled by them to occupy the body, or a part of the body, which they have left. Since, therefore, these things were well known to the divine scholars, and they also perceived the nature of a corrupt soul and its alliance to the—

---

<sup>u</sup> Reiske, with his usual stupidity <note>Thomas Taylor, the translator, often used harsh language against scholars like Johann Jakob Reiske who prioritized grammar over philosophy</note> where simple word corrections <note>original: "merely verbal emendations"</note> are not concerned, says that this Egyptian is Plotinus, whose country was Lycopolis, in Egypt. But what instance can be found in all antiquity of a student speaking of his teacher in such an indefinite manner? Is it not much more probable that this Egyptian is the priest mentioned by Porphyry in his *Life of Plotinus*? That priest, at the request of a certain friend of Plotinus (who was, perhaps, Porphyry himself), exhibited to Plotinus in the temple of Isis at Rome his attendant spirit <note>original: "familiar dæmon"</note>—or, in modern language, the guardian angel of that philosopher.

bodies from which they were separated <note>original: "divulged"</note>, and the pleasure they received from a union with them. They very properly avoided animal food so that they might not be disturbed by foreign <note>original: "alien"</note> souls—which are impure and were violently separated from the body—and which are attracted to things of a similar nature. Likewise, they avoided it so that they might not be hindered by the presence of evil spirits <note>original: "dæmons"</note> while approaching the highest God alone [or without being burdened with things of a foreign nature]*.

48. Experience abundantly taught these scholars of the divine <note>original: "theologians"</note> that the nature of a similar body attracts the soul. For this reason, those who wish to receive the souls of prophetic animals into themselves swallow their most vital parts, such as the hearts of crows, moles, or hawks. They believe that by doing this, they have a soul present with them that predicts the future like a god, entering into them through the ingestion <note>original: "intromission"</note> of the animal's body.

49. Very properly, therefore, the philosopher—who is also the priest of the God who is above all things—will abstain from all animal food. This is because he is earnestly endeavoring to approach the "alone" God through himself "alone," <note>This refers to the Neoplatonic concept of the soul's solitary journey to the ultimate One.</note> without being disturbed by any external attendants. Such a person is also cautious because he is well acquainted with the necessities of nature. For he who is truly a philosopher is skilled in and an observer of many things; he understands the works of nature and is wise, temperate,

* In accordance with this, the Pythagorean Demophilus beautifully observes: <note>original Greek: Γυμνος αποσταλεις σοφος, γυμνιτευων καλεσει τον πεμψαντα· μονου γαρ του μη τοις αλλοτριοις πεφορτισμενου επηκοος ο Θεος.</note> "The wise man, having been sent here naked, should invoke the one who sent him while remaining naked. y This expression of "approaching *alone* to the *alone* God" was derived by Porphyry from his master, the great Plotinus, who divinely concludes his *Enneads* as follows: <note>original Greek: και ουτω Θεων και ανθρωπων θειων και ευδαιμονων βιος, απαλλαγη των αλλων των τηδε, ανηδονος των τηδε, φυγη μονου προς μονον</note> "This, therefore, is the life of the Gods, and of divine and happy men: a release from all other earthly concerns, a life unaccompanied by human pleasures, and a *flight of the alone to the alone*."

temperate and modest, and is in every way his own savior. Just as a priest of a specific god is skilled in arranging the statues of that deity and in his secret rites <note>original: "orgies" — in the ancient sense, these were secret religious rituals or festivals, not necessarily sexual in nature</note>, mysteries, and similar ceremonies, so also the priest of the Highest God is skilled in how that God’s statue—the soul—ought to be formed, and in the purifications and other practices through which he is joined to this divinity.

50. If, therefore, the priests and diviners of local sacred rites command themselves and others to abstain from graves, from unholy people, from menstrual cycles <note>original: "menstrual purgations"</note>, and from sexual intercourse <note>original: "venereal congress"</note>, as well as from vulgar or mourning spectacles and sounds that excite the passions—because the presence of impure people often causes disturbances for the diviner (which is why it is said that sacrificing at the wrong time causes more harm than good)—then will he who is the priest of the Father of all things allow himself to become a tomb <note>original: "sepulchre"</note> for dead bodies? Will such a person, filled with defilement, try to associate with the Transcendent God? It is enough that we must take "parts of death" from fruits to support our current life. However, this is not yet the place for that discussion. We must, therefore, investigate further what pertains to sacrifices.

51. Someone might say that we would abolish a great part of divination—specifically, the inspection of internal organs <note>original: "viscera"; referring to the practice of extispicy, or reading omens in the guts of sacrificed animals</note>—if we stop killing animals. But the person making this objection should also be willing to kill human beings. For it is said that future events are even more visible in human organs than in those of beasts; indeed, many foreign nations <note>original: "Barbarians"</note> practice divination using human entrails. Just as it would be a sign of great injustice and bottomless greed <note>original: "inexhaustible avidity"</note> to kill members of our own species for the sake of divination, it is also unjust to slay a non-rational animal for this purpose. But it does not

<summary>Porphyry argues that a priest of the Supreme God must maintain even greater purity than local priests, specifically by refusing to eat animal flesh. He also refutes the argument that animal sacrifice is necessary for divination by pointing out the inherent injustice of killing for such purposes.</summary>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that a true philosopher does not need the traditional, bloody methods of divination (like inspecting animal entrails) because he seeks a direct, internal connection with the divine.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>

It does not belong to the present discussion to investigate whether gods, or daemons <note>original: "dæmons" — in Greek philosophy, these are spiritual beings intermediate between gods and humans, not necessarily evil spirits</note>, or a soul liberated from the animal body exhibit signs of future events to those who explore such signs through the indications provided by the internal organs <note>original: "viscera"; referring to the practice of reading omens in the guts of sacrificed animals</note>.

52. Nevertheless, we allow those whose lives revolve around external matters—having once acted impiously toward themselves—to be carried along toward whatever they are drawn to. But we rightly say that the person we call a philosopher, who is separated from external concerns, will not be disturbed by daemons, nor will he be in need of diviners or the internal organs of animals. For he earnestly endeavors to be separated from the very things for which divinations are performed. He does not commit himself to weddings only to bother a diviner about the marriage, nor does he inquire about business, a servant, an increase in property, or any other object of common pursuit. The subjects of his investigation are not clearly indicated by any diviner or the organs of animals. Instead, as we have said, approaching the supreme God through himself—the God who is established in his own true, inner depths—he receives from there the precepts of eternal life. He moves toward that goal by focusing the whole of himself, and rather than seeking a diviner, he prays that he may become a companion who converses with <note>original: "confabulator"</note> the mighty Jupiter <note>original: "Jupiter" — here referring to the supreme deity or the Intellect of the universe</note>.

53. For if such a person is driven by some necessary circumstance, there are good spirits who will indicate and prevent what may happen and what must be avoided through dreams, symbols, and omens for the person living in this manner, who is a servant of the divine. For it is only necessary to turn away from evil and to know what is most honorable in the world, and everything in the universe that is good, friendly, and familiar. But vice and an ignorance of divine matters are dreadful; through them, a man is led to despise and defame things of which he has no knowledge. This is because nature does not proclaim these truths with a voice that can be heard by the ears.

<summary>Porphyry argues that a true philosopher does not need animal sacrifice or divination for worldly concerns because he seeks direct communion with God and is guided by good spirits.</summary>
<keywords>divination, daemons, viscera, philosopher, Jupiter, spiritual guidance, internal life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that animal sacrifice and the subsequent consumption of meat are not necessary for a spiritual life, even if one believes in divination. He begins to provide historical examples of human sacrifice to show that just because a sacrifice happened, it does not justify eating the victim.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM [ANIMAL FOOD]</header>

But since Nature herself is of the intellect <gloss>²</gloss>, she initiates those who honor her through the intellect. Even if one were to accept the art of divination for the purpose of predicting the future, it does not necessarily follow that animal flesh must be eaten. Similarly, it does not follow that animal food should be introduced just because it is appropriate to sacrifice to gods or daemons <note>original: "dæmons"; in Greek philosophy, these are spiritual beings intermediate between gods and humans, not necessarily evil spirits</note>. For not only the history related by Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle</note>, but many other accounts inform us that in ancient times men were sacrificed; yet it must not be inferred from this that humans are to be eaten.

54. To show that we do not make these claims lightly, and that what we have said is abundantly confirmed by history, the following accounts testify. In Rhodes, on the sixth day of June, a man was sacrificed to Saturn <note>original: "Saturn"; the Roman name for the Greek Titan Cronus</note>. This custom prevailed for a long time before it was eventually changed into a more humane form of sacrifice. One of those men who had been sentenced to death by a public decision was kept in prison until the festival of Saturn <note>original: "Saturnalia"</note> began. As soon as this festival commenced, they brought the man out of the city gates, opposite the temple of Aristobulus <note>a title meaning "Best Counselor"</note>, and after giving him wine to drink, they cut his throat. In the island now called Salamis, but formerly named Coronis, a man was sacrificed in the month the Cyprians call Aphrodisius. This sacrifice was made to Agraule, the daughter of Cecrops and the nymph Agraulis. This custom continued until the time of Diomedes. After—

---

<gloss>²</gloss> Nature, considered as the last of the causes that create this physical and perceptible world, "limits," says Proclus in his commentary on the *Timaeus* <note>original: "Proclus in Tim."</note>, "the progressions of incorporeal essences, and is full of forms and powers through which she governs the affairs of this world. She is indeed a Goddess, when considered as being deified, though not in the primary sense of the word. Through her highest point, she encompasses the heavens, but through them, she rules over the changing realm of generation; and she everywhere weaves together individual natures in a wonderful connection with the whole." See more on this subject in my translation of that work.

<summary>Porphyry argues that practicing divination or sacrifice does not justify eating meat, citing historical instances of human sacrifice—such as those in Rhodes and Salamis—to demonstrate that "sacred" acts do not always dictate what is appropriate to consume.</summary>

Afterwards it was changed, so that a man was sacrificed to Diomedes <note>original: "Diomed"</note>. The temples of Minerva <note>Athena</note>, of Aglauros <note>original: "Agraule"</note>, and Diomedes were all contained within the same enclosure. The man who was chosen to be killed was first led by young men three times around the altar; afterward, the priest pierced him in the stomach with a lance. He was then thrown onto the funeral pyre and entirely consumed by fire.

55. This sacred custom was, however, abolished by Diphilus, the king of Cyprus, who lived around the time of Seleucus the theologian. In his place, Damon <note>original: "Dæmon"</note> substituted an ox for a human, making the later sacrifice of equal religious value to the former. Amosis also abolished the law of sacrificing humans in the Egyptian city of Heliopolis; the truth of this is confirmed by Manetho in his book *On Antiquity and Piety*. These sacrifices were made to Juno <note>Hera</note>. The priests would conduct an investigation as if they were trying to find pure calves that had been marked by a seal. Three men were sacrificed on the day appointed for this purpose, but Amosis ordered that three waxen images be substituted in their place. In Chios, they also sacrificed a man to "Omadius" Bacchus <note>see note below</note>, where the victim was torn to pieces; Euelpis of Carystus says the same custom was practiced in Tenedos. To this we may add that the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note>, according to Apollodorus, sacrificed a man to Mars <note>Ares</note>.

56. Moreover, the Phoenicians, in times of great calamity,

---

<note>This title "Omadius" is used in two of the Orphic hymns (Hymn 51, line 7, and Hymn 29, line 5). The following seems to be the reason why Bacchus (Dionysus) is called by this name: In Orphic Theology, Bacchus represents the "intellect" and Ippa represents the "soul of the world." Orpheus says that the intellect is carried on the head of the soul. We are told this by Proclus in his commentary on the *Timaeus* (page 124). Jacob de Rhoer, the editor of this work, was therefore completely mistaken when he said: "I do not doubt that *Omadius Bacchus* (original Greek: ὠμάδιος Διόνυσος) is the same as the *raw-eater* (original Greek: ὠμηστής; original Latin: *crudivorus*)." Furthermore, Scaliger, in his translation of the Hymns, incorrectly translates *Omadius* as "porter" (original Latin: *bajulus*). In the theology, Bacchus is the one being carried; he does not carry Ippa.</note>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s extensive catalog of historical human sacrifices across different cultures. He argues that the historical existence of human sacrifice does not justify cannibalism, just as the ritual sacrifice of animals should not necessarily justify eating them.</meta>

either of war, or excessive drought <note>original: "dryness"</note>, or plague <note>original: "pestilence"</note>, sacrificed some one of their dearest friends, who was selected by vote for this purpose. The Phoenician history also is full of instances of men being sacrificed; this history was written by Sanchoniatho in the Phoenician tongue and was translated into Greek in eight books by Philo Byblius. Furthermore, Ister, in his collection of the *Cretan sacrifices*, says that the Curetes <note>Attendants of the gods in Crete</note> formerly sacrificed children to Saturn <note>the Greek Titan Cronus</note>. And Pallas, who is the most reliable of those who have collected what pertains to the mysteries of Mithras, says that under the Emperor Hadrian <note>original: "Adrian"</note> the sacrificing of men was nearly totally abolished. For, prior to his time, in Laodicea in Syria, they anciently sacrificed a virgin to Minerva <note>Athena</note>, but now they sacrifice a deer <note>original: "stag"</note>. 

The Carthaginians, too, who live in Libya, formerly sacrificed men; but this custom was abolished by Iphicrates. And the Dumatii, a people of Arabia, annually sacrificed a boy whom they buried under the altar, which they used as a cult statue. Phylarchus narrates that it was the general custom of all the Greeks, before they went to war, to sacrifice <note>original: "immolate"</note> men. I omit to mention the Thracians and Scythians, and also the Athenians, who killed the daughter of Erechtheus and Praxithea. And even at present, who is ignorant that in the great city of Rome, in the festival of Jupiter Latialis, they cut the throat of a man? Human flesh, however, is not on this account to be eaten, even though a man should be sacrificed through some necessity. For when a famine takes place during a siege, some of the besieged feed on each other, yet at the same time those who do so are deemed loathsome <note>original: "execrable"</note>, and the deed is thought to be impious.

57. After the first war likewise, waged by the Romans against the Carthaginians in order to obtain Sicily, when the mercenary soldiers of the Phoenicians revolted, and the African troops deserted along with them, Hamilcar <note>original: "Amilcar"</note>, who was surnamed Barca <note>original: "Barkas"</note>, in attacking the Romans, was

<summary>Porphyry lists various historical instances of human sacrifice among the Phoenicians, Greeks, Romans, and others, noting that many of these rituals were eventually abolished. He argues that just as cannibalism is considered loathsome even in dire necessity, the ritual sacrifice of a being does not make its flesh acceptable to eat.</summary>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that historical or ritualistic precedents for killing do not justify eating meat. He moves from discussing human sacrifice to discussing historical instances of cannibalism during war, arguing that these exceptions do not create a moral rule.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.</header>

[Hamilcar] <note>original: "Amilcar"</note> was reduced to such a scarcity of food that at first his men ate those who fell in battle. Later, when these were gone, they ate their captives; thirdly, they ate their servants; and finally, they attacked and devoured their own fellow soldiers, who were selected by lot to be slaughtered for this purpose. But Hamilcar, taking charge of the men in his power, ordered his elephants to trample the soldiers who had acted in this manner. He believed it was not sacred to allow them to remain among other men any longer. 

He did not accept that humans should be eaten just because certain people had been driven to do so. Neither did his son, Hannibal, agree; when he was leading his army into Italy, he was advised by a certain person to train his troops to feed on human flesh so they would never be in want of food. Therefore, it does not follow that because famine and war have caused the eating of other animals, it is also necessary to feed on them for the sake of pleasure—just as we do not admit that humans are to be eaten for such reasons. Nor does it follow that because animals are sacrificed to certain powers, it is also necessary to eat them. Even those who sacrifice humans do not, for that reason, feed on human flesh. Through these arguments, it has been demonstrated that it does not necessarily follow that animals are to be eaten simply because they are sacrificed.

58. Furthermore, it is confirmed by theologians themselves that those who understood the nature of the powers in the universe offered blood sacrifices to <term>dæmons</term> <note>In Neoplatonism, these are intermediary spirits between gods and humans; they are not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense</note>, not to the Gods. For they also assert that among dæmons, some are malevolent <note>original: "malefic"</note>, while others are benevolent. These benevolent spirits will not bother us if we offer them the first-fruits <note>The first portion of a harvest or meal, dedicated to a deity</note> of only those things which we ourselves eat, and by which we nourish either the soul or the body. Therefore, after we have added a few more observations to show that the natural, unspoiled ideas of the common people agree with a correct opinion regarding the Gods, we shall conclude—

<summary>Porphyry argues that cannibalism during famine was punished by leaders like Hamilcar and Hannibal, proving that extreme necessity does not justify a practice as a moral norm. He also asserts that blood sacrifices are intended for lesser spirits (dæmons) rather than the true Gods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument against animal sacrifice. this book. Those poets, therefore, who are wise, even in a small degree, say:

> What man is so gullible and mindless,
> What man so ignorant, as to think the gods
> Rejoice in fiery bile and fleshless bones—
> Food not even fit for hungry dogs—
> Or think that they will ever reward such givers?

> My offerings to the gods shall be of cakes
> And frankincense alone; for I make my sacrifice
> To deities, not to friends.

59. Apollo also, when he orders men to sacrifice according to ancestral customs <note>original: "paternal institutes"</note>, appears to refer everything to ancient practice. But the ancient custom of sacrificing was, as we have shown before, with cakes and fruits. For this reason, sacrifices were called *thusiai*, *thuelai*, and *thumelai* <note>original: $\vartheta\upsilon\sigma\iota\alpha\iota, \vartheta\upsilon\eta\lambda\alpha\iota, \vartheta\upsilon\mu\varepsilon\lambda\alpha\iota$—these terms all share a root related to smoke or incense</note>. Even the phrase "the act of sacrificing" <note>original: $\alpha\upsilon\tau\text{o }\text{ }\tau\text{o }\vartheta\upsilon\varepsilon\iota\nu$ (*auto to thuein*)</note> signified the same thing as "to offer incense" <note>original: $\tau\text{ov }\vartheta\upsilon\mu\iota\alpha\nu$ (*tou thumian*)</note>, which we now call "to sacrifice something extra" <note>original: $\varepsilon\pi\iota\vartheta\upsilon\varepsilon\iota\nu$ (*epithuein*)</note>. For what we now call "to sacrifice" <note>original: $\vartheta\upsilon\varepsilon\iota\nu$ (*thuein*)</note>, the ancients called "to perform or make" <note>original: $\varepsilon\rho\delta\varepsilon\iota\nu$ (*erdein*)</note>. 

> They perform <term>hecatombs</term> <note>A massive public sacrifice, traditionally of 100 oxen</note> of bulls or goats
> Offered to Apollo.

60. But those who introduced extravagance into sacrifices were unaware that, along with it, they also introduced a swarm of evils: namely superstition, luxury, the idea that the divine nature may be bribed <note>original: "corrupted"</note> by gifts, and the belief that sacrifices can compensate for injustice. Otherwise, why would some make an offering <note>original: "oblation"</note> of three animals with gilded horns, while others offer hecatombs? And why did Olympias, the mother of Alexander the Great, sacrifice a thousand of each species of animal, unless lavishness had eventually reached such a point that

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument against the belief that the Gods require or desire animal sacrifice. He suggests that such beliefs lead to moral corruption, as people assume they can "buy off" the Gods for their misdeeds.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
# ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK II.

superstition? But when the young man was informed that the Gods rejoiced in magnificent sacrifices, and, as they say, in solemn banquets of oxen and other animals, how could he possibly act wisely, even if he wanted to? Furthermore, when he believed that these sacrifices were acceptable to the Gods, how was it possible for him not to imagine that he was permitted to act unjustly? He would think he could clear himself of his wrongdoings <note>original: "exonerate himself from erroneous conduct"</note> through sacrifices. But if he had been convinced that the Gods have no need of these things, and that they look instead to the character <note>original: "manners"</note> of those who approach them—*and believe that a right opinion of the Gods, and of reality itself, is the greatest sacrifice possible*—how could he fail to be self-controlled, holy, and just?

61. To the Gods, indeed, the most excellent offering <note>original: "oblation"</note> is a pure mind and a soul free from worldly passions, along with a modest gift of our own property and other things. This should not be done carelessly, but with the greatest eagerness. For the honors which we pay to the Gods should be accompanied by the same readiness as that with which we offer the best seat to worthy men, and the way we stand up to greet and salute them. It should not be like the reluctant speed with which we pay a tax <note>original: "tribute"</note>. For a person must not use language like the following toward God:

> If, Philinus, you recall to mind
> And love me for the benefits which I
> Conferred on you, it is well; since for the sake
> Of these alone my generosity was bestowed.

For divinity is not satisfied with such claims as these. And hence Plato says in his *Laws* that it is the role of a good man to sacrifice and to be always in communication with the Gods through prayers, offerings, sacrifices, and every kind of religious worship; but for a bad man, great effort spent on the Gods is ineffective and in vain. For the good man knows what ought to be sacrificed, and from what it is necessary to abstain;

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's discussion on the nature of true piety, moving from the idea that the Gods value character over sacrifice to the importance of maintaining one's philosophical principles despite social pressure or the habits of the corrupt.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE, &c.</header>

...what things should be offered to the divine, and what types of first-fruits should be sacrificed. However, a bad person who honors the Gods based on his own corrupt disposition and pursuits acts more impiously than piously in doing so. 

For this reason, Plato believed that a philosopher should not associate <note>original: "be conversant with"</note> with people of corrupt habits; this is neither pleasing to the Gods nor useful to humanity. Instead, the philosopher should try to guide such people toward a better way of life. If he cannot achieve this, he must be careful that he does not himself fall into their depravity. 

Plato adds that once a philosopher has entered the right path, he should continue along it without fearing danger from the masses, or any insults <note>original: "blasphemy"</note> that might occur. It would be a terrible thing <note>original: "a thing of a dire nature"</note> if the Syrians refuse to taste fish, the Hebrews avoid pork <note>original: "swine"</note>, and most Phoenicians and Egyptians abstain from eating cows—and though many kings have tried to change these customs, the people who follow them would rather face death than violate the law [which forbids them to eat these animals]. And yet, would we choose to violate the laws of nature and divine commands simply because we fear other people or their threats of harm? 

The divine assembly <note>original: "choir"</note> of Gods and holy men might rightly be outraged if they see us paying attention to the opinions of corrupt people or being frightened by their threats. This is especially true since we spend every day contemplating how we may become [philosophically] detached <note>original: "dead"</note> from the distractions of this present life.

<summary>Porphyry argues that philosophers must remain committed to divine laws and natural principles regardless of social pressure. He contrasts the unwavering dietary commitments of various cultures with the philosopher's duty to remain uncorrupted by the opinions of the masses.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from Porphyry's discussion of how individual piety and the philosophical life are affected by meat-eating to a broader exploration of justice. He begins Book 3 by shifting the focus from the philosopher's relationship with the Gods to the ethical treatment of other living beings.</meta>

### ON
# ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.
## BOOK THE THIRD.

---

1. In the two previous books, Firmus Castricius <note>Firmus Castricius was a Roman friend and student of Porphyry to whom this work is addressed.</note>, we have demonstrated that eating animal meat contributes neither to self-control <note>original: "temperance"</note> and simple living, nor to the religious devotion <note>original: "piety"</note> that is essential for a life of philosophical contemplation <note>original: "the theoretic life"</note>; instead, it is actually harmful to it. However, since the most beautiful part of justice consists of devotion to the Gods, and because this is mainly achieved through abstaining from meat, there is no reason to fear that we will violate our duties to other humans while we maintain our devotion to the Gods. 

Socrates, therefore, says—contradicting those who argue that pleasure is the highest good—that even if every pig and goat agreed with that idea, he would never be convinced that our happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note> was found in the enjoyment of physical pleasure <note>original: "corporeal delight"</note>, as long as the mind <note>original: "intellect"</note> rules over all things. Similarly, we say that even if all wolves and vultures were to praise the eating of meat, we would not admit they spoke rightly, because humans are by nature harmless <note>original: "innoxious"</note> and should avoid seeking pleasure for themselves by hurting others. 

We will now move on to the discussion of justice. Since our opponents claim that justice should only apply to members of our own species—and for this reason deny that animals, which they call "irrational," can be wronged by humans—let us present the true view, which is also the Pythagorean <note>original: "Pythagoric"</note> view. We will demonstrate that every soul that shares in...

<summary>Porphyry begins the third book by arguing that true justice involves being harmless to others, refuting the idea that humans only owe ethical treatment to other humans and not to animals.</summary>
<keywords>justice, Socrates, Pythagoras, ethics, abstinence, Firmus Castricius, animal rights, harmlessness</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for extending justice to animals by examining the nature of reason. Porphyry challenges the Stoic view that animals are completely irrational, arguing instead that they possess both internal thought and external expression.</meta>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

sense and memory are rational. Once this is proven, we can extend justice to every animal—a point that even our opponents would then have to admit. We will now summarize what the ancient philosophers have said on this subject.

2. According to the doctrine of the Stoics <note>The Stoics were a school of Hellenistic philosophy that taught that virtue is the only good and that animals, lacking reason, fall outside the sphere of justice.</note>, there are two kinds of reason: one is internal, and the other is external $^a$. Furthermore, they say reason can be either "right" (correct) or "erroneous" (mistaken). It is necessary, then, to explain which of these two types animals are supposedly lacking. Are they deprived of right reason alone? Or are they entirely destitute of both internal reason and externally expressed reason?

The Stoics seem to claim that animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> suffer from an entire lack of reason, not just a lack of "right" reason. For if they merely denied that animals possess *right* reason, then animals would not be considered "irrational" beings. Instead, they would be "rational" beings, just like almost all humans are according to the Stoics. In their view, perhaps only one or two "wise men" can be found who possess right reason, while the rest of humanity is depraved. Although some people make progress toward wisdom and others remain deeply depraved, the Stoics consider all humans to be rational. 

Therefore, Porphyry argues, it is only because of their own self-love that the Stoics claim all other animals are irrational, using that term to mean a total lack of reason. However, if we are to speak the truth, reason is clearly visible in all animals. In many of them, it is so highly developed that it nearly reaches perfection.

3. Since reason is two-fold—one kind consisting of external speech and the other in the internal disposition of the soul—we shall begin with the external kind, which is expressed through the voice. If external reason is defined as the voice which, using the tongue, signifies the internal passions <note>original: "passions," meaning the emotions or states of the soul</note> of the soul (which is the most common definition and one not unique to a single philosophical sect), and if it is used to indicate...

---

$^a$ This *external reason* <note>original Greek: "logos prophorikos" (λoγoς πρoφoρικoς), meaning "uttered reason" or speech</note> is speech.

<summary>Porphyry challenges the Stoic claim that animals lack reason, arguing that if reason is defined as the ability to think and communicate feelings, then animals clearly possess it.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument against the Stoic claim that animals lack reason. Having established that reason can be internal (thought) or external (speech), he now argues that animal vocalizations constitute a form of rational speech that is simply foreign to human ears.</meta>

## ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK III

(regarding the conception of internal reason)—if this is the case, in what way are animals that have a voice deficient? Do they not rationally perceive the manner in which they are inwardly affected before it is vocally expressed by them? By a "discursive perception," <term>discursive perception: a form of reasoning that moves step-by-step from one thought to another, rather than knowing something instantly by intuition.</term> I mean the perception produced by the silent discourse which takes place in the soul. 

Since, therefore, whatever is expressed vocally by the tongue is reason—in whatever manner it may be expressed, whether in a foreign <note>original: "barbarous"</note> or a Greek, a canine or a bovine mode—other animals that are vocal also participate in it. Humans, indeed, speak in accordance with human laws of speech, but other animals speak in accordance with the laws which they received from the Gods and nature. 

But if we do not understand what they say, what does that matter? For the Greeks do not understand what is said by the Indians, nor do those who are educated in Athens <note>original: "Attica"</note> understand the language of the Scythians, or Thracians, or Syrians. The sound of the one falls on the ears of the other like the clatter <note>original: "clangor"</note> of cranes. Yet, to those who speak them, these vocal sounds can be written and articulated, just as ours can be by us. 

Nevertheless, the vocal sounds of the Syrians, for instance, or the Persians, are inarticulate to us and cannot be expressed by our writing, just as the speech of animals is unintelligible to all men. When we hear the Scythians speak, we perceive only a noise and a sound with our ears, but we are ignorant of the meaning of what they say. To us, their language appears to be nothing but a clatter, having no clear articulation, and appearing to employ only a single sound, whether longer or shorter. The variety of their speech is not at all meaningful to us, but to them, the vocal sounds are intelligible and have great distinctions, in the same way that our language has to us. 

The same thing occurs in the vocal sounds of other animals. For the several species of these understand the language which is adapted to them, but we only hear a noise, the meaning of which we are ignorant of, because no one who has learned...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal vocalizations are a form of rational speech governed by natural laws, comparing the human inability to understand animals to the way Greeks fail to understand foreign human languages.</summary>

If, however, it is necessary to believe in the ancients, as well as those who have lived in our times and the times of our fathers, there are some among them who are said to have heard and understood the speech of animals. Thus, for instance, this is narrated of Melampus and Tiresias <note>Melampus and Tiresias were famous seers in Greek mythology said to have gained the power to understand birds</note>, and others of their kind. The same thing is related of Apollonius of Tyana <note>A 1st-century Greek philosopher and miracle worker</note> in a time not long before our own. For it is told of him that once, when he was with his associates, a swallow happened to be present and chirping; he said that the swallow was indicating to other birds that a donkey laden with corn had fallen down outside the city, and that as a result of the donkey's fall, the corn was spread on the ground<gloss>b</gloss>. <margin>b Philostratus tells this story about Apollonius in his biography of him.</margin> One of my own associates also informed me that he once had a boy for a servant who understood the meaning of all the sounds of birds, and who said that all of them were prophetic and announced what would shortly happen. He added that the boy was deprived of this knowledge by his mother; fearing that he would be sent to the Emperor as a gift, she poured urine into his ear while he was asleep.

4. However, even if we set aside these stories due to the innate <note>original: "connascent"</note> tendency toward skepticism <note>original: "incredulity"</note> that is born within us, I think no one is ignorant of the fact that there are some nations even now who understand the sounds of certain animals because of a natural affinity with them. Thus, the Arabs understand the language of crows, and the Tyrrhenians <note>An ancient people of Italy, often identified with the Etruscans</note> understand eagles. And perhaps all men would understand the language of all animals if a dragon were to lick their ears <note>In Greek myth, dragons or serpents licking one's ears was a common way for a mortal to gain the gift of prophecy or animal speech</note>. Indeed, the variety and difference in the vocal sounds of animals indicate that they are significant. Hence, we hear one sound when they are terrified, but another, of a different kind, when they call their associates; another when they summon their...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that animal sounds are a form of rational language. He provides examples of birds imitating human speech and the deceptive "crocotta" as evidence that animals possess the mental capacity for language, even if humans don't always understand them.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>

[they use one sound] to call their young to food, another when they lovingly embrace each other, and another when they incite one another to battle. Their vocal sounds are so diverse that even those who have spent their entire lives observing them find it extremely difficult to determine their meaning because there are so many variations. Therefore, diviners who make predictions from the behavior of ravens and crows identify the differences in sounds up to a certain point, but they ignore the rest because they are too difficult for humans to grasp. 

Yet when animals speak to one another, these sounds are clear and meaningful to them, even if we do not understand them. If, however, it is evident that they imitate us, learn the Greek language, and understand their keepers, who could be so bold <note>original: "impudent"</note> as to deny that they are rational simply because he does not understand what they are saying? Crows, magpies, robins, and parrots imitate humans and remember what they have heard. They are obedient to their teacher while being taught, and many of them use what they have learned to identify those who have misbehaved in the house. 

But there is also the Indian hyena, which the locals call the *crocotta* <note>The crocotta was a mythical dog-wolf of India or Ethiopia, often linked to the hyena in ancient natural histories</note>. It speaks in such a human-like way—and without any teacher—that it goes to houses and calls out the name of a person it knows it can easily defeat. It imitates the voice of the person most dear to the victim, who would most likely respond to the call. Even though the Indians know about this trick, they are still deceived by the similarity; obeying the call, they come outside and are killed. 

If, however, not all animals imitate us or are capable of learning our language, what does that prove? For not every human is quick to learn or capable of imitating—I won't even say the sounds of animals—but even the five dialects of the Greek language. Furthermore, some animals perhaps do not speak because they have not been taught, or because they are hindered by the physical structure <note>original: "ill conformation"</note> of their vocal organs. For example, when I was in Carthage, I raised a tame partridge that I had caught...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals possess rational speech, citing bird imitation and the human-like calls of the "crocotta" hyena as proof of their intellectual capacity. He suggests that our failure to understand them is a human limitation, not a lack of animal reason.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal intelligence, focusing on animals that respond to human calls and the fallacy of judging rationality based on whether humans can understand an animal's "language."</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM [ANIMAL FOOD]</header>

flying, and which, in the course of time and by associating with us, became so exceedingly mild that it was not only diligently attentive to us, caressing and sporting with us, but even uttered a sound corresponding to the sound of our voice. As far as it was capable, it answered us; and it did this in a manner different from the way partridges are accustomed to call to each other. For it did not utter a corresponding sound when we were silent, but only when we spoke to it.

5. It is also recorded that some "dumb" animals obey their masters with more readiness than any domestic servants. For instance, a lamprey was so accustomed to the Roman Crassus <note>Marcus Licinius Crassus (c. 115–53 BC), a wealthy Roman general and politician</note> that it would come to him whenever he called it by its name. Because of this, Crassus was so affectionately attached to it that he mourned its death intensely, even though he had previously endured the loss of three of his own children with more composure. Many also report that the eels in Arethusa <note>A famous fountain in Syracuse, Sicily</note>, and the fish called *saperdæ* near the Mæander River, are obedient to those who call them. 

Therefore, is not the mental image <note>original: "imagination"</note> of a speaking animal the same, whether it actually reaches the tongue or not? And if this is the case, is it not absurd to call the voice of man alone "uttered reason" <note>original: "external reason"; this refers to the Stoic concept of *logos prophorikos*, or reason expressed through speech</note>, while refusing to give that name to the voices of other animals? For this is just as if crows should think that their voice alone is "uttered reason," and that we are irrational animals because the meaning of the sounds we utter is not obvious to them. Or, it is as if the inhabitants of Attica <note>The region surrounding Athens, whose residents were famously proud of their refined dialect</note> should claim the title of "speech" for their dialect alone, and consider everyone else irrational who is ignorant of the Attic tongue. Yet, the inhabitants of Attica would likely understand the croaking of a crow sooner than they would understand the language of a Syrian or a Persian. 

But is it not absurd to judge whether a being is rational or irrational based on whether we happen to understand the meaning of their vocal sounds, or based on their silence or speech? Following that logic, someone might even say that the God who is above all things, as well as the other [divine beings]...

He contends that human inability to understand animal vocalizations does not prove animals lack reason, comparing it to humans being unable to understand foreign languages.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument against the idea that speech is the only indicator of reason. He argues that just because humans and animals do not share a language, it does not mean animals lack the internal faculty of thought.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD. — BOOK III.</header>

Gods <note>The text here concludes a comparison: if we judge rationality only by human speech, we might mistakenly conclude that even the Gods are not rational.</note> are not rational because they do not speak. The Gods, however, silently indicate their will, and birds understand their will more rapidly than men. When they have understood it, they communicate it to men as well as they are able, and different birds act as the messengers to men for different Gods. Thus, the eagle is the messenger of Jupiter <note>original: Zeus</note>, the hawk and the crow of Apollo, the stork of Juno <note>original: Hera</note>, the *crex* <note>The corn crake or a similar long-legged rail</note> and the bird of night <note>The owl</note> of Minerva <note>original: Athena</note>, the crane of Ceres <note>original: Demeter</note>, and other birds serve as messengers for other deities. 

Moreover, those among us who observe animals and are raised alongside them know the meaning of their vocal sounds. The hunter, for instance, can tell from the barking of his dog whether the dog is searching for a hare, or has already found it; whether he is pursuing the game, whether he has caught it, or if he is on the wrong track because he has lost the scent. In the same way, a cowherd knows when a cow is hungry, thirsty, or weary, and when she is driven by sexual desire <note>original: "incited to venery"</note> or is looking for her calf, based on her different lowings <insert>from her different lowings</insert><sup>c</sup>. A lion also shows through his roaring that he is threatening; a wolf shows by his howling that he is in a bad condition; and shepherds know what their sheep want from the way they bleat.

6. Furthermore, animals are not ignorant of the meaning of human voices when we are angry, when we speak kindly to them, call them, pursue them, ask them to do something, or give something to them. In short, they are not ignorant of anything usually said to them, but are appropriately obedient to it. This would be impossible for them to do unless their reasoning faculty was active <note>original: "intellection energized"</note> as a result of being stimulated by a similar faculty in us. Even the intensity of their emotions is calmed by certain musical melodies <note>original: "modulations"</note>; for example, stags, bulls, and—

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals are rational beings that communicate with the Gods and humans. He illustrates this by listing birds as divine messengers and showing how humans (like hunters and shepherds) understand the specific meanings behind animal sounds.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that animals possess a form of rational thought, specifically addressing the logical processes observed in hunting dogs and the capacity for animals to learn and teach.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

other animals, from being wild, become tame. Those, too, who are decidedly of the opinion that brute animals are deprived of reason, yet admit that dogs have a knowledge of <term>dialectic</term> <note>The art of investigating or discussing the truth of opinions; logical argumentation.</note> and make use of the <term>syllogism</term> <note>A form of reasoning in which a conclusion is drawn from two given or assumed premises.</note> which consists of several <term>disjunctive propositions</term> <note>A logical statement that presents "either/or" alternatives.</note>. This occurs when, in searching for their game, they happen to come to a place where there are three roads. For they reason in this way: "The beast has fled either through this road, or through that one, or through the remaining road. But it has not fled through this one, nor through that one; therefore, it must have fled through the remaining third road." After this logical process, they resume their pursuit along that road. 

It may, however, be easily argued that animals do these things naturally because they were not taught by anyone to do them—as if we also were not granted reason by nature, even though we likewise give names to things because we are naturally adapted to do so. Besides, if it is necessary to believe Aristotle, animals are seen to teach their offspring not only matters pertaining to survival but also how to utter vocal sounds; as the nightingale, for instance, teaches her young to sing. And as he also says, animals learn many things from each other and many from men. The truth of what he asserts is testified by all trainers of colts, by every jockey, horseman, and charioteer, and by all hunters, herdsmen, keepers of elephants, and masters of wild beasts and birds. 

Therefore, he who evaluates things correctly will be led by these instances to attribute intelligence to brute animals. But he who is inconsiderate and ignorant of these things will be induced to act rashly, his inexhaustible greed <note>original: "avidity"</note> working within him against them. For how is it possible that he should not defame and slander <note>original: "calumniate"</note> animals, when he has already determined to cut them in pieces as if they were stones? However, Aristotle, Plato, Empedocles, Pythagoras, Democritus, and all such as...
