<sup>d</sup> <note>Porphyry derived this from the treatise of Plutarch, in which he investigates whether land animals are more sagacious than aquatic animals. (Original Latin title: *De sollertia animalium*)</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals demonstrate logical reasoning (such as a dog's use of elimination when tracking) and the capacity to learn and teach, refuting the idea that they are merely unthinking machines.</summary>
<keywords>animal intelligence, logic, syllogism, Aristotle, dialectic, learning, animal training, Porphyry, Plutarch</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that animals possess internal reason. He argues that the difference between human and animal reason is a matter of degree rather than a fundamental difference in nature.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

Those who have endeavored to discover the truth concerning animals have acknowledged that they participate in reason.

7. But it is now necessary to show that brute animals have internal reason. The difference, indeed, between our reason and theirs appears to consist—as <term>Aristotle</term> mentions somewhere—not in essence, but in the "more and the less" <note>The difference is one of degree or intensity rather than a difference in kind.</note>. This is similar to the opinion held by many that the difference between the Gods and us is not essential, but consists in this: that in them there is a greater, and in us a lesser, accuracy of the reasoning power.

And indeed, as far as it pertains to the senses and the rest of physical organization—regarding the sense organs and the flesh—nearly everyone will grant that these are arranged similarly in us as they are in brutes. For they not only share with us natural passions and the behaviors produced through them, but we may also observe in them conditions that are unnatural and diseased. 

No one of sound mind, however, would say that brutes are incapable of reasoning because of the difference between their body types and ours, especially when he sees that there is a great variety of physical types among men, depending on their race and the nations to which they belong; yet, at the same time, it is granted that all of them are rational. 

A donkey, therefore, can be afflicted with <term>catarrh</term> <note>An old medical term for an inflammation of the mucous membranes in the airways.</note>, and if the disease spreads to his lungs, he dies in the same manner as a man. A horse, too, is subject to <term>purulence</term> <note>The formation or discharge of pus, indicating infection.</note> and wastes away because of it, just like a human. He is likewise attacked by shivering, the gout, fever, and madness, in which case he is also said to have a "depressed countenance" <note>A look of deep lethargy or sadness.</note>. A mare, when pregnant, if she happens to smell a lamp that has just been extinguished, suffers a miscarriage in the same manner as a woman. An ox, and likewise a camel, are subject to—

---

<note>
This was the opinion of the <term>Stoics</term> <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that emphasized logic and ethics, often viewing humans as uniquely rational.</note>, but it is highly erroneous. For the supreme divinity, being <term>superessential</term> <note>original: "superessential"; existing above or beyond the very nature of "being" or essence itself.</note>, transcends even intellect itself, and much more transcends reason (which is an evolved perception of things). This is also the case with every other deity according to <term>Platonic theology</term>, when considered according to his <term>hyparxis</term> <note>original: "hyparxis"; a Greek term meaning "existence" or "subsistence," used by Neoplatonists to describe the highest, most essential "spark" or summit of a divine being.</note>, or summit. See my translation of <term>Proclus</term> on the *Theology of Plato*.
</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals possess internal reason and that the differences between human and animal minds are matters of degree, not essence. He supports this by pointing out the physiological and pathological similarities between humans and animals, such as shared susceptibility to specific diseases.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, internal reason, animal intelligence, Stoicism, pathology, Neoplatonism, Proclus, hyparxis, superessential</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument from page 101, where he lists various physical ailments that both humans and animals suffer in common, such as respiratory issues and inflammations, to prove their biological similarity.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

...fever and insanity. A raven becomes scabby and suffers from leprosy <note>original: *lepra*; likely referring to a scaly skin disease or mange</note>. A dog is also afflicted with these, as well as with gout and madness <note>rabies</note>. Furthermore, a pig is subject to hoarseness, and a dog even more so; this is why this disease in humans is named after the dog: <term>cynanche</term> <note>original: *kynanche*, from the Greek words for "dog" and "to choke"; a historical term for a severe sore throat or respiratory obstruction</note>. These things are known to us because we are familiar with these animals; we remain ignorant of the diseases of other animals only because we do not live alongside them.

Castrated animals also become more feminine. Because of this, when roosters are castrated, they no longer crow; their voices become feminine, similar to the voices of men who have lost their testicles. Likewise, it is impossible to distinguish the bellowing or the horns of a castrated bull from those of a cow. When stags are castrated, they no longer shed their antlers but retain them in the same way that eunuchs retain their hair <note>Ancient biologists, including Aristotle, observed that eunuchs did not suffer from male-pattern baldness</note>. If they are castrated before they have grown antlers, they never produce them, just as it happens to those who are made eunuchs before they have grown a beard. Thus, the bodies of nearly all animals are affected in the same way as ours regarding the physical afflictions to which they are subject.

8. Consider, then, whether all the emotions of the soul in animals are not also similar to ours. It is not the exclusive domain of man to perceive flavors by taste, colors by sight, odors by smell, sounds by hearing, or cold, heat, and other physical objects by touch; the senses of animals are capable of the same perceptions. Animals are not deprived of their senses simply because they are not men, just as we are not to be deprived of our reason simply because the Gods—if they possess it—are rational beings. 

Regarding the senses, however, other animals appear to surpass us greatly. What man can see as sharply as a <term>dragon</term>? <note>In this context, the author refers to the Greek *drakon*, a large serpent or snake, which was legendary for its keen eyesight</note>. (This is not even referring to the mythical Lynceus <note>A figure in Greek mythology said to have such keen sight he could see through trees and stones</note>). It is for this reason that the poets use the word *drakein* to mean "to see" <note>original: δρακειν (*drakein*); the Greek verb for seeing or looking is etymologically related to the word for serpent</note>. Similarly, an eagle sees a hare from a great height. What man hears more sharply than cranes, who...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals share the same physical vulnerabilities and sensory capabilities as humans, often surpassing humans in the sharpness of their senses.</summary>
<keywords>animal physiology, castration, cynanche, sensory perception, eagle, dragon, ancient biology, Porphyry</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that animals and humans share many traits. Having established their physical similarities in the previous pages, he now focuses on the superiority of animal senses and the shared capacity for reason.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

...are able to hear from such a great distance that it is beyond the reach of human sight? And as for smell, almost all animals so greatly surpass us in this sense that things which they can detect and are obvious to them remain hidden from us; for instance, they can recognize and smell different kinds of animals by their tracks. This is why men use dogs as their guides for the purpose of discovering the hiding places of a boar or a stag. 

Furthermore, while we are only slowly aware of changes in the atmosphere, these are immediately perceived by other animals; because of this, we look to them for signs of the future state of the weather. Regarding plant juices and flavors, they also know the distinctions between them so accurately that their knowledge of which are disease-causing, healthy, or harmful <note>original: morbific, salubrious, and deleterious</note> surpasses the knowledge of doctors. 

Aristotle says that animals whose sensory powers are more refined are also more prudent <note>original: *phronimos*; in Greek philosophy, this refers to practical wisdom or foresight</note>. While the differences in physical bodies can make it easier or harder to be affected by external things, or make the exercise of reason more or less quick, these physical traits are not capable of changing the "essence" of the soul. Physical differences cannot change the nature of the senses, alter the passions, or make an animal entirely abandon its true nature. 

It must be granted, therefore, that animals participate more or less in reason, rather than being perfectly deprived of it. We must not admit the idea that one animal has reason while another does not. However, just as within a single species of animals one body is more healthy and another less so—and just as there is a great difference between a naturally good and a naturally bad disposition during a disease—the same is true for souls. One soul is naturally good, while another is corrupt <note>original: depraved</note>; and among corrupt souls, one has more corruption than another. Even among good men, there is no perfect equality; for Socrates, Aristotle, and Plato are not all good in the exact same way. Nor is there a total agreement in their opinions. Therefore, it does not follow that just because we have more intelligence than other animals, it is for this reason...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals possess superior sensory perception compared to humans and participate in the essence of reason, even if humans possess it to a higher degree.</summary>
<keywords>animal intelligence, sensory perception, Aristotle, Socrates, Plato, reason, essence of the soul, prudence</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that differences in physical ability or excellence between species do not imply a total lack of internal reason or soul. He moves from discussing sensory perception to the demonstration of practical wisdom and self-preservation in the animal kingdom.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

[it does not follow] they are to be deprived of intelligence. We must not say that partridges do not fly simply because hawks fly higher, nor that other hawks do not fly because the bird called the *phassophonos* <note>original: "phassophonos"; likely a variety of hawk or falcon</note> flies higher than they do, and higher than all other birds. Someone might admit that the soul is influenced by the body <note>original: "co-passive"; meaning the soul and body experience sensations together</note>, and that the soul is affected by the body when the latter is in a state of health or illness; however, in this case, the soul by no means changes its fundamental nature. If the soul is only influenced by the body and uses it as an instrument, she may be able to accomplish many things through it that we cannot—even when that body is organized differently from ours. When the body is affected in a certain way, the soul may sympathize with it, and yet still not change its own proper nature.

9. It must be demonstrated, therefore, that there is a rational power in animals and that they are not deprived of wisdom <note>original: "prudence"; referring to *phronesis*, or practical intelligence</note>. First, each animal knows whether it is weak <note>original: "imbecile"</note> or strong; as a result, it defends certain parts of itself while attacking with others. Thus, the panther uses its teeth, the lion its claws and teeth, the horse its hooves, the ox its horns, the rooster its spurs, and the scorpion its sting. The serpents in Egypt, however, use their spit (which is why they are called *ptuades* <note>original: πτυαδες; "spitters"</note>), and with this, they blind the eyes of those who approach them. In this way, every different animal uses a different part of itself for attack in order to save its own life. 

Furthermore, some animals—specifically those that are robust—feed and live far away from men. Others, which are of a less dominant nature <note>original: "ignoble"</note>, live far from stronger animals and, instead, dwell nearer to humans. Of these, some stay at a distance from larger animals, such as sparrows and swallows that build their nests in the roofs of houses; but others associate closely with men, as, for instance, dogs. They likewise change their places of residence at certain [times]...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals possess a rational soul and practical wisdom, evidenced by their self-awareness regarding their physical strengths and their strategic use of defenses and habitats to ensure survival.</summary>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that animals possess a form of reason and practical wisdom. He moves from discussing the physical defenses of animals to their ability to learn, their capacity for memory, and the nature of their rationality compared to humans and divine beings.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

times, and they know everything that contributes to their own advantage. Similarly, a reasoning energy of this kind can be seen in fish and birds. All these details were gathered extensively by the ancient writers in their works concerning the wisdom <note>original: "prudence"; referring to *phronesis*, or practical intelligence</note> of animals. These matters are also discussed at length by Aristotle, who says that every animal exquisitely contrives a home that serves its survival and safety.

10. However, anyone who argues that these behaviors are simply natural to animals fails to realize that they are asserting that animals are rational by nature. If one does not admit this, then one must also conclude that reason does not exist in us naturally, nor does it reach perfection through growth, even though we are naturally suited to receive it. A divine nature <note>A reference to a god or a higher spiritual being</note>, indeed, does not become rational <gloss>g</gloss> through learning, for there was never a time when such a being was irrational. Instead, rationality is inherent <note>original: "consubsistent"</note> to its very existence. A divine being is not barred from being called rational just because it did not acquire reason through training. Yet, just as with humans, many things are taught to animals by nature, while other things are learned through discipline. Animals <note>original: "Brutes"</note> learn some things from one another, and are taught other things by humans, as we have mentioned. They also possess memory, which is a primary requirement for exercising reason and wisdom. Furthermore, they have vices and can be envious, though their bad qualities are not as extensive as those of humans; their vices are of a milder nature. This is evident because a builder can never lay a house's foundation unless he is sober, nor can a shipwright properly place

---

<gloss>g</gloss> Reason in a divine intellect exists as a cause—or in a way superior to human reason—and therefore is not a "discursive energy" <term>discursive energy; original Greek: *diexodikē energeia*, meaning reason that moves step-by-step through a process</term>, but is instead an evolved cause of things. Although reason is "discursive" (moving from one thought to another) in a divine soul, it differs from our reason because it perceives an entire form all at once, rather than in steps as we do when we reason.

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal behavior is evidence of natural rationality and memory, noting that while animals can learn from nature or humans, their "vices" are less severe than those of mankind. He also distinguishes between human discursive reasoning and the immediate, causal reasoning of divine beings.</summary>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument from Book III of "On Abstinence from Animal Food," where he asserts that animals possess rationality. Having previously discussed their ability to learn and remember, he now compares the orderly and "virtuous" behavior of animals in procreation and social life against the often-disordered behavior of humans.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM [ANIMAL FOOD]</header>

...the keel of a ship, unless he is in good health; nor can a farmer <note>original: "husband-man"</note> plant a vine, unless he applies his mind to the task; yet nearly all men, even when they are intoxicated, are capable of begetting children. This, however, is not the case with other animals. They reproduce only for the sake of having offspring. For the most part, once the males have made the female pregnant, they no longer attempt to mate with her; nor, if they should try, would the female allow it.

The sheer magnitude of the lustful insolence and lack of self-control <note>original: "intemperance"</note> found in men regarding these matters is evident. In other animals, however, the male is aware of the female's labor pains <note>original: "parturient throes"</note> and, for the most part, shares in those same pains—as is clearly seen in roosters <note>original: "cocks"</note>. Others help the females hatch their eggs, such as male doves. They also provide a suitable place for the birth of their offspring, and after the young are born, both parents clean them and themselves. Anyone who observes carefully will see that everything they do proceeds in an orderly manner. They show affection to those who feed them, recognize their masters, and give warning signs when someone acts suspiciously <note>original: "insidiously"</note>.

11. Who, likewise, is unaware of how much social animals <note>original: "gregarious animals"</note> maintain justice toward one another? This sense of justice is preserved by ants, by bees, and by other similar animals. And who is ignorant of the faithfulness <note>original: "chastity"</note> of female ring-doves toward the males with whom they pair? They have been known to kill those found to have committed adultery. Or who has not heard of the justice of storks toward their parents? <note>Referring to the ancient Greek concept of "antipelargosis," the belief that young storks cared for their elderly parents by feeding them and helping them fly.</note>

In various species of animals, a specific virtue is prominent, to which each species is naturally adapted. Should we deny that they are rational simply because this virtue is natural and consistent? It might be necessary to deny them rationality if their actions were not the proper results of virtue and rational wisdom <note>original: "sagacity"</note>. However, if we simply do not understand how these works are achieved because we are unable to penetrate the reasoning they use...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals exhibit more self-control and social "justice" than humans, particularly in their mating habits and care for offspring, which serves as evidence of their inherent rationality.</summary>
<keywords>animal rationality, procreation, social justice in animals, virtue, ants, bees, storks, temperance</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that animals possess a form of rationality. He compares our inability to fully understand an animal's mind to our inability to see the mind of the sun, yet we acknowledge the sun's intelligence through its effects. He then discusses the mutual dependence and natural "justice" that exists between humans and domestic animals.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD. — BOOK III.</header>

...we should not, for this reason, accuse them of being irrational. No one is able to penetrate the mind of that divinity, the sun; yet, based on its works, we agree with those who prove it to be an intellectual and rational being <note>original: "essence"</note>.

12. One might rightly wonder at those who admit that justice originates <note>original: "derives its subsistence"</note> from the rational part of the soul, yet call wild animals—those that have no association with men—savage and unjust, while failing to extend justice to the animals that *do* live with us. These domestic animals, like humans, would lose their lives if they were deprived of human society. Birds, dogs, and many four-legged animals <note>original: "quadrupeds"</note>—such as goats, horses, sheep, donkeys <note>original: "asses"</note>, and mules—would perish if they were separated from mankind. Nature, the creator <note>original: "fabricator"</note> of their bodies, designed them to need humans, and designed humans to require their help. In this way, Nature created an innate sense of justice in them toward us, and in us toward them. 

It is not surprising if some animals are savage toward people. What Aristotle says is true: if all animals had plenty of food <note>original: "nutriment"</note>, they would not act ferociously toward each other or toward humans. Friendships and enmities among animals are created because of food—even if it is meager—and because of the territories they inhabit. But if humans were reduced to the same desperate conditions <note>original: "straits"</note> as animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> regarding food, how much more savage would we become than the animals we consider wild? War and famine prove this truth; in such times, men do not even stop themselves from eating one another. And even without war and famine, they eat animals that are raised alongside them and are perfectly tame.

13. Someone might say, however, that animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> are indeed rational, but that they lack a certain relationship <note>original: "habitude"</note>, proximity, or alliance with us. But anyone who claims this will, first of all, be turning them into irrational animals by...

<summary>Porphyry argues that the mutual dependence between humans and domestic animals creates a natural bond of justice, and suggests that animal "savagery" is merely a result of food scarcity rather than a lack of reason.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, animal rationality, justice, Aristotle, domestic animals, nature, scarcity, ethics of eating animals</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument for the rationality of animals. Having previously discussed how domestic animals and humans share a mutual need and a form of natural justice, Porphyry now addresses the counter-argument that animals are not rational because they do not form legal pacts with humans. He also contrasts animal loyalty with human ingratitude.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

...result of stripping them of any connection <note>original: "alliance"</note> to our own nature. Next, he will argue that their relationship with us depends only on the usefulness <note>original: "utility"</note> we gain from them, rather than their sharing in reason. However, our goal is to show that animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> are rational beings, not to investigate whether a formal agreement <note>original: "compact"</note> exists between them and us. For even among humans, not everyone forms an alliance with us, yet no one would say that a person is irrational just because they have not entered into a pact. 

On the other hand, many animals are enslaved by men. As one writer rightly said, although they are in a state of servitude due to human wickedness <note>original: "improbity"</note>, they nevertheless use their own wisdom and justice to make their masters act as their servants and caretakers <note>original: "curators"</note>. Furthermore, the vices of animals are obvious, and these especially prove their rationality. They experience envy; the males compete with each other for the favor of the females, and the females for the attention of the males. 

There is one vice, however, that they do not have: acting deceitfully <note>original: "insidiously"</note> toward those who help them. Instead, they are perfectly loyal <note>original: "benevolent"</note> to those who are kind to them, placing so much confidence in them that they will follow wherever they are led, even to slaughter and obvious danger. Even if someone feeds them for his own benefit rather than theirs, they will still feel kindly toward their owner. In contrast, humans often act more hostiley toward the person who has supported <note>original: "nourished"</note> them than toward anyone else; nor do they pray for any death so much as the death of their own benefactor.

14. Indeed, the actions of animals are carried out with so much deliberation <note>original: "consideration"</note> <note>original Greek: Οὕτω δ' ἔστι λογιστικὰ ὧν δρᾷ — "In this way they are calculating in what they do"</note>, that they frequently realize that the food set out for them is nothing more than...

---

<note>In the original Greek text: "In this way they are calculating in what they do, etc." For the word "calculating" <note>original: λογιστικὰ (logistika)</note>, the scholar Lipsius suggests reading "rational" <note>original: λογικὰ (logika)</note>, and Meerman suggests "logic" <note>original: λογιστική (logistike)</note>. However, there is no reason to substitute another word, as Platonic writers use "the reasoning faculty" <note>original: τὸ λογιστικὸν (to logistikon)</note> as equivalent to "that which reasons" <note>original: τὸ λογιζόμενον (to logizomenon)</note>.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal rationality is proven by their complex emotions like envy and their profound loyalty to human caretakers, which he contrasts with human ingratitude. He rejects the idea that a lack of legal "compacts" between species proves animals lack reason.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's defense of animal intelligence, specifically focusing on their ability to recognize danger and their capacity to learn complex human skills.</meta>

...than a trap <note>original: "snare"</note>, even if they approach it because of lack of self-control or hunger. Some of them do not approach it immediately, while others approach it slowly. They also try to see if it is possible to take the food without falling into danger, and frequently—because reason overcomes their desire <note>original: "rationality vanquishing passion"</note>—they leave without being harmed. Some of them even mock and urinate on the traps set by men. Others, however, because of their greed—even though they know they will be caught—are no different than the companions of Odysseus <note>original: "associates of Ulysses"</note>; they would rather die than go without eating. 

Likewise, some people have argued quite well that animals are far more prudent than we are, based on the environments they are assigned to inhabit. For just as those beings who dwell in the ether <note>original: "æther"; the high, pure air of the heavens where the gods were thought to reside</note> are rational, so too—they say—are the animals that occupy the region closest to the ether, which is the air. Next, aquatic animals differ from these, and finally, terrestrial <note>original: "terrene"</note> animals differ from aquatic ones in their degrees of rationality. We belong to the class of terrestrial animals, living in the very dregs <note>original: "sediment"</note> of the universe. For while we should not assume the Gods possess greater excellence simply because of the places they inhabit <note>original: "illuminate"</note>, in the case of mortal beings, this hierarchy may be accepted.

15. Furthermore, since animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> acquire knowledge of the arts—even human arts—and learn to dance, drive a chariot, fight in single combat, walk on ropes, write and read, play the flute <note>original: "pipe"</note> and the harp, shoot arrows, and ride: since this is the case, can you any longer doubt whether they possess the power to learn <note>original: "power which is receptive of art"</note>? For the capacity for these arts is clearly visible in them. Where else would they receive these skills, unless reason—where the arts reside—was inherent in them? They do not hear our voices as mere sound; they also perceive the specific meaning of the words, which is a result of rational intelligence. But our opponents argue that animals perform poorly what is done by men. To this we reply, that

He also notes a cosmological hierarchy where humans, as land-dwellers, occupy a lower rational tier than beings of the air or ether.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that animals possess a form of reason, refuting the claims of those who use human standards—like the existence of cities or written laws—to deny animal intelligence.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

nor do men perform all things well. If we do not admit this, it would mean that some men would be victors in a contest for no reason, and others would be defeated for no reason. Our opponents also claim that animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> do not consult with one another, form assemblies, or act in a judicial capacity. But tell me, do all men do these things? Among the masses, does action not often happen before deliberation? And how can anyone prove that animals do not consult one another? No one can produce an argument sufficient to prove that they do not. In fact, those who have written detailed accounts of animals show the exact opposite. 

As for other objections raised by our adversaries in their speeches, they are perfectly frivolous—such as the claim that animals have no cities of their own. But neither do the Scythians <note>An ancient nomadic people from central Eurasia known for their mobile lifestyle.</note>, who live in wagons, nor do the Gods. Our opponents also add that animals have no written laws. To this we reply that neither did humans have them while they were happy. It is said that Apis <note>A legendary king and lawgiver of Argos.</note> was the first to establish laws for the Greeks when they were in need of them.

16. Because of their own greed <note>original: "voracity"</note>, men claim that animals do not possess reason; but the Gods and holy men honor them as if they were sacred suppliants <note>A person making a humble or earnest plea to someone in power; here, animals seeking protection from the gods.</note>. Because of this, the God <note>The Oracle of Apollo at Delphi.</note> told Aristodicus of Cyme <margin>See the first book of Herodotus, chapter 159.</margin> that the sparrows were his suppliants. Socrates also—and before him, Rhadamanthus—used to swear oaths by animals. 

The Egyptians consider them to be Gods, whether they actually believed this to be true or intentionally depicted the Gods in the forms of oxen, birds, and other animals so that people would abstain from killing animals as much as they abstain from killing men. Or perhaps they did this for more mystical reasons <note>The more mystical reason why the Egyptians worshiped animals appears to be this: they believed a living being was a better image of divinity than a lifeless statue. Therefore, they revered animals as visible and living representations of the invisible powers of the Gods. See Plutarch's treatise *On Isis and Osiris*.</note>. Similarly, the Greeks associated a ram with the...

<summary>Porphyry refutes arguments against animal reason by pointing out that many human societies lack cities or written laws, and notes that both the Gods and ancient philosophers treated animals with sacred respect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that animals possess a spiritual and rational nature, evidenced by how the ancients honored them in religion, myth, and divine titles.</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>

the statue of Jupiter <note>Roman name for the king of the gods, Zeus.</note>, but gave the horns of a bull to the statue of Bacchus <note>Dionysus, the god of wine and frenzy.</note>. They likewise fashioned the statue of Pan from the form of a man and a goat; but they represented the Muses and the Sirens as winged, and also Victory <note>Nike</note>, Iris, Love <note>Eros</note>, and Hermes. Pindar, too, in his hymns, represents the Gods—when they were driven out by Typhon <note>A monstrous giant in Greek mythology who challenged Zeus for control of the cosmos.</note>—not as resembling men, but as other animals. And Jupiter, when in love with Pasiphae <note>In most standard myths, Zeus became a bull to carry off Europa; Pasiphae was the queen of Crete who fell in love with a bull. Porphyry may be referencing a specific variant or making a rare error here.</note>, is said to have become a bull; at another time, he is said to have been changed into an eagle and a swan. Through all of this, the ancients indicated the honor which they paid to animals. They showed this in an even greater degree when they asserted that Jupiter was nursed by a goat. 

The Cretans, following a law established by Rhadamanthus <note>A legendary king of Crete, son of Zeus, known for his absolute justice.</note>, swore by all animals. Nor was Socrates joking when he swore by the dog and the goose; in doing so, he swore in accordance with the just son of Jupiter [Rhadamanthus]. Nor did Socrates speak in sport when he said that swans were his fellow-servants <note>A reference to Plato's *Phaedo*, where Socrates argues that swans sing at their death not from sorrow, but because they are prophetic birds of Apollo and rejoice at the good things in the afterlife.</note>. Furthermore, fables obscurely suggest that animals have souls similar to ours when they say that the Gods, in their anger, changed men into animals <note>original: "brutes"</note>, and that once they were so changed, the Gods afterwards pitied and loved them. For stories of this kind are told about dolphins and halcyons <note>Kingfishers</note>, and about nightingales and swallows.

17. Each of the ancients who had been successfully nursed by animals boasted of this more than of their own parents and teachers. Thus, one boasted of having been nursed by a she-wolf, another by a female deer <note>original: "hind"</note>, another by a she-goat, and another by a bee. Semiramis <note>A legendary queen of Assyria.</note> gloried in having been brought up by doves, Cyrus in being nursed by a dog, and a Thracian in having a swan for his nurse (who also took the name of his nurse). 

Because of this, the Gods also obtained their surnames: Bacchus was called *Hinnuleus* <note>original: "Hinnuleus," meaning "like a young stag or fawn."</note>; Apollo was called *Lyceus* <note>original: "Lyceus," meaning "wolf-like."</note> and *Delphinius* <note>original: "Delphinius," meaning "of the dolphin."</note>; and Neptune and Minerva were called *Equestris* <note>original: "Equestris," meaning "of horses."</note>. Even Hecate <note>Goddess of magic and the night.</note> is more gracious when she is invoked by the names of a bull, a dog, and a lioness. If, however, those who sacrifice animals and eat them assert that they are irra- <note>The text breaks off here and continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>Porphyry lists numerous mythological examples where gods took animal forms or were raised by animals to show that the ancients considered animals to be sacred and rational. He argues that the divine titles and ancient oaths by animals prove they were held in high esteem rather than seen as mindless creatures.</summary>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that animals possess reason. He refutes the claim that animals are irrational by comparing such excuses to the extreme behaviors of "barbarian" tribes and begins a discussion on the requirements of justice toward sentient beings.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

[...They call animals] irrational in order to lessen the crime of eating them. By this same logic, the Scythians <note>An ancient nomadic people from the Eurasian steppes; in Greek literature, they were often used as examples of "barbaric" behavior.</note>—who are said to eat their own parents—could similarly claim that their parents are destitute of reason.

18. Through these arguments, and others which we shall mention later when narrating the opinions of the ancients, it is demonstrated that animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> are rational beings. While reason in most of them is indeed imperfect, they are nevertheless not entirely deprived of it. Since, however, our opponents say that justice only applies to rational beings, how is it possible not to admit that we should also act justly toward animals? 

We do not extend the concept of justice to plants because there appears to be much in them that is unconnected with reason. Furthermore, although we use the fruits of plants, we are not accustomed to cut down the entire trunk to get them. We collect corn and legumes <note>original: "leguminous substances"</note> when they have flowered, fallen to the earth, and are already dead. In contrast, no one uses the flesh of dead animals for food (with the exception of fish) unless the animals have been destroyed by violence. Therefore, in these actions, there is much injustice. 

As Plutarch also says¹, it does not follow that just because our nature is in need of certain things, and we use those things, we should therefore act unjustly toward everything. We are permitted to use other things to a certain extent in order to procure the necessary means of survival—even if taking anything from plants while they are living is considered a slight injury to them. However, to destroy other living things for the sake of luxury and the enjoyment of pleasure is perfectly savage and unjust. 

Abstaining from these things neither shortens our life nor prevents us from living happily. If, indeed, the destruction of animals and the eating of flesh were as essential as air, water, plants, and fruits—without which it is impossible to live—this injustice would be a necessary part of our nature. But if many priests of the...

---

¹ See the *Symposiacs* <note>original: "Symposiacs," also known as *Table Talk* (*Quaestiones Conviviales*)</note> of Plutarch, book 9, chapter 8.

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals possess reason and therefore deserve just treatment. He contrasts the necessary consumption of plants with the unnecessary and "savage" slaughter of animals for luxury.</summary>
<keywords>animal rationality, justice, Scythians, Plutarch, luxury vs. necessity, ethics of eating, plant vs. animal consumption</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that justice should be extended to animals. He draws a distinction between the "necessary" use of animals for labor or by-products and the "unnecessary" act of slaughtering them for food.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>

### ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.

The gods and many barbarian <note>original: "barbarians"; referring here to non-Greek cultures that practiced vegetarianism or specific purifications.</note> kings have been attentive to purity. Furthermore, if countless species of animals never taste food of this kind—yet still live and fulfill their natural purpose according to their nature—is it not absurd for someone to claim that we are compelled to wage war with certain animals? Is it not absurd to suggest that we cannot live peaceably with those with whom it is possible to do so? Such a person thinks either that we should live without exercising justice toward anything, or that if we did exercise justice toward all things, we could not continue to exist.

Just as among men, a person who seizes the wealth of others or oppresses a region or city for the sake of their own safety, or for the safety of their children or country, has necessity as a pretext for their injustice. However, someone who acts this way to acquire wealth, or for gluttony <note>original: "satiety"</note> and luxurious pleasure—satisfying desires that are not necessary—appears to be inhospitable, intemperate, and wicked <note>original: "depraved"</note>.

In the same way, the divine <note>original: "divinity"</note> pardons the harm done to plants, the use of fire and water, the shearing of sheep, the milking of cows, and the taming of oxen (subjugating them to the yoke) when it is done for the safety and survival of those who use them. But to deliver animals to be slaughtered and cooked—and thus to be filled with murder—not for the sake of nutrition and satisfying the needs of nature, but making pleasure and gluttony the goal of such conduct, is transcendently unjust and terrible. For it is enough that we use the offspring of horses, asses, and bulls as our substitutes for hard labor—as Aeschylus <note>A famous ancient Greek playwright (c. 525–456 BC) known as the father of tragedy.</note> says—even though they have no need to labor themselves. By being tamed and subjugated to the yoke, they ease our toil.

19. But regarding the person who thinks we should not eat an ox, nor destroy and corrupt spirit and life just to place delicacies and elegant treats on the table for the sake of gluttony: what are they depriving our life of that is truly necessary for our safety or helpful for virtue? To compare

<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: To compare</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that while the necessary use of animals for labor or by-products (like milk and wool) is excusable, slaughtering them for the sake of luxury and gluttony is an act of extreme injustice. He contends that humans can survive and remain virtuous without eating meat, just as many animals do.</summary>
<keywords>animal ethics, justice, gluttony, necessity, Aeschylus, labor, vegetarianism, purity</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument from the previous page, shifting from the "necessity" of using animal products like wool and milk to the ethical distinction between plants and animals. He critiques the Stoic philosophers Zeno and Chrysippus, who argued that animals exist solely for human benefit.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

Comparing plants, however, with animals violates the natural order of things. Animals are naturally sensitive; they are capable of feeling pain, being terrified, and being hurt, and for these reasons, they can be victims of injustice. Plants, on the other hand, are entirely devoid of sensation. Because of this, nothing external can be evil, hurtful, or injurious to them. Sensation is the foundation of all kinship <note>original: "alliance"; the Stoic concept of *oikeiosis*, or the sense of belonging to a community.</note> and the basis for identifying what is foreign to our nature. 

Zeno <note>Zeno of Citium (c. 334–262 BC), the founder of the Stoic school of philosophy.</note> and his followers assert that this kinship is the foundation of justice. Yet, is it not absurd to claim that we must act justly toward certain humans who live by sense alone—possessing no intellect or reason—and who even surpass the most terrifying wild beasts in cruelty, anger, and plunder <note>original: "rapine"</note>? Such people may even be murderous toward their own children and parents, or act as tyrants and the tools of kings. Is it not absurd, I say, to believe we owe justice to such people, but owe no justice to the ox that plows for us, the dog that lives with us, or the animals that nourish us with their milk and clothe our bodies with their wool? Is such an opinion not completely irrational and absurd?

20. But, by Zeus <note>original: "Jupiter"; the Roman name for the Greek king of the gods.</note>, the claim made by Chrysippus <note>Chrysippus (c. 279–206 BC), a Greek Stoic philosopher who argued that animals had no rights because they lacked reason.</note> is considered very probable by our opponents: that the Gods made us for their own sake and for the sake of each other, and that they made animals for our sake. He argues that horses were made to assist us in battle, dogs to hunt with us, and leopards, bears, and lions to give us the opportunity to practice our courage <note>original: "fortitude"</note>. 

Regarding the pig—for here the irony <note>original: "pleasantry"</note> of Chrysippus is most entertaining—he claims it was made for no other purpose than to be sacrificed. He suggests that God mixed a soul into the flesh of this animal as if it were salt, simply to keep the meat from rotting <note>original: "procure for us excellent food"; referring to the Stoic idea that a pig's soul was merely a preservative for its meat.</note> so that it might provide us with good food. Furthermore, to provide us with an abundance of broth and luxurious suppers, he claims divinity provided us with all various kinds of shellfish, the sea snails used for purple dye <note>original: "purples"</note>, sea-nettles, and various kinds of birds <note>original: "winged animals"</note>. According to him, these were created for no other reason than to supply us with variety and pleasure.

<summary>Porphyry argues that because animals have sensations and feelings, they deserve justice, unlike plants. He mocks the Stoic philosopher Chrysippus for suggesting that animals were created by the gods solely as tools or food for human luxury.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Zeno, Chrysippus, Stoicism, justice for animals, sensation, animal soul, anthropocentrism, vegetarianism</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s rebuttal of the Stoic argument that animals were created solely for human consumption. He uses the skeptical arguments of Carneades to highlight the logical flaws in claiming that being slaughtered is a "benefit" to an animal.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

[Nature] provides man with an abundance of pleasure. In doing so, she surpasses all nurses in kindness and fills this earthly realm with countless pleasures and enjoyments. However, anyone who thinks these claims are probable and reflect the divine should consider how they would respond to the argument of Carneades <note>Carneades (c. 214–129 BC) was a skeptic philosopher known for challenging the dogmatism of the Stoics.</note>: that everything produced by nature is *benefited* when it achieves the purpose for which it was designed and created. 

In this context, *benefit* should be understood broadly, meaning what the Stoics call *useful*. For example, he says the pig was produced by nature specifically to be slaughtered and used for food; therefore, when it undergoes this, it reaches its intended purpose and is "benefited." 

But if God fashioned animals for the use of men, what use do we have for flies, lice, bats, beetles, scorpions, and vipers? Some of these are repulsive to the sight, disgusting to the touch, smelly, and have harsh, unpleasant sounds. Others, on the contrary, are simply deadly to anyone who encounters them. 

Regarding the baleen whales, sawfish <note>original: *pistrices*; a term used in antiquity for sawfish or large, terrifying sea monsters</note>, and other species of whales—countless numbers of which (as Homer says<sup>m</sup>) are nourished by "loud-sounding Amphitrite" <note>Amphitrite: a sea goddess and wife of Poseidon in Greek mythology</note>—does the Creator <note>original: *Demiurgus*; the artisan-like figure responsible for fashioning and maintaining the universe</note> not teach us that they were created for the benefit of the natural world as a whole<sup>n</sup>? 

If our opponents admit that not everything was created for us or for our benefit, their argument becomes very confused and unclear. Furthermore, we cannot avoid acting unjustly if we attack and harm animals that were not made for our sake, but were created—just as we were—according to the laws of nature (that is, for the sake of the universe). I will not even mention that if we define justice solely by utility...

---

<sup>m</sup> Odyssey, Book XII, line 96.
<sup>n</sup> The latter part of this sentence, which in the original Greek is "Why did the Creator not teach us in what way they are useful to nature?" Valentinius translates this most erroneously as, "Why did the Creator of things not teach us how they were made by nature for our use?" <note>The translator, Thomas Taylor, is correcting a previous scholar, Valentinius, who misinterpreted Porphyry's point that creatures exist for the universe's sake, not humans'.</note>

<summary>Porphyry critiques the Stoic idea that animals exist only for human benefit, pointing out that many creatures (like pests and sea monsters) are useless or harmful to humans. He argues that animals exist for the sake of the natural order of the universe, and harming them is therefore an act of injustice.</summary>
<keywords>Carneades, Stoics, utility, animal ethics, teleology, Demiurge, Amphitrite, baleen whales, nature's purpose</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s rebuttal of the Stoic argument that animals exist solely for human use. He argues that if we follow that logic, humans could just as easily be seen as food for predators, and he discusses the psychological impact of killing animals on human character.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

things which pertain to us, we shall be forced to admit that we were created for the sake of the most destructive animals, such as crocodiles, whales <note>original: *balænæ*</note>, and serpents. For we receive no benefit from them; instead, they seize and destroy humans who cross their path and use them for food. In doing this, they act no more cruelly than we do—except that they commit this injustice out of hunger and necessity, while we do so out of arrogant recklessness and for the sake of luxury. We frequently put on spectacles in theaters and massacre the majority of animals during hunts. 

By acting this way, a murderous temperament and a brutal nature are strengthened within us, making us indifferent to pity. We may add that those who first dared to perform these slaughters blunted the greater part of human gentleness and made it ineffective. The <term>Pythagoreans</term> <note>Pythagoreans: followers of the philosopher Pythagoras (c. 570–495 BC), who practiced vegetarianism and believed in the transmigration of souls between humans and animals.</note>, however, viewed gentleness toward beasts as a practice of humanitarianism and compassion. Therefore, how is it possible that they should not inspire us to justice more than those who claim that the justice usually shown toward men will be undermined if we do not slaughter animals? For habit is most powerful in increasing those passions in man that were gradually introduced into his nature.

21. Our opponents argue: "Just as the immortal is opposed to the mortal, the incorruptible to the corruptible, and the incorporeal to the corporeal, so too must the irrational nature be opposed to the rational nature which exists in the world. In so many pairs of opposites, this one alone should not be left incomplete or missing." 

[Our opponents speak in this way] as if we did not agree with this, or as if we had not already shown that there is plenty of the irrational among living things. For there is an abundance of it in all things that lack a soul, and we do not need any other opposite to that which is rational. Indeed, everything that is deprived of a soul is irrational...

<summary>Porphyry argues that killing animals for sport and luxury hardens the human heart against pity, whereas the Pythagorean practice of kindness to animals fosters general compassion. He also refutes the philosophical claim that animals must be "irrational" simply to provide a logical opposite to human "rationality."</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoreans, rational vs irrational, cruelty, animal slaughter, habit, justice, Stoic opposition</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s analysis of the Stoic argument regarding the classification of living beings. On the previous page, he began addressing the claim that nature is divided into opposing pairs (mortal/immortal, corporeal/incorporeal). Here, he refutes the idea that "rational" and "irrational" are simple opposing categories that neatly divide all living creatures.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

[...corporeal], being irrational and without intellect, is opposed to that which possesses reason and *dianoia* <note>original: *dianoia*; the discursive energy of reason, or the process of step-by-step logical thinking.</note>. If, however, someone should choose to claim that it is not nature in general, but specifically "animated nature" <note>original: "the animated nature"; meaning living things that possess a soul or life force.</note> that is divided into those with imagination and those without, or those with sensation and those without—so that these opposing traits (possessing a quality versus lacking it) are balanced <note>original: "equiponderant"</note> within the same category—whoever says this speaks absurdly. 

For it would be ridiculous to look within the category of living beings for something that lacks sensation, or something that does not use imagination, because every living being is naturally designed to be sensitive and to possess imagination. Therefore, he cannot rightly demand that one part of living nature be rational and another irrational. He is speaking to people who believe that nothing experiences sensation without also sharing in intelligence, and that nothing qualifies as an "animal" unless it possesses opinion and reasoning—just as every animal naturally possesses senses and impulses. 

For nature—which they correctly claim produced all things for a specific purpose and toward a specific goal—did not make an animal sensitive merely so that it could be passively affected by its environment or have sensory perceptions. Rather, because there are many things that are beneficial and appropriate for it, and many things that are harmful <note>original: "foreign"</note> to it, an animal would not be able to survive for even the shortest amount of time unless it learned how to avoid some things and pursue others. 

Sensation provides every animal with this knowledge of both types of things. However, the ability to grasp and pursue what is useful, and to repel <note>original: "depulsion"</note> and avoid what is destructive and painful, could not possibly exist in animals if they were incapable of reasoning, judging, and remembering, and if they did not naturally possess the power of mental attention <term>animadversive power</term> <note>original: "animadversive power"; the capacity of the mind to turn its attention toward something and perceive it consciously.</note>.

<summary>Porphyry argues that sensation and intelligence are inseparable in living beings, claiming that animals must possess reason and judgment to survive and navigate their environments.</summary>
<keywords>rationality in animals, animated nature, sensation, dianoia, animadversive power, survival instinct, Stoic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument against the Stoic view of animals. Having previously argued that sensation and intelligence are inseparable, he now provides philosophical and anecdotal evidence to show that "sensible perception" (sensation) cannot function without "intellection" (intellectual activity).</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

For if you completely strip animals of expectation, memory, planning, preparation, hope, fear, desire, and anger, then their eyes, ears, senses, and imagination <note>original: "phantasy"; the faculty of forming mental images.</note> will be of no benefit to them, as they would serve no purpose. It would actually be better for them to be deprived of these senses than to labor, feel pain, and be distressed without possessing the power to repel these troubles. 

There is, however, a treatise by Strato the physiologist <note>Strato of Lampsacus (c. 335–c. 269 BCE) was a Peripatetic philosopher known as "the Physicist" or "Physiologist" because of his focus on natural science.</note> in which he demonstrates that it is impossible to have a sensory perception of anything without the active use of the intellect. For we often scan the letters of a book with our eyes, or hear words with our ears, yet they remain hidden from us and escape our notice when our intellect is focused on other things. But afterward, when the mind returns to the subject it had previously ignored, it uses memory to review and process each of those previous details. This is why it is said by Epicharmus <note>A Greek dramatist and philosopher (c. 524–c. 435 BCE).</note>:

-> It is the mind alone that sees and hears, <-
-> And all else is deaf and blind. <-

For the objects that strike the eyes and ears do not produce a sensory perception by themselves unless the intellect is present. On this account, when King Cleomenes <note>Likely Cleomenes I or III of Sparta.</note> was asked for his opinion on a recited poem that everyone else was applauding, he left the decision to those asking him the question; for he said that at that moment, his intellect was away in the Peloponnesus <note>The southern peninsula of Greece, home to the Spartans.</note>. Therefore, it is necessary for intellect to be present in all those who possess sensory perception.

22. Even if we were to grant that the senses do not require the intellect to perform their specific work, the senses still distinguish between what is beneficial <note>original: "appropriate"</note> and what is harmful <note>original: "foreign"</note>. When the senses recognize this difference, the animal must exercise the power of memory: it must dread what will cause pain, desire what will be beneficial, and—if that benefit is absent—it must plan how it may be...

<summary>Porphyry argues that sensory perception is impossible without intellect, citing the philosopher Strato and an anecdote about King Cleomenes to show that the mind must be present for the eyes and ears to function. He asserts that animals must possess intellect to process sensory data, feel emotions like hope or fear, and distinguish between what is helpful or harmful.</summary>

<keywords>Strato the Physiologist, Epicharmus, King Cleomenes, intellect vs. sensation, animal intelligence, memory, sensory perception, phantasy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that animals possess rational faculties. He is responding to Stoic philosophers (the "opponents") who claimed that animals only act on instinct. Porphyry argues that the very definitions the Stoics use for mental processes like memory, preparation, and design are clearly visible in animal behavior.</meta>

<page-num>119</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

at present, and will find methods of pursuing and investigating what is advantageous, and of avoiding and fleeing from hostile occurrences. Indeed, our opponents, in their *Introductions* [as they call them], everywhere teach these things with a tedious long-windedness. They define "design" as an indication of perfection; the "tendency of intellect" toward the object of its perception as an "impulse prior to an impulse"; "preparation" as an "action prior to action"; and "memory" as the "comprehension of some past thing <gloss>p</gloss>, the perception of which, when it was present, was obtained through the senses." There is not one of these faculties which is not rational, and all of them are present in all animals.

Thus, too, with respect to intellectual thoughts: those which are stored in the mind are called by them *notions* <note>original: ἔννοιαι (ennoiai)</note>; but when they are in motion through a reasoning process <note>original: "discursive energy"; the mental act of moving from one idea to another to reach a conclusion.</note>, they name them *perceptions obtained by a reasoning process* <note>original: διανοήσεις (dianoēseis)</note>. 

Regarding all the "passions" <note>In ancient philosophy, passions were not just emotions but "movements of the soul" like anger or fear.</note>—as they are commonly acknowledged to be the result of flawed natures and opinions—it is wonderful that our opponents should overlook the actions and movements of animals. Many of these are the effects of anger, many of fear, and, by Zeus <note>original: "by Jupiter"; Taylor uses the Roman name for the Greek king of the gods.</note>, of envy and competition as well. Our opponents themselves punish dogs and horses when they do wrong; they do this not in vain, but in order to make them better, producing in them, through the pain, a sorrow which we call "repentance." 

Furthermore, the name of the pleasure which is received through the ears is *charming* <note>original: κήλησις (kēlēsis), an ear-alluring sweetness</note>; and the delight which is received through the eyes is called *enchantment* <note>original: γοητεία (goēteia)</note>. Each of these, however, is experienced by animals. For instance, stags and horses are allured by the harmony produced from reed-pipes and flutes; and the...

---

However, instead of "proposition" (axiōmatos), I read it as "thing" (pragmatos). Felicianus <note>Giovanni Bernardino Feliciano, a 16th-century translator of Porphyry.</note> also appears to have found this reading in his manuscript copy of this work; for his version of the passage is, "or memory is the comprehension of a past thing, which the senses had perceived when it was present" <note>original: vel memoriam rei præteritæ comprehensionem, quam præsentem sensus perciperat</note>.

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals possess rational mental states—such as memory, design, and even repentance—which are usually reserved for humans in Stoic philosophy. He notes that animals respond to music and discipline, proving they have complex internal lives.</summary>
<keywords>Stoicism, animal intelligence, memory, notions, emotions, animal training, Greek philosophy, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's critique of Stoic philosophy. He argues against the Stoic claim that animals only act "as if" they have emotions and intelligence, while simultaneously questioning why nature would give animals the "seeds" of reason if they cannot reach the "perfection" of human virtue.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

Crabs, called *paguri* <note>original Greek: *παγουροι* (pagouroi)</note>, are drawn out from their caves by the music of reed pipes. Similarly, the fish called the *thrissa* is said to come out from its hiding places when it hears harmonious music. 

However, those who speak foolishly about such matters claim that animals feel neither joy, nor rage, nor terror. They argue that animals do not plan for what they need, nor do they remember. Instead, they say that a bee *as if* remembers, a swallow *as if* provides what is necessary, a lion is *as if* angry, and a stag is *as if* afraid. I honestly do not know how to answer those who say that animals do not see or hear, but only *as if* see and *as if* hear; that they do not make vocal sounds, but only *as if* make them; and that, essentially, they do not live, but only *as if* live. Any truly intelligent person will quickly admit that these claims are no more sensible than the previous ones and are just as lacking in evidence. 

However, when I compare the manners and lives of humans to those of animals, I see much corruption in animals. I see no obvious pull toward virtue as a main goal, nor any improvement or desire to improve. This makes me wonder why Nature gave the beginnings of perfection—the capacity for reason—to creatures that are never able to reach the final goal of it <note>q</note>. 

But our opponents do not find this absurd at all. For they admit that a parent's love for their children is the foundation of human society and justice. Yet, even though they see that this same affection is strong and abundant in animals, they still deny that animals have any share in justice. This claim is as flawed as the nature of mules; though mules are not missing any reproductive organs, having both a penis and a vulva,

<note>q This doubt might be resolved by admitting that animals possess an imperfect rationality—essentially the "dregs" of the rational faculty. This allows them to form a link in the chain of being between humans and *zoophytes* <term>zoophytes: organisms like sponges or corals that were historically seen as halfway between animals and plants</term>, just as zoophytes link animals to plants. Therefore, because animals have an imperfect reason, they possess only the "beginning" of perfection without the ability to reach the end.</note>

<summary>Porphyry mocks the Stoic argument that animals only act "as if" they have senses and emotions, arguing instead that animals possess a baseline of reason and social affection, even if they cannot reach human-level virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument against Stoic philosophers who deny reason to animals. He argues that reason exists in varying degrees across all living beings, and that the absence of "perfect" reason (wisdom) in animals does not mean they lack the faculty of reason entirely.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

and take pleasure in using these parts, yet they cannot achieve the goal of reproduction. Consider the matter in another way: Is it not ridiculous to say that such great men as Socrates, Plato, and Zeno were no less flawed than any slave, and that they resembled slaves in stupidity, lack of self-control, and injustice? <note>Porphyry is referencing a Stoic paradox: that all non-wise people are equally "vicious" or foolish.</note> If that is the case, why then blame the nature of animals as being neither pure nor formed with enough accuracy to attain virtue? By doing so, you are attributing to them a total lack of reason, rather than just a depravity or weakness of it. This is especially true since our opponents acknowledge that there is a vice belonging to the rational part of the soul, which every animal is full of. For we can see that fear, lack of self-control, injustice, and malice are present in many animals.

23. Someone might think that a nature not suited to achieve the "rightness of reason" <note>original: "rectitude of reason"</note> does not possess reason at all. In the first place, that person is no different from someone who imagines that an ape is not naturally capable of ugliness, or that a tortoise is not naturally capable of slowness, simply because the former cannot achieve beauty and the latter cannot achieve speed. In the second place, this is the opinion of someone who does not see the obvious differences between things. 

Reason is indeed born into us by nature, but right and perfect reason is acquired through study and discipline. Therefore, all living beings participate in reason, but our opponents cannot name a single man who possesses perfect reason and wisdom naturally, even though the number of men is beyond counting. Just as the sight of one animal differs from that of another, and the flight of one bird differs from another (for hawks and grasshoppers do not see in the same way, nor do eagles and partridges), so too does not every creature that possesses reason also possess genius and sharpness in the highest perfection. 

Indeed, there are many signs in animals of social cooperation, courage, and cleverness in obtaining what they need, as well as in managing their own affairs <note>original: "economical conduct"</note>. On the other hand, there are also signs in them of injustice, fear, and foolishness. Therefore, it is a question for some as to which...

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that animals possess varying degrees of reason and moral qualities. He uses specific examples of animal behavior to illustrate that they exhibit virtues like parental care and cleanliness, and argues that the differences between species are a matter of degree rather than a total lack of faculty.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

Are terrestrial or aquatic animals more excellent? <note>r: Plutarch has written a very clever treatise on this subject, titled "Whether Land or Sea Animals are Cleverer."</note> Evidence of these qualities is clear when comparing storks with hippopotamuses <note>original: "river horses"</note>: for storks care for their fathers, but hippopotamuses destroy theirs so that they may mate <note>original: "have connexion"</note> with their mothers. 

This is likewise seen when comparing doves with partridges: for partridges hide and destroy their eggs if the female, while she is sitting on them <note>original: "during her incubation"</note>, refuses to mate with the male. Doves, however, take turns sitting on the nest, alternately keeping the eggs warm; first they both feed the young, and later the male strikes the female with his beak to drive her back to the eggs and her young if she has stayed away from them for too long. 

Antipater <note>A Stoic philosopher who argued that animals lack reason.</note>, however, when he criticizes donkeys <note>original: "asses"</note> and sheep for their lack of cleanliness, somehow overlooks lynxes and swallows. Of these, the lynx removes and entirely conceals and buries its waste, while the swallow teaches its young to throw theirs out of the nest. 

Furthermore, we do not say that one tree is more "ignorant" than another, in the same way we say a sheep is more "stupid" than a dog. Nor do we say that one herb is more "timid" than another, as we say a deer <note>original: "stag"</note> is more timid than a lion. Just as among stationary things, one is not "slower" than another, and among silent things, one is not "less vocal" than another, so too in all things where the power of thought <term>intellection</term> is completely absent, one thing cannot be said to be more timid, more dull, or more self-indulgent than another. 

Because these qualities are present in varying degrees in different beings, they produce the diversity we perceive among animals. It is not surprising that humans so greatly surpass other animals in teachability <term>docility</term>, wisdom <term>sagacity</term>, justice, and social cooperation. After all, many "brutes" <note>An archaic term for non-human animals.</note> surpass all humans in body size, speed <note>original: "celerity of foot"</note>, and also in strength of sight and accuracy of hearing; yet humans are not, for this reason, considered deaf, blind, or powerless. We still run, even if we are slower than—

***

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals display complex behaviors like parental care and cleanliness, suggesting they possess a degree of reason. He contends that while humans may excel in certain intellectual traits, animals' varying traits prove they are not "irrational" but simply different in degree.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, animal intelligence, Plutarch, Antipater, animal ethics, reason, parental care in animals, cleanliness in animals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that animals possess reason. He explains that while animals may have different degrees of intelligence compared to humans, their susceptibility to mental illnesses like madness proves they possess rational faculties that can become "disordered."</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

...among deer <note>original: "stags"</note>, and we see, though not as sharply as hawks. Nature has not deprived us of strength and size, though our possession of these is nothing when compared to the strength and bulk of the elephant and the camel. Hence, in a similar manner, *we must not say that animals <note>original: "brutes"</note>, simply because their intelligence <note>original: "intellection"</note> is duller than ours and because they reason less effectively than we do, neither think logically <note>original: "energize discursively"</note> nor possess intelligence and reason. Instead, we must admit that they possess these faculties, though in a weak <note>original: "imbecile"—in this context meaning feeble</note> and cloudy <note>original: "turbid"</note> manner, just as a dull and disordered eye still participates in the act of seeing.*

24. Innumerable examples could be brought forward to prove the natural wisdom <note>original: "sagacity"</note> of animals, if many such stories had not already been collected and told by others. But this subject must be considered even further. For it appears that if a physical part or a mental power is naturally designed to receive a certain quality, it is also liable to fall into an unnatural state of being <note>original: "preternatural mode of subsistence"</note> when it becomes damaged or diseased. 

For example, the eye is designed for seeing, so it is liable to blindness; the leg is designed for walking, so it can become lame; and the tongue is designed for speech, so it can develop a stammer. Nothing else is subject to these specific defects. Blindness does not happen to something that is not naturally designed to see, nor lameness to something not designed to walk; nor can something that lacks a tongue be prone to stammering, lisping, or being mute <note>original: "dumb"</note>. 

Consequently, an animal cannot be delirious, stupid, or insane if intelligence and the logical process of reasoning <note>original: "discursive energy of reason"</note> are not naturally part of its nature. It is not possible for anything to be affected by a condition <note>original: "passively affected"</note> unless it possesses a power that can be lost, mutilated, or deprived. Furthermore, I have encountered mad dogs and also rabid horses; and some people claim that oxen and foxes can go mad. 

The example of dogs, however, is sufficient for our purpose. It is an undeniable fact that testifies that the animal possesses a significant degree of reason and logical thought <note>original: "discursive energy"</note>; for it is this very faculty that becomes disturbed and confused when the animal suffers from madness.

<summary>Porphyry argues that animals must possess reason because they are susceptible to mental illnesses like madness, which can only affect a being that has a rational mind to begin with.</summary>

...is founded, is fury and madness. When animals are affected this way, we do not see any change in the physical quality of their sight or hearing. It is absurd to deny that a person is "beside themselves," or that their intellectual, reasoning, and memory powers are corrupted when they are afflicted with mental illness <note>original: "melancholy"—in ancient medicine, an imbalance of "black bile" thought to cause delirium or madness</note> or delirium. (Indeed, it is commonly said of the insane that they are "not themselves," but have lost their connection to reason). 

Likewise, consider someone who thinks that rabid <note>original: "mad"</note> dogs suffer from anything other than a distortion of their natural intellectual power—the power intended for reasoning and memory. This distortion becomes so full of internal chaos that it causes them to fail to recognize those they love most and to abandon their usual way of life. Anyone who thinks this way either overlooks the obvious, or, if they do perceive the truth, they are willfully arguing against it. These are the arguments presented by Plutarch in many of his treatises against the Stoics and Peripatetics <note>The Stoics and Peripatetics were rival philosophical schools that generally argued that animals lacked reason and therefore fell outside the boundaries of justice.</note>.

25. But Theophrastus <note>A student and successor of Aristotle, known for his work on biology and ethics.</note> employs the following reasoning: We say that those who are born from the same sources—meaning from the same father and mother—are naturally related to each other. Furthermore, we believe that those who share the same ancestors are also related to us. This is also how we view our fellow citizens, because they share the same land and are united to us by the bonds of social association. We do not necessarily think these fellow citizens are related through common ancestors, unless it happens that their first forefathers were the original sources of our own race, or were descended from the same ancestors. 

Therefore, I believe we should say that a Greek is related and has an affinity to a Greek, and a non-Greek <note>original: "Barbarian"—to the ancient Greeks, this term referred to anyone who did not speak the Greek language.</note> to a non-Greek, and all humans to each other for one of two reasons: either because they originate from the same ancestors, or because they share the same food, customs, and biological category <note>original: "genus"</note>. In this same way, we must admit that all...

<summary>Porphyry argues that animal madness is proof of animal reason and introduces Theophrastus’s theory that all humans—and potentially all living things—share a natural kinship based on common origins or shared traits.</summary>
