<meta>This page continues the argument from Theophrastus regarding the biological and spiritual kinship between humans and other animals. It moves from the idea that humans share common ancestors to the idea that animals possess similar physical and psychological "principles."</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

human beings share an affinity and are kindred with one another. Furthermore, the basic physical principles of all animals' bodies are naturally the same. I do not say this in reference to the primary elements <note>The four classical elements: earth, air, fire, and water</note> of their bodies, because plants are also made of those; rather, I mean the seed, the flesh, and the innate types of biological fluids <note>original: "connascent genus of humours"—the internal liquids like blood and bile that were thought to govern health and temperament</note> that are inherent in animals. 

But animals are much more closely linked to each other because they naturally possess souls, which do not differ from one another—specifically in terms of desire and anger, and beyond these, in their reasoning faculty, and, above all, in their senses. Just as it is with their bodies, some animals have more perfect souls and others less so, yet all of them naturally share the same fundamental principles. This is evident from the similarity of their emotions <note>original: "passions"—meaning the feelings or experiences an animal undergoes</note>. If, however, what we have said is true—that this is how the characters of animals are formed—then all species are indeed capable of intellect; they simply differ in their lifestyles and in the "temperature" <note>original: "temperature"—here referring to the specific mixture or balance of physical elements</note> of the primary elements of which they are composed. If this is admitted, then the animal kingdom shares a kinship and is allied with us. For, as Euripides <note>A famous Athenian playwright (c. 480–406 BCE) known for his tragedies</note> says, they all have the same food, the same breath of life, and the same "purple streams" of blood; he also demonstrates that the common parents of all of them are Heaven and Earth.

26. Therefore, since animals are allied to us, if it should appear—as Pythagoras <note>Ancient Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BCE) who taught that souls are immortal and migrate between human and animal bodies</note> held—that they are assigned the same type of soul that we are, then a person may justly be considered wicked if he does not abstain from acting unjustly toward his own kindred. The fact that some animals are wild <note>original: "savage"</note> is no reason for our kinship with them to be cut off <note>original: "abscinded"</note>. We can find some men who are no less harmful <note>original: "malefic"</note>—and even more so—to their neighbors than wild animals are, and who are driven to injure anyone they meet as if they were propelled by a storm of their own depravity. Because of this, we execute <note>original: "destroy"</note> such men; yet we do not say they are no longer related to humans of a gentle nature. In the same way, therefore, even if some animals are wild, they should be...

<summary>Porphyry argues that humans and animals share a fundamental kinship through their physical makeup (flesh and humors) and their psychological traits (reason, senses, and emotions). He invokes Pythagoras and Euripides to support the idea that acting cruelly toward animals is a form of injustice against one's own relatives.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding the biological and spiritual kinship between humans and other animals. It asserts that because of this kinship, the same principles of justice that apply to humans should be extended to the animal kingdom.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

...should be destroyed, just like men who are savage; but our relationship <note>original: "habitude"</note> or alliance with other and wilder animals is not to be abandoned for this reason. But neither tame nor savage animals should be eaten, just as we do not eat unjust men. Currently, however, we act with great injustice: we destroy tame animals simply because some other animals are savage and "unjust," and then we feed on those that are tame. Regarding tame animals, we commit a double injustice: first, by killing them despite their tameness, and second, by eating them. In short, their death is brought about specifically so they can be used for food.

The following arguments may also be added to these. Anyone who claims that a person who extends justice as far as the animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> corrupts the concept of justice is ignorant. Such a person does not actually preserve justice, but instead increases their own pleasure, which is the enemy of justice. By admitting that pleasure is the ultimate goal of our actions, justice is clearly destroyed. After all, is it not obvious to everyone that justice is increased through abstinence? A person who abstains from eating any living being <note>original: "every thing animated"</note>—even those animals that do not directly benefit society—will be much more careful not to harm members of his own species. For one who loves the entire category <term>genus: here referring to the broad biological group of all living animals</term> will not hate any specific species of animal. The greater his love for the whole category of life, the more he will preserve justice toward every part of that category—especially those to whom he is most closely allied. Therefore, whoever admits that they are allied to all animals will not harm any animal. 

But someone who limits justice only to human beings is like a person trapped in a cramped space, ready to cast aside the prohibition against injustice at any moment. Thus, the Pythagorean banquet <note>A vegetarian meal, following the teachings of Pythagoras</note> is more pleasing than the Socratic banquet <note>While Socrates was known for moderation, he did not strictly forbid meat as the Pythagoreans did</note>. For Socrates...

³ In the original Greek, the text reads: "The greater the category of animals is, the more he will preserve this justice toward the part and that which is his own." On this passage, Reisk <note>Johann Jakob Reiske (1716–1774), a German physician and scholar known for his editions of Greek texts</note> observes, "Perhaps instead of 'the greater the category,' it should read 'The greater the affinity <note>original Greek: η οικειωσις (he oikeiosis)</note> toward the category of animals, the more (certainly) he will preserve justice toward the part,' etc." But, instead of "affinity," it appears to me that "love" <note>original Greek: η φιλια (he philia)</note> should be substituted.

<summary>Porphyry argues that extending justice to animals strengthens human morality, suggesting that universal compassion for all living beings prevents injustice within human society.</summary>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's argument that justice must be extended to all living creatures. He refutes the idea that justice is merely a social contract between humans, arguing instead that true justice is rooted in the rational soul’s ability to avoid harming any harmless being.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK III.</header>

It is often said that hunger is the best sauce for food; but Pythagoras said that to injure no one, and to find joy in justice, is the sweetest sauce. Likewise, the avoidance of animal food will also be the avoidance of unjust conduct regarding what we eat. For God has not designed the world in such a way that we cannot sustain ourselves without injuring others; if this were the case, he would have bound us to a nature that is essentially rooted in injustice. 

Do those people not seem ignorant of the true nature of justice who think it was only introduced as a social contract <note>original: "alliance"</note> between men? If that were the case, justice would be nothing more than a form of philanthropy <note>original: "philanthropy" here refers specifically to a love of fellow humans rather than a love of all life</note>. In reality, justice consists in abstaining from harming anything that is not harmful <term>noxious: harmful, poisonous, or destructive</term> to us. Our understanding of the "just man" must be formed according to this latter definition, not the former.

Therefore, since justice consists in not harming anything, it must be extended to every living <note>original: "animated"</note> creature. On this account, the essence of justice also consists in the rational part of the soul ruling over the irrational part, and the irrational part being obedient to reason. For when reason governs and the irrational part obeys its commands, it follows by absolute necessity that a person will be harmless <note>original: "innoxious"</note> toward everything. When the passions are restrained and desire and anger fade away, and reason holds its proper authority, a likeness to a higher nature—and to the divine—immediately follows. 

The higher nature in the universe is entirely harmless; because it possesses a power that preserves and benefits all things, it lacks nothing itself. We, however, through the practice of justice, are harmless toward all things; yet, because we are bound to a mortal body, we still require certain necessities. But taking what is necessary does not harm even plants when we take what they shed; nor fruits when we use those that are no longer living; nor sheep when, through shearing, we benefit them rather than harm them. By partaking of their milk, we in return provide them with every proper attention and care—

<summary>Porphyry argues that true justice is not just a human social contract but a universal principle of non-harm (innocuity) that must extend to all living creatures. He equates this self-restraint with the rule of reason over passion and a likeness to the divine.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, justice, non-harm, rational soul, animal rights, divinity, philanthropy, vegetarianism, ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's discussion on the nature of justice. He argues that true justice involves the soul's restraint over bodily desires and the extension of harmlessness to all living beings, moving from the specific (family) to the universal (all animals), as a way of becoming more like the divine.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK III.</header>

...tion. <note>likely "moderation" or "abstention," continuing from the previous page</note> Hence, the just man appears to be one who deprives himself of things pertaining to the body; yet he does not, in reality, injure himself. For, by this management of his body and by self-control <term>continence: self-restraint, especially regarding physical appetites</term>, he increases his inward good—that is, his likeness <note>original: "similitude"</note> to God.

27. Therefore, by making pleasure the end of life, true justice cannot be preserved. This is because neither those things that are primarily useful according to nature, nor those that are easily attainable, are enough to bring happiness to fulfillment <note>original: "give completion to felicity"</note>. In many instances, the impulses of our irrational nature, along with perceived utility and need <note>original: "indigence"</note>, have been and still are the sources of injustice. For men claimed they were deprived of animal food in order to keep their physical bodies free from distress, and to satisfy the cravings of their animal nature. 

But if becoming like the divine <note>original: "assimilation to divinity"</note> is the goal of life, then harmless <note>original: "innoxious"</note> conduct toward all things will be maintained to the highest degree. Consider the following comparison: a person led by their passions is harmless only toward their own children and spouse, but treats others with contempt or deceit; this is because their irrational side dominates them, leaving them obsessed with mortal concerns. In contrast, the person led by reason maintains harmless conduct toward their fellow citizens, and even more so toward strangers and all humanity, because they have subjected their irrational nature. Such a person is more rational and divine than the first. 

In the same way, the person who does not limit harmless behavior to humans alone, but extends it to other animals, is even more like the divine. If it were possible to extend this even to plants, they would preserve that divine image to an even greater degree. However, since this is not possible, we may join the ancients† in lamenting the defect of our nature: that we are composed of such conflicting and clashing principles that we are unable to preserve our...

<note>† Porphyry here particularly alludes to Empedocles.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that true justice and likeness to God are achieved through self-restraint and extending harmless conduct beyond humans to include all animals. He suggests that while our physical nature makes total harmlessness toward all life (including plants) impossible, we should strive for the highest degree of non-injury.</summary>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that justice is rooted in the soul's independence from bodily needs. He explains that as the soul descends into the material world, it becomes "needy," and this lack leads to injustice and the harming of other living beings.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK III.</header>

the divine part is indestructible <note>original: "incorruptible"</note> and in all respects harmless <note>original: "innoxious"</note>. We are not without needs in all things; the cause of this is our birth into the physical world <term>generation: the process of being born into a physical, mortal state</term> and our becoming needy through the constant physical waste <note>original: "corporeal efflux," referring to the body's natural loss of substance</term> which we endure. This state of want forces us to seek safety and comfort from external things, which are necessary for the existence of our mortal part. 

Therefore, the person who depends on a greater number of external things is more tightly bound <note>original: "agglutinated"</note> to poverty. The more his needs increase, the more he is distanced from the divine and becomes a companion of lack <note>original: "penury"</note>. For whoever is like God possesses true wealth through that likeness. But a person who is [truly] rich and has no needs harms nothing. As long as someone harms another—even if he should possess the greatest wealth and all the acres of land on the earth—he is still poor and has "Need" as his close companion. 

On this account, he is also unjust, godless, and wicked, and he is enslaved to every kind of depravity. This corruption is produced when the soul falls into matter because it lacks true goodness. Everything, therefore, is worthless <note>original: "nugatory"</note> to a person as long as they wander from the principle of the universe; they are needy in all things as long as they do not direct their attention to Plenty <note>original: "Porus," the Greek personification of abundance and resourcefulness</note>. 

He likewise gives in to the mortal part of his nature while he remains ignorant of his real self. Injustice is powerful at persuading and corrupting those who belong to her empire because she works with Pleasure to win over her followers. However, just as the person who has experienced both a better and a worse way of life is a more accurate judge than one who has only experienced one of them, the same is true in choosing what is proper. The person who judges from a higher perspective is a safer judge of what is less excellent than the person who judges from a lower perspective. Hence, the person who lives according to the higher mind <term>intellect: the rational, divine part of the soul</term> will more accurately define what is worth choosing and what is not than the person who lives under the dominion of irrational—

<summary>Porphyry argues that true wealth comes from divine self-sufficiency, whereas physical needs and the pursuit of pleasure lead to poverty of the soul and injustice toward others. He asserts that a rational person who understands the divine is better equipped to judge what is truly valuable than one ruled by bodily desires.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's discussion on the nature of justice and the soul. <page-type>text</page-type>

rationality. For the former has passed through the irrational life, having been associated with it from the beginning; but the latter, having had no experience of an intellectual life, persuades those who resemble him and acts with futility <note>original: "nugacity"</note>, like a child among children. 

If, however, our opponents say, "If all people were persuaded by these arguments, what would become of us?" Isn't it obvious that we would be happy? Injustice would be wiped out among humans, and justice would dwell <note>original: "be conversant"</note> with us in the same way it exists in the heavens. But right now, this question is exactly like people wondering what the lives of the Danaids <term>Danaids: in Greek mythology, the daughters of Danaus who were punished in the underworld by having to eternally fill a jar with holes using leaky sieves</term> would be like if they were freed from the task of drawing water in a sieve and trying to fill a vessel full of holes. For they are uncertain what would happen if we stopped trying to satisfy our passions and desires—satisfaction which always drains away because of a lack of true goodness. Seeking true good fills the damaged cracks of the soul more than even the greatest of external necessities. 

Do you ask, then, "What should we do?" We should imitate those who lived in the Golden Age <term>Golden Age: a mythological period of primordial peace and harmony when humans were said to live in a state of innocence and did not kill animals for food</term>; we should imitate those of that time who were truly free. For Modesty, Nemesis <note>Nemesis: here referring to the sense of "righteous indignation" or the divine moral order</note>, and Justice lived among them because they were satisfied with the fruits of the earth.

> ->The fertile earth spontaneously yields for them<-
> ->her fruits in great abundance. <note>original: Hesiod, Works and Days, v. 117</note><-

Those who are freed from slavery obtain for themselves what they previously had to provide for their masters. In the same way, once you are freed from the servitude of the body and from your slavish focus on the passions the body creates, you should change your habits. Previously, you fed those passions in many different ways with external things; now, nourish yourself in every way with internal goodness. Take up what is rightfully yours and stop violently taking things that are foreign to your true nature and your real good.

<margin>Hesiod. *Works and Days*. v. 117.</margin>

<summary>Porphyry argues that human desires are like a leaky vessel that can never be filled, and that true happiness comes from imitating the non-violent lifestyle of the Golden Age. He encourages the reader to abandon the "slavery" of bodily passions in favor of internal spiritual goodness.</summary>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book 4 of Porphyry’s treatise. The previous page concluded the arguments of Book 3, ending with a discussion on the "Golden Age" and how the soul’s desires lead to injustice. Here, Porphyry transitions into a historical defense of vegetarianism, promising to refute the claim that no wise nations have ever practiced abstinence from meat.</meta>

### ON

# ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.

## BOOK THE FOURTH.

---

1. In the preceding books, Castricius, we have nearly answered all the arguments used to defend the eating of flesh for the sake of a lack of self-restraint <note>original: "incontinence"</note> and excess. Such arguments offer shameless excuses for meat-eating by claiming our nature is more needy than it actually is. Two specific inquiries, however, still remain. In one of these, the promise of some "advantage" or benefit especially deceives those who have been corrupted by pleasure. Furthermore, we shall disprove <note>original: "confute"</note> the claim made by our opponents that no wise man, nor any nation, has ever rejected animal food. This assertion leads listeners into great injustice because they are ignorant of true history. We will also endeavor to provide solutions to the question of "advantage" and reply to other inquiries.

2. We shall begin with the history of the abstinence practiced by certain nations. In this account, what is recorded about the Greeks will first claim our attention, as they are the most closely related to us and the most appropriate witnesses we can cite. Among those who have concisely and accurately collected an account of Greek history is the Peripatetic <term>Peripatetic: a member of the philosophical school founded by Aristotle</term> philosopher Dicæarchus <note>Dicæarchus of Messana was a Greek philosopher, cartographer, and historian</note>. In describing the original <note>original: "pristine"</note> life of the Greeks, he says that the...

---

<note>original: "a. There were many celebrated men of this name among the ancients, concerning which see [original: "vid."] Fabricius, *Bibliotheca Graeca* [Greek Library], Book III, chapter 11."</note>

<summary>Porphyry begins Book 4 by stating his intent to disprove the idea that no wise nations or individuals have historically abstained from meat, starting his evidence with the ancient Greeks as described by the historian Dicæarchus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historian and philosopher Dicæarchus regarding the "Golden Age" of humanity. It expands on the idea that the first humans were spiritually superior and naturally avoided killing animals.</meta>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

ancients, being born with a kinship to the gods, were naturally most excellent and led the best life. When compared to us of the present day—who are composed of a corrupted and most base matter—they were thought to be a golden race, and they killed no animals whatever. The truth of this, he also says, is confirmed by the poets, who call these ancients the "golden race" and assert that every good thing was available to them.

->The fertile earth spontaneously produced for them<-
->A plentiful and unenvied store of fruits;<-
->In blissful peace then, unknown to conflict,<-
->The worthy passed their lives with the worthy.<gloss>b</gloss><-

In explaining these assertions, Dicæarchus says that a life of this kind existed under the reign of Saturn <note>Saturn: In Roman mythology, the god of the Golden Age, identified with the Greek Cronus</note>—if it is proper to consider it as a thing that once actually existed and was not celebrated in myth in vain. He suggests that if we set aside the extremely mythical elements, we may interpret this as a natural historical account. All things, therefore, are very properly said to have been then spontaneously produced; for

---

<gloss>b</gloss> These lines are from Hesiod, *Works and Days*, line 116 <note>original: "Hesiod. Oper. 116."</note>. The different ages of mankind, however, which are celebrated by Hesiod in his *Works and Days*, signify the different lifestyles which individuals of the human species pass through. As Proclus <note>Proclus: A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on Greek mythology and philosophy</note> beautifully observes in his commentary on Hesiod, they may be understood as a triad: the *golden*, the *silver*, and the *brazen* <note>original: "brazen" (bronze)</note> ages. By the *golden* age, an intellectual life is implied. Such a life is pure, undisturbed <note>original: "impassive"</note>, and free from sorrow; gold is an image of this purity and lack of suffering because it is never subject to rust or decay. Such a life is also very properly said to be under Saturn, because Saturn is an *intellectual* god, or a god characterized by pure intellect. By the *silver* age, a rustic and natural life is implied, in which the attention of the rational soul is entirely directed to the care of the body, but without descending into extreme wickedness. And by the *brazen* age, a dreadful, tyrannical, and cruel life is implied, which is entirely driven by emotion and proceeds to the very extreme of vice. The order of these metals also harmonizes, as Proclus observes, with the order of these lifestyles. “For,” he says, “gold is *solar-form* <note>original: "solar-form" (resembling the sun)</note>, because the sun is solely immaterial light. But silver is *lunar-form* <note>original: "lunar-form" (resembling the moon)</note>, because the moon partakes of shadow, just as silver partakes of tarnish. And *brass* <note>bronze</note> is *earthly*, in that it does not have a nature similar to a luminous body and is full of much corruption.”

<summary>This page discusses the historian Dicæarchus's account of the Golden Age, a time when humans were spiritually pure and abstained from killing animals. It includes a detailed philosophical interpretation by Proclus of Hesiod's "Ages of Man," linking different metals to levels of human intellect and morality.</summary>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the account by the philosopher and historian Dicæarchus regarding the lifestyle of the earliest humans, focusing on how their simple diet led to health and peace before the introduction of animal husbandry and war.</meta>

### ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK IV

People did not obtain anything through hard labor because they were unfamiliar with the art of agriculture and, in short, had no knowledge of any other craft. This very circumstance was also why they lived a life of leisure, free from toil and worry. If we agree with the judgment of the most skillful and refined physicians, it was also why they were free from disease. *For no medical rule contributes more to health than the one advising us not to produce an abundance of bodily waste <note>original: "excrement." In ancient medical theory, disease was often blamed on "superfluities" or waste products caused by overeating or poor digestion.</note>, a rule the original Greeks followed by keeping their bodies pure.* 

They did not consume food that was heavier than their physical nature could handle—choosing instead what their bodies could easily process—nor did they eat more than was moderate, despite how easily it was found. In fact, they usually ate less than enough because food was scarce. Furthermore, there were no wars or internal conflicts among them. No reward worth fighting for existed to drive anyone into such disputes. Consequently, the core of that life was leisure and rest from necessary chores, along with health, peace, and friendship. 

But for those in later times—who suffered many evils by desiring things far beyond simple moderation—this original lifestyle became highly desirable, as one might expect. The simple and improvised diet of these first humans is revealed by the proverb used in later times, *enough of the oak* <note>original: "enough of the oak" (Greek: ἅλις δρυός). A common ancient Greek proverb used when someone moves on to a better or different way of life, referring to the time when humans supposedly lived on acorns from oak trees.</note>. This saying was likely introduced by the person who first changed the ancient way of living. 

A pastoral life <term>pastoral life: A lifestyle centered on the herding and raising of livestock (sheep, cattle, etc.) rather than gathering wild plants.</term> followed this period. In this era, people began to acquire excess possessions and started dealing with animals. Realizing that some animals were harmless while others were harmful and wild, they tamed the former and attacked the latter. At the same time, war was introduced alongside this new way of life. These things, says Dicæarchus, are not just our own assertions, but...

<summary>Dicæarchus explains that the earliest humans were healthy and peaceful because their simple, plant-based diet prevented bodily disease and social conflict. He notes that the transition to herding animals (a pastoral life) brought about the accumulation of wealth and the beginning of warfare.</summary>

<keywords>Dicæarchus, Golden Age, health, diet, enough of the oak, origins of war, pastoralism, ancient Greece</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s citation of the historian Dicæarchus, transitioning from the description of the earliest "Golden Age" humans to the rise of agriculture, private property, and the Spartan laws of Lycurgus.</meta>

# ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK IV

This has been discussed by those who have recorded many specific historical details. As possessions grew large enough to merit attention, some people ambitiously tried to acquire them for their own use—encouraging others to do the same—while others focused on protecting what they had already gathered. As time went on, and people focused on what seemed useful, they eventually adopted a third way of life: agriculture. 

This is the account given by Dicæarchus <note>original: "Dicæarchus" (c. 350–285 BC), a Greek philosopher and historian who argued that human civilization had declined from a primitive state of peace and vegetarianism.</note> in his description of the customs of the ancient Greeks and the blessed life they once lived. Their abstinence from animal food contributed to this lifestyle as much as anything else. During that period, there was no war because injustice had been completely removed. However, war was later introduced among humans alongside injustice toward animals, fueled by the desire to outdo one another in the accumulation of wealth. Because of this, it is astonishing how bold those people are who claim that refusing to eat animals is the "mother of injustice." Both history and experience prove the opposite: that war and injustice were introduced at the same time as the slaughter of animals.

3. This connection was later recognized by the Spartan <note>original: "Lacedemonian."</note> lawgiver Lycurgus. Although the eating of animals was common in his time, he structured his society <note>original: "polity."</note> so that meat was required as little as possible. The property assigned to each individual did not consist of herds of oxen, flocks of sheep, or an abundance of goats, horses, and money. Instead, wealth was based on the possession of land that could produce seventy *medimni*<sup>c</sup> of barley for a man and twelve for a woman, along with a proportional amount of "liquid fruits" <note>This likely refers to agricultural products like olive oil and wine.</note>. He believed this amount of nutrition was enough to maintain a healthy physical constitution and good health, and that nothing else was needed to achieve them. For this reason, it is said that upon returning to his country,

---

<sup>c</sup> The *medimnus* <note>original: "medimnus." A Greek unit of dry measure.</note> was a measure containing six bushels.

<summary>Porphyry argues that the shift from a simple gathering lifestyle to agriculture and the ownership of livestock led to the birth of greed, injustice, and warfare. He cites the Spartan lawgiver Lycurgus as an example of a leader who prioritized land and grain over livestock to maintain a healthy, peaceful society.</summary>

<keywords>Dicæarchus, Lycurgus, Sparta, agriculture, animal food, origins of war, medimni, vegetarianism, history of injustice</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Spartan reforms under Lycurgus, specifically focusing on the redistribution of land and the replacement of gold and silver currency with iron to eliminate greed and luxury.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>

After he had been away from the city for some time, he noticed while passing through the fields that the grain <note>original: "corn," which in this period referred to cereal crops like wheat or barley, not maize.</note> had just been harvested. Seeing that the threshing-floors and the piles of grain were equal in size and uniform, he laughed. He remarked to those present that all of Laconia <note>The region of Greece where Sparta was located.</note> seemed like an estate belonging to many brothers who had just divided the land equally among themselves. 

He added that since he had driven luxury out of Sparta, it was also necessary to abolish the use of gold and silver money. In its place, he introduced iron alone as a substitute. This iron money was extremely bulky and heavy, yet of very little value. To store an amount worth only ten minae <note>original: "minæ." A Greek unit of currency; ten minae was a significant sum, but Lycurgus made the iron version so heavy it was impractical for bribery or hoarding.</note>, one would need a large room, and to move it, a cart pulled by two oxen. 

Once this was ordained, many types of injustice were wiped out from Sparta <note>original: "Lacedæmon."</note>. After all, who would try to steal, or allow themselves to be corrupted by bribes, or cheat or rob another person? It was impossible to hide what had been taken, impossible to own it in a way that others would envy, and impossible to gain any profit from melting it down. 

Along with money, unnecessary crafts were also banished, as Spartan goods were no longer produced for profit. Iron money could not be used for trade with other Greeks; they did not value it and, in fact, ridiculed it. Consequently, it was illegal to buy foreign goods—which Lycurgus viewed as having no real worth—and merchant ships no longer sailed into Spartan ports. No paid public speakers <note>original: "verbal sophist."</note>, pointless fortune-tellers, pimps <note>original: "bawd."</note>, or jewelers working in gold and silver were allowed to enter Laconia, because money there was useless. 

In this way, luxury—deprived of its temptations and its fuel—slowly withered away on its own. Similarly, those who possessed great wealth gained no more advantage than those who did not. There was no way to spend or display abundance; it was so hindered by obstacles that wealth was forced to remain unused and idle. Therefore, the household furniture that was necessary for daily life—such as beds, chairs, and tables—was crafted with the highest quality; and the...

<summary>This page describes how Lycurgus eliminated greed and luxury in Sparta by redistributing land equally and replacing precious metal currency with heavy, low-value iron money.</summary>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Spartan social reforms under Lycurgus, moving from the abolition of currency to the design of practical objects and the establishment of communal meals (the syssitia).</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>

The Spartan cup, which was called a *cothon* <term>A drinking vessel with a curved rim designed to trap sediment.</term>, was, as Critias says, especially famous during military campaigns. In these expeditions, the water they had to drink was often unpleasant to look at, but its appearance was hidden by the color of the cup. Furthermore, the sediment <note>original: "the turbid part"</note> in the water would get caught against the protruding rim of the cup so that the water reached the mouth in a cleaner state. We are informed by Plutarch, however, that the lawgiver Lycurgus was the reason for these designs. Because the craftsmen were freed from producing useless, decorative works, they instead demonstrated the beauty of their art in things of a practical and necessary nature.

4. To oppose luxury to an even greater degree and to remove the greedy desire for wealth, Lycurgus introduced a third and most excellent political institution: the practice of citizens eating and drinking together in public. In this way, they would all share the same required food in common. They would no longer be fed at home, reclining on extravagant couches and sitting at elegant tables, served by craftsmen and chefs. He believed that eating in private caused men to be fattened in the dark like greedy animals, ruining both their bodies and their morals by falling into every kind of luxury and overindulgence <term>original: "repletion"</term>. Such a lifestyle would require excessive sleep, hot baths, constant rest, and the kind of medical attention usually given to the sick. 

This reform was indeed a great achievement; but an even greater result, as Theophrastus says, was that Lycurgus caused wealth to be ignored and to have no value, because the citizens ate at common tables and the food was so simple. There was no use for, nor any enjoyment of, riches. In short, there was nothing to please the eye and no way to show off one's wealth in the whole arrangement, because both the poor and the rich sat at the same table. 

Therefore, it was commonly said that in Sparta alone, Plutus <note>The Greek god of wealth, often depicted as blind so that he distributes riches without regard to merit.</note> was seen to be truly blind, lying there like a lifeless and unmoving picture. It was not possible for the citizens to feast at home first and then go to the common tables with their appetites already satisfied <note>original: "satiated"</note>. For the rest of the citizens would care-

<meta>This page continues the description of Spartan communal meals (the syssitia) established by Lycurgus, detailing the mandatory contributions of members and the educational role these meals played for Spartan children.</meta>

...closely watched anyone who did not eat and drink with them and insulted him as an undisciplined person, and as one who behaved weakly <note>original: "effeminately"</note> regarding the common food. For this reason, these common tables were called *phiditia*; either because they were the cause of friendship and goodwill, as if they were *philitia* <note>original: *philitia*, assuming the Greek letter 'd' (delta) was used instead of 'l' (lambda)</note>, or because they accustomed men toward frugality and a simple diet <note>original: "προς ευτελειαν και φειδω" (pros euteleian kai pheidō)</note>. The number of those who gathered at the common table was fifteen, more or less. Each person brought every month, for the purpose of supplying the table: a *medimnus* <term>A Greek dry measure, roughly equivalent to 1.5 bushels or 52 liters.</term> of flour, eight *choes* <note>original: "choas"</note> <term>A liquid measure; eight *choes* would be approximately 6 gallons.</term> of wine, five pounds of cheese, two and a half pounds of figs, and, besides all these, a very small amount of money.

5. Consequently, the children of those who ate so sparingly and temperately attended these common tables as if they were schools of self-restraint <note>original: "temperance"</note>. There, they also heard political discussions and were spectators of noble <note>original: "liberal"</note> sports. Here, likewise, they learned to joke sharply without being crude <note>original: "scurrility"</note>, and to receive the biting jokes of others without becoming angry. For this was considered a classic Spartan <note>original: "Laconic"</note> trait: to be able to endure sharp-witted jokes. However, if someone could not endure them, he was permitted to ask the speaker to stop, and the teaser would immediately fall silent.

Such, therefore, was the frugality of the Spartans <note>original: "Lacedæmonians"</note> regarding their diet, even though it was legally established for the sake of the general public. Because of this, those who lived under this system of government <note>original: "polity"</note> are said to have been braver and more self-controlled, and paid more attention to doing what is right <note>original: "rectitude"</note>, than those who came from other communities which are corrupted in both their souls and bodies. It is also evident that complete abstinence is suited to such a government as this, while luxurious food belongs to corrupt communities. If, likewise, we—

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for abstinence from animal food by looking at the practices of various nations beyond Sparta, specifically focusing on the religious roles of priests and their influence on the welfare of the state.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

If we direct our attention to other nations that valued fairness, gentleness, and devotion <note>original: "piety"</note> to the Gods, it becomes clear that they established abstinence for the safety and benefit of either all or at least some of the citizens. These individuals, by offering sacrifices and worshipping the Gods on behalf of the city, could make amends <note>original: "expiate"</note> for the sins of the people. In the sacred mysteries, the altar-boy performs his duties precisely as commanded to make the Gods favorable <note>original: "propitious"</note> to all those who have been initiated. Similarly, regarding *muesis* <term>A stage of initiation into the ancient mysteries, from the Greek word for "closing the eyes" to the outside world.</term> [on behalf of all those being initiated <note>original Greek: "αντι παντων των μυουμενων" (anti pantōn tōn myoumenōn)</note>], priests in nations and cities achieve a similar effect by sacrificing for all the people, and through their devotion, they encourage the Gods to look after the welfare of their citizens.

Regarding priests, eating any animals at all is forbidden to some, while others are only prohibited from eating specific animals, whether you look at the customs of the Greeks or of foreign nations <note>original: "barbarians"</note>, which vary from place to place. If all these customs are viewed collectively as a single rule, it is found that they abstain from all animals. Therefore, if those who oversee the safety of cities and are entrusted with religious devotion abstain from animal meat, how can anyone dare to claim that such abstinence is harmful to society?

6. Chæremon the Stoic <note>A 1st-century CE philosopher and historian of Egypt.</note>, in his account of the Egyptian priests—whom he says the Egyptians considered to be philosophers—tells us that they chose temples as the places where they could pursue philosophy.

---

The phrase "of luxury" <note>original Greek: "της τρυφης" (tēs tryphēs)</note> is missing; so instead of "of eating," we should read "of the luxury of eating." My theory is confirmed by the translation by Felicianus <note>Giovanni Bernardo Feliciano, a 16th-century translator.</note>, which says: "It is clear that for this man abstinence was characteristic, but for the others, luxury of living was the norm <note>original Latin: "Huic autem abstinentiam, cæteris luxuriam victus fuisse peculiarem perspicuum est."</note>."

<sup>f</sup> Those being initiated "closed their eyes" (which is what *muesis* <note>original: "μύησις"</note> means). According to Hermias in his commentary on the *Phaedrus* <note>original: "Hermias in Phædrum"</note>, they no longer received these divine mysteries through the senses, but through the pure soul itself. See my *Dissertation on the Eleusinian and Bacchic Mysteries*.

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's description of the Egyptian priests, as recorded by Chæremon the Stoic. It details their seclusion, their religious devotion, and the disciplined lifestyle that resulted from their constant focus on the divine.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>

[philoso]phize. Living among the statues of the Gods is something that aligns with the entire desire of the soul as it strives toward the contemplation of their divinity. Because of the divine veneration they received by living in temples, they lived in safety; everyone honored these philosophers as if they were some kind of sacred animals. They also lived a solitary life, only interacting with others during solemn sacrifices and festivals. At all other times, the priests were almost inaccessible to anyone who wanted to speak with them. For it was required that anyone approaching them must first be purified and abstain from many things; this was effectively a common sacred law regarding the Egyptian priests. 

But these [philosophic priests], having abandoned all other occupations and human labors <insert>g</insert>, dedicated their entire lives to the contemplation and worship of divine beings <note>original: "natures"</note> and to divine inspiration. Through divine inspiration, they secured honor, safety, and piety for themselves. Through contemplation, they attained knowledge <note>original: "science"</note>; and through both, they practiced a certain secret <note>original: "occult"</note> discipline of character <note>original: "manners"</note> that was worthy of ancient times <insert>h</insert>. Being constantly engaged with divine knowledge and inspiration removes a person from all greed <note>original: "avarice"</note>, suppresses the passions, and encourages a life of the mind <note>original: "intellectual life"</note>. They were careful to be frugal in their diet and clothing, and they also practiced self-control and endurance; in all things, they were attentive to justice and fairness <note>original: "equity"</note>. They were also held in great respect <note>original: "venerable"</note> because they rarely socialized with other people. During the periods of so-called "purifications," they hardly even interacted with their closest relatives or those of their own rank; nor were they seen by anyone unless it was necessary

<meta>This page continues the description of the Egyptian priests' disciplined lifestyle, focusing on their public conduct, physical self-control, and specific dietary restrictions intended to maintain spiritual purity.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

for the necessary purposes of purification. For the sanctuary was inaccessible to those who were not purified, and they dwelt in holy places for the purpose of performing sacred rituals <note>original: "divine works"</note>; but at all other times they associated more freely with those who lived as they did. They did not, however, associate with anyone who was not a religious person. They were always seen near the Gods, or near the statues of the Gods; they were seen either carrying these statues, preceding them in a sacred procession, or arranging them in an orderly manner with modesty and seriousness. Each of these actions was not the result of pride, but an indication of some physical principle <note>original: "physical reason"; referring to the symbolic meaning behind their movements</note>. Their dignified seriousness was also apparent from their behavior. For their walking was orderly, and their appearance was calm; and they were so intent on preserving this serious facial expression that they did not even blink when they did not wish to. They seldom laughed, and when they did, their laughter went no further than a smile. They always kept their hands tucked within their garments. Each priest also wore a symbol indicating the specific rank <note>original: "order"</note> he was assigned in sacred matters, for there were many ranks of priests. 

Their diet was also sparse and simple. Regarding wine, some of them did not drink it at all, while others drank very little of it because it is harmful to the nerves, causes heaviness in the head, hinders creativity <note>original: "invention"</note>, and acts as an incentive for sexual <note>original: "venereal"</note> desires. In many other things, they also conducted themselves with caution. They never used bread during periods of purification; and during the times when they were not actively purifying themselves, they were accustomed to eating bread with hyssop <term>hyssop: a bushy plant used in ancient times for cleaning sacred spaces and for medicinal/purification purposes</term> cut into small pieces. For it is said that hyssop greatly purifies the nutritional quality <note>original: "power"</note> of bread. For the most part, they abstained from oil—the majority of them entirely. If they ever used it with vegetables <note>original: "pot-herbs"</note>, they took very little of it, only as much as was enough to soften the taste of the herbs.

<summary>This page details the physical discipline and strict dietary laws of Egyptian priests, emphasizing their composed public demeanor and their avoidance of wine and oil to maintain mental and spiritual clarity.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian priests, purification, hyssop, temperance, religious rituals, asceticism, diet</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Egyptian priests' lifestyle, moving from their general conduct and physical discipline to their specific dietary restrictions and the complex rules governing ritual purity.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD—BOOK IV.</header>

7. It was forbidden for them to consume food or drink produced outside of Egypt, a rule that significantly helped exclude luxury from the lives of these priests. They also abstained from all fish caught in Egypt, and from four-legged animals that had solid hoofs <note>such as horses or donkeys</note> or multi-toed paws, as well as from animals without horns. Likewise, they avoided all meat-eating birds. Many of them, however, entirely abstained from all animal products; during periods of ritual purification, this abstinence was practiced by all of them, for at those times they did not even eat an egg.

Moreover, they rejected other things without being criticized for doing so. For instance, among cattle, they rejected the females and also any males that were twins, speckled, of a different color, or had an irregular physical form. They also rejected animals that had already been tamed and "consecrated to labor" <note>referring to oxen used for plowing and farm work</note>, those that resembled animals held in high honor, those that were considered images of something divine, those that had only one eye, or those that bore a resemblance to the human form. 

There are also countless other rules pertaining to the art of those called <term>moschosphragistai</term> <note>original: μοσχοσφραγισταί; these were "sealers of calves," officials who inspected and marked animals with a seal to certify them as ritually pure for sacrifice</note>, about which entire books have been written. But these observations are even more detailed regarding birds; for example, they believed a turtle-dove <note>original: "turtle"</note> should not be eaten. This is because it is said that a hawk frequently releases this bird after catching it, sparing its life as a reward for having had sexual "connection" with it. Therefore, to ensure they did not accidentally consume a turtle-dove of this kind, the Egyptian priests avoided the entire species. 

These were certain common religious ceremonies; however, there were other ceremonies that varied according to the specific class of priests and were tailored to the various divinities they served. Nevertheless, celibacy <note>original: "chastity"</note> and purification rituals were required of all priests. When the time arrived for them to perform specific sacred rites, they spent several days in preparatory ceremonies...

<summary>This page details the strict dietary prohibitions of Egyptian priests, including bans on foreign food, specific animals based on their physical traits, and certain birds, while also introducing the specialized officials who certified ritual purity.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian priests, dietary laws, ritual purity, animal sacrifice, moschosphragistai, bird symbolism, abstinence</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Egyptian priests' lifestyle, specifically focusing on their periods of purification, physical discipline, and daily schedule of worship and study.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

Some practiced these rites for forty-two days, while others did so for a longer or shorter period, but never for less than seven days. During this time, they abstained from eating any animals, as well as from all leafy vegetables and legumes <note>original: "pot-herbs and leguminous substances"; this refers to plants like beans, peas, and lentils</note>. Above all, they abstained from sexual intercourse with women; they also never at any time had sexual relations with men. They also washed themselves with cold water three times every day: namely, when they rose from bed, before dinner, and when they went to sleep. If they happened to be polluted in their sleep by a nocturnal emission, they immediately purified their bodies in a bath. They also used cold bathing at other times, though not as frequently as on the occasions mentioned above. Their bed was woven from the branches of the palm tree, which they call *bais*; and their pillow <note>original: "bolster"</note> was a smooth, semi-cylindrical piece of wood <note>This refers to the traditional Egyptian wooden headrest, designed to keep the head cool and preserve elaborate hairstyles or wigs during sleep</note>. Throughout their entire lives, they exercised themselves in enduring hunger and thirst and were accustomed to eating only small amounts of food.

8. This is also a testimony to their self-restraint <note>original: "continence"</note>: although they did not exercise through walking or riding, they lived free from disease and were strong enough to endure moderate labor. Consequently, they carried many heavy burdens during the performance of sacred rituals and accomplished many service-related tasks that required more than common strength. They divided the night into the observation of the celestial bodies <note>astronomy and astrology</note>, and sometimes devoted part of it to rituals of purification. They distributed the day into the worship of the Gods, celebrating them with hymns three or four times: in the morning, at noon when the sun is at its highest point, in the evening, and when the sun is setting in the west. The rest of their time they devoted to mathematical and geometrical studies <note>original: "arithmetical and geometrical speculations"</note>, always working to achieve something new or make a discovery—in short, they were continually exercising their intellectual skills. On winter nights, they were also occupied with these same pursuits, being vigilantly engaged in literary...

<summary>This page details the ascetic discipline of Egyptian priests, including their strict fasting, celibacy, ritual washing, and their daily schedule divided between religious worship and scientific study.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian priests, purification rites, celibacy, asceticism, astronomy, mathematics, ritual washing, bais, sacred rituals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the Egyptian priests' disciplined lifestyle, moving from their physical austerities to their social isolation from foreign influences and their theological beliefs regarding the presence of the soul in all living creatures.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>

pursuits, as they paid no attention to the acquisition of external goods and were freed from the slavery of that bad master: excessive expense. Hence, their unwearied and constant labor testifies to their endurance, but their self-control <note>original: "continence"</note> is shown by their freedom from the desire for external possessions. To sail away from Egypt <note>that is, to leave the country</note> was also considered one of the most unholy things by them, because they were careful to avoid foreign luxury and habits; for this appeared to them to be legal only for those who were forced to do so by royal necessity. Indeed, they were very anxious to continue observing the traditions <note>original: "institutes"</note> of their country, and those who were found to have violated them, even in a small degree, were expelled from the college of the priests. 

The true method of practicing philosophy was also preserved by the prophets, the **sacred robe-keepers** <note>original: *hierostolistæ*<sup>i</sup></note>, the sacred scribes, and the **astrologers** <note>original: *horologi*</note>, or those who calculate birth charts. But the rest of the priests—including the **shrine-bearers** <note>original: *pastophori*<sup>k</sup></note>, temple curators, and ministers of the gods—were similarly dedicated to purity, yet not with the same accuracy or great self-control as the priests we have been discussing. Such are the details narrated about the Egyptians by a man who loved the truth, was an accurate writer, and who practiced philosophy vigorously and soundly among the Stoics <note>This likely refers to Chaeremon of Alexandria, a Stoic philosopher and Egyptian priest of the 1st century AD</note>.

9. The Egyptian priests, through the progress they made by this spiritual exercise and their imitation of the divine, knew that divinity does not exist in humans alone. They understood that the soul is not enshrined only in humans on earth, but that it passes through almost all animals. For this reason, in fashioning the images of the gods, they used every kind of animal; for this purpose, they mixed the human form with the forms of wild beasts, and again combined the bodies of birds with the body of a

<sup>i</sup> That is, those to whose care the sacred robes <note>original: "vestments"</note> were committed.
<sup>k</sup> These were so named because they carried the small shrines <note>original: "receptacles"</note> in which the images of the gods were kept.

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Egyptian theology and the symbolic meaning behind their zoomorphic (animal-headed) deities, explaining how these forms represent the interconnectedness of divine, human, and animal life.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

man. For a certain deity was represented by them in human form up to the neck, but the face was that of a bird, a lion, or some other animal. Likewise, another divine image had a human head, but the other parts belonged to different animals, some positioned lower and others higher. Through this, they demonstrated that humans and animals <note>original: "brutes and men"</note>, by the decree of the Gods, share a connection with each other, and that tame and wild animals are raised alongside us, not without the agreement of a specific divine will. 

For this reason, a lion is worshipped as a God. A certain district <term>Nomos: a provincial administrative division of ancient Egypt</term> of Egypt is named Leontopolis <note>literally "City of the Lion"</note>, another is called Busiris <note>named after the ox</note>, and another Lycopolis <note>literally "City of the Wolf"</note>. They honored the power of God—which extends to all things—through the animals that live among us and which each of the Gods bestows. 

They also respected water and fire most among the elements, seeing them as the primary causes of our survival. These elements are displayed in their temples; even now, when opening the sanctuary of Serapis <note>a Graeco-Egyptian deity</note>, the ritual is performed using fire and water. The hymn-singer pours a liquid offering <term>libation: a ritual pouring of a liquid as an offering to a deity</term> of water and displays fire while standing on the temple's threshold, invoking the God in the Egyptian language. 

Honoring these elements, they especially respect those things that share in them significantly, as they possess more of what is sacred. Beyond these, they honor all animals. In the village of Anubis, they even worship a man; in that place, they perform sacrifices to him and burn offerings in his honor on an altar, though shortly afterward he only eats the food provided for him as a human. Therefore, just as it is necessary to abstain from eating humans, it is also necessary to abstain from other animals. 

Furthermore, because of their superior wisdom and connection with the divine, the Egyptian priests discovered which animals are more acceptable to the Gods when dedicated to...

<summary>This page explains that Egyptian animal worship and zoomorphic deities symbolize the divine connection between all living things. It argues that because animals share in this divine order and the sacred elements of fire and water, they should be excluded from the human diet just as humans are.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of why the ancient Egyptian priests revered specific animals, focusing on the symbolic and spiritual properties they attributed to creatures like the hawk and the beetle.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>

<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>

than man. Thus they found that a hawk is dear to the sun, since its entire nature consists of blood and spirit. It also sympathizes with humans <note>original: "commiserates man"</note> and mourns over a dead body, scattering earth on the eyes, in which these priests believe a solar light resides. They likewise discovered that a hawk lives for many years and that, after it leaves this life, it possesses a power of divination. They believed it becomes highly rational and capable of foreseeing the future <note>original: "prescient"</note> once it is freed from the body, and that it brings perfection to statues and influences <note>original: "moves"</note> temples. 

A beetle will be detested by someone who is ignorant and unskilled in divine matters, but the Egyptians honor it as a living <note>original: "animated"</note> image of the sun. For they believed every beetle is male; it releases its reproductive seed in a muddy place, and having shaped it into a sphere, it turns the ball of seed around in a way similar to the movement of the sun in the heavens. It also follows a cycle of twenty-eight days, which is a lunar period. In a similar manner, the Egyptians reason philosophically about the ram, the crocodile, the vulture, and the ibis—and, in short, about every animal. Because of their wisdom and superior knowledge of divine matters, they eventually came to revere all animals¹. A person without learning, however, does not even suspect that these priests—refusing to be swept along with the crowd of common people who know nothing, and refusing to walk the path of ignorance—were led to honor things that the common public thinks are worthless. They moved beyond the illiterate masses and the lack of knowledge that affects everyone at first.

10. This also, no less than the points mentioned above, led them to believe that animals should be honored [as images of the Gods]: they discovered that the soul of every animal, when freed from the body, was rational. They found it could foresee the future, possessed the power of an oracle, and was capable of doing everything that a human soul can accomplish when separated from the body. Consequently, they very properly

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Egyptian religious practices, moving from the symbolic worship of animals to the specific funeral rites and moral justifications of the Egyptian nobility.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>

They honored these animals and abstained from harming them as much as possible. However, because the reasons why the Egyptians revered the gods through animals would require an extensive <note>original: "copious"</note> discussion that would exceed the limits of this current treatise, what has been explained regarding this specific detail is sufficient for our purpose.

Nevertheless, one thing should not be omitted: when the Egyptians buried those of noble birth, they privately removed the stomach <note>original: "belly"</note> and placed it in a chest. Along with the other rituals they performed for the body of the deceased, they lifted the chest toward the sun, calling upon it as a witness. At the same time, an oration for the dead person was delivered by one of those in charge of the funeral. The speech, which Euphantus<sup>m</sup> translated <note>original: "interpreted"</note> from the Egyptian language, was as follows:

> “O sovereign Sun, and all you Gods who give life to humanity, receive me and deliver me to the eternal Gods to live among them. For I have always piously worshipped those divinities which my parents pointed out to me for as long as I lived in this world, and I have always honored those who gave birth to me <note>original: "procreated my body"</note>. Regarding other people, I have never killed anyone, nor have I defrauded anyone of what they entrusted to me, nor have I committed any other atrocious deed. If, therefore, I have done anything wrong during my life by eating or drinking things that are forbidden <note>original: "unlawful"</note>, I did not commit this error myself, but through these,”

(The speaker here pointed to the chest in which the stomach was contained.) After saying this, he threw the chest into the river Nile, but buried the rest of the body as being pure. In this way, they believed an apology should be offered to the divine for what they had eaten and drunk, and for the arrogant conduct they were led into by the cravings of the stomach.

<sup>m</sup> Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a prominent German classical scholar.</note> is of the opinion that this *Euphantus* is the same person as the *Ecphantus* mentioned by Iamblichus (in the *Life of Pythagoras*) as one of the Pythagoreans. See Fabricius’s *Greek Bibliography*, book 2, chapter 13 <note>original: "Fabric. Bibl. Græc. lib. ii. c. 13."</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dietary laws and philosophical lifestyles among various nations, moving from the rituals of the Egyptians to the customs of the Jews and specifically the sect of the Essenes.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>

11. Among the peoples known to us, the Jews—before their legal institutions were first overthrown by Antiochus <note>Antiochus IV Epiphanes, the Seleucid king whose actions led to the Maccabean Revolt.</note>, and later by the Romans when the temple in Jerusalem was captured and became accessible to everyone (though previously it was inaccessible), and the city itself was destroyed—always abstained from many animals. They particularly abstained from swine, as they still do today. During that time, there were three kinds of philosophers among them. One group was led by the Pharisees, another by the Sadducees, and the third—which seems to have been the most respected <note>original: "venerable"</note>—was the Essenes <note>original: "Essæans"</note>.

The lifestyle of this third group was as follows, as Josephus frequently testifies in many of his writings. In the second book of his *Jewish War* <note>original: "Judaic History"</note> (which consists of seven books), in the eighteenth book of his *Antiquities* (which consists of twenty books), and in the second of the two books he wrote *Against the Greeks* <note>Usually known as *Against Apion*.</note>, he speaks of these Essenes. He says they are of Jewish descent and are more friendly toward one another than other people are. They avoid pleasures, considering them to be a vice <note>original: "vicious"</note>, but they believe that self-control <note>original: "continence"</note> and not yielding to the passions constitute virtue. 

They reject marriage, but they take in the children of others and educate them in their studies <note>original: "disciplines"</note> while they are still young, treating them as their own kin and training them in their own customs. They do this not to abolish marriage or the continuation of families, but to avoid the perceived lack of restraint <note>original: "lasciviousness"</note> in women. 

They also despise wealth, and their practice of sharing possessions in common is remarkable; no one among them is wealthier than the rest. It is a law among them that those wishing to join their sect must surrender their property to the community. Consequently, among them one sees neither the misery <note>original: "abjectness"</note> of poverty nor the arrogance <note>original: "insolence"</note> of wealth, but the posses- <note>The text continues on the next page with "possessions."</note>

<summary>Porphyry describes the three philosophical sects of the Jews, focusing on the Essenes' disciplined lifestyle, communal property, and rejection of marriage as recorded by the historian Josephus.</summary>
<keywords>Jews, Pharisees, Sadducees, Essenes, Josephus, Jerusalem, self-control, communal living, asceticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the Essene sect's communal lifestyle, specifically their shared property, hospitality, and daily religious rituals.</meta>

possessions of each being mingled with those of the rest, there was one property shared by all of them, as if they had been brothers. They likewise conceived oil to be a stain to the body, and if anyone, even unintentionally, was anointed, he would [immediately] wipe his body. For they considered it beautiful to be unrefined <gloss>n</gloss> <note>original: "squalid"</note> and to be always clothed in white garments. Furthermore, officials <note>original: "curators"</note> of the common property were elected by votes to manage the resources for the use of everyone. They do not live in only one city, but in each city many of them dwell together; those who come among them from other places, if they belong to their sect, share equally in their possessions as if they were their own. Likewise, those who first encounter these strangers behave toward them as if they were intimate acquaintances. For this reason, when they travel, they carry nothing with them for the sake of expenses. They neither change their garments nor their shoes until they are entirely torn or worn out by time. They neither buy nor sell anything, but each of them gives what he possesses to whoever is in need, and receives in return whatever will be useful to him. Nevertheless, each of them freely provides for others of their sect whatever they may lack without any payment <note>original: "remuneration"</note>.

12. Moreover, they are uniquely pious toward the Divine. Before the sun rises, they speak of nothing worldly <note>original: "profane"</note>, but they offer up certain prayers to the sun which they had received from their ancestors, as if pleading for it to rise. Afterwards, they are sent by their supervisors to practice the various crafts in which they are skilled. Having labored strenuously in these crafts until the fifth hour <note>Approximately 11:00 AM.</note>, they are afterwards gathered together in one place; there, being wrapped in linen coverings <note>original: "teguments"</note>, they wash their bodies with cold water. After this purification, they enter into their own—

See my translation of Iamblichus' Life of Pythagoras.</note>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the daily life and strict initiation rituals of the Essenes (referred to as Essæans), specifically focusing on their communal meals and the three-year probationary period for new members.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>

proper home, into which no person with different beliefs <note>original: "heterodox person"</note> is permitted to enter. Being pure themselves, they go into the dining room as if into a sacred temple <note>original: "fane"</note>. In this place, when all of them are seated in silence, the baker arranges the bread in order, and the cook distributes to each of them one vessel containing one kind of food. However, before they take this food, which they consider pure and sacred, a priest prays; it is against their law for anyone to taste the food before the prayer. After dinner, the priest prays again. In this way, they worship the Divine both when they begin and when they finish eating. 

Afterward, they take off these garments, treating them as sacred, and return to their work until the evening. When they return, they eat and drink in the same manner as before, with any strangers who happen to be present sitting with them. No shouting or noise ever disturbs the house in which they live; instead, they speak to each other in an orderly way. To those outside the house, the silence of those within appears as if it were some awe-inspiring <note>original: "terrific"</note> mystery. The cause of this quietness is their constant sobriety, and the fact that their food and drink are measured to be only what is sufficient for the needs of nature. 

Those who are very eager to join their sect are not immediately admitted. Instead, they must remain outside the community for a year, following the same diet. During this time, the Essenes <note>original: "Essæans"</note> give them a small shovel <note>original: "rake"; used for burying waste according to their laws of purity</note>, a belt <note>original: "girdle"</note>, and a white garment. If, during that time, they have given sufficient proof of their self-control <note>original: "continence"</note>, they proceed to a closer following of the sect’s rules and use purer water for ritual washing <note>original: "purposes of sanctity"</note>; however, they are still not yet permitted to live with the Essenes. After this display of endurance, their character is tested for two more years, and those who appear to deserve to join after this period are finally admitted into the society.

13. Before a new member is allowed to touch the communal food, however, he must first take a solemn <note>original: "terrible"</note> oath, in the first place,

<meta>This page continues the description of the oaths taken by the Essenes (Essæans) during their initiation and the severe consequences for those who are expelled from the community for breaking their laws.</meta>

that he will piously worship the Divine; in the next place, that he will maintain justice toward all people, and that he will neither intentionally, nor even when ordered, injure anyone; in the third place, that he will always hate the unjust, but vigorously assist the just; and in the fourth place, that he will act faithfully toward all men, but especially toward the rulers of the land, since no one becomes a ruler without the permission of God; in the fifth place, that if he should become a ruler, he will never employ his power for arrogant or wicked <note>original: "insolently iniquitous"</note> purposes, nor will he outshine those who are subject to him in his dress or any other more luxurious decoration; in the sixth place, that he will always love the truth and be an enemy to liars; in the seventh place, that he will keep his hands from theft and his soul pure from dishonest <note>original: "unholy"</note> gain <note>This was a very necessary oath for these Essenes to take; as the Jewish people in general, if we may believe Tacitus <note>A Roman historian (c. 56–120 AD) who wrote about the Jews in his *Histories*.</note> and other ancient historians, were described as a people excessively focused on profit.</note>; and, in the eighth place, that he will conceal nothing from those of his own sect, nor divulge anything pertaining to the sect to others, even if someone should threaten him with death to compel him. 

In addition to these things, they also swear that they will not pass on the teachings <note>original: "dogmas"</note> of the sect to anyone in any way other than how they received them; that they will likewise abstain from robbery <note>Since the Essenes appear to have been an exception to the rest of the Jewish people of that time, the reason they took this specific oath is obvious.</note>, and protect the books of their sect with the same care as they do the names of the angels. Such, therefore, are their oaths. 

But those among them who commit crimes and are expelled <note>original: "ejected"</note> meet a terrible fate. Because they are bound by their oaths and their customs, they are not permitted to receive food from others; instead, eating only herbs and having their bodies waste away from hunger, they die. Therefore, the Essenes <note>original: "Essæans"</note>, pitying many of these unfortunate men, receive them back into their society in their final moments, believing that they have suffered sufficiently for their offenses by having...
