--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Essenes' way of life, focusing on their strict practices regarding hygiene, their resilience under Roman torture, and their beliefs about the soul's immortality.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>

...been punished for these crimes until they were on the brink of the grave. They also give a small rake to those who intend to join their sect. This is so that when they sit down for the purpose of relieving themselves <note>original: "exonerating the belly"</note>, they may dig a trench a foot deep and completely cover themselves with their garment; they do this so that they do not act disrespectfully <note>original: "contumeliously"</note> toward the sun by polluting the rays of the God.

Indeed, their simplicity and frugality regarding their diet is so great that they do not require a bowel movement until the seventh day after eating. They spend that seventh day singing hymns to God and resting from their labor. From this disciplined lifestyle, they acquire such great endurance that even when they are tortured, burned, or suffer every kind of excruciating pain, they cannot be persuaded to either blaspheme their lawgiver <note>likely referring to Moses or the founder of the Essene sect</note> or to eat foods they are not accustomed to.

The truth of this was proven during their war with the Romans. During that time, they neither flattered their tormentors nor shed any tears. Instead, they smiled in the midst of their torments and mocked those who inflicted them. They cheerfully gave up their lives <note>original: "emitted their souls"</note>, knowing that they would possess them again. 

This opinion was firmly established among them: that while their bodies were corruptible and the matter of which they consisted was not stable, their souls were immortal and would endure forever. They believed that souls, proceeding from the most subtle ether <note>In ancient philosophy, ether was the "fifth element," a pure, celestial substance that filled the space of the universe beyond the terrestrial sphere.</note>, were drawn down by a natural flow and became entwined with bodies. However, they believed that when souls are no longer held back by the bonds of the flesh, they—as if liberated from a long slavery—will rejoice and ascend to the heavenly regions. 

Because of this way of life, and from being so practiced in truth and piety, there were many among them who had a foreknowledge of future events. This is reasonable to expect, as they were familiar from their youth with sacred books, various rituals of purification, and the declarations of the prophets. Such is the order (or sect) of the Essenes <note>original: "Essæans"</note> among the Jews.

<summary>This page describes the Essenes' ritual hygiene, their legendary physical endurance during the Jewish-Roman War, and their philosophical belief in the soul as an immortal entity trapped in a mortal body.</summary>
<keywords>Essenes, Jewish-Roman War, immortality of the soul, ether, asceticism, prophecy, ritual purity, Roman history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dietary laws and the historical origins of meat-eating, moving from the specific practices of the Essenes to the broader laws of the Jewish legislator and the history of the Syrians and Phoenicians.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM</header>

14. All of them, however, were forbidden to eat the flesh of pigs <note>original: "swine"</note>, or fish without scales, which the Greeks call *selachia* <note>original: "σελαχια"</note>, meaning cartilaginous fish <note>These include sharks and rays, which have skeletons made of cartilage rather than bone</note>; they were also forbidden from eating any animal that has solid hoofs. Likewise, they were forbidden not only from eating but also from killing animals that fled to their houses as refugees <note>original: "supplicants"</note>. Nor did the lawgiver <note>original: "legislator," referring to Moses</note> permit them to kill animals that were parents along with their young. Instead, he ordered them to spare—even in a hostile land—and not put to death the beasts that assist us in our labors. 

The lawgiver was not afraid that the species of animals which are not sacrificed would increase so much in number that they would cause a famine among men if they were spared from slaughter. He knew, first of all, that animals that produce many offspring <note>original: "multiparous"</note> live for only a short time. Secondly, he knew that many of them perish unless men take care of them. Furthermore, he knew that other animals would attack those that increased excessively. We see evidence of this in the fact that we abstain from many animals—such as lizards, worms, flies, serpents, and dogs—yet we are not afraid of starving by abstaining from them, even though they reproduce abundantly. Finally, eating an animal is not the same thing as killing it. We destroy many of the animals mentioned above, but we do not eat any of them.

15. Furthermore, it is also reported that the Syrians formerly abstained from animals and, for this reason, did not sacrifice them to the Gods. Later, they began to sacrifice them to turn away certain evils, yet they still did not allow a diet of meat. In time, however—as Neanthes of Cyzicus and Asclepiades the Cyprian say—around the time of Pygmalion <note>a Phoenician king of Tyre, not the sculptor of legend</note>, who was Phoenician by birth but ruled over the Cyprians, the eating of meat was allowed because of a transgression of the following kind. Asclepiades relates the story in his treatise concerning Cyprus and Phoenicia as follows: 

In the beginning, they did not sacrifice anything living <note>original: "animated"</note> to the Gods; nor was there any...

<meta>This page continues the story of the first animal sacrifice and how humans began eating meat, moving then to the dietary customs of the Syrians and the religious order of the Persian Magi.</meta>

...the law pertaining to such a thing, because it was prohibited by natural law. It is said, however, that on a certain occasion when one life <note>original: "soul"</note> was required in exchange for another, they sacrificed a victim for the first time; and when this took place, the whole of the victim was then consumed by fire. But afterwards, when the victim was being burned, a portion of the flesh fell on the ground. It was picked up by the priest, who burned his fingers in the process; he instinctively moved them to his mouth as a remedy for the pain caused by the burn. Having tasted the roasted flesh in this way, he then desired to eat more of it, and he could not refrain from giving some to his wife. 

Pygmalion <note>The King of Tyre/Cyprus in Phoenician mythology</note>, however, became aware of this circumstance and ordered both the priest and his wife to be thrown headlong from a steep rock. He gave the priesthood to another person, who not long after performed the same sacrifice, and after eating the flesh of the victim, suffered the same fate as his predecessor. However, as the practice continued to spread, and as men performed the same kind of sacrifice—yielding to their desires by not only killing but feeding on flesh—the act was eventually no longer punished. Nevertheless, abstinence from fish continued among the Syrians until the time of Menander <note>A famous Greek dramatist, c. 342–290 BCE</note>; for he says:

> Take the Syrians, for example:
> When they are led by lack of self-control to eat fish,
> Their bellies and their feet become swollen.
> Then, covered in sackcloth, they sit in the public road
> Upon a dunghill, so that the Goddess, appeased 
> By their lowly state, may forgive the crime.

These men are considered so great and venerable by the Persians that Darius, the son of Hystaspes, had it engraved on his tomb, among other things, that he had been the master of the Magi. They...

<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD: BOOK IV</header>

The Magi are also divided into three classes <note>original: "genera"</note>, as we are informed by Eubulus, who wrote the history of Mithra in a work consisting of many books. In this work, he says that the first and most learned class of the Magi neither eat nor kill any living creature, but strictly adhere to the ancient practice of abstaining from animals. The second class eats certain animals, but they do not kill any that are domesticated. Nor does the third class, like other people, eat all kinds of animals. 

For the primary doctrine held by all of them is the transmigration <note>reincarnation</note> of souls; and they also seem to indicate this in the mysteries of Mithra. In these mysteries—vaguely signifying that we have something in common with animals <note>original: "brutes"</note>—they are accustomed to call us by the names of different animals. Thus, they call the males who participate in these same mysteries "lions," the females "lionesses," and those who serve these rites "crows." Regarding their leaders, whom they call "fathers," they follow the same custom: these are named "eagles" and "hawks." 

Furthermore, he who is initiated into the Leontic <note>lion-related</note> mysteries is clothed in various animal forms <gloss>q</gloss>. Regarding these details, Pallas, in his treatise concerning Mithra, explains the cause. He says that while it is a common opinion that these names refer to the circle of the zodiac, the true and accurate meaning is that they vaguely signify something pertaining to human souls. <note>
<gloss>r</gloss> Proclus, however, in his Scholia <note>commentaries</note> on the *Cratylus* of Plato, gives a much more theological account of the origin of the name of Proserpine, as follows:

"Socrates now presents these three life-giving <note>original: "vivific"</note> unities <note>original: "monads"</note> in a logical order: Ceres, Juno, and Proserpine. He calls the first the mother, the second the sister, and the third the daughter of the Creator <note>original: "Demiurgus"</note> [Jupiter]. All of them, however, participate in the whole process of creation <note>original: "fabrication"</note>. The first does so in a transcendent <note>original: "exempt"</note> and intellectual manner; the second in a primary <note>original: "fontal"</note> way, and at the same time in a way suited to a governing principle <note>original: *archikōs*</note>; and the third in a manner suited to both a governing principle and a leader <note>original: *archikōs kai hegemonikōs*</note>.

Of these Goddesses, the last is assigned triple powers, and in an indivisible and uniform way encompasses three unities of Gods. She is called Core <note>original: *korē*—meaning 'the maiden'</note> because of the purity of her essence and her undefiled excellence in her various generations. She also possesses a first, middle, and final empire. According to her highest peak, she is called Diana by Orpheus; according to her middle, she is Proserpine; and according to the end of her order, she is Minerva. Likewise, according to an essence that transcends the other powers of this triple life-giving order, the dominion of Hecate is established; but according to a middle power, which generates whole things, is the dominion of the Soul; and, according to intellectual focus <note>original: "conversion"</note>, is the dominion of Virtue <gloss>*</gloss>. Ceres, therefore, existing on high among the gods beyond this world <note>original: "supermundane"</note>, uniformly extends this triple order of divinities. Together with Jupiter, she generates Bacchus, who in an indivisible manner presides over the divisible creative process <note>original: "partible fabrication"</note>. But below, in conjunction with Pluto, she is specifically seen according to her middle characteristic; for it is this which, extending everywhere, grants life <note>original: "vivification"</note> to the lowest parts of..."

</note>

<meta>This page continues the theological explanation of Proserpine’s name and nature, specifically quoting the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus. It connects Proserpine to other goddesses like Maia, Ceres, and Minerva, explaining her role as a "middle" deity who bridges the celestial and the underworld.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

to her a ringdove. And Maia is the same as Proserpine, acting as a midwife and a nurse <note>original: "obstetric"</note>. For this Goddess organizes all things. Hence she is called Proserpine because she especially associates with Pluto and, together with him, organizes the outer limits <note>original: "extremities"</note> of the universe in an orderly manner. 

According to her highest limits, she is said to be a virgin and to remain undefiled; but according to her middle nature, she is joined with Hades <note>Pluto</note> and gives birth to the Furies in the subterranean regions. She, therefore, is also called Ceres, but in a different way than the celestial <note>original: "supermundane"</note> and ruling Ceres. For the one is the connective unity of the three life-giving <note>original: "vivific"</note> principles; but the other is the middle of them, possessing within herself the characteristics of both extremes. Hence, in the Proserpine who is joined with Pluto, you will find the qualities of Hecate and Minerva; but these extremes exist in her secretly <note>original: "occultly"</note>, while her middle characteristic shines forth. This is the characteristic of the "ruling soul," which in the celestial Ceres was of a *ruling* <note>original: "supermundane"</note> $\ast$ nature, but here exists according to a worldly <note>original: "mundane"</note> quality.

Proclus further observes, "that Proserpine is named either by judging forms and separating them from each other—thus obscurely hinting at the end of destruction <note>original: "subversion of slaughter"; Greek: δια το κρινειν τα ειδη, και χωριζειν αλληλων ως του φονου την αναιρεσιν αινιττομενον</note>—or by separating souls perfectly from bodies through a turning toward higher things, which is the most fortunate 'slaughter' and death for those who are worthy of it <note>Greek: η δια το χωριζειν τας ψυχας τελεως εκ των σωματων δια της προς τα ανω επιστροφης, οπερ εστιν ευτυχεστατος φονος και θανατος τοις αξιουμενοις ταυτου</note>. 

But the name *Pherephatta* <note>an ancient name for Proserpine/Persephone</note> is applied to Proserpine according to her contact with the world of generation; but according to wisdom and counsel, it is applied to Minerva. At the same time, however, all the titles by which she is distinguished are suited to the perfection of the soul. On this account, she is also called Proserpine and not by the names of the extremes, since the deity who was abducted by Pluto is this 'middle' deity. The extremes, meanwhile, remain firmly established in themselves; according to which Ceres is said to remain a virgin."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of goddess symbolism and ritual purity, specifically focusing on the goddess Ceres (Demeter) and the dietary prohibitions within the Eleusinian mysteries as a means for spiritual ascent.</meta>

The goddess is terrestrial <note>referring to the aspect of the goddess associated with the earth</note>, and so likewise is **Ceres**. To this Goddess, also, a cock is consecrated; and for this reason, those who are initiated in her mysteries abstain from domestic birds. In the Eleusinian mysteries, likewise, the initiated are ordered to abstain from domestic birds, from fish and beans, pomegranates and apples. These fruits are considered as equally defiling to the touch as a woman who has recently given birth or a dead body. 

But whoever is acquainted with the nature of divinely-luminous appearances <term>Greek: phasmata; refers to spiritual visions or manifestations of the gods</term> also knows why it is necessary to abstain from all birds. This is especially true for the person who hastens to be liberated from terrestrial concerns and to be established among the celestial Gods. 

Vice, however, as we have frequently said, is quite capable of defending itself, especially when it pleads its case among the ignorant. Hence, among those who are only moderately corrupt, some think that a warning <note>original: "dehortation"</note> of this kind is nothing but vain babbling and—as the proverb says—the silliness <note>original: "nugacity"</note> of old women; others are of the opinion that it is mere superstition. But those who have made greater advances in wickedness <note>original: "improbity"</note> are prepared not only to blaspheme those who encourage and demonstrate the propriety of this abstinence, but even to slander purity itself as a form of sorcery <note>original: "enchantment"</note> or pride. They, however, suffer the punishment of their sins from both Gods and men; they are, in the first place, sufficiently punished by having a depraved disposition <gloss>that is, by their own depravity</gloss>. We shall, therefore, further mention another foreign nation, renowned and—

*...and at the same time intellectual order, and is wholly absorbed in the intelligible. As we are also informed by the philosopher Proclus (in his commentary on Plato’s "Cratylus"): “She [Night] is the model <note>original: "paradigm"</note> for Ceres. For immortal Night is the nurse of the Gods <gloss>according to the poet Orpheus</gloss>. Night, however, is the source of nourishment <note>original: "aliment"</note> in an intellectual sense: for the 'intelligible' is, as the Chaldean Oracle says, the nourishment of the intellectual orders of Gods. But Ceres, first of all, separates the two kinds of nourishment <gloss>nectar and ambrosia</gloss> among the Gods.” He adds, “Hence our sovereign mistress <term>Greek: despoina; lady or mistress</term>, Ceres, not only generates life, but also that which gives perfection to life; and she does this for all beings, from the highest heavenly natures down to the very last. For virtue is the perfection of souls.”*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dietary laws and piety, transitioning from the customs of the Eleusinian mysteries to the practices of Indian sages to demonstrate that abstinence from animal food is a widespread mark of holiness.</meta>

just, and believed to be pious in divine concerns, and then pass on to other particulars.

17. Since the social structure <note>original: "polity"</note> of the Indians is divided into many parts, there is one tribe among them of men who are divinely wise, whom the Greeks are accustomed to call Gymnosophists <term>literally "naked philosophers," a term used by Greeks for Indian ascetics</term>. There are two sects of these: the Brahmins <note>original: "Bramins"</note> preside over one, while the Samanaeans <note>referring to Shramanas, a tradition of non-Vedic Indian ascetics including Buddhists and Jains</note> lead the other. The lineage of the Brahmins receives this divine wisdom by hereditary succession, in the same way one inherits a priesthood. The Samanaeans, however, are elected and consist of those who choose to dedicate themselves to divine knowledge.

The details regarding them are as follows, as narrated by the Babylonian Bardesanes <note>a Gnostic scholar and historian from the 2nd century</note>, who lived in the time of our fathers and was familiar with those Indians who were sent to the Emperor <note>original: "Cæsar"</note> along with Dandamis <note>original: "Damadamis"</note>. All the Brahmins originate from one stock, for all of them are descended from a single father and mother. But the Samanaeans are not the offspring of a single family; as we have said, they are gathered from every tribe of the Indians. 

A Brahmin is not a subject of any government, nor does he contribute taxes or service to the state alongside others. Among those who are philosophers, some dwell on mountains and others near the river Ganges. Those who live on the mountains feed on autumn fruits and on cows' milk curdled <note>original: "coagulated"</note> with herbs. Those who reside near the Ganges also live on autumn fruits, which grow in abundance around that river. The land there produces new fruit almost year-round, along with a great deal of wild rice <note>original: "rice, which grows spontaneously"</note>, which they use when there is a shortage of autumn fruit. To taste any other kind of nourishment, or, in short, to even touch animal food, is considered by them to be an act of extreme

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s account of the Indian philosophers, describing the specific religious practices, solitary lifestyles, and ascetic habits of the Brahmins and the Samanaeans (Shramanas).</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>

impurity and impiety. This is one of their core principles <note>original: "dogmas"</note>. They also worship the Divine with piety and purity. They spend the day, and the greater part of the night, in hymns and prayers to the Gods; each of them has a cottage to himself and lives, as much as possible, alone. For the Brahmins <note>original: "Bramins"</note> cannot bear to remain with others, nor to speak much; when they are forced to do so, they afterwards withdraw and do not speak for many days. They also fast frequently. 

The Samanaeans <note>referring to Shramanas, a tradition of non-Vedic Indian ascetics including Buddhists and Jains</note> are, as we have said, elected to their positions. When anyone desires to be enrolled in their order, he goes to the rulers of the city and abandons the city or village he inhabited, along with all the wealth and property he possessed. After having the hair of his head and beard removed <note>original: "the superfluities of his body cut off," referring to the practice of tonsure</note>, he receives a robe and departs to join the Samanaeans. He never returns to his wife or children, if he happens to have any, nor does he pay any attention to them or think that they belong to him at all. Regarding his children, the king provides what is necessary for them, and his relatives provide for the wife. Such is the life of the Samanaeans. 

They live outside the city and spend the whole day in conversation regarding the Divine. They also have houses and temples built by the king, which are managed by stewards who receive a stipend <note>original: "emolument"</note> from the king for the purpose of supplying the residents with food <note>original: "nutriment"</note>. Their diet consists of rice, bread, seasonal fruits <note>original: "autumnal fruits"</note>, and vegetables <note>original: "pot-herbs"</note>. When they enter their house, the sound of a bell serves as the signal of their entrance; at this sound, those who are not Samanaeans depart, and the Samanaeans immediately begin to pray. After praying, when the bell sounds again, the servants give each Samanaean a platter—for no two of them eat out of the same dish—and feed them with rice. To those who desire a variety of food, a vegetable dish or some seasonal fruit is added. After they have eaten as much as is required, they proceed without any delay to

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s description of the Samanaeans (Shramanas) in India, focusing on their social standing and their philosophical views on death and the immortality of the soul.</meta>

their usual jobs <note>original: "accustomed employments"</note>. All of them are also unmarried and have no possessions. Both they and the Brahmins <note>original: "Bramins"</note> are so highly respected by the other Indians that even the king visits them. He asks them to pray and petition <note>original: "supplicate"</note> the Gods whenever a disaster strikes the country, or to give him advice on how to act.

18. They have such an attitude toward death that they endure this present life only reluctantly, viewing it as a kind of slavery <note>original: "servitude"</note> to nature. Therefore, they are eager to free their souls from their bodies. For this reason, they often choose to die <note>original: "depart from life"</note> even when they appear to be in good health and are not suffering or driven to despair by any misfortune. Even though they announce their intention to commit suicide in advance, no one tries to stop them. Instead, everyone declares that those who leave this life in such a way are happy. They give instructions to the servants and relatives of the dying person; both the philosophers and the common people firmly believe the claim that souls associate with one another in the next life. As soon as those who have heard these final instructions receive them, they commit the body to the fire <note>referring to the practice of cremation</note> so that the soul may be separated from the body in the purest way possible. Thus, they die honored by all the Samanaeans. These men say goodbye to their dearest friends as they go to their deaths more easily than other people say goodbye to fellow citizens departing on long journeys. They grieve for themselves because they must continue living, but they declare that those who have died are blessed because they have now obtained their immortal inheritance <note>original: "allotment"</note>. There is no philosopher-for-hire <note>original: "sophist," here used to mean a cynical or clever rhetorician</note>, like those found among the Greeks today, among either the Samanaeans or the Brahmins, who would express doubt or ask, "If everyone followed your example [by committing suicide], what would become of us?" Nor are human affairs thrown into chaos because of them. Not everyone follows their example, and those

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s discussion of the Indian Samanaeans (Shramanas) and Brahmins, specifically focusing on how the laws of a nation treat these holy men differently than the common people, and how their strict lifestyle serves as a high moral standard.</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

who have [departed this life], may be said to have been the cause of fair legislation rather than a source of confusion for various nations. Furthermore, the law did not force the Samanaeans and Brahmins <note>original: "Bramins"</note> to eat meat <note>original: "animal food"</note>; but, while it allowed others to feed on flesh, it permitted these men to be a law unto themselves and honored them as being superior to ordinary law. Nor did the law subject these men to the punishments it inflicts on others—as if they were the primary committers of injustice—but it reserved such penalties for others.

Therefore, to those who ask what would happen if every person imitated such characters, the saying of Pythagoras must be the answer: if all people were kings, the journey through life would be difficult, yet monarchical government <note>original: "regal government"</note> is not to be avoided for that reason. And we might also say that if everyone were virtuous <note>original: "worthy"</note>, no political system <note>original: "administration of a polity"</note> could be found that would adequately preserve the dignity that such integrity <note>original: "probity"</note> deserves. Nevertheless, no one would be so foolish as to think that people should not strive with all their heart to become virtuous.

Indeed, the law permits common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> many other things (besides eating meat) which it does not grant to a philosopher, nor even to one who manages the affairs of government correctly. For the law does not admit every craftsman <note>original: "artist"</note> into the government, even though it does not forbid the practice of any craft or profession. Instead, it excludes those who work in degrading and unrefined <note>original: "vile and illiberal"</note> trades*, and essentially everyone lacking in justice and other virtues, from participating in the management of public affairs. In the same way, the law does not stop common people from associating with prostitutes <note>original: "harlots"</note>—even though it imposes a fine on the prostitutes themselves—but it considers it shameful and beneath those who are even moderately good to have any connection with them. Furthermore, the law does not forbid a man from spending his entire life in a tavern, yet at the same time this...

* <note>original: "Banausoi"</note> <unclear>Banausoi</unclear>, meaning "unrefined laborers" <note>original: "dirty mechanics and bellows-blowers"</note>. This is a term used by Plato in his *Rivals* to describe those who work with their hands <note>original: "experimentalists"</note>.

<summary>Porphyry argues that while the law allows common people to eat meat and engage in "low" behaviors, philosophers and holy men like the Samanaeans and Brahmins are held to a higher standard that places them above ordinary legislation. He uses a quote from Pythagoras to explain why a society needs high moral examples even if everyone cannot realistically live up to them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that what is permissible for the general public is often shameful for a philosopher or a person of high character. Porphyry moves from discussing the laws of specific cultures to the universal religious expectations of purity and abstinence.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>

...is most disgraceful even to a man of moderate worth. It appears, therefore, that the same thing must also be said with respect to diet. For what is permitted to the common people <note>original: "multitude"</note> must not likewise be granted to the best of men. A philosopher, in particular, should establish for himself those sacred laws that the gods—and those men who follow the gods—have instituted. 

Furthermore, the sacred laws of nations and cities appear to have prescribed purity for holy men and have forbidden <note>original: "interdicted"</note> them from eating animal food. They have also forbidden the common people from eating certain animals, either out of piety or because of some harm the food would cause. Therefore, it is necessary either to imitate priests or to obey the commands of all legislators; but in either case, anyone who is truly law-abiding and pious should abstain from all animals. For if those who are only partially pious abstain from certain animals, then he who is pious in every respect will abstain from all animals.

19. I had almost forgotten to cite <note>original: "adduce"</note> the words of Euripides, who asserts that the prophets of Jupiter <note>The Roman name for the Greek god Zeus</note> in Crete abstained from animals. The chorus addresses King Minos on this subject as follows:

20. For holy men were of the opinion that purity consisted in a thing not being mixed with its opposite, and that such a mixture is a defilement. Hence, they thought that nourishment should be taken from fruits and not from dead bodies. They believed that we should not defile our own natural administration by introducing something into our nature that was once alive <note>original: "animated"</note>. They conceived that the slaughter of animals—since they are sensitive beings—and the act of depriving them of their souls, is a defilement to the living. They further believed that the pollution is much greater when a body that was once sensitive, but is now deprived of sense, is mixed with a sensitive and living being. 

Therefore, universally, the purity pertaining to piety consists in rejecting and abstaining from many things, in abandoning those things that are of a contrary nature, and in adopting those things that are appropriate and harmonious. For this reason, sexual relations <note>original: "venereal connexions"</note> are associated with defilement. In these acts, a conjunction takes place between the female and the male; the seed, when retained by the woman and causing her to become pregnant, defiles the soul through its close association with the physical body. However, when it does not produce a child, it still pollutes because it becomes a lifeless mass. The sexual connection of males with males also defiles, because it is an emission of seed into what is essentially a dead body <note>Porphyry argues that since no life can come from it, the seed is wasted and becomes "dead," causing ritual impurity</note>, and because it is contrary to nature. In short, all sexual activity, as well as the emission of seed during sleep, causes defilement.

...saying, that “it is evident from the hymns of Orpheus that Zagreus <note>original: Ζαγρεύς</note> was a name given to Bacchus <note>Dionysus</note> at his sacred rites.” However, the word Zagreus is not to be found either in the hymns of Orpheus or in any other of the Orphic writings that are still in existence.

<summary>Porphyry cites Euripides to show that ancient priests practiced vegetarianism and avoided ritual pollutions like death and childbirth. He argues that purity requires avoiding the "mixture" of life with death, which includes eating meat and certain sexual acts.</summary>

is polluted, because the soul becomes mingled with the body and is drawn down toward pleasure. The passions of the soul likewise defile it when the irrational and "effeminate" part becomes entangled with reason, the internal "masculine" part. <note>In ancient philosophy, "masculine" was often associated with active reason and "effeminate" with passive emotion or the physical body.</note> For, in a certain respect, defilement and pollution represent the mixing of things that are different in nature, especially when the cleansing <note>original: "abstersion"</note> of this mixture is difficult. Because of this, when dyes <note>original: "tinctures"</note> are produced through mixing—where one type is blended with another—this mixture is called a defilement.

->As when some woman with a lively red<-
->Stains the pure ivory ——————<-

says Homer <gloss>z</gloss>. And again, painters call the mixing of colors "corruptions." It is also common to call that which is unmingled and pure "incorruptible," and to call that which is genuine "unpolluted." For water, when mixed with earth, is corrupted and is no longer pure. But water which flows freely <note>original: "diffluent"</note> and runs with violent speed leaves behind the earth that it carries in its stream.

->When from a clear and everlasting fountain<-
->It flows downward ——————<-

as Hesiod says <gloss>a</gloss>. For such water is healthy because it is uncorrupted and unmixed. Likewise, a woman who does not receive the discharge of semen <note>original: "exhalation of seed"</note> into herself is said to be "uncorrupted." Thus, the mixture of opposites is corruption and defilement. For the mixture of dead with living bodies—the insertion of beings that were once alive and sensing <note>original: "sentient"</note> into animals, and of dead flesh into living flesh—may be reasonably supposed to introduce defilement and stains to our nature; just as the soul is polluted when it is placed within the body. Hence, anyone who is born is polluted by the mixture of

<summary>Porphyry defines ritual and spiritual pollution as the "mixture" of opposite things, such as the soul with the body or dead flesh with a living person. He uses literary examples from Homer and Hesiod to illustrate that purity exists only in things that are unmixed and genuine.</summary>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s philosophical exploration of purity as the state of being unmixed. He argues that just as water is polluted by mud, the soul is polluted by its association with the body and the consumption of animal flesh, which clouds its rational powers.</meta>

<page-num>165</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD — BOOK IV.</header>

his soul with the body; and he who dies defiles his body by leaving it as a corpse, which is different and foreign to that which possesses life. Likewise, the soul is polluted by anger and desire, and the many passions for which diet is, in a certain way, a contributing cause. But just as water flowing through a rock is purer than water running through marshes (because it does not carry much mud with it), so too the soul that manages its own affairs in a "dry" body—one not moistened by the juices of foreign flesh—is in a far better condition, is more uncorrupted, and is more ready for intellectual activity. It is also said that thyme, which is the driest and sharpest to the taste, provides the best honey for bees. 

Therefore, the <term>dianoëtic</term> <note>original: "dianoëtic"; referring to the soul's power of logical reasoning or discursive thought</note>, or discursive power of the soul, is polluted—or rather, the person who acts through reason is polluted—when this activity is mixed with the activities of either the imagination or the <term>doxastic</term> <note>original: "doxastic"; relating to belief or opinion rather than certain knowledge</note> power. Purification consists in a separation from all these things, and the wisdom suited to divine matters requires abandoning everything of this kind. 

Likewise, the proper nourishment <note>original: "nutriment"</note> of each thing is that which essentially preserves it. Thus, you might say that the nourishment of a stone is what causes it to continue being a stone and to remain firmly in a stony <note>original: "lapideous"</note> form. The nourishment of a plant is what preserves its growth and fruiting; and for a living body, it is what preserves its physical structure. However, it is one thing to nourish and another to fatten; and one thing to provide what is necessary, but another to seek out luxury. Therefore, there are various kinds of nourishment, just as there are various natures among the things being nourished. While it is necessary for all things to be nourished, we should earnestly strive to "fatten" our most important parts. Thus, the nourishment of the rational soul is what keeps it in a rational state. This nourishment is the intellect; therefore, the soul should be nourished by the intellect, and we should earnestly strive to see that it is "fattened" [by it].

<summary>Porphyry argues that a diet of animal flesh "moistens" and pollutes the soul, whereas a "dry" state facilitates intellectual clarity. He defines true nourishment as that which preserves a thing's essence, concluding that the rational soul must be nourished and strengthened by the intellect rather than by physical luxury.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, purification, rational soul, dianoëtic, doxastic, intellect, diet and philosophy, abstinence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that spiritual purity requires minimal physical indulgence. He previously discussed how the soul is weighed down by bodily passions and heavy foods, comparing a pure soul in a "dry" body to the best honey coming from the driest thyme.</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>
<header>ON ABSTINENCE FROM ANIMAL FOOD</header>

...through this purity, rather than by making the flesh <term>pinguid</term> <note>original: "pinguid"; meaning fatty, oily, or physically heavy</note> through <term>esculent</term> <note>original: "esculent"; edible</note> substances. For the intellect preserves eternal life for us, but the body, when fattened, causes the soul to be famished because its hunger for a blessed life is never satisfied. A heavy body increases our mortal part—which is inherently irrational <note>original: "insane"</note>—and prevents us from attaining an immortal state of being. It also defiles the soul by <term>corporifying</term> <note>original: "corporifying"; making the soul more material or bodily in nature</note> her and dragging her down to that which is foreign to her nature. 

Consider the magnet: it imparts, as it were, a soul to the iron placed near it; and the iron, though it is very heavy, is lifted up and runs toward the "spirit" of the stone. Should a person, then, who is suspended from the immaterial and divine intellect, be anxiously busy in procuring food that fattens the body, which is only an obstacle to intellectual perception? Should he not rather—by reducing what is necessary for the flesh to a small and easily obtained amount—be *himself* nourished by clinging to God more closely than iron clings to a magnet? I wish, indeed, that our nature was not so prone to decay, and that it were possible for us to live free from trouble, even without the nourishment derived from fruits.

O that, as Homer$^b$ says, we did not require either meat or drink, so that we might be truly immortal! The poet spoke beautifully here, signifying that food is an aid not only to life, but also to death. If, therefore, we did not even require vegetable food <note>original: "aliment"</note>, we would be even more blessed, as we would be closer to immortality. 

But as it is, being in a mortal condition, we make ourselves (if it is proper to say so) even more mortal by remaining ignorant of a certain truth: by adding to this mortality, the soul—as Theophrastus says—does not merely pay a high "rent" to the body by being its inhabitant, but gives herself entirely over to it$^c$. He references Homer and Theophrastus to illustrate that physical nourishment is a burden that binds the immortal soul to a mortal, decaying state.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that a life of philosophical purity requires distancing oneself from the needs and "heaviness" of the physical body. He moves from discussing the internal state of the soul to addressing anthropological counterarguments about meat-eating cultures.</meta>

<page-num>167</page-num>
<header>ANIMAL FOOD.—BOOK IV.</header>

It is much to be wished that we could easily obtain the life celebrated in fables, in which hunger and thirst are unknown. By doing so—by stopping the ever-flowing river of the body—we might in a very short time be present with the most excellent natures. He who approaches these natures, since divinity is there, becomes a god himself. But how is it possible not to lament the condition of most of mankind, who are so shrouded in darkness that they cherish their own evil? In the first place, they hate themselves and the one who truly created them, and afterwards, they hate those who warn them and call on them to return from intoxication <note>original: "ebriety"</note> to a sober state of being. Therefore, leaving aside such matters, is it not necessary to move on to what remains to be discussed?

21. Those, then, who use the examples of the Nomads <note>original: "Nomades"</note>, the Cave-dwellers <note>original: "Troglodytæ"</note>, or the Fish-eaters <note>original: "Ichthyophagi"</note> to oppose the legal traditions of the nations we have mentioned, are unaware that these people were forced by necessity to eat animals because the region they inhabit is so barren <note>original: "infecundity"</note>. Their land is so desolate that it does not even produce plants, but only rocky shores and sands. This necessity is shown by the fact that they cannot even use fire because they lack fuel; instead, they dry their fish on rocks or on the shore. These people live in this manner out of sheer necessity. 

There are, however, certain nations whose customs are crude and who are naturally savage; but it is not right for fair judges to insult human nature based on such examples. If we did, we would not only doubt whether it is proper to eat animals, but also whether we might eat humans and adopt all kinds of other savage behaviors. It is said, for instance, that the Massagetae and the Derbices consider those of their relatives who die naturally <note>original: "spontaneously"</note> to be most miserable. Therefore, to prevent their dearest friends from dying naturally, they kill them when they are old and eat them. The Tibareni throw their closest relatives from rocks, even while—

---

<margin>d See Diodorus Siculus, Book III, 32.</margin>

<summary>Porphyry argues that meat-eating in certain cultures results from geographic necessity or inherent savagery rather than natural law, asserting that these "primitive" examples should not dictate the behavior of a rational or civilized person.</summary>

living, when they are old. And with respect to the Hyrcanians <note>original: "Hyrcani"</note> and Caspians <note>original: "Caspii"</note>, the former exposed the living, but the latter the dead, to be devoured by birds and dogs. The Scythians bury the living with the dead and slit the throats of loved ones on the funeral pyres of the deceased by whom they were especially beloved. The Bactrians <note>original: "Bactrii"</note> likewise throw those among them who are old, even while still living, to the dogs. And Stasanor, who was one of Alexander’s governors <note>original: "prefects"</note>, nearly lost his territory by trying to abolish this custom. 

However, just as we do not undermine humane behavior <note>original: "mildness of conduct"</note> toward men because of these examples, we should not imitate those nations that eat meat out of necessity. Instead, we should follow the example of the pious and those who consecrate themselves to the Gods. For Democrates<sup>e</sup> says that to live poorly—and not wisely, temperately, and piously—is not to truly live, but to be a long time in dying. <note>original: "not to live in reality, but to die for a long time."</note>

22. It now remains for us to bring forward a few examples of certain individuals as evidence in favor of abstinence from animal food. For the lack of such examples was one of the accusations urged against us. We learn, therefore, that Triptolemus was the most ancient of the Athenian legislators. Regarding him, Hermippus<sup>g</sup>, in the second book of his treatise *On Legislators*, writes as follows: “It is said that Triptolemus established laws for the Athenians. And the philosopher Xenocrates asserts that three of his laws still remain in Eleusis, which are these: *Honor your parents; Sacrifice to the Gods from the fruits of the earth; Do not harm animals.*” Two of these, therefore, he says, are—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the laws established by Triptolemus, the legendary Athenian figure credited with introducing agriculture and civilization. It explores the possible motivations behind his law against eating animals and introduces the laws of Draco regarding religious worship.</meta>

<page-num>169</page-num>

properly established. It is necessary that we should, as much as possible, repay our parents for the benefits they have given us, and that we should offer the first-fruits of the things useful to our life—which the Gods have provided—to the Gods themselves. Regarding the third law, he is uncertain about Triptolemus's intention in ordering the Athenians to abstain from eating animals. Did he do this, he asks, because he thought it was a terrible thing to kill natures related to our own, or because he realized that the most useful animals would be wiped out by humans for food? 

Wishing, therefore, to make our lives as gentle as possible, he tried to protect those animals that live alongside humans and are particularly tame. Alternatively, perhaps because he had commanded that men should honor the Gods by offering them first-fruits, he added this third law, believing that this form of worship would last longer if animal sacrifices were not made to the Gods. Although Xenocrates mentions many other possible reasons for the passing of these laws (though they may not be entirely accurate), what has been said is enough for our purpose: that abstaining from animal meat was one of the legal principles <note>original: "institutes"</note> of Triptolemus. Therefore, those who later broke this law—driven by extreme necessity or unintentional mistakes—fell into the habit of slaughtering and eating animals, as we have already shown. The following is also cited as a law of Draco:

> “Let this be an eternal *sacred law* <gloss>h</gloss> for the people of Attica, and let its authority prevail forever: that the Gods and local <note>original: "indigenous"</note> Heroes are to be worshiped publicly, in accordance with the laws of the land passed down by our ancestors; and also that they be worshiped privately, according to the means of each individual, combined with respectful <note>original: "auspicious"</note> words, the

<meta>This page begins Porphyry’s famous Neoplatonic allegorical interpretation of a passage from Homer's Odyssey. It introduces the description of the Cave of the Nymphs and begins an argument for why the description should be read symbolically rather than historically.</meta>

->ON<-

# THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS,

->IN THE<-

## THIRTEENTH BOOK OF THE ODYSSEY.

---

1. What does Homer obscurely signify by the cave in Ithaca, which he describes in the following verses?

> “High at the head a branching olive grows,
> And crowns the pointed cliffs with shady boughs.
> A cavern pleasant, though involved <note>original: "involv’d"</note> in night,
> Beneath it lies, the Naiads' <note>Nymphs of flowing water, such as springs and rivers. Original: "Naiades’"</note> delight:
> Where bowls and urns of workmanship divine
> And massive <note>original: "massy"</note> beams in native marble shine;
> On which the nymphs amazing webs display,
> Of purple hue, and exquisite array.
> The busy bees within the urns secure
> Honey delicious, and like nectar pure.
> Perpetual waters through the grotto glide,
> A lofty gate unfolds on either side;
> That to the north is accessible <note>original: "pervious"</note> to mankind;
> The sacred south to immortals is assigned <note>original: "consign’d"</note>.”

<margin>This Cronius, the Pythagorean, is also mentioned by Porphyry in his *Life of Plotinus*.</margin>
That the poet, indeed, does not record <note>original: "narrate"</note> these details <note>original: "particulars"</note> from historical information is evident from this: those who have given us a description of the island have, as Cronius says, made no mention of such a cave being found in it. This likewise, he says, is manifest: it would be absurd for Homer to expect that in

<summary>Porphyry introduces a symbolic analysis of the Cave of the Nymphs from Homer's Odyssey, arguing that since historical descriptions of Ithaca do not mention such a cave, the passage must be an allegory.</summary>
<keywords>Homer, Odyssey, Cave of the Nymphs, Porphyry, Cronius, Neoplatonism, Ithaca, Naiads, allegory, Pythagoreanism</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument of Cronius, a Pythagorean philosopher, who contends that Homer’s description of the Cave of the Nymphs in the Odyssey is an allegory rather than a geographical fact.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>

->ON THE<-

# CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.

—he should expect to be believed by describing a cave created merely through poetic license, and by artificially opening a path for Gods and men in the region of Ithaca. It is equally absurd to suppose that nature herself would point out, in this specific place, one path for the descent of all mankind and another path for all the Gods. For indeed, although the whole world is full of Gods and men, it is impossible to believe that in this Ithacan cave, men descend and Gods ascend. 

Cronius, therefore, having established these points, says it is evident—not only to the wise but also to ordinary people—that the poet uses the veil of allegory to conceal a mysterious meaning. This forces us to investigate what the "gate of men" and the "gate of the Gods" actually signify. We must ask why he asserts that this cave of the Nymphs has two gates, and why it is described as both pleasant and dark <note>original: "obscure"</note>, since darkness is by no means delightful but is instead something that causes avoidance and horror. 

Likewise, what is the reason that it is not simply called the cave of the Nymphs, but is accurately specifically named the cave of the Nymphs called Naiads <note>Nymphs associated with fresh water, such as springs and fountains</note>? Why is the cave represented as containing bowls and amphoras <note>original: "amphoræ"; tall, two-handled jars used for storing wine or oil</note>, when there is no mention of them holding any liquid? Instead, bees are said to deposit their honey in these vessels as if they were hives. 

Furthermore, why are long weaving beams placed here for the Nymphs? And why are these beams not made of wood or some other flexible material, but of stone, just like the amphoras and bowls? While the stone vessels are somewhat less confusing, the idea that the Nymphs should weave purple garments on stone beams is a wonder to both the eyes and the ears. For who would believe that Goddesses weave garments in a cave shrouded in darkness and on stone beams—especially when the poet asserts that the Goddesses' purple webs were visible? Finally, it is remarkable that the cave should have a double entrance: one made for the descent of men, and the other for the ascent of Gods.

<summary>Porphyry, citing Cronius, highlights the many logical inconsistencies in a literal reading of Homer's cave, such as stone weaving beams and separate gates for gods and mortals, to prove the passage is an allegory.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Cronius, Homer, Odyssey, Cave of the Nymphs, allegory, Naiads, symbolism, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s line of questioning regarding the symbolic details in Homer’s description of the Cave of the Nymphs, moving from the physical objects inside the cave to its geographic orientation and its historical existence.</meta>

<page-num>173</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

Furthermore, why is it said that the gate accessible to men faces the north wind, while the portal of the Gods faces the south? Why didn't the poet use the west and the east for this purpose? Almost all temples have their statues and entrances facing east; those who enter them face west when standing before the statues to honor and worship the Gods. 

Because this narrative is filled with such obscure details, it cannot be a simple fiction created just for pleasure, nor a straightforward historical account of a specific place. Instead, the poet—who also symbolically <note>original: "mystically"</note> places an olive tree near the cave—must be indicating something allegorical. The ancients found it very difficult to investigate and explain all these details; now, with their help, we will try to reveal the secret meaning of this allegory. 

Those people who think the cave and the story about it are nothing more than the poet's fiction seem to have written very carelessly about the location. The most accurate writers of geography, such as Artemidorus of Ephesus <note>A Greek geographer active around 100 BC</note>, write in the fifth book of his eleven-volume work: "The island of Ithaca, covering an area of eighty-five stadia <note>A "stadium" was an ancient unit of distance. 85 stadia is approximately 10.5 miles</note>, is about twelve stadia from Panormus, a port in Cephalonia <note>original: "Cephalenia"</note>. It has a harbor named Phorcys, where there is a shore, and on that shore a cave where the Phaeacians <note>A seafaring people in Greek mythology who helped Ulysses return home</note> are said to have placed Ulysses." 

Therefore, this cave is not entirely a creation of Homer's imagination. However, whether the poet describes the cave as it actually exists or added his own inventions, the same questions remain—whether we are investigating the poet’s intent or the intent of those who originally dedicated the cave. For the ancients never established temples without symbolic myths <note>original: "fabulous symbols"</note>, nor—

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that Homer’s description of the Cave of the Nymphs is not a mere literary invention but a symbolic representation of ancient theological truths regarding the nature of the physical universe.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

...does Homer rashly narrate the details pertaining to these matters. But the more one tries to show that this description of the cave is not a fiction created by Homer, but was dedicated to the Gods long before his time, the more this sacred cave will be found to be full of ancient wisdom. For this reason, it deserves to be investigated, and its symbolic dedication must be fully revealed.

2. The ancients, indeed, quite appropriately dedicated a cave to the world, whether considered as a whole or in its individual parts. Thus, they viewed the earth as a symbol of the matter from which the world is made; because of this, some believed that matter and earth are the same thing, using the cave to represent the world that was generated from matter. Generally, caves are natural formations that grow out of the earth, enclosed by a single mass of stone; their interior is hollow, while their exterior extends over a vast stretch of land. Since the world was produced spontaneously—<insert>meaning it was created by an internal rather than an external cause</insert>—and since it holds itself together, it is closely related to matter. In a hidden sense, matter is called "stone" and "rock" because of its sluggish nature and its resistance to taking on a specific form; at the same time, the ancients asserted that matter is infinite because it lacks a form of its own. However, because matter is constantly in flux and lacks its own inherent shape, it relies on external forms to gain *morphe* <gloss>morphe <note>original: "δι' ου μορφονται" (through which things are formed). Simplicius (a 6th-century philosopher) explains that *morphe* refers to the color, shape, and size of a surface.</note></gloss> and become visible. Therefore, the ancients considered flowing water, darkness, or—as the poet puts it—the "obscurity" of the cavern to be fitting symbols for the contents of the physical world, because of the matter with which it is connected. Through matter,

<summary>Porphyry explains that Homer’s cave is an ancient symbol for the material world, where the stony, hollow interior represents the nature of matter and its resistance to taking on form.</summary>
<keywords>Homer, Porphyry, Cave of the Nymphs, matter, symbolism, morphe, Simplicius, cosmology, allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that the world is symbolically represented as a cave. It transitions from a general philosophical description of the cave's nature to historical examples of cave-worship, specifically focusing on the Persian mysteries of Mithra and the role of Zoroaster.</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

Therefore, the world is obscure and dark; but through its connecting power and the orderly distribution of form—from which it also receives the name *world* <note>The Greek word *cosmos* (world) also means "order" or "ornament."</note>—it is beautiful and delightful. Hence, it may very properly be called a cave; it is lovely to one who first enters it because it participates in these forms, but it is obscure to one who surveys its foundation and examines it with the eye of the intellect. Thus, its exterior and superficial parts are pleasant, but its interior and deep parts are obscure, [and its very bottom is darkness itself]. 

In this way, the Persians also symbolically represent the descent of the soul into the sublunary <term>sublunary</term> <note>The region below the moon; in ancient cosmology, this was the realm of change, decay, and physical matter.</note> regions and its return from them by initiating the mystic—or the person admitted to the secret sacred rites—in a place they call a cavern. For, as Eubulus says, Zoroaster was the first to consecrate a natural cave in the nearby mountains of Persia. This cave was lush with flowers and contained fountains, and he dedicated it in honor of Mithra, the maker and father of all things. According to Zoroaster, this cave represented the world that Mithra had fabricated. Furthermore, the things contained within the cavern were arranged at proportional intervals to serve as symbols of the elements and climates of the world.

3. After Zoroaster, it became common for others to perform the rites of the mysteries in caves and dens, whether they occurred naturally or were man-made. For just as they established temples, groves, and altars to the celestial Gods, but only altars to the terrestrial Gods and heroes, and pits and cells to the subterranean divinities—so they dedicated caves and dens to the world. They did the same for the Nymphs, because of the water that trickles or flows through caverns, over which the Naiades <term>Naiades</term> <note>Water nymphs who presided over fountains, wells, and brooks.</note> preside, as we shall shortly observe.

---

<margin>
<sup>d</sup> "Nymphs," says Hermias in his Commentary <note>original: "Scholia"</note> on the *Phaedrus* of Plato, "are Goddesses who preside over regeneration and serve Bacchus, the offspring of Semele. For this reason they dwell near water; that is, they are concerned with generation <note>The process of souls entering into physical birth.</note>. This Bacchus provides for the regeneration of the entire sensory world."
</margin>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of the cave as a religious and philosophical symbol. preside. However, the ancients did not only use the cavern as a symbol of the world—or of a physical nature that is born and perceived by the senses—as we have said. They also used it as a symbol of all invisible powers; for just as caverns are obscure and dark, the essence of these powers is hidden <note>original: "occult"</note>. 

Therefore, Saturn <note>The Roman name for the Titan Cronus, who ruled during the Golden Age.</note> created a cavern in the ocean itself and concealed his children there. Similarly, Ceres <note>Demeter, the goddess of agriculture.</note> raised Proserpine <note>Persephone, daughter of Demeter and queen of the underworld.</note> with her nymphs in a cave. Many other examples of this kind can be found in the writings of the theologians <note>Porphyry refers to ancient poets like Orpheus or Hesiod as "theologians" because they wrote about the origins of the gods.</note>. It is clear that the ancients dedicated caves to nymphs, and especially to the Naiads <note>Water nymphs.</note> who live near fountains and take their name from the streams they oversee. This is shown in a hymn to Apollo, which says: 

"The nymphs living in caves shall draw out fountains of intellectual waters for you (according to the divine voice of the Muses), which are the offspring of an earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> spirit. From this, waters bursting through every river shall show to mankind a perpetual flow of sweet streams*." 

Because of this, it seems to me that the Pythagoreans, and Plato after them, demonstrated that the world is like a cavern or a den. For the powers that guide souls speak this way in a verse by Empedocles:

->Now at this secret cavern we've arrived.<-

In the seventh book of the *Republic*, Plato says: "See men as if they are living in an underground cavern, a dwelling like a den, with an entrance open to the light that stretches across the whole cave." When the other person in the dialogue says, "You present an unusual and wonderful comparison <note>original: "similitude"</note>," Socrates replies: "My friend Glauco, this whole image must be applied to what was said before. We must compare <note>original: "assimilating"</note> this place—which we see with our eyes—to the habitation of a prison, and the light of the fire within it to the power of the sun."

---

* These lines are not found in any of the hymns currently known that are attributed to Homer.

<summary>Porphyry explains how ancient mythology and philosophers like Plato and Empedocles used the cave as a symbol for the physical world and the hidden nature of divine powers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of the cave as a philosophical symbol, specifically distinguishing between the "sensible" (physical) world and the "intelligible" (spiritual) world. He explains why the cave described by Homer is a representation of the material world due to its connection with water and the "Naiades" (water nymphs).</meta>

<page-num>177</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

4. It is therefore clear from this that ancient theologians considered caverns to be symbols of the world and of earthly <note>original: "mundane"</note> powers. We have already noted that they also viewed a cave as a symbol of the spiritual <note>original: "intelligible"</note> essence, though they were led to this conclusion by different reasoning. 

They believed a cave symbolizes the physical <note>original: "sensible"</note> world because caverns are dark, stony, and damp. They claimed the world is like this because of the matter of which it consists, and because of its resistant and ever-changing <note>original: "flowing"</note> nature. However, they also thought it symbolized the spiritual world because that realm is invisible to the senses and possesses a firm, stable essence. In the same way, individual <note>original: "partial"</note> powers are hidden, especially those that exist within matter. They created these symbols by observing the natural formation of caves—their dark, nighttime, and stony character—and not, as some suspect, simply by looking at the shape of a cavern. For not every cave is spherical, as is evident from this cave in Homer with its two entrances. 

But since a cavern has a twofold meaning, this specific cave must be understood as an image of the physical world rather than the spiritual realm. Because it contains streams of water that flow perpetually, it represents a material existence <note>original: "essence"</note> rather than a spiritual state <note>original: "intelligible hypostasis"</note>. For this reason, it is dedicated to nymphs—not mountain nymphs or *rural*$^f$ nymphs, but specifically to the Naiads, who are named after streams of water. We specifically call the powers that preside over waters "Naiads," and this term is also commonly applied to all souls descending into physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note>. 

The ancients believed that these souls rest upon water that is inspired by divinity, as Numenius <note>A 2nd-century philosopher who influenced Neoplatonism.</note> says. He adds that, for this reason, a prophet asserts that "the Spirit of God moved on the waters." The

<summary>Porphyry explains that while caves can represent either the spiritual or physical realms, Homer’s cave represents the physical world because of its dark, watery nature. He links water nymphs (Naiads) to the descent of souls into physical life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that moisture is the primary characteristic of the material world and the process of incarnation. He links Egyptian mythology, Heraclitean philosophy, and Stoic physics to show why souls entering physical bodies are associated with water and dampness.</meta>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

For this reason, the Egyptians also represent all spirits <note>original: "dæmons"</note>, as well as the sun and all the planets <gloss>g</gloss>, not standing on anything solid, but on a sailing vessel. This is because souls descending into physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note> fly toward moisture. Because of this, Heraclitus says, "that moisture appears delightful and not deadly to souls," though the fall into physical life is a delight to them. Elsewhere, speaking of souls without bodies, he says, "We live their death, and we die their life." Therefore, the poet <note>likely Homer</note> calls those in physical life *humid* <note>moist</note>, because they have souls that are *profoundly* soaked in moisture. On this account, such souls delight in blood and moist seed; similarly, water is the nourishment for the souls of plants. Some also believe that the bodies in the air and in the heavens are nourished by vapors from springs and rivers, and other such evaporations. The Stoics, meanwhile, assert that the sun is nourished by evaporation from the sea, the moon by the vapors of springs and rivers, and the stars by the evaporation of the earth. According to them, the sun is an intellectual structure formed from the sea, the moon from river waters, and the stars from earthly vapors.

5. It is necessary, therefore, that souls—whether they are physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> or non-physical—when they attract a body to themselves, should lean toward humidity. This is especially true for those about to be bound to blood and moist bodies; they take on a physical form <note>original: "be corporalized"</note> as a result of being drenched in moisture. This is why the souls of the dead are summoned <note>original: "evocated"</note> by the pouring out of bile and blood; and souls...

However, after the words "and indeed all," it seems necessary to insert "the planets." For Martianus Capella, in book 2 of *On the Marriage of Philology*, says of the sun: "There he catches sight of a certain ship, steering the courses of all nature with diverse desires, filled with a mass of flames and surrounded by blessed goods. <meta>This page continues Porphyry’s Neoplatonic interpretation of Homer’s Cave of the Nymphs. It explains how souls entering the physical world attract moisture, turning their "spirit-vehicle" into a visible, cloud-like form, and why the dark, watery cave is a perfect symbol for both the human soul and the physical universe.</meta>

<page-num>179</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

...who love the body, by attracting a moist spirit, condense this humid vehicle <note>original: "pneumatic vehicle," the subtle body or "breath" believed to carry the soul</note> like a cloud. For moisture condensed in the air constitutes a cloud. But as the spirit-vehicle is condensed within these souls, it becomes visible through an excess of moisture. Among these, we must include those ghostly apparitions <note>original: "apparitions of images"</note> which appear to people, formed from a spirit colored by the power of imagination. 

However, pure souls are averse to physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note>; so that, as Heraclitus says, "*a dry soul is the wisest.*" Therefore, in this state, the spirit becomes moist and more watery through the desire for sexual union <note>original: "coition"</note>, as the soul attracts a humid vapor by leaning toward physical birth. Souls, therefore, that are entering physical birth are the Nymphs called Naiades. For this reason, it is customary to call brides "nymphs" <note>original: "married Nymphs"</note>, as they are joined to the process of birth; it is also why water is poured into baths from fountains, rivers, or eternal streams.

6. This world, then, is sacred and pleasant to souls who have now entered into nature, and to the guardian spirits of birth <note>original: "natal dæmons"</note>, even though it is essentially dark and obscure—or "periodic" <note>original Greek: περιοδικὸς, *periodikos*, referring to the cycles of time or the world’s rotation</note>. Because of this, some have suspected that souls are also of an obscure nature—or "airy" <note>original Greek: ἀερωδεας, *aerōdeas*</note>—and essentially consist of air. Therefore, a cavern—which is both pleasant and dark—is an appropriate symbol to be dedicated to souls on earth, matching its likeness to the world itself. In this world, as if in the greatest of all temples, souls reside. 

Similarly, a cavern with perpetually flowing streams is well-suited to the Nymphs who preside over waters. Let this cavern, therefore, be dedicated to souls; and, among the more specific powers, to the nymphs who preside over streams and fountains, and who are for this reason called "fontal" <note>spring-dwelling</note> and "Naiades." What, then, are the different symbols—some suited to souls, others to aquatic powers—that allow us to understand that this cavern is dedicated to both in common? Let the stone bowls, then, and the jars <note>original: "amphoræ"</note> be symbols of the water-dwelling Nymphs. For these are indeed the symbols of Bacchus, but their composition is

...made of clay <note>original: "fictile," meaning made of earth or ceramic</note>, that is, consists of baked earth. These are friendly to the vine, the gift of the God <note>Dionysus/Bacchus</note>, since the fruit of the vine is brought to a proper maturity by the celestial fire of the sun. But the stony bowls and jars <note>original: "amphoræ"</note> are in the most eminent degree adapted to the Nymphs who preside over the water that flows from rocks. 

And for souls that descend into physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note> and are occupied in physical activities <note>original: "corporeal energies"</note>, what symbol can be more appropriate than those instruments used for weaving? Hence, also, the poet <note>Homer</note> ventures to say "that on these the Nymphs weave purple webs, admirable to the view." For the formation of the flesh is on and about the bones, which in the bodies of animals resemble stones. Therefore, these instruments of weaving consist of stone and not of any other material. But the "purple webs" will evidently be the flesh which is woven from the blood. For purple woollen garments are tinted by blood <note>referring to the dye extracted from the Murex snail</note>, and wool is dyed from animal juice. The generation of flesh, also, is through and from blood. 

Add, too, that the body is a garment with which the soul is invested—a thing wonderful to the sight, whether this refers to the composition of the soul or contributes to the binding together <note>original: "colligation"</note> of the soul to the whole of a visible essence. Thus, also, Proserpine <note>Persephone</note>, who is the supervising guardian of everything produced from seed, is represented by Orpheus as weaving a web<sup>h</sup>; and the heavens are called by the ancients...

<meta>This page continues the symbolic analysis of Homer’s Cave of the Nymphs, moving from the symbolism of weaving to the significance of honey and bees in ancient mystery traditions.</meta>

...a veil, because it serves as the clothing or "vestment" of the celestial Gods.

7. Why, then, are the jars <note>original: "amphoræ"</note> described as being filled with honeycombs instead of water? For in these jars, Homer says the bees store their honey. This is clear from the Greek word *tithaibōssō* <note>original: "τιθαιβωσσειν"</note>, which means "to deposit food" <note>original: "τιθεναι την βοσιν"</note>. Since honey is the food of bees, theologians have used honey as a symbol for many different things because it possesses multiple properties; it is both cleansing <note>original: "cathartic"</note> and a preservative. For this reason, bodies are preserved from decaying by honey, and long-standing <note>original: "inveterate"</note> sores are purified by it. Furthermore, it is sweet to the taste and is collected from flowers by bees, which are believed to be "ox-begotten" <term>ox-begotten</term> <note>Ancient naturalists believed bees were spontaneously generated from the carcasses of oxen, a process called "bugonia."</note>. Therefore, when those who are initiated into the Leontic sacred rites <term>Leontic rites</term> <note>The "Lion" grade of the Mithraic mysteries.</note> pour honey instead of water on their hands, they are commanded by the initiator to keep their hands pure from everything that causes trouble <note>original: "molestation"</note> and from everything harmful and offensive. Other initiators into these same mysteries use fire, which is naturally cleansing, as a proper means of purification. They also use honey to purify the tongue from the defilement of evil. The Persians, however, when they offer honey to the guardian of fruits, consider it a symbol of a preserving and defending power. Hence some per-<meta>catchword: per-</meta>

***

"...mortal and immortal natures; in a way, this reminds us that adding mortal species <note>original: "genera"</note> completes the 'woven' life of the universe. It also triggers our memory of the divine Orphic myth and provides us with the underlying causes for the unfinished tapestries of Proserpine <note>Persephone</note>." — See volume 2, page 356, of my translation of Proclus's *Commentary on the Timaeus*.

The *unfinished tapestries of Proserpine* are also mentioned by the poet Claudian in his poem **On the Rape of Proserpine**, in the following verse:

->She felt the presence of the Goddesses, and abandoned her unfinished work.<-
-><note>original: "Sensit adesse Deas, imperfectumque laborem Deserit."</note><-

I will only add that ancient theologians considered the weaver's shuttle to be a symbol of *separation*, the cup a symbol of *giving life* <note>original: "vivific"</note>, the scepter a symbol of *ruling*, and the key a symbol of *guarding* power.
