<meta>This page continues the discussion on the symbolic meaning of honey, nectar, and ambrosia in Greek mythology and Neoplatonic philosophy, specifically focusing on their roles as symbols for divine providence and the preservation of the soul.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.

...Some persons have thought that the nectar and ambrosia¹, which the poet [Homer] pours into the nostrils of the dead for the purpose of preventing putrefaction, is actually honey; since honey is the food of the Gods. On this account, also, the same poet somewhere calls nectar *erythron* <note>original: "ερυθρον"</note>; for such is the color of honey [that is, it is a deep yellow]. But whether or not honey is to be taken for nectar, we shall examine more accurately elsewhere. In Orpheus, likewise, Saturn <note>The Titan Cronus</note> is ensnared by Jupiter <note>Zeus</note> through honey. For Saturn, being filled with honey, is intoxicated; his senses are darkened as if from the effects of wine, and he sleeps. This is just as Porus, in the *Banquet* <note>The *Symposium*</note> of Plato, is filled with nectar; for wine was not (says he) yet known. The Goddess Night...

¹ The theological meaning of nectar and ambrosia is beautifully unfolded by Hermias in his commentaries <note>original: "Scholia"</note> on the *Phaedrus* of Plato, published by Ast, Leipzig, 1810, p. 145. There he informs us "that *ambrosia* is analogous to dry nourishment, and that, on this account, it signifies a stable foundation in primary causes; but that *nectar* is analogous to moist food, and that it signifies the providential attention of the Gods to secondary natures [lower forms of existence]. The former is named based on a *deprivation of the mortal and corruptible* <note>original: "κατα στερησιν του βροτου και φθαρτου"</note>; but the latter is named based on a *deprivation of the deadened division and the tomb* <note>original: "κατα στερησιν του νεκρου μερισμου και του ταφου"</note>." And when the Gods are represented as acting through providence, they are said to drink nectar. Thus Homer writes in the beginning of the 4th book of the *Iliad*:

> Now with each other, on the golden floor
> Seated near Jove, the Gods converse; to whom
> The venerable Hebe nectar bears,
> In golden goblets; and as these flow round,
> The immortals turn their careful eyes on Troy.
> <note>original: Greek text of Iliad 4.1-4</note>

For then they providentially attend to the Trojans. The possession, therefore, of immutable providence by the Gods is signified by their drinking nectar; the exertion of this providence is signified by their beholding Troy; and their communicating with each other in providential actions is signified by their receiving the goblets from each other.

<summary>Porphyry discusses the symbolic equivalence of honey, nectar, and ambrosia in ancient myth, explaining how these substances represent divine providence and the preservation of the soul from corruption.</summary>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the symbolic meaning of honey in Orphic theology, specifically its role in the myth of Saturn’s (Cronus) intoxication and castration by Jupiter (Zeus), illustrating how divine powers descend into the realm of physical generation.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.

Night, too, in the writings of Orpheus, advises Jupiter <note>Zeus</note> to make use of honey as a trap. For she says to him:

> "When you see Saturn <note>Cronus</note> stretched beneath the lofty oaks,
> drunk with the honey produced by the bees,
> and sunk in intoxication <note>original: "ebriety"</note>¹, bind the God tightly."

This, therefore, occurs; and Saturn, having been bound, is castrated in the same manner as Heaven <note>Ouranos</note>. By this, the theologian [Orpheus] obscurely suggests that divine natures become "bound" through pleasure and are drawn down into the realms of generation <term>the cycle of physical birth and becoming</term>. He also suggests that when they are dissolved in pleasure, they release certain seminal powers. For this reason, Saturn castrates Heaven when he descends to earth out of a desire for sexual intercourse². However, among theologians, the sweetness of honey signifies the same thing as the pleasure arising from sexual union, by which Saturn was ensnared and castrated. For Saturn and his planetary sphere are the first of the celestial bodies that move in a direction contrary to the course of the Heavens <note>original: "Cœlum"</note>. Nevertheless, certain powers descend both from the Heavens (the sphere of the fixed stars) and from the planets. But Saturn receives the powers of Heaven...

---

¹ <note>Intoxication, when attributed to divine natures by ancient theologians, signifies a divine "superessential" energy—an energy superior to the intellect. Therefore, when Orpheus says that Saturn was intoxicated with honey or nectar, the meaning is that he was then acting providentially in a divine and super-intellectual manner.</note>

² <note>Porphyry, though he excelled in philosophical knowledge, was deficient in theological knowledge; his comments here on the castrations of Saturn and Heaven are a clear example of this. Ancient theologians used "preternatural" or "unnatural" stories to symbolize the transcendent nature of the Gods. They used "irrational" elements to represent a power more divine than all reason, and "base" or "shameful" acts to represent incorporeal beauty. In the myths Porphyry mentions here, the reproductive organs must be understood as symbols of "prolific power" (the power to create or bring forth). The "castration" of these parts signifies the movement or progression of this power from a higher level of existence into a lower one. Thus, the myth means that the creative powers of Saturn are called forth into action by Jupiter, and those of Heaven by Saturn—Jupiter being lower than Saturn, and Saturn being lower than Heaven. See the *Apology for the Fables of Homer* in volume 1 of my translation of Plato.</note>

<summary>This page explores the Orphic myth of Saturn being intoxicated by honey and castrated by Jupiter, interpreting honey as a symbol for the pleasure that draws divine powers into the physical world of "generation." The translator provides a Neoplatonic correction, arguing that these myths symbolize the orderly progression of creative power from higher to lower divine spheres.</summary>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of honey as a symbol in ancient theology, moving from its role in the myths of Saturn and Jupiter to its association with aquatic Nymphs, the moon, and the journey of the soul.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.

<margin>Jupiter</margin> And Jupiter <note>Zeus</note> receives the powers of Saturn <note>Cronus</note>. Since honey is used in purification rituals and as an antidote to decay <note>original: "putrefaction"</note>, and because it represents the pleasure that draws souls down toward physical birth <term>generation</term>, it is a symbol well-suited to the water Nymphs. This is because of the pure, rot-resistant nature of the waters they oversee, their power to cleanse, and their role in the process of birth—for water is essential to the creation of life. 

For this reason, the poet <note>Homer</note> says that the bees deposit their honey in bowls and amphorae <note>two-handled jars</note>. The bowls symbolize fountains; in a similar way, a bowl is placed near Mithra <note>a Persian sun god whose mysteries were popular in the Roman Empire</note> to represent a fountain. The amphorae, meanwhile, represent the vessels we use to draw water from those fountains. Fountains and streams are naturally associated with water Nymphs, and even more so with those "Nymphs" that are actually souls. The ancients specifically called souls "bees," seeing them as the creators of sweetness. Therefore, Sophocles is quite accurate when he says of souls:

> In wandering swarms, from the dead,
> A humming sound is heard.

8. The priestesses of Ceres <note>Demeter, goddess of the harvest</note> were also called "bees" by the ancients, as they were initiated into the mysteries of the earthly Goddess. Proserpine <note>Persephone</note> herself was called "the honied one." The moon, which presides over the process of birth, was also called a "bee" and a "bull." Indeed, Taurus <note>the Bull</note> is the astrological "exaltation" or peak of the moon’s power. 

There was an ancient belief that bees were "ox-begotten" <note>the ancient myth of *bugonia*, where bees were thought to be born from the carcasses of oxen</note>. This name is also given to souls as they enter the cycle of physical birth. Furthermore, the God who is secretly connected to the process of birth is called a "stealer of oxen." 

To this we may add that honey is considered a symbol of death—which is why it is customary to offer honey-based drinks to the gods of the underworld <note>original: "terrestrial Gods"</note>. In contrast, gall <note>bile</note> is considered a symbol of life. This might be a hidden way of saying that the life of the soul "dies" through the pursuit of pleasure, but the soul regains its true life through bitterness or hardship. This is why bile is sacrificed to the Gods. Alternatively, it may be because death frees the soul from all trouble <note>original: "molestation"</note>, while the present life...

<summary>Porphyry explains the multifaceted symbolism of honey and bees in ancient Greek and Mithraic mysteries, linking them to the soul's descent into physical life, purification, and the moon.</summary>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of honey and bees as symbols of the soul. It explains why souls entering the physical world are compared to bees and why certain plants, like beans, were used as symbols for the nature of reincarnation.</meta>

<page-num>185</page-num>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.

The present life is laborious and bitter. All souls, however, entering into the cycle of physical birth <term>generation</term>, are not simply called bees. That name is reserved for those who will live justly in this world and who, after performing actions acceptable to the Gods, will return again to their kindred stars. For this insect loves to return to the place where it first originated and is remarkably just and sober. This is why offerings <note>original: "libations"</note> made with honey are called "sober." 

Bees also do not land on beans, which the ancients considered a symbol of physical birth <term>generation</term> proceeding in a straight line without bending. This is because this legume is almost the only seed-bearing plant whose stalk is hollow throughout, without any joints or knots^m. We must therefore conclude that honeycombs and bees are appropriate and common symbols of the water Nymphs, and of souls that are, in a sense, married to the moist and shifting nature of physical birth <term>generation</term>.

9. Therefore, in the most ancient times, caves were dedicated to the Gods long before temples were built for them. Because of this, the <term>Curetes</term> <note>mythological attendants who protected the infant Zeus</note> in Crete dedicated a cavern to Jupiter <note>Zeus</note>; in Arcadia, a cave was sacred to the Moon and to Lycean <term>Pan</term>; and in Naxus, one was dedicated to Bacchus <note>Dionysus</note>. Wherever <term>Mithra</term> <note>a Persian sun god whose mysteries were popular in the Roman Empire</note> was known, people worshiped the God in a cavern. 

Regarding this cave in Ithaca <term>Ithacensian</term>, <term>Homer</term> was not content to say it had two gates; he adds that one gate faced the north, while the other—which was more divine—faced the south. He also says that the northern gate allows for descent, but he does not specify whether this is also true for the southern gate. Of that gate, he only says: “It is inaccessible to men, but it is the path of the immortals.”

10. It remains, therefore, to investigate what is indicated—

^m Therefore, when <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>the famous Greek philosopher and mathematician</note> urged his followers to abstain from beans, he meant to warn them to be careful of a continuous and unending descent into the realms of physical birth <term>generation</term>.

Since, however, a cavern is an image and symbol of the world, as Numenius and his close associate <note>original: "familiar"</note> Cronius assert, there are two extremities in the heavens: namely, the winter tropic <note>the winter solstice</note>, than which nothing is more southern, and the summer tropic <note>the summer solstice</note>, than which nothing is more northern. The summer tropic is in Cancer, and the winter tropic in Capricorn. And since Cancer is nearest to us, it is very properly attributed to the Moon, which is the nearest of all the heavenly bodies to the earth. But as the southern pole, by its great distance, is invisible to us, Capricorn is attributed to Saturn, the highest and most remote of all the planets. 

Again, the signs from Cancer to Capricorn are situated in the following order: the first of these is Leo, which is the house of the Sun; afterwards Virgo, which is the house of Mercury; Libra, the house of Venus; Scorpio, of Mars; Sagittarius, of Jupiter; and Capricorn, of Saturn. But from Capricorn in an inverse order, Aquarius is attributed to Saturn; Pisces, to Jupiter; Aries, to Mars; Taurus, to Venus; Gemini, to Mercury; and, in the last place, Cancer to the Moon.

11. Theologians <note>original: "theologists"</note> therefore assert that these two gates are Cancer and Capricorn, but Plato calls them "entrances." Of these, theologians say that Cancer is the gate through which souls descend, but Capricorn is the gate through which they ascend. Cancer is indeed northern and adapted to descent, but Capricorn is southern and adapted to ascent<sup>n</sup>. The northern parts, like—

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>
<page-num>187</page-num>

likewise pertain to souls descending into generation. And the gates of the cavern which are turned to the north are those where the soul's journey begins from the Milky Way <note>original: "galaxy"</note>, as they start to fall into earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> bodies.

On this account, since those who are about to descend are still in the sign of *Cancer* and have not yet left the Milky Way, they are still ranked among the order of the Gods. However, when they fall and arrive at the sign of the *Lion* (Leo), they enter the beginning <note>original: "exordium"</note> of their future condition in the flesh. In the *Lion*, the first stages of birth and the primary experiences of human nature begin. Because the sign of *Aquarius* is opposite to the *Lion*—setting just after the *Lion* rises—funeral rites are performed for departed souls when the sun is in *Aquarius*. This is because the sun is then moving through a sign that is contrary or adverse to human life.

From the point where the zodiac and the Milky Way touch each other, the soul descends. It moves from a round figure—which is the only divine form—and is pulled out into the shape of a cone by its downward flow. Just as a line is generated from a single point and stretches into length from something indivisible, so the soul passes from its own point (the monad <note>the "monad" refers to a single, indivisible unit or unity</note>) into the duad <note>the "duad" refers to the number two, signifying the beginning of division and multiplicity</note>, which is its first extension. This is the essence that Plato, in the *Timaeus*, calls both "indivisible" <note>original: "impartible"</note> and at the same time "divisible" <note>original: "partible"</note> when he speaks of the nature of the Soul of the World. For like the Soul of the World, the soul of man is indivisible if we consider the simplicity of its divine nature; yet it is divisible if we consider how it is spread through the world, or in man’s case, through the members of the physical body.

> “As soon, therefore, as the soul gravitates toward a body in this first stage of her production, she begins to experience a 'material tumult'—that is, matter flowing into her essence. This is what Plato remarks in the *Phaedo*: that the soul is drawn into the body reeling as if from recent intoxication. By this, he signifies the new drink of matter's rushing flood, which makes the soul polluted and heavy, drawing it down into an earthly situation. The constellation of the Cup <note>original: "starry cup"; refers to the constellation Crater</note> placed between *Cancer* and the *Lion* is a symbol of this mystic truth. It signifies that descending souls first experience intoxication in that part of the heavens through the influx of matter. 
> 
> Therefore, oblivion <note>forgetfulness</note>—the companion of intoxication—begins to creep silently into the recesses of the soul. For if souls retained the memory of divine matters (which they were conscious of in the heavens) during their descent into bodies, there would be no disagreement among men about divinity. But indeed, all people drink of oblivion while descending, though some drink more and others less. On this account, though truth is not obvious to everyone on earth, everyone still forms opinions about it; this is because a **defect of memory is the origin of opinion**. But those who discover the most truth are those who have drunk the least of oblivion, because they easily remember what they had known before in the heavens.”

rightly said to be accessible <note>original: "pervious"</note> to the descent of men; but the southern gates are not the avenues of the Gods, but—

> "Therefore, as the soul falls with this initial weight from the zodiac and the Milky Way into each of the underlying spheres, it is not only clothed with the addition of a luminous body, but it also produces the specific motions it will carry out in each respective orb. Thus, in the sphere of Saturn, it acts <note>original: "energizes"</note> through the power of reasoning and intellect; in the sphere of Jupiter <note>original: "Jove"</note>, through the power of action <note>original: "practic power"</note>; in the orb of the Sun, through the nature of the senses and imagination; in the planet Venus, through the motion of desire; in the orb of Mercury, through the power of speaking and interpreting what it perceives; and finally, when it enters the lunar globe, through a plant-like or vegetative nature <note>referring to basic biological growth</note> and the power of acting upon the physical body. And this lunar sphere, just as it is the last among the divine orders, is the first in our earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> situation. For this body, being the sediment <note>original: "dregs"</note> of divine natures, is the first substance of animal life. And this is the difference between earthly and heavenly <note>original: "supernal"</note> bodies (by which I mean the heavens, the stars, and the higher elements): the heavenly bodies are called upward to be the seat of the soul and deserve immortality because of the very nature of their region and their imitation of the sublime. In contrast, the soul is drawn down to these earthly bodies and is therefore said to 'die' when it is enclosed in this fallen region and the seat of mortality. It should not cause any distress that we have so often mentioned the death of the soul, which we have already declared to be immortal. For the soul is not extinguished by its own proper death, but is only overwhelmed for a time. It does not lose the benefit of eternal existence by its temporary submersion. Once it deserves to be purified from the contagion of vice through its complete refinement from the body, it will be restored to the light of eternal life and will return to its original integrity and perfection."

The powers, however, of the planets, which cause the soul’s activities in the various planetary spheres, are more accurately described by Proclus on page 260 of his admirable *Commentary on the Timaeus*, as follows:

<note>original Greek: ει δε βουλει και οτι των αγαθων πλανητων Σεληνη μεν αιτια τοις θνητοις της φυσεως... των δε γνωστικων Κρονος, διηρηται γαρ παντα τα ειδη τα αλογα εις ταυτας</note>

But the Sun is the Creator <note>original: "Demiurgus"</note> of everything perceived by the senses, because he is the cause of both sight and visibility. --- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "gates" through which souls travel, moving from the general concept of these celestial openings to specific cultural examples in Roman and Egyptian traditions.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>189</page-num>
<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

...of souls ascending to the Gods. For this reason, the poet does not say that they are the avenues of the Gods, but of the "immortals." This title also applies to our own souls, which are essentially <note>original: *per se*</note> immortal. 

It is said that Parmenides mentions these two gates in his treatise *On the Nature of Things*, and that they are also known to the Romans and Egyptians. The Romans celebrate their festival of Saturnalia when the Sun is in Capricorn. During this festivity, slaves wear the shoes of free men, and all things are shared among them in common. By this ceremony, the lawgiver subtly suggests that through this gate of the heavens, those who are currently born as slaves will be liberated through the festival of Saturn and the astrological house attributed to Saturn (which is Capricorn), when they live again and return to the source <note>original: "fountain"</note> of life. 

Since the path from Capricorn is suited for ascent*, the Romans call that month in which the Sun turns from Capricorn toward the east and directs its course to the north *Januarius*, or *January*, from the word *janua*, meaning a gate. 

However, for the Egyptians, the beginning of the year is not Aquarius, as it is with the Romans, but Cancer. The star Sothis, which the Greeks call the Dog Star <note>Sirius</note>, is near Cancer. The rising of Sothis marks the new moon for them, representing the beginning <note>original: "principle"</note> of the world's creation <note>original: "generation"</note>. Because of this, the gates of the cavern described by Homer are not dedicated to the east and west, nor to the signs of the equinox (Aries and Libra). Instead, they are dedicated to the north and south—to those celestial signs which are the most southerly and the most northerly.

---

> ...imagination; for the Sun is the producing cause of the imaginative essence itself, since the senses and imagination are one. But Venus is the cause of our desires <note>original: "epithymetic appetites"</note>; and Mars causes the spirited or angry impulses <note>original: "irascible motions"</note> that are in accordance with nature. Jupiter is the common cause of all vital powers, while Saturn is the source of all intellectual <note>original: "gnostic"</note> powers. All irrational forms are divided among these planets.

---

* Where the original text says "descent" <note>original Greek: *katabasin*</note>, it seems clearly necessary to read "ascent" <note>original Greek: *anabasin*</note>. Porphyry stated previously that Capricorn is the gate through which souls ascend.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the symbolic orientation of the cave, moving from the specific celestial gates (Cancer and Capricorn) to the role of Mithra and the elemental power of the winds in the journey of the soul.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>190</page-num>
<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

...because this cave was sacred to souls and water nymphs. These locations are suited for souls descending into physical birth <term>original: "generation"; the Neoplatonic term for the cycle of material existence</term> and for those later separating themselves from it. For this reason, a place near the equinoctial circle <note>the celestial equator</note> was assigned to Mithra <note>the primary deity of the Mithraic Mysteries, often associated with the sun</note> as his appropriate seat. On this account, he carries the sword of Aries (the Ram), which is a sign associated with war <note>Aries is ruled by Mars</note>. He is likewise depicted riding the Bull, which is the astrological sign of Venus. Both Mithra and the Bull are considered the demiurge <term>the divine craftsman or creator of the material world</term> and the lord of physical birth<gloss>p</gloss>. He is positioned near the equinoctial circle, with the northern regions on his right hand and the southern regions on his left. The ancients also arranged the southern hemisphere toward the south because of its heat, while the northern hemisphere was directed toward the north due to the coldness of the north wind.

12. It was also very reasonable for the ancients to connect the winds with souls entering into physical birth and those again separating from it. They did this because, as some believe, souls attract a spirit and possess a spirit-like essence <note>original: "pneumatic essence," from the Greek *pneuma* (breath/spirit)</note>. The north wind is suited for souls falling into birth; for this reason, the northern blasts are said to refresh those who are dying and can scarcely draw their breath. In contrast, the southern gales dissolve life. Because the north wind is intensely cold, it congeals [as it were, the animal life] and holds it fast in the coldness of earthly birth. But the south wind, being hot, dissolves this life and sends the soul upward to the heat of a divine nature. Since our earthly home is in the north, it is natural that souls born here should be familiar with the north wind, while those who exchange this life for a better one should be familiar with the south wind. This is also why the north wind is powerful at its beginning, while the south wind...

---

<gloss>p</gloss> Hence Phanes, or the First-Born <note>original: "Protogonus"</note>, who is the model of the universe and was absorbed by Jupiter, the Demiurge, is represented by Orpheus as having the head of a bull among the various other heads that adorn him. In the Orphic hymn dedicated to him, he is called "bull-roarer."

<summary>Porphyry explains the symbolic role of Mithra as a creator deity positioned between the celestial poles and discusses how the north and south winds represent the "congealing" of souls into physical bodies and their "dissolution" back into the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Mithra, generation, Demiurge, Phanes, Orpheus, north wind, south wind, pneumatic essence, equinoctial circle, astrology</keywords>

wind at its termination. For the north wind is situated directly over the inhabitants of the northern part of the globe, while the south wind is at a great distance from them. A blast from very remote places is slower than one from nearby. However, when the wind is gathered together <note>original: "coacervated"</note>, it blows abundantly and with vigor. Because souls enter into physical birth <term>original: "generation"; the Neoplatonic term for the cycle of material existence</term> through the northern gate, this wind is described as "amatory" <note>motivated by love or desire, attracting the soul to the body</note>. For, as the poet says:

> Boreas, enamored of the sprightly group,
> Concealed his godhead in a flowing mane.
> With a disguised voice, he neighed to his loves,
> And chased the dappled beauties across the meadow:
> From this union twelve others were born, unrivaled in kind,
> As swift as their mother mares and their father, the wind <gloss>q</gloss>.

It is also said that Boreas abducted <note>original: "ravished"; in Greek mythology, this refers to the forceful carrying away of a person</note> Orithya <gloss>r</gloss> from—

---

<gloss>q</gloss> Homer, *Iliad*, book 20, line 223, etc.

<gloss>r</gloss> This fable is mentioned by Plato in the *Phaedrus*, and is beautifully explained as follows by Hermias in his Scholia <note>original: "Scholia"; ancient line-by-line commentaries on a text</note> on that dialogue: "Two solutions can be given for this fable: one is historical and more ethical, while the other moves us from specific parts to universal wholes. The first is as follows: Orithya was the daughter of Erechtheus and the priestess of Boreas. Every wind has a presiding deity, which the <term>telestic art</term> (the ritual practice pertaining to sacred mysteries) religiously cultivates. The god was so favorable to Orithya that he sent the north wind to protect the country; additionally, he is said to have assisted the Athenians in their naval battles. Orithya, therefore, became 'enthusiastic' <note>filled with divine inspiration or possessed by a god</note> by being possessed by her specific god, Boreas. She no longer acted <note>original: "energizing"</note> as a human being—for living creatures cease to act according to their own nature when possessed by higher powers—and she died under this divine influence. Thus, she was said to have been abducted by Boreas. This is the more 'ethical' or human-centered explanation of the story.

"But the second explanation, which applies the story to universal principles without completely overturning the first, is as follows: divine myths often use human events and histories to serve the teaching of universal truths. It is said, then, that Erechtheus is the god who rules over the three elements: air, water, and earth. Sometimes, however, he is considered the ruler of the earth alone, or the presiding deity of Attica. Orithya is the daughter of this deity; she represents the fertile power of the Earth, which is spread out alongside the—"

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the myth of Boreas (the north wind) and Orithya, moving from the north wind's association with physical birth to the south wind's association with the divine and the "philosophical death" of the soul.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>192</page-num>
<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

by whom he fathered <note>original: "begot"</note> Zetis and Calais. But as the south is attributed to the Gods, when the Sun is at its noon position <note>original: "meridian"</note>, the curtains in temples are drawn before the statues of the Gods. This is done in observance of the Homeric rule <note>original: "precept"</note>, "that it is not lawful for men to enter temples when the Sun is inclined toward the south"; for this is the path of the immortals. Therefore, when the God is at his noon altitude, the ancients placed a symbol of midday and of the south at the gates of temples\*; for this reason, it was also forbidden to speak at all times in other gates as well, because gates were considered sacred. Thus, the Pythagoreans and the wise men among the Egyptians forbade speaking while passing through doors or gates; for at that moment they venerated in silence that God who is the source of all things <note>original: "principle of wholes"</note>.

13. Homer likewise knew that gates are sacred, as is—

---

The name *Erectheus* signifies an "unfolding." For it represents the productive <note>original: "prolific"</note> power of the Earth, flourishing and restored according to the seasons. But Boreas represents the providence of the Gods, illuminating lower <note>original: "secondary"</note> natures from above. The providence of the Gods in the world is symbolized by Boreas because this divinity blows from high places. The elevating power of the Gods is symbolized by the south wind, because this wind blows from low to high places; furthermore, *things situated toward the south are considered more divine*. Therefore, divine providence causes the productive power of the Earth (or of the Athenian land) to rise and become visible.

> "Orithya may also be said to be a soul aspiring after things above, derived from *oroun* <note>to stir or rush</note> and *theio* <note>to run</note>, according to the Athenian custom of adding a letter at the end of a word, which in this case is an 'a.' Such a soul, therefore, is carried away <note>original: "ravished"</note> by Boreas blowing from above. But if Orithya was hurled from a cliff, this is also appropriate; for such a soul dies a philosophical death rather than a physical one, and abandons a life based on her own deliberate choices even while she lives a physical life. And philosophy, according to Socrates in the *Phaedo*, is nothing other than a preparation <note>original: "meditation"</note> for death."

\* In the original Greek, it says: "they would place a symbol of midday and of the south at the door, when the god [the sun] reaches his noon height" <note>original Greek: "ιστασαν αν και συμβολον της μεσημβριας και του νοτου, επι τη θυρη, μεσημβριαζοντος του θεου"</note>. Lucas Holstenius translates this very incorrectly as follows: "They therefore establish the south wind as a symbol of midday; since the god hangs over the doorway at the noon hour" <note>original Latin: "Austrum igitur meridiei symbolum statuunt; cum deus meridiano tempore ostio immineat."</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sacred gates, specifically focusing on the gates of the heavens guarded by the Hours and the "gates of the Sun" (the zodiac signs Cancer and Capricorn), which relate to the soul's journey into physical existence.</meta>

<page-num>193</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<sig>O</sig>

It is evident from his portrayal of Oeneus, who, when offering a prayer <note>original: "supplicating"</note>, shakes the gate:

->He shakes the gates and prays to his son <sup>u</sup>.<-

Homer also knew of the gates of the heavens, which are entrusted to the guardianship of the Hours. These gates originate in cloudy places and are opened and shut by the clouds. For he says:

->Whether they close the dense clouds, or unfold them wide <sup>x</sup>.<-

For this reason, these gates make a roaring <note>original: "bellowing"</note> sound, because thunder echoes through the clouds:

->Heaven’s gates open spontaneously to the powers;<-
->Heaven’s roaring portals, guarded by the Hours <sup>y</sup>.<-

Elsewhere, he likewise speaks of the "gates of the Sun," referring to the zodiac signs of Cancer and Capricorn; for the Sun travels as far as these signs when it descends from the north to the south, and from there ascends again to the northern regions. Capricorn and Cancer are located along the Milky Way <note>original: "galaxy"</note>, positioned at the furthest points of this circle; Cancer at the northern end and Capricorn at the southern end. 

According to Pythagoras, the *people of dreams* <sup>z</sup> are the souls who are said to be gathered in the Milky Way. This circle is named for the milk used to nourish souls when they fall into physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note>. For this reason, those who call forth <note>original: "evocate"</note> the souls of the dead offer them a drink-offering <note>original: "libation"</note> of milk mixed with honey; they do this because souls are drawn toward birth through the allurements of sweetness, and milk is naturally produced at a person's birth. 

Furthermore, the southern regions produce small bodies, as it is common for heat to thin <note>original: "attenuate"</note> them to the greatest degree. In contrast, all bodies born in the north are large, as is—

---

<sup>u</sup> Iliad, book 11, verse 579.
<sup>x</sup> Iliad, book 8, verse 395.
<sup>y</sup> Iliad, book 8, verse 393.
<sup>z</sup> In the 24th book of the *Odyssey* (verse 11), Homer says that the souls of the suitors, in their descent to the region of spirits, passed beyond the *people of dreams*.

<summary>Porphyry interprets Homeric "gates" as symbols for the heavens and the zodiac signs of Cancer and Capricorn, which serve as entry and exit points for souls. He connects the Milky Way and the use of milk in rituals to the process of a soul entering physical birth.</summary>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's interpretation of Homeric symbolism, shifting from the specific gates of the Sun (Cancer and Capricorn) to the broader geographical and cosmological dualities of North/South and East/West as they relate to the journey of the soul.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>
<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

This is evident among the Celts, the Thracians, and the Scythians; these regions are humid and abound with pastures. For the word Boreas <note>The North Wind</note> is derived from *Bora* <note>original Greek: Βορᾶ</note>, which signifies nourishment. Hence, the wind which blows from a land abounding in nourishment is called Boreas <note>original Greek: Βορρέας</note>, as being of a nutritive nature. 

From these causes, therefore, the northern parts are adapted to the mortal tribe, and to souls that fall into the realms of physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note>. But the southern parts are adapted to that which is immortal <margin>Hence, the southern parts of the earth have always been more favorable to genius than the northern parts.</margin>, just as the eastern parts of the world are attributed to the Gods, but the western to daemons <note>original: dæmons; here referring to intermediate spirits</note>. 

For, because nature originates from diversity, the ancients everywhere made that which has a twofold entrance to be a symbol of the nature of things. This is because the progression of the soul is either through that which is intelligible <note>the realm of pure thought and spirit</note>, or through that which is sensible <note>the realm of the physical senses</note>. And if it is through that which is sensible, it is either through the sphere of the fixed stars, or through the sphere of the planets. And again, it is either through an immortal, or through a mortal progression. One center, likewise, is above, but the other beneath the earth; and one is eastern, but the other western. 

Thus, too, some parts of the world are situated on the left, but others on the right hand: and night is opposed to day. On this account, also, harmony consists of, and proceeds <margin>In the original Greek, the word is *toxeuei* (shoots/aims); but instead of it, I read *proiesi* (proceeds/sends forth).</margin> through contraries. Plato also says that there are two openings <margin>See my translation of the 10th book of his *Republic*.</margin>, one of which affords a passage to souls ascending to the heavens, but the other to souls descending to the earth. And, according to theologians, the Sun and Moon are the gates of souls, which ascend through the Sun, and descend through the Moon. With Homer, likewise, there are two jars <note>original: "tubs"; referencing the *pithoi* of Zeus</note>,

->From which he fills the lot of every one,<-
->Distributing blessings to some, and ills to others <margin>Iliad, book 24, verse 528.</margin>.<-

<summary>Porphyry explains the symbolic duality of the cosmos, associating the North with mortality and "generation" (physical birth) and the South with immortality. He references Plato's "two openings" and Homer's "two jars" to illustrate the soul's journey between the sensible and intelligible worlds.</summary>

<keywords>Boreas, North vs South, generation, immortality, daemons, Plato's Republic, Homer's Iliad, Sun and Moon gates, cosmological duality, the soul's journey</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of the soul's journey, transitioning from the geographical symbolism of North and South to the psychological symbolism of vessels (tubs) used by Plato. The main text continues a discussion on the duality of nature, while the extensive marginal notes provide a deep Neoplatonic commentary on a famous passage from Plato’s dialogue, the *Gorgias*.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>
<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

<margin>
* The passage in the *Gorgias* of Plato, to which Porphyry here alludes, is as follows: — 
> "Socrates: Indeed, as you also say, life is a heavy burden. I would not be surprised if Euripides was right when he said: 'Who knows whether living is actually dying, and dying is actually living?' And perhaps we are, in reality, dead. For I have heard from wise men that we are currently dead, and that the body is our tomb <note>original: sepulchre</note>. However, the part of the soul that contains our desires is of such a nature that it can be easily persuaded and tossed up and down. For this reason, a certain clever man—perhaps a Sicilian or an Italian—created a myth where he called this part of the soul a 'tub' <note>original: "a tub, by a derivation from the probable and the persuasive." In Greek, *pithos* (tub) sounds like *pithanos* (persuasive).</note>. Likewise, he called those who are foolish or lack intellect 'uninitiated.' He further said that the undisciplined and open nature of that part of the soul where desires live was like a leaky <note>original: pierced</note> tub, because its greed can never be satisfied."

What is said here by Plato is beautifully explained by Olympiodorus in his *Manuscript Commentary* on the *Gorgias*, as follows: — 
> "Euripides (in the play *Phryxus*) says that to live is to die, and to die is to live. When the soul comes into this world, just as she gives life to the body, she also experiences a loss <note>original: privation</note> of her own life through this connection; and this is an evil state. Therefore, when she is separated from the body, she truly lives. She 'dies' here because she participates in a loss of life, as the body becomes the source of all evils. For this reason, it is necessary to master the body.

> "The meaning of the Pythagorean myth <note>original: Pythagoric fable</note> introduced here by Plato is this: We are said to be dead because, as we observed before, we experience a loss of true life. The tomb we carry around with us is the body, as Plato himself explains. 'Hades' <note>the underworld</note> represents the 'unseen' <note>In Greek, *Aides* was often etymologically linked to *aeides*, meaning "unseen" or "obscure"</note>, because we are situated in darkness, the soul being enslaved to the body. The 'tubs' are our desires. They are called this either because we rush to fill them as if they were vessels, or because desire persuades us that something is beautiful. The 'initiated'—that is, those who have perfect knowledge—pour their efforts into a solid tub; they have their tub full, or in other words, they possess perfect virtue. But the 'uninitiated'—those who possess nothing perfect—have leaky <note>original: perforated</note> tubs. Those who are enslaved to desire always wish to fill it and become even more desperate; for this reason, they have leaky tubs that are never full. The 'sieve' <note>the tool used to carry water to the tubs</note> represents the rational soul when it is mixed with the irrational. The rational soul is called a circle because it seeks itself and is itself sought; it finds itself and is itself found. But the irrational soul imitates a straight line, since it does"
</margin>

However, in the *Gorgias*, Plato uses "tubs" to represent souls. Some of these are harmful <note>original: malefic</note>, while others are helpful <note>original: beneficent</note>; some are guided by reason <note>original: rational</note>, while others are guided by impulse <note>original: irrational</note>*.

<summary>This page discusses the Orphic and Pythagorean metaphor of the "tub" (vessel) from Plato's Gorgias, interpreting the body as a tomb and the desires of the soul as leaky vessels that can never be satisfied.</summary>
<keywords>Gorgias, Porphyry, Euripides, Olympiodorus, Neoplatonism, Pythagoreanism, Hades, allegory of the tub, soul and body, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page continues the interpretation of Plato’s vessel metaphors, specifically the "tubs" mentioned in the *Gorgias*, and links them to the dual gates of Homer’s Cave of the Nymphs and Hesiod's mythology.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>
<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

Souls, however, are like tubs, because they contain activities <note>original: energies</note> and dispositions <note>original: habits</note> within themselves, as if in a vessel. In Hesiod's writings, too, we find one tub closed, but the other opened by Pleasure, who scatters its contents everywhere, leaving only Hope behind. For in those instances where a corrupt soul, being scattered throughout matter, abandons the proper order of its own essence, it is accustomed to feed itself with the pleasing prospects of favorable hope.

14. Since, therefore, every two-fold entrance is a symbol of nature, this Homeric cave rightly has not just one portal, but two gates. These differ from each other according to the nature of the beings they serve: one belongs to the Gods and good spirits <gloss>demons <note>original: δαιμοσι</note></gloss>, while the other belongs to mortals and corrupt natures. For this reason, Plato took the opportunity to speak of "bowls" and uses "tubs" instead of "amphorae" <term>amphorae: ancient ceramic jars used for transport and storage</term>, and two openings—as we have already observed—instead of two gates. Pherecydes of Syros also mentions recesses and trenches, caverns, doors, and gates; through these, he obscurely indicates the descent <note>original: generations</note> of souls into bodies and their eventual separation from these material realms. And this concludes our explanation of the Homeric cave, which we believe we have sufficiently revealed without bringing in further evidence from ancient philosophers and theologians, which would unnecessarily lengthen our discussion.

15. One particular point, however, remains to be explained,

---

> ...does not return back to itself like a circle. Therefore, as far as the sieve is circular, it is an image of the rational soul; but, because it is placed beneath the straight lines formed by the holes, it represents the irrational soul. These straight lines are located in the middle of the cavities. Hence, by the "sieve," Plato signifies the rational soul in subjection to the irrational soul. But the "water" is the constant flow of nature: for, as Heraclitus says, *"moisture is the death of the soul."*

In this extract, the intelligent reader will easily perceive that the hidden <note>original: occult</note> meaning of the *tubs* is more systematically <note>original: scientifically</note> explained by Olympiodorus than by Porphyry.

<note>f In the original Greek: "and some indeed [gates] belonging to both the gods and the good." <note>original: και τας μεν, θεοις τε και τοις αγαθοις προσυκουσαϲ</note>. But after the word "good," I have no doubt we should insert "spirits" <note>original: δαιμοσι / daimones</note>.</note>

<summary>Porphyry concludes his explanation of the Cave of the Nymphs' two gates, interpreting them as paths for divine and mortal souls. He also provides a commentary by Olympiodorus that uses the metaphor of a sieve to distinguish between the rational and irrational parts of the soul.</summary>

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's symbolic analysis of Homer’s Cave of the Nymphs, moving from the gates of the cave to the significance of the olive tree planted at its summit.</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>
<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

...and that is the symbol of the olive tree planted at the top of the cavern. Homer appears to indicate something very remarkable <note>original: admirable</note> by giving it such a position, for he does not merely say that an olive grows in this place, but that it flourishes on the summit of the cavern:

> “High at the head a branching olive grows,
> Beneath, a gloomy grotto’s cool recess.”

The growth of the olive in such a location is not accidental <note>original: fortuitous</note>, as some might suspect, but contains the hidden meaning <note>original: enigma</note> of the cavern. Since the world was not produced randomly or by chance, but is the work of divine wisdom and an intellectual nature, an olive tree—the symbol of this wisdom—flourishes near this cavern, which is an image of the physical world. For the olive is the plant of Minerva <note>Minerva: The Roman name for the Greek goddess Athena, who represents wisdom.</note>, and Minerva is wisdom itself. Since this Goddess was produced from the head of Jupiter <note>Jupiter: The Roman name for Zeus, king of the gods.</note>, the theologian <note>original: theologist; here referring to the ancient poets or myth-makers who interpreted divine matters</note> discovered an appropriate place for the olive by consecrating it at the summit of the port. By this, he signifies that the universe is not the result of a random event or the work of irrational fortune, but is the offspring of an intellectual nature and divine wisdom. This wisdom is distinct from the universe in its essence, yet remains near to it by being established at the summit of the whole port—that is, governing the whole with perfect wisdom due to the excellence of its nature.

Since, however, an olive is evergreen <note>original: ever-flourishing</note>, it possesses a certain quality that is perfectly suited to the cycles <note>original: revolutions</note> of souls in the world; for this cave, as we have said, is sacred to such souls. In summer, the white undersides of the olive leaves tend to turn upward, but in winter, the whiter side of the leaves is bent downward. Because of this, men also extend olive branches during prayers and supplications, signaling <note>original: ominating</note> through this gesture that they shall exchange the sorrowful darkness of danger for the bright light of security and peace. The olive, therefore, being naturally evergreen, bears fruit that provides relief from labor <note>original: auxiliary of labour; likely referring to the use of olive oil for fuel in lamps or as a restorative ointment for the body</note> [by...

<summary>Porphyry interprets the olive tree at the summit of the Cave of the Nymphs as a symbol of divine wisdom (Athena/Minerva) presiding over the created world. He explains how the tree's physical characteristics, such as its evergreen leaves, represent the soul's transition from darkness to light.</summary>
<keywords>Homer, Porphyry, Minerva, Athena, Jupiter, Zeus, olive tree symbolism, divine wisdom, allegory, Cave of the Nymphs, soul's journey</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's allegorical interpretation of Homer’s "Cave of the Nymphs," focusing on the olive tree as a symbol of divine wisdom and the figure of Ulysses as a soul navigating the material world.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

[the olive is its reward]; it is also sacred to **Minerva**; it provides crowns for those who are victorious in physical struggles; and it offers a friendly branch to the humble petitioner <note>original: suppliant petitioner</note>. In the same way, the world is governed by an intellectual nature and is guided by a wisdom that is eternal and ever-flourishing. Through this wisdom, the rewards of victory are granted to those who conquer the struggles in the race of life as a reward for their intense labor and patient endurance. Furthermore, the **Demiurgus** <term>Demiurgus</term> <note>The "Divine Craftsman" or creator of the physical universe in Platonic philosophy.</note>, who binds and holds the world together in ways that cannot be described <note>original: in ineffable comprehensions</note>, gives strength to suffering and humble souls.

<margin>V</margin>

16. In this cave, therefore, says **Homer**, all external possessions must be set aside. Here, naked and dressed as a humble beggar <note>original: assuming a suppliant habit</note>, suffering in body, casting away everything unnecessary, and turning away from the distractions of the senses, it is necessary to sit at the foot of the olive tree. There, one must consult with **Minerva** <note>The goddess of wisdom, known as Athena to the Greeks.</note> to find the most effective way to destroy that hostile crowd of passions <note>original: rout of passions</note> which hide and wait in the secret corners of the soul. Indeed, it seems to me that it was for good reason that **Numenius** <note>A Neopythagorean philosopher who influenced later Neoplatonism.</note> and his followers believed the character of **Ulysses** <note>The Roman name for Odysseus, the hero of the Odyssey.</note> in the **Odyssey** represented a man who passes in an orderly way over the dark and stormy sea of generation <term>sea of generation</term> <note>A metaphor for the turbulent world of physical existence, birth, and death.</note>. He eventually reaches that place where storms and seas are unknown, and finds a nation:

> "Who never knew salt, nor heard the billows roar."

17. Again, according to **Plato**, the deep, the sea, and a tempest are images of the material world <note>original: material nature</note>. For this reason, I believe the poet named the port after **Phorcys**. For he says, "It is the port of the ancient sea-god **Phorcys**$^g$." Likewise, the daughter of this God is men—

---

$^g$ **Phorcys** is one among the group of nine <note>original: ennead</note> Gods who, according to **Plato** in the **Timaeus**, create the process of physical birth <note>original: fabricate generation</note>. Concerning this deity, **Proclus** <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher (412–485 AD).</note> observes, "that just as the **Jupiter** in this group of nine causes the hidden divisions and separations of forms created by **Saturn** to become visible, and as **Rhea** calls them forth into movement and birth; so **Phorcys** places them into matter, produces things that can be sensed <note>original: sensible natures</note>, and organizes the visible world."

<summary>Porphyry interprets the olive tree and the character of Ulysses as symbols of divine wisdom and the soul's journey through the material world. He explains that the sea represents the chaos of physical existence, which the soul must navigate to reach spiritual peace.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Homer, Odyssey, Minerva, Ulysses, Numenius, Demiurgus, Phorcys, Plato, allegory, Neoplatonism, sea of generation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's allegorical interpretation of Homer’s "Cave of the Nymphs," where Ulysses (Odysseus) represents the soul's journey away from the material world and toward spiritual wisdom.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.</header>

...mentioned in the beginning of the *Odyssey*. From **Thoosa**, the **Cyclops** was born, whom **Ulysses** <note>original: Ulysses; the Latin name for Odysseus.</note> deprived of sight. This deed of **Ulysses** became the occasion for him to be reminded of his errors until he was safely landed in his native country. For this reason, a seat under the olive tree is appropriate for **Ulysses**, as one who prays to the divine and seeks to appease his birth-spirit <term>natal dæmon</term> <note>original: natal dæmon; a personal guardian spirit or guiding force assigned to a soul at birth.</note> with the branch of a petitioner.

For it is not simply or easily possible for anyone to be liberated from this physical life <note>original: sensible life; life perceived through the senses.</note> by merely blinding this spirit and making its powers ineffective. Instead, whoever dares to do this will be pursued by the anger <gloss>h</gloss> of the sea-gods and material gods. These must first be appeased through sacrifices, labors, and patient endurance—at one time fighting against the passions, and at another using charms and clever deceptions, transforming oneself in every possible way. This is done so that, after finally stripping away the torn garments <note>The "torn garments" symbolize the physical body or the distractions of the material world that hide the soul's true nature.</note> by which his true person was concealed, he may reclaim the lost kingdom of his soul. 

Even then, he will not be free from labors. True liberation happens only when he has completely crossed the stormy sea of life and, while still living, becomes so distanced from material and worldly concerns <note>original: marine and material works</note>—due to his deep focus on spiritual realities <note>original: intelligible concerns</note>—that he mistakes an oar for a winnowing fan <term>corn-van</term> <note>original: corn-van; a shovel-like tool used to toss grain into the air so the wind can blow away the chaff.</note>.

18. However, one must not think that these interpretations are forced or are merely the guesses of clever men. When we consider the great wisdom of the ancient world, and how much **Homer** excelled in intellectual wisdom <note>original: prudence</note> and an accurate knowledge of every virtue, it cannot be denied that he has obscurely...

---

...essence, so that there may be divisions of creative principles <note>original: productive principles [or forms]</note> not only in nature and souls, and in the spiritual essences that exist before them, but also in physical things. *For this is the specific nature of divine creation <term>fabrication</term>.*"

<gloss>h</gloss> "The anger of the Gods," says **Proclus** <note>A Greek Neoplatonist philosopher (412–485 AD) who wrote extensively on the spiritual meaning of myths.</note>, "is not a sign of any emotion in them, but instead shows our own inability to receive their light <note>original: illuminations</note>."

<summary>Porphyry explains the blinding of the Cyclops and Ulysses' journey as an allegory for the soul's struggle to overcome material passions and return to its spiritual home. He emphasizes that true liberation requires both the defeat of worldly desires and a total refocusing on divine wisdom.</summary>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new treatise by Porphyry, moving from the allegorical interpretation of Homer to a foundational exploration of Neoplatonic metaphysics regarding the relationship between the physical and the spiritual.</meta>

<page-num>201</page-num>

# AIDS TO THE

### PERCEPTION OF

# SPIRITUAL REALITIES.
<note>original title: "Auxiliaries to the Perception of Intelligible Natures." In Neoplatonism, "intelligible" refers to things known by the mind/intellect rather than the senses.</note>

---

## SECTION I.

1. Every physical body exists in a specific place; however, nothing that is fundamentally non-physical <note>original: "essentially incorporeal"</note>, or anything of that nature, possesses a physical location.
2. Because non-physical things are superior to all bodies and locations, they are present everywhere—not by being spread across a distance <note>original: "not with interval"</note>, but by being present in their entirety in every place <note>original: "impartibly"</note>.
3. Non-physical things are not present in bodies by being in a specific spot. Instead, they are present with them when they choose to be, by inclining <note>original: "verging"</note> toward them as much as their nature allows. They are not, however, present in a spatial sense, but rather through their relationship <note>original: "habitude"</note>, proximity, and shared nature.
4. Non-physical things are not present within bodies by their actual substance <note>original: "hypostasis"</note> or essence, for they do not mix with physical matter. Instead, by inclining toward bodies, they grant them a certain power that is closely connected to physical existence. This inclination <note>original: "tendency"</note> creates a secondary power that acts upon the body.
5. The **Soul**, indeed, is a middle ground between an indivisible substance <note>original: "impartible essence"</note> and a substance that is divided among physical bodies. However, **Intellect** <note>Pure Mind</note> is solely an indivisible substance. On the other hand, qualities and material forms are divided and spread throughout bodies.
6. Not every thing <note>original: "Not every thing which acts on another, effects..." The sentence breaks off here at the end of the page.</note> that acts upon another does so by physical contact or touch.

<note>
In the original Greek, the text says: "Not that which acts upon another, does so by approach and touch..." <note>original: "Ου το ποιουν εις αλλο, πελασιν και αφην ποιει, α ποιει..."</note>. However, it is clear from the logic of the entire passage that it should read, "Not **every** thing that acts..."
</note>

<vocab>
**Incorporeal:** Non-physical or spiritual; having no body.
**Impartibly:** Without being divided; present as a whole rather than in parts.
**Habitude:** The state or relation of one thing to another.
**Hypostasis:** The fundamental underlying substance or individual reality of a thing.
**Intellect:** In this philosophy, the "Nous" or divine mind, which is higher than the human soul.
</vocab>

<summary>Porphyry begins a series of philosophical propositions explaining how non-physical realities like the Soul and Intellect interact with the physical world without being trapped in physical space.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, incorporeal, Soul, Intellect, metaphysics, substance, hypostasis, space and place</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Aids to the Perception of Spiritual Realities," focusing on the distinction between the soul and the body, the nature of death, and the different ways we perceive various levels of reality.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
<header>AIDS TO THE</header>

...that which it does effect by being near or in contact; however, those natures which affect anything through proximity and contact do so only accidentally.

7. The soul is bound to the body by turning toward physical desires <note>original: "corporeal passions"</note>; it is liberated again by becoming unaffected by the body.

8. What nature binds, nature also dissolves; and what the soul binds, the soul likewise dissolves. Nature, indeed, bound the body to the soul; but the soul binds herself to the body. Nature, therefore, releases the body from the soul; but the soul releases herself from the body.

9. Hence, there are two types of death: the first is universally known, in which the body is released from the soul; but the second is unique to philosophers, in which the soul is released from the body. One type of death does not necessarily follow the other. <note>Porphyry suggests that a philosopher can achieve a spiritual separation from the body's influence while still being physically alive.</note>

10. We do not understand all things in the same way, but in a manner suited to the essence of each. We understand intellectual objects through the intellect; but those things that pertain to the soul, we understand through reason. We perceive plants **spermatically** <term>spermatically: according to their generative or "seed-like" formative principles</term>; but we perceive bodies **idolically** <term>idolically: as images or reflections (from the Greek "eidolon")</term>. Finally, that which is above all these, we perceive in a way that transcends intellect and transcends existence <note>original: "super-intellectually and super-essentially"</note>.

---

<note>original note b: The Greek article "o" (the) is missing here in the original text before "eteros" (the other).</note>

<note>original note c: Knowledge exists according to the nature of the person who knows, rather than the nature of the thing being known. Therefore, knowledge is either superior to, equal to, or inferior to the object being studied. For example, the rational soul's knowledge of physical things is superior to those things; its knowledge of itself is equal to itself; but its knowledge of the divine is inferior to the divine. Porphyry is therefore not entirely correct in what he says here. This concept—that knowledge conforms to the knower rather than the known—actually originated from the divine Iamblichus, as we are told by Ammonius in his commentary on Aristotle's treatise *On Interpretation* <note>original: "De Interpretatione"</note>, and it was adopted by Proclus in his commentary on the *Parmenides*. Boethius likewise uses this reasoning in Book 5 of *The Consolation of Philosophy* regarding the foreknowledge of the divine. None of his commentators, however, have identified the source from which it was derived.</note>

<summary>Porphyry describes the soul’s relationship to the body as a voluntary bond that can be dissolved through philosophical discipline, and he categorizes different ways of knowing based on the nature of the object being perceived.</summary>
<keywords>soul-body dualism, Neoplatonism, philosophical death, modes of perception, Iamblichus, epistemology, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Aids to the Perception of Spiritual Realities," further exploring the nature of non-physical substances, the hierarchy of life, and the relationship between causes and their effects.</meta>

<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>PERCEPTION OF SPIRITUAL NATURES <note>original: "intelligible natures"</note></header>

11. When non-physical realities <term>hypostases: fundamental underlying substances or spiritual realities</term> descend toward the material world, they are divided into parts and spread across individual things, which results in a loss of their power. However, when they rise through their own activities beyond physical bodies, they become unified. They then exist together all at once through an overwhelming abundance of power.

12. It is not only physical bodies that can share the same name while being different in nature <term>homonymous: having the same name but a different definition or essence</term>; life itself is one of those things that exists in many different ways. For the life of a plant is different from that of a sentient animal <note>original: "animated being"</note>. The life of a spiritual essence <note>original: "intellectual essence"</note> differs from the type of existence that lies even beyond pure spirit. Similarly, the life of the soul <note>original: "psychical life"</note> differs from the life of the divine mind. All these levels are truly "alive," even though nothing that originates from them possesses a life exactly like theirs.

13. Everything that creates by its very essence produces something that is inferior to itself <note>original footnote: Because here the creator is "primarily" what the created thing is "secondarily." This refers to Proclus’s *Theological Elements*.</note>. Furthermore, everything that is created is naturally drawn back toward its creator. Among these creating natures, however, some do not turn back at all toward the beings they have created; others turn toward them only partially; and some turn only toward their offspring without returning to themselves.

14. Everything that is created receives the cause of its existence from something other than itself, since nothing is produced without a cause. Those created things that exist through a combination of parts are, for this reason, subject to decay <note>original: "corruptible"</note>. But those that are simple and not made of parts, existing as a single fundamental reality <term>hypostasis: a distinct substance or individual reality</term>, are impossible to dissolve and are therefore indestructible. They are still called "created," not because they are made of parts, but because they depend on a specific cause for their existence. Physical bodies, then, are "created" in two ways: because they depend on a cause that produces them, and because they are composed of parts. But the soul and the mind <note>original: "intellect"</note> are only considered "created" in the sense that they depend on a cause...

---

<vocab>hypostases, homonymous, subsistence, psychical, progeny, incorruptible, hypostasis, Proclus, Theological Elements</vocab>

<summary>Porphyry explains the hierarchy of existence, noting that spiritual realities lose power as they divide into the physical world but regain it when unified. He also distinguishes between "created" things that are made of parts (and thus decay) and simple spiritual essences that are eternal but still depend on a higher cause.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, hypostasis, hierarchy of life, causality, spiritual vs physical, Porphyry, Proclus, incorruptibility</keywords>

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of the soul and the intellect (Divine Mind), specifically addressing why the Mind is a multiplicity and how it relates to its objects of thought.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>
<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE</header>

...from a cause, and not as composite parts. Because of this, physical bodies are generated, capable of being broken apart and destroyed; but the soul and the intellect are "unborn" (in a material sense), because they are not made of parts. For this reason, they cannot be broken apart or destroyed. However, they are still "generated" in the sense that they depend on a prior cause.

15. The Intellect <note>the Divine Mind or *Nous*</note> is not the ultimate principle of all things; for the intellect is many things; but, prior to *the many*, it is necessary that there should be *the one*. It is clear, however, that the intellect is a multiplicity. It is always thinking of its own ideas, which are numerous rather than single, and these ideas are nothing other than the intellect itself. Therefore, if the intellect is identical to its ideas, and those ideas are many, the intellect itself must be many things. 

That the intellect is identical to the objects of its thought <note>original: "intelligibles"</note> can be proven in this way: if there is anything the intellect observes, it must see it either as something contained within itself or as something located in something else. It is obvious that the intellect contemplates or observes; for the act of thinking <note>original: "intellection"</note> is what makes it an intellect. If you take away the act of thinking, you destroy its very essence. Therefore, by looking at the nature of knowledge, we must examine how the intellect perceives. All the cognitive <term>gnostic: relating to the power of knowing or perceiving</term> powers we possess are generally classified as the senses, the imagination, and the intellect. The power that uses the senses, however, observes by reaching out toward external things. It does not become one with the objects it observes, but only receives an impression of them by directing its energy toward them. <meta>catchword: When,</meta>

---

* Porphyry here summarizes the rational cognitive <note>original: "gnostic"</note> powers of the soul under the term "intellect," because, being rational, they are extensions of the Intellect in its truest sense. These powers, starting from the lowest level, are opinion, *dianoia* <term>dianoia: the process of discursive or logical reasoning</term>, and the peak of *dianoia*. This peak is the "intellect" of the human soul—the power by which we perceive the truth of fundamental principles <note>original: "axioms"</note> through direct, intuitive insight. *Dianoia* is the step-by-step activity of reason; it is the power that reasons logically, drawing its starting points from the higher intellect. And "opinion" is the power that knows *that* something is true, but does not understand the cause or *why* it is true.

<summary>Porphyry argues that the Intellect (Mind) is a multiplicity because it contains many ideas, implying a more singular "One" must exist above it. He also distinguishes between the different ways humans gain knowledge: through the senses, imagination, and logical reasoning.</summary>

<keywords>Intellect, The One, The Many, Intelligibles, Gnostic powers, Dianoia, Discursive Reason, Intuition, Porphyry, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s discussion of how different powers of the soul—senses, imagination, and intellect—interact with their objects. On the previous page, he established that the intellect is identical to the objects it contemplates.</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>
<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

Therefore, when the eye sees a visible object, it is impossible for it to become identical to the thing it perceives; for sight could not occur if there were no distance <note>original: "interval"</note> between the eye and the object of its perception. In the same way, if an object being touched were the same as the thing touching it, the distinction between them would disappear. From this, it is clear that the senses, and the mind when it uses the senses, must always look toward an external object in order to perceive something physical <note>original: "sensible"</note>.

In the same way, the imagination <note>original: "phantasy"</note> also always focuses on something external. By reaching outward in this manner, it creates or prepares an image; this outward reach indicates that the object it perceives is merely a representation of something in the outside world. This is how these two powers—the senses and the imagination—function. Neither of them perceives an object (whether physical or non-physical) by turning inward and concentrating on itself.

In the intellect <note>the Divine Mind or *Nous*</note>, however, the perception of its objects does not happen in this way. Instead, it is achieved by turning inward and looking at itself. If the intellect were to go outside of itself to examine its own activities—trying to be the "eye" that watches them and the "sight" that perceives essences—it would fail to understand anything. Therefore, just as the senses relate to physical objects, the intellect relates to abstract truths <note>original: "intelligibles"</note>. The senses, by reaching out to external things, find objects located in physical matter. But the intellect examines abstract truths by drawing into itself rather than reaching outward<sup>f</sup>. 

For this reason, some people are of the opinion that the <term>hypostasis: the fundamental reality or actual nature of a thing</term> of the intellect differs from that of the imagination in name only. To them, the imagination in a rational being appeared to be the same thing as intelligence. However, because these people made everything dependent on matter and physical nature, it follows that they would also make the intellect dependent on these things. But our intellect examines...

---

<sup>f</sup> In the original Greek: *if not extending outward* <note>original: "εἰ δὲ μὴ ἔξω ἐκτεινόμενος"</note>; but instead of *if not* <note>original: "εἰ δὲ μὴ"</note>, it seems obviously necessary to read it as *neither* <note>original: "οὐδὲ μὴ"</note>.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>Porphyry distinguishes between sensory perception, which looks outward at external matter, and intellectual perception, which looks inward at its own essence. He critiques materialist views that equate the intellect with the imagination.</summary>
<keywords>perception, intellect, imagination, phantasy, hypostasis, intelligible natures, sensible objects, Porphyry, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s explanation of how the human mind distinguishes between physical objects and intellectual truths. It follows from the previous page's discussion on how the senses look outward while the intellect looks inward.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>
<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES</header>

both physical bodies and other substances. Therefore, it perceives them as being located in a specific place. However, because the true objects of the intellect <note>the Divine Mind or *Nous*</note> exist outside of matter, they are not located anywhere in a physical sense<sup>g</sup>. It is clear, then, that these intellectual realities must be united with the intellect itself. If these intellectual realities reside within the intellect, it follows that when the intellect understands these truths, it observes both the truth and itself; by turning inward, it perceives through thought because it is moving toward these truths. Yet, if the intellect understands many different things rather than just one, the intellect itself must be plural. But because *the One* <note>the ultimate, absolute principle in Neoplatonism</note> exists before the "many," it is necessary for *the One* to exist before the intellect.

16. Memory is not merely the storage of mental images <note>original: "imaginations" or "phantasms"</note>, but the power of calling forth anew <note>original: "de novo"</note> those concepts that the mind had previously focused on<sup>h</sup>.

17. The soul, indeed, contains the "reasons" <note>original: "forms" or "logoi"</note> of all things, but it acts upon them either when it is triggered by something external or when it turns its attention inward toward them. When it is called forth by external things, it directs the senses toward the outside world. But when it enters into itself, it becomes occupied with intellectual concepts. Therefore, one might say that neither the senses nor intellectual perceptions can exist without the imagination <note>original: "phantasy"</note>. Just as in an animal, the senses cannot function without being physically affected...

<sup>g</sup> In the original Greek: *but if they are outside of matter, they would be nowhere* <note>original: "εξω δε οντων υλης, ουδαμου αν ειη ταυτα"</note>. Holstenius, completely misunderstanding the meaning, translated this very incorrectly as: "But if they are outside of matter, it can by no means happen" <note>original: "At si extra materiam sint, neutiquam id fieri poterit"</note>. Later, Porphyry clarifies that God, the intellect, and the soul do not exist within any physical location.

<sup>h</sup> In the original Greek: *Memory is not the preservation of images, but the bringing forward of things studied as new acquisitions* <note>original: "η μνημη ουκ εστι φαντασιων συντηρια, αλλα των μελετηθεντων προβαλλεσθαι εκ νεας προβληματα"</note>. However, for the word "problems" (*problēmata*), I read "acquisitions" (*proslēmmata*). This power, which Porphyry uses to define memory, is stable in nature. Therefore, memory is the *stability of knowledge*, just as immortality is the *stability of life*, and eternity is the *stability of being*.

<summary>Porphyry argues that while the senses perceive objects in physical space, the intellect perceives abstract truths within itself, separate from location. He also redefines memory as the active recall of knowledge rather than just the storage of images.</summary>
