--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument regarding the relationship between the soul's intellectual functions and the imagination. It transitions from the necessity of sense organs for physical perception to the role of mental images in human thought.</meta>

<page-num>207</page-num>
<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

...just as perceptions require physical sense organs, in the same way, intellectual thoughts <note>original: "intellections"</note> do not occur without the imagination <note>original: "phantasy"</note>. Perhaps, however, it may be said in response to this that just as a physical impression in a sense organ is the natural companion of a sensing animal, so, by analogy, a mental image <note>original: "phantasm"</note> is the companion of the soul's intellectual activity in a human being, when that human is considered as a physical animal<sup>i</sup>.

18. The soul is a substance <note>original: "essence"</note> without physical size <note>original: "magnitude"</note>; it is immaterial, cannot be destroyed <note>original: "incorruptible"</note>, finds its existence in life, and derives that life from within itself.

19. The way physical bodies are affected <note>original: "passivity"</note> is different from the way non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note> natures are affected. The physical state of being acted upon involves change <note>original: "mutation"</note>; however, the ways the soul adapts and is affected are actually active powers <note>original: "energies"</note>. These are not at all like the heating <note>original: "calefactions"</note> and cooling <note>original: "frigefactions"</note> of physical bodies. Therefore, if physical passivity is accompanied by change, it must be said that all non-physical natures are "impassive" <note>meaning they cannot be changed or harmed by external forces</note>. This is because those substances that are separated from matter and physical bodies exist entirely in a state of activity. However, those things that are connected to matter and bodies are themselves unaffected, even though the physical structures in which they are observed are affected. For when a living creature perceives through its senses, the soul (meaning the rational soul) appears to be similar to an independent harmony<sup>k</sup>, which of itself moves the strings adapted...

---

<sup>i</sup> See the notes on the 3rd book of my translation of Aristotle's treatise *On the Soul*, and also my translation of Plotinus *On Felicity*. "The imagination," <note>original: "phantasy"</note> says Olympiodorus (in his commentary on Plato's *Phaedo* <note>original: *in Platonis Phæd.*</note>), "is an obstacle to our intellectual concepts. For this reason, when we are moved by the divine influence of inspiration, if the imagination interferes, the inspired activity <note>original: "enthusiastic energy"</note> stops. This is because inspiration and imagination are opposites. If it were asked whether the soul is able to act without the imagination, we reply that its ability to perceive universal truths proves that it can. Therefore, it has perceptions independent of the imagination; at the same time, however, the imagination follows the soul in its activities, just as a storm pursues a person sailing on the sea."

<sup>k</sup> The comparison of the soul to harmony is explained more accurately as follows by Olympiodorus, in his *Commentary on the Phaedo of Plato*, than it is in this place by Porphyry: "Harmony exists in three ways. For it is either harmony itself, or it is that which is first harmonized and which is so in its entirety, or it is that which..."

<summary>Porphyry defines the soul as an immaterial, self-living substance and argues that while the imagination often accompanies human thought, the soul's intellectual perceptions are ultimately independent of physical change.</summary>
<keywords>soul, essence, imagination, phantasy, intellection, incorporeal, passivity, Olympiodorus, Plato, Aristotle, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s discussion on the nature of the soul and the body, specifically using the analogy of musical harmony to explain how the non-physical (incorporeal) governs the physical.</meta>

<page-num>208</page-num>
<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

...to harmony; but the body is similar to the inseparable harmony in the strings <note>original: "chords"</note>, [that is, to the harmony which cannot exist separate from the strings]. But the living being <note>original: "animal"</note> is the cause of the motion because it is an animated being. It is, however, analogous to a musician because it possesses harmony; but the bodies which are struck through sensory experience <note>original: "sensitive passion"</note> are analogous to the harmonized strings of a musical instrument. For in this instance, also, the abstract <note>original: "separate"</note> harmony is not affected by the movement, but the strings are. And the musician, indeed, moves according to the harmony which is in him; yet the strings would not be moved musically, even if the musician wished them to be, unless the laws of harmony ordered this to take place.

20. Non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note> natures are not categorized like physical bodies, according to a shared membership in the same class <note>original: "genus"</note>; instead, they derive their name simply from being the opposite of bodies <note>original: "from a mere privation with respect to bodies"</note>. Hence, nothing prevents some of them from existing as true beings, while others exist as "non-beings" <note>referring to different levels of reality in Neoplatonic philosophy</note>. Some are prior to bodies, and others come after bodies; some are separate from, and others inseparable from bodies. Some have an independent existence <note>original: "subsistence by themselves"</note>, while others are dependent on <note>original: "indigent of"</note> things different from themselves for their existence. Some are defined by their constant activities <note>original: "energies"</note> and self-starting <note>original: "self-motive"</note> lives, while others exist alongside specific qualities of life and activity. For they exist according to a negation of the things which they are *not*, rather than according to the affirmation of the things which they *are*.

21. The properties of matter, according to the an—

---

<gloss>There is one harmony which is essential; another which is self-moved; and a third which is secondary and only partially participates in harmony. The first of these must be assigned to the intellect, the second to the soul, and the third to the body. This last one is also perishable <note>original: "corruptible"</note> because it exists within a physical subject; but the other two are imperishable because they are neither made of parts <note>original: "composites"</note> nor dependent on a subject. Hence, the rational soul is analogous to a musician, but the animated body to harmonized strings; for the soul exists separately, but the strings are inseparable from the musical instrument.</gloss>

<vocab>incorporeal, genus, subsistence, energies, self-motive, intellect, soul, musician, matter</vocab>

<summary>Porphyry uses the analogy of a musician and instrument strings to explain that the soul (the musician) directs the body (the strings) according to abstract principles of harmony. He also explains that non-physical entities are defined by what they are not—namely, they are not physical bodies—and vary in their level of independence and reality.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, soul and body, musical analogy, incorporeal natures, harmony, intellect, subsistence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of non-physical ("incorporeal") natures, finishing a list of definitions from ancient philosophers regarding the nature of matter.</meta>

<page-num>209</page-num>
<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLECTUAL REALITIES <note>original: "INTELLIGIBLE NATURES"</note></header>

...ancients, are the following: Matter is non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note>, for it is different from physical bodies. It is without life, for it is neither intellect nor soul, nor is it alive by its own nature <note>original: "vital from itself [i. e. essentially]"</note>. It is also formless, variable, infinite, and powerless. Therefore, it is neither true being, nor yet simple non-being. It is not "non-being" in the same way that motion is; rather, it is true non-being—the image and phantom <note>original: "phantasm"</note> of physical mass <note>original: "bulk"</note>, because it is the primary container of mass. It is likewise powerless and a mere desire for existence <note>original: "subsistence"</note>; it has a kind of stability, but not in a permanent way, and it always appears to be the opposite of itself. Thus, it is both small and great, more and less, lacking and overflowing. It is always in a state of "becoming"—rising into existence—yet it does not remain, and it is unable to escape; it is the deficiency of all being. Therefore, in whatever form it presents itself, it deceives. Even if it appears to be great, it is actually small. It resembles a fleeing mockery, evading all pursuit and vanishing into non-existence <note>original: "non-entity"</note>. Its flight does not happen across physical space, but occurs through its abandonment of real being. Consequently, the images that exist within it are even more unreal than the matter itself—just like in a mirror, where the object being reflected is in one place, and its image is in another. It also appears to be full yet contains nothing, even though it seems to possess everything¹.

22. All physical sensations and changes <note>original: "passions"</note> exist in the same place where decay <note>original: "corruption"</note> exists; for the ability to be affected by external things is the path to decay. Whatever can experience this passivity <note>the state of being acted upon by an external force</note> is also subject to decay. However, nothing non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note> can be decayed. Some non-physical things either simply exist or do not exist, meaning they are not "passive" at all. For something that is passive should not be of this stable kind, but rather something that can be changed in its qualities and decayed by the properties of the things...

---

¹ What Porphyry says here regarding matter is derived from Plotinus's treatise, *On the Impassivity of Incorporeal Natures*, to my translation of which I refer the reader.

<sig>P</sig>

<summary>Porphyry defines "matter" as an illusory, powerless "non-being" that acts as a mirror for forms without possessing any reality of its own. He distinguishes between the physical world, which is subject to external change and decay, and the non-physical realm, which is unchanging and impassive.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Plotinus, Neoplatonism, matter, non-being, incorporeal, corruption, passivity, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s metaphysical definitions regarding matter and form, transitioning into the relationship between the soul, the body, and the "intellectual essence" (the nature of the mind/spirit).</meta>

<page-num>210</page-num>
<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLECTUAL REALITIES</header>

...that enter into it, and cause it to be acted upon <note>original: "passive"</note>. For the change in quality of something inherent is not produced by chance. Therefore, matter itself does not suffer change <note>In this philosophical context, "suffer" or "passion" means to be acted upon by an external force or to undergo change</note>; for it is, by its own nature, without any quality. Nor do the forms which enter into and depart from matter suffer; rather, the "passion" <note>the state of being acted upon</note> exists within the composite made of matter and form, the very being of which consists in the union of the two. For this composite—within the opposing powers and qualities of the things that enter it and produce change—is seen to be the subject of them. 

Because of this, those things whose life is derived from the outside, and does not come from themselves, are capable of experiencing <note>original: "suffering"</note> both the participation in and the loss <note>original: "privation"</note> of life. But those beings whose existence consists in an unchanging <note>original: "impassive"</note> life must necessarily possess a permanent life; just as a loss of life, insofar as it is a loss, is accompanied by a lack of change <note>original: "impassivity"</note>. 

Therefore, just as being changed and "suffering" belong to the composite of matter and form (which is the body), while matter itself is exempt from this; so also, to live and to die, and to suffer through the sharing of life and death, is seen in the composite of soul and body. Nevertheless, this does not happen to the soul itself, because the soul is not something made of life and the loss of life, but consists of life alone. The soul possesses this because its essence is simple, and its nature <note>original: "reason [or form]"</note> is self-moving <note>original: "self-motive"</note>.<sup>m</sup>

23. An intellectual essence <note>The core nature of Divine Mind or Intellect</note> is so consistent in its parts that the same<sup>n</sup> things exist in both a specific <note>original: "partial"</note> intellect and an all-perfect, universal intellect. In a universal intellect, however, specific natures exist universally; but in a specific intellect, both universals and particulars exist specifically.

24. Regarding that essence whose existence is found in life, and whose experiences <note>original: "passions"</note> are themselves forms of life: even its death consists of a certain kind of life, and not a total loss of life.

---

<note><sup>m</sup> See my translation of the previously mentioned treatise by Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founder of Neoplatonism and Porphyry’s teacher</note>.</note>
<note><sup>n</sup> For the original Greek *ta onta* ("the beings") here, I read *ta auta* ("the same").</note>

<summary>Porphyry explains that while physical bodies (composites of matter and form) can change and die, the soul and the "intellectual essence" are inherently alive and unchanging. He argues that the soul is "self-moving" and does not "suffer" change the way physical matter does.</summary>

<page-num>211</page-num>
<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLECTUAL REALITIES. 211</header>

... because the loss <note>original: "deprivation"</note> of life in this essence is not an experience of change <note>original: "passion"</note>, nor is it a path that leads entirely to a non-living state of being.

25. In incorporeal lives <note>lives of beings without physical bodies</note>, progressions occur while the lives themselves remain firm and stable; nothing belonging to them is corrupted or changed into the underlying nature <note>original: "hypostasis"</note> of things lower than them. Because of this, the things they bring into existence are not produced through decay or change. Nor do these incorporeal lives exist like physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note>, which involves both decay and change. Therefore, they are unborn and indestructible, and for this reason, they are revealed <note>original: "unfolded into light"</note> without birth and without corruption.

26. Regarding that nature which is beyond the mind <note>intellect</note>, many things are stated through intellectual thought; however, it is better observed by a pausing of intellectual activity than by the activity itself. This is similar to a person who is asleep: many things are said about him by those who are awake while he is in that state, but the true knowledge and understanding of his sleeping condition can only be obtained through sleep itself. For the "like is known by the like"; because *all knowledge is a process of becoming like the object of knowledge.*

27. Regarding that which is "non-being," we either create this concept because we ourselves are separated from real being, or we have a pre-existing idea of it as something connected to being. Therefore, if we are separated from being, we do not have a prior concept of the "non-being" which is *above* being; rather, our knowledge in this case is only that of a false experience <note>original: "passion"</note>, like what happens to a man when he loses his sense of self. For just as a man can, by his own effort, be truly raised to the "non-being" which is above being, so also, by departing from being, he can fall away...

---

° For this reason, it is beautifully said in the *Key* <note>original: "Clavis"</note> *of Hermes Trismegistus*, "*that the knowledge of the good* [or the supreme principle of things] *is a divine silence, and the resting of all the senses.*" See also, on this subject, a most admirable extract from *Damascius*, *On First Principles* <note>original Greek: <term>περὶ αρχῶν</term></note>, at the end of the 3rd volume of my *Plato*.

<summary>Porphyry argues that incorporeal beings are unchanging and that the highest reality, which transcends the mind, is best understood through a state of "divine silence" rather than active intellectual effort.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s discussion on the nature of "non-being" and moves into a detailed explanation of how non-physical (incorporeal) entities, such as the soul or intellect, interact with physical bodies without being restricted by physical laws like space and mass.</meta>

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE</header>

he is led to a state of non-existence <note>original: "non-being"</note>, which is a falling away from true being.

28. The physical essence <note>original: "hypostasis"</note> of a body is no obstacle at all to that which is essentially non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note>; it cannot prevent it from being where, and in such a way, as it wishes to be. Just as that which lacks physical mass <note>original: "bulk"</note> cannot be grasped by a body and has no relation to it, so too, that which has mass cannot block or hide a non-physical nature; instead, it exists before it as if it were nothing <note>original: "a non-entity"</note>. A non-physical nature does not move through physical space <note>original: "pervade locally"</note> when it wishes to pass from one thing to another, because "place" only exists in relation to physical mass. Nor is it squeezed or pressured by bodies. Anything connected to mass can be compressed and moved from place to place; however, that which is entirely without mass or size cannot be held back by physical matter and does not experience physical movement. Therefore, through a certain state of being <note>original: "disposition"</note>, it is found to be wherever it is inclined to be—existing, in relation to physical space, *everywhere* and yet *nowhere*<sup>p</sup>. Through *this certain state of being*, it is either above the heavens or is contained within a specific part of the world. Yet, when it is within a part of the world, it is not visible to the eyes; rather, its presence is revealed through its actions.

29. If a non-physical nature is contained within a body, it must not be shut inside it like a wild animal in a cage <note>original: "den"</note> (for no physical body is capable of enclosing or holding it in that way), nor is it held in the body like a bladder holds liquid or air. Instead, it must give rise <note>original: "give subsistence"</note> to certain powers that reach toward the outside world through its union with the body. It is through these powers that, when it descends, it becomes intertwined <note>original: "complicated"</note> with the body. Its connection with the body, therefore, is brought about through an indescribable <note>original: "ineffable"</note>...

---

<sup>p</sup> For that which is truly non-physical is *everywhere* in potential <note>original: "virtually"</note>—that is, in its power and effectiveness—but it is *nowhere* in terms of physical location.

<summary>Porphyry explains that non-physical realities like the soul are not restricted by physical space or mass; they exist "everywhere and nowhere" and interact with the body through specific powers rather than physical containment.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on how the soul binds itself to the body through attachment and begins an explanation of the cosmic hierarchy. It describes how universal, perfect beings always look upward toward their source, while individual souls—like those of humans—can fall into error by looking downward toward matter.</meta>

<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

extension. Therefore, nothing else binds the soul; it binds itself to the body. Because of this, it is not freed from the body simply because the body is wounded or dies <note>original: "corrupted"</note>. Instead, the soul liberates itself by turning away from its emotional attachment <note>original: "adhering affection"</note> to the body.

30. None of the essential realities <note>original: "hypostases"</note> that function as whole, perfect systems ever turn toward what they have produced <note>original: "progeny"</note>. Instead, all perfect realities are drawn upward toward what created them <note>original: "generators"</note>, all the way up from the physical universe <note>original: "mundane body"</note>. For this universe, being perfect, is drawn toward its own soul, which is intellectual; because of this, the universe moves in a circle <note>A reference to the eternal, circular motion of the heavens in ancient cosmology</note>. The soul of the universe is drawn toward pure Intellect, and Intellect is drawn toward the first principle of all things [God]. 

Thus, all beings strive toward this first principle as much as they can, starting from the lowest levels of existence. This ascent to the first principle can be direct or indirect. Therefore, these natures do not just desire the highest God; they enjoy his presence to the fullest extent of their capacity. However, in individual <note>original: "partial"</note><sup>q</sup> realities—those capable of leaning toward many different things—there is also a desire to turn toward what they have created. This is where error and blameworthy disbelief arise. These souls are harmed by matter because they are capable of turning toward it, even though they are also capable of turning toward the divine. 

Thus, "perfection" allows lower things to exist while keeping them focused on the highest realities. Conversely, "imperfection" causes higher <note>original: "primary"</note><sup>r</sup> things to turn toward lower things, leading them to love beings that have already turned away from God.

31. God is everywhere because he is nowhere <note>This means God is omnipresent because He is not limited by physical space or a specific location</note>. This is also true of Intellect and the soul; for each of these is—

---

<sup>q</sup> For the Greek term <term>μερισταις</term> <note>original: "meristais," meaning "divisible things"</note> here, I read <term>μερικαις</term> <note>original: "merikais," meaning "partial things"</note>. Porphyry is speaking here of essences that are the opposite of *those that function as wholes*, as is evident from the entire paragraph.

<sup>r</sup> The "primary natures" Porphyry mentions here are rational, individual souls, such as our own. The beings higher than these are never turned toward things lower than themselves.

<summary>Porphyry explains the soul's self-binding nature and the Neoplatonic hierarchy, where perfect beings look upward toward God while individual souls risk falling by focusing on matter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of being, moving from the movement of souls to the paradoxical nature of existence where higher realities (God, Intellect, Soul) are simultaneously "everywhere and nowhere" in relation to the physical world.</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>
### AUXILIARIES TO THE [PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES]

God is everywhere because he is nowhere. But God is indeed everywhere and nowhere with respect to all things that follow him <margin>a. In the original Greek, I read "himself" [*autos*] instead of "there" [*autou*].</margin>; and he alone is as he is, and as he wills himself to be. Intellect <note>original: "Intellect" — referring to the Platonic *Nous*, or pure divine Mind</note> is in God, but is everywhere and nowhere with respect to the natures that come after it. And the soul is in God and in Intellect, and is everywhere and nowhere in relation to the body <margin>b. The original reads: "the soul in Intellect and God is everywhere and nowhere in body," but it seems necessary to read: "and the soul is in Intellect and God, and is everywhere and nowhere in body."</margin>. 

But the body is in the soul, and in Intellect <margin>c. "And in Intellect" is omitted in the original Greek, but it ought to be inserted, as is evident from the Latin version by Lucas Holstenius.</margin>, and in God. And as all beings and non-beings come from and exist within God, he is neither those beings nor non-beings, nor does he exist within them. For if he were only "everywhere," he would be all things and exist in all things; but since he is also "nowhere," all things are produced through him and are contained within him because he is everywhere. They are, however, distinct from him because he is "nowhere." 

Likewise, Intellect—being everywhere and nowhere—is the cause of souls and of the natures that come after souls; yet Intellect is not the soul, nor is it the natures that follow the soul, nor does it exist within them. This is because it is not only everywhere, but is also nowhere with respect to the natures that come after it. Similarly, the soul is neither the body nor in the body, but is the cause of the body; because while it is everywhere, it is also nowhere in respect to the body. This progression of things in the universe extends down to that which is unable to be both "everywhere" and "nowhere" at once, but instead partially participates in each of these <margin>x. The irrational life (animal soul) is a thing of this kind, which is partly separable and partly inseparable from the body. Therefore, as far as it is inseparable from the body, it partakes of being "everywhere" [in the body]; but, as far as it is separable, it partakes of being "nowhere."</margin>.

32. The soul does not exist on the earth [when it is occupied with earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> natures] in the same manner...

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic concept that higher spiritual realities like God, Intellect, and the Soul are "everywhere" because they sustain all things, yet "nowhere" because they are distinct and transcendent. It also clarifies that the physical body exists within the soul, rather than the soul being contained by the body.</summary>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the soul and the physical world, specifically addressing how the soul "descends" into the afterlife or earthly existence without physically moving through space.</meta>

<page-num>215</page-num>
# PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES

...as bodies move toward <note>original: "accede to"</note> the earth; however, the soul’s existence on the earth signifies that it is presiding over earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> bodies. In the same way, the soul is said to be in Hades when it presides over its image <gloss>y</gloss>, which is naturally suited to exist in a specific place but has its fundamental reality <note>original: "hypostasis"</note> in darkness. Therefore, if Hades is a dark subterranean place, the soul—though not torn away <note>original: "divulsed"</note> from true being—will exist in Hades by attracting its image to itself. 

For when the soul departs from the solid physical body, it is accompanied by the spirit which it had gathered from the starry spheres. But because of its clinging attachment to the body, the soul exercises a fragmented reason; through this, it maintains a specific disposition <note>original: "habitude"</note> toward a body of a certain quality while performing the activities of life. Consequently, because of this attachment to the body, the forms of the imagination <note>original: "phantasy"</note> are impressed upon the spirit, and thus the image is attracted by the soul. 

The soul, however, is said to be in Hades because the spirit takes on a formless and dark nature. And as a heavy and moist spirit sinks down into subterranean places, the soul is said to "go under the earth." This does not mean that the essence of the soul moves from one place to another or exists within a physical space; rather, it takes on the dispositions of bodies that are naturally suited to change location and occupy space. Such bodies receive the soul according to their fitness, based on how they are inclined toward it. 

For the soul finds a body appropriate to its own internal state. Therefore, when the soul is oriented toward a purer state, it has an innate <note>original: "connascent"</note> body that approaches an ethereal nature; this is an ethereal body. But when it moves from reason toward the activities of the imagination, then its innate body takes on a solar form. And when it becomes weakened <note>original: "effeminate"</note> and intensely stirred by physical forms, it is then connected with a lunar form...

***

<gloss>y</gloss> That is, the animal spirit or pneumatic soul, in which the rational soul undergoes its punishments in Hades.

<summary>Porphyry explains that the soul's presence in "Hades" or on earth is not a physical movement, but a result of its attachment to a "spirit" or "image" that takes on different forms (ethereal, solar, or lunar) based on the soul's purity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's explanation of how the soul relates to the physical world, specifically focusing on the metaphors of "moisture" (ignorance/physicality) and "dryness" (wisdom/intellect), and the philosophical distinctions between material and spiritual existence.</meta>

# AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES

body. However, when the soul enters bodies made of humid vapors, a total ignorance of true reality <note>original: "real being"</note> follows, along with darkness and a state of spiritual infancy.

Furthermore, when leaving the body, if the soul still possesses a spirit clouded by humid vapors <note>original: "turbid from humid exhalations"</note>, it attracts a shadow to itself and becomes heavy. A spirit of this kind naturally tries to sink into the depths of the earth, unless some other cause pulls it in the opposite direction. Just as the soul necessarily lives on the earth when it is enclosed in this shell-like <note>original: "testaceous"</note> and earthly garment <note>the physical body</note>, so too, when it attracts a moist spirit, it is necessarily surrounded by a shadowy image. The soul attracts this moisture when it constantly tries to associate with nature—a realm where processes occur through moisture and which exists beneath the earth rather than upon it. However, when the soul earnestly tries to depart from nature, she becomes a "dry splendor"—shining clearly without shadows, clouds, or mist. For moisture creates a mist in the air, while dryness produces a clear radiance <note>original: "dry splendour"</note> from its exhalations.

33. The following things are truly said of the physical <note>original: "sensible"</note> and material world:
* That it is entirely spread out and scattered;
* That it is constantly changing;
* That its existence consists of differences;
* That it is a composite <note>made of parts</note>;
* That it exists by itself as a subject or recipient of other things;
* That it is perceived in terms of space <note>original: "place"</note> and physical size <note>original: "bulk"</note>.

Other similar properties are also attributed to it. In contrast, the following characteristics are attributed to truly existing being <note>the realm of pure intellect and spirit</note>, which exists by its own power: 
* That it is always firmly rooted in itself;
* That its existence is perpetually similar and the same;
* That its essence is defined by sameness;
* That it is unchangeable in its core nature;
* That it is simple and uncompounded;
* That it can neither be broken apart <note>original: "dissoluble"</note> nor occupy a physical space;
* That it is not spread out into physical size;
* That it is neither created <note>original: "generated"</note> nor capable of being destroyed.

Other similar properties are attributed to it.

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic concept of the "spirit-vehicle" (pneuma), explaining how the soul's attachment to "moisture" or material desires weighs it down, while "dryness" or intellectual purity allows it to rise. It also provides a clear list of the opposing characteristics of the material world versus the spiritual realm of "True Being."</summary>

<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

By adhering to these definitions, we should neither assert anything contradictory to them concerning the different natures of physical <note>original: "sensible"</note> and truly existing beings, nor should we agree with those who do.

### SECTION II.

34. There is one kind of virtue belonging to the **political character** <note>In this context, "political" refers to the life of a citizen in society (from the Greek "polis"), rather than modern party politics.</note>, and another belonging to the person who turns toward contemplation. This latter person is called **theoretic**, for they are now an observer of intellectual and spiritual <note>original: "intelligible"</note> realities. There are also other virtues belonging to the **intellect** itself, insofar as it is intellect and separate from the soul. 

The virtues of the political character, which involve the moderation of the passions, are defined by following and obeying the reasoning regarding what is appropriate in our actions. Therefore, because they aim at harmless interaction with neighbors, these virtues are called "political" from the idea of social fellowship. 

In this civic context:
* **Prudence** resides in the reasoning part of the soul.
* **Fortitude** resides in the spirited <note>original: "irascible"</note> part.
* **Temperance** consists of the agreement and harmony <note>original: "symphony"</note> of the desiring part <gloss>That part of the soul which is the source of various desires; the "epithymetic" part.</gloss> with the reasoning part.
* **Justice** consists of each of these parts performing its proper role with respect to governing and being governed.

But the virtues of the person who proceeds to the contemplative life consist in a departure from earthly concerns. Because of this, they are called **purifications** <note>original: "cathartic virtues"</note>, as they are seen in the act of refraining from bodily actions and avoiding sympathy with the body. For these are the virtues of the soul elevating itself toward true reality. 

The political virtues, therefore, bring order to the mortal person and serve as the forerunners of purification. It is necessary that those who are being refined by the purifying virtues should first abstain from doing anything primarily in connection with the body. Therefore, in...

<summary>Porphyry distinguishes between "political virtues," which govern social harmony and the moderation of passions, and higher "purifying virtues," which involve detaching the soul from physical concerns to focus on contemplation.</summary>
<keywords>political virtues, contemplation, purification, prudence, fortitude, temperance, justice, soul, intellect, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 241 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the soul's virtues transition from the "political" or civic level (moderating the passions) to a higher level of purification and intellectual activity.</meta>

<page-num>218</page-num>
<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLECTUAL REALITIES</header>

Through these purifications, choosing not to form opinions based on the body but to operate through the intellect alone, **prudence** is established; this virtue reaches its perfection by functioning intellectually with purity. Not being affected by the body's impulses in the same way constitutes **temperance**. Not fearing the soul's departure from the body—as if it were entering a void or non-existence—creates **fortitude**. Finally, when reason and intellect lead without resistance from the irrational parts of the soul, **justice** is produced. 

The disposition associated with the **civic virtues** <note>original: "political virtues"; these are the virtues of a person living in society.</note> is therefore found in the moderation of the passions; their goal is for a human to live in harmony with nature. However, the disposition associated with the **contemplative virtues** <note>original: "theoretic virtues"</note> is found in **apathy** <note>This philosophical apathy (from the Greek *apatheia*) is not—as many today mistakenly believe—a lack of feeling or "insensibility," but rather the perfect mastery of reason over the passions.</note>, the goal of which is to become like God.

Since there are two kinds of purification—one being the act of purifying and the other being the state of those who have been purified—the **cleansing virtues** <note>original: "cathartic virtues"</note> are examined according to both meanings. The ultimate goal of purification is to become pure. However, since the act of purifying and the state of being pure both involve the removal <note>original: "ablation"</note> of everything foreign, the "good" that results from them is different from the act that does the cleaning. If that which is being purified was good before it was defiled by impurity, then purification alone is enough. However, what remains after the cleaning is what is truly good, not the cleaning process itself. 

The nature of the soul was not perfectly Good prior to its purification, but it is "good-form" <note>original: "boniform"; meaning it has the potential or shape of the Good.</note>—it is capable of participating in the Good. If this were not the case, the soul would never have found itself in a state of evil. Therefore, the soul's "good" consists of being united to its creator <note>original: "generator"</note>, while its "evil" consists of its association with things lower than itself. This evil is twofold: one part arises from associating with earthly natures, and the other from doing so with an excess of passion. Consequently, all the civic virtues that liberate the soul from one of these evils may be called—

<summary>Porphyry defines the higher "contemplative" virtues (prudence, temperance, fortitude, and justice) as states of intellectual purity and mastery over the body, leading toward a god-like state of "apathy." He explains that the soul is not the absolute Good itself, but is "good-form," meaning it can choose to unite with its creator or fall into evil through earthly attachments.</summary>

<keywords>prudence, temperance, fortitude, justice, civic virtues, contemplative virtues, apathy, purification, cathartic virtues, boniform, soul, passions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of virtues, moving from the "cleansing" virtues (which purify the soul from bodily concerns) to the "intellectual" and "paradigmatic" virtues, which represent the highest levels of spiritual and divine reality.</meta>

<page-num>219</page-num>

# PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES

<note>original: "nated virtues"</note> The aforementioned virtues are honorable, but the **cleansing virtues** <note>original: "cathartic virtues"</note> are even more honorable. They liberate the soul from evil insofar as it is a soul. It is necessary, therefore, that once the soul is purified, it should unite with its source <note>original: "generator"; referring to the Divine Intellect or the One.</note>. Consequently, the virtue of the soul after its spiritual reorientation <note>original: "conversion"; the Neoplatonic concept of *epistrophe*, where the soul turns back toward its divine origin.</note> consists in a rigorous knowledge of true reality; however, this cannot happen unless that reorientation occurs first.

There is, therefore, another category of virtues beyond the cleansing and the civic virtues: these are the **virtues of the soul acting intellectually**. In this state, **wisdom** and **prudence** consist of contemplating the realities held within the Intellect. **Justice** consists of performing one’s proper duty in harmony with the Intellect and acting according to its guidance. **Temperance** is the soul's inward turning toward the Intellect. And **fortitude** is a state of being undisturbed <note>original: "apathy"; in this context, it means a state of being unaffected by external passions, mirroring the "impassive" or unchanging nature of the divine.</note>, modeled after the nature of that which the soul gazes upon, which is by its own nature beyond suffering or change. These virtues, just like the others, are interconnected and follow one another.

The fourth species of virtues is that of the **perfect models** <note>original: "paradigms"; these are the ideal, divine blueprints or "Forms" that exist within the Divine Intellect.</note> existing within the Intellect. These are superior to the virtues of the soul and serve as their original blueprints; the virtues of the soul are merely likenesses of them. In the Intellect, all things exist simultaneously as these perfect models. Here, **prudence** is pure knowledge; **wisdom** is the Intellect that knows all things. **Temperance** is that which is focused on itself. The specific work of the Intellect is the fulfillment of its own duty, and this is **justice**<sup>b</sup>. Finally, **fortitude** is a state of constancy—remaining pure within itself through an abundance of power. There are, therefore, four categories of virtues. Some belong to the Intellect, are part of its very essence, and serve as the perfect models. Others belong to the soul as it looks toward the Intellect and—

---

<sup>b</sup> The words "and justice" <note>original: και δικαιοσυνη</note> are omitted in the original text. However, it is clear from Plotinus’s treatise *On the Virtues* that they should be included. What Porphyry says in this section regarding the virtues is derived from that work.

<summary>This page outlines a four-tier hierarchy of virtues in Neoplatonism, ascending from civic and purifying virtues to those of the soul's intellectual activity and, finally, the "paradigmatic" virtues within the Divine Intellect itself.</summary>

<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES</header>

...being filled from it <note>Referring to the "paradigmatic virtues" from the previous page, which flow from the highest source.</note>. Others belong to the soul of man as it purifies itself and becomes cleansed from the body and from irrational passions. And others are the virtues of the human soul that bring order to the person by providing measure and limits to our irrational nature, thereby producing moderation in our passions. *And indeed, he who possesses the greater virtues necessarily also possesses the lesser; but the opposite is not true, for he who has the lesser does not necessarily have the greater virtues.* Nor will the person who possesses the greater virtues primarily <note>original: "precedaneously"</note> act according to the lesser ones, except insofar as the necessities of their mortal nature require. 

The goal <note>original: "scope"</note> of the virtues is, as we have said, fundamentally different depending on the virtue. For the goal of the *political virtues* <note>The "civic" virtues related to living well in society.</note> is to give measure to the passions in their natural, practical activities. But the goal of the *purifying virtues* <note>original: "cathartic virtues"</note> is to entirely erase the memory of the passions; and the goal of the rest of the virtues follows the logic of what has been said before. 

Hence, he who acts according to the *practical virtues* is a *virtuous man*; but he who acts according to the *purifying virtues* is an *angelic man*, or is even a *beneficent spirit* <note>original: "good dæmon"; in Neoplatonism, a *daimon* is a presiding spirit or a being between humans and gods, not an evil entity.</note>. He who acts according to the *intellectual virtues* alone is *a God*; but he who acts according to the *archetypal virtues* <note>original: "paradigmatic virtues"; the primary forms of virtue as they exist in the Divine Mind.</note> is the *father of the Gods*. We, therefore, ought to pay special attention to the *purifying virtues*, since we may obtain these during our present life. Through these, we may ascend to the more honorable virtues. Therefore, it is necessary to examine to what degree purification may be extended: for it is a separation from the body and from the emotional impulses <note>original: "passive motion"</note> of the irrational part of the soul. How this may be achieved, and to what extent, must now be explained.

In the first place, it is necessary that anyone who intends to acquire this purification should—as the foundation and basis of it—know themselves to be a soul bound in a foreign thing, and in a different kind of essence. In the second place, as a step following from this foundation, they should withdraw <note>original: "collect himself"</note> from the body, and as it were from...

<summary>Porphyry defines the hierarchy of virtues, explaining that while higher virtues encompass lower ones, the ultimate goal of a philosopher is to transcend civic moderation through purification (catharsis) to reach intellectual and divine states. He begins outlining the process of purification by identifying the soul as an entity distinct from the physical body.</summary>

...different places, so as to be disposed in a manner perfectly unaffected <note>original: "impassive"</note> with respect to the body. For he who acts uninterruptedly according to sense perception, though he may not do this with an clinging affection or the enjoyment resulting from pleasure, yet, at the same time, his attention is scattered <note>original: "dissipated"</note> around the body, because he has come into contact with it through the senses <margin>Instead of "according to her," here it is necessary to read, "according to sense perception" <note>original Greek: κατ’ αἴσθησιν</note>.</margin>. But we are addicted to the pleasures or pains of sensible objects <note>things perceived through the senses</note> with an eagerness and a shared feeling <note>original: "converging sympathy"</note>; it is from this disposition that we must be purified. *This, however, will be achieved by accepting only necessary pleasures, and the sensation of them, merely as remedies or as a liberation from pain <margin>Conformably to this, as we have before observed, Aristotle says in the 7th Book of his Nicomachean Ethics, "that bodily pleasures are remedies against pain, and that they fill up the needs of nature, but they perfect no activity <note>original: "energy"</note> of the rational soul."</margin>, in order that the rational part of the soul may not be blocked in its activities.* 

Pain must also be removed. But if this is not possible, it must be mildly diminished. And it will be diminished if the soul does not "suffer along with" <note>original: "is not copassive with"</note> the body. Anger, likewise, must be removed as much as possible, and must never be premeditated. But if it cannot be entirely removed, deliberate choice must not be mixed with it; instead, the unpremeditated movement must be merely an impulse of the irrational part of the soul. *That which is unpremeditated, however, is weak and insignificant.* 

All fear must likewise be driven out. For he who is suited to this purification will fear nothing. Here, however, if fear should occur, it will be unpremeditated. Therefore, anger and fear must only be used for the purpose of warning <note>original: "admonition"</note>. But the desire for everything base must be completely rooted out. Such a person also, insofar as he is a purifying <note>original: "cathartic"</note> philosopher, will not desire food and drink except for what is necessary. Neither must there be anything premeditated in natural sexual <note>original: "venereal"</note> connections; *but if this should take place, it must only be as far as that sudden impulse of the imagination which...*

<summary>Porphyry outlines the practical discipline of the "purifying virtues," explaining how a philosopher must detach the soul from bodily sensations, anger, and fear to maintain rational focus. He argues that physical pleasures should only be treated as remedies for pain, rather than ends in themselves.</summary>

*remains active <note>original: "energizes"</note> even in sleep.* In short, the intellectual soul of the purified man must be liberated from all these bodily inclinations <note>original: "corporeal propensities"</note>. He must also strive to ensure that whenever the irrational nature of bodily passions is stirred, it moves without shared feeling <note>original: "sympathy"</note> and without focused attention <note>original: "animadversion"</note>; so that these movements may be instantly dissolved because of their closeness to the power of reason. However, this will not happen while purification is still in progress; it will only occur in those whose reason governs without resistance. Therefore, in these individuals, the lower part of the soul will so respect <note>original: "venerate"</note> reason that it will feel resentful if it is moved at all, because it failed to remain quiet in the presence of its master, and it will rebuke itself for its own weakness <note>original: "imbecility"</note>. At first, these are only ways to moderate the passions, but they eventually lead to a state of peace <note>original: "apathy," from the Greek *apatheia*, meaning freedom from emotional disturbance</note>. For when the tendency to "suffer along with" the body <note>original: "copassivity"</note> is entirely removed, then this peace is present in the person who has been purified from such vulnerability. For passion is stirred only when reason provides the spark by leaning toward the irrational nature.

35. Everything that is located somewhere is located there according to its own nature, and not against its nature. For a body, which exists in matter and physical mass, to be "somewhere" means to be in a specific place. Therefore, for the "body of the world" <note>the physical universe</note>—which is material and has mass—to be everywhere means to be spread out across space <note>original: "extended with interval"</note> and to exist within the boundaries of that space. However, existing in a specific place does not apply at all to the world of pure intellect <note>original: "intelligible world"</note>, nor, in general, to anything that is non-physical <note>original: "immaterial"</note> and inherently without a body. This is because such things have no mass and no spatial distance <note>original: "interval"</note>. Therefore, the everywhere-ness <note>original: "ubiquity"</note> of a non-physical nature is not tied to location. Consequently, it will not have one part here and another part there; if that were the case, it would not be outside of space, nor without distance. Wherever it is, the whole of it is there. It is not in one place while being absent from another, for then it would be contained by one location but separated from another. Nor is it far from this thing but near to that, in the same way that

<summary>Porphyry explains that a perfected soul reaches a state of "apathy" where the body's passions no longer disturb reason, and he argues that non-physical things exist in their entirety everywhere because they are not restricted by physical space.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of the relationship between the physical (sensible) world and the intellectual (intelligible) world. It specifically examines how non-physical, indivisible entities can interact with physical, divisible objects without losing their own nature.</meta>

<page-num>223</page-num>

<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

...distance <note>original: "remoteness"</note> and nearness are only spoken of in relation to things that exist in space, measured by physical distances <note>original: "intervals"</note>. Therefore, the physical <note>original: "sensible"</note> world is related to the intellectual <note>original: "intelligible"</note> world through distance, but a truly non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note> nature is present within the physical world in an indivisible <note>original: "impartibly"</note> way, without any spatial distance.

Furthermore, when an indivisible thing exists within something that has physical dimensions, it is present as a whole in every single part of it; it remains one and the same in number throughout every part. Therefore, that which is indivisible <note>original: "impartible"</note> and lacks internal variety <note>original: "without multitude"</note> appears to stretch out into a physical size <note>original: "magnitude"</note> and become many when it is closely connected with something that is naturally composed of many parts and possesses physical size. In this relationship, the physical object receives the non-physical essence only in the way it is capable of receiving it, not as the non-physical essence truly is. 

Conversely, that which is divisible <note>original: "partible"</note> and composed of many parts is received by that which is naturally indivisible and unified. It is received in an indivisible and non-plural way, and it is in this manner that it is present with it. That is to say: the indivisible is present without parts, without plurality, and without a specific location in space—according to its own nature—even when it is with that which is divisible, naturally multiple, and exists in space. However, the thing that is divisible, multiplied, and spatial is present with the indivisible essence in a divisible, multiple, and local way.

Therefore, when studying these different types of natures, it is necessary to maintain and not confuse the unique characteristics of each. More importantly, we should not imagine or believe that non-physical things have the properties that belong to bodies, or anything of that kind. After all, no one would ever mistakenly attribute the characteristics of a truly non-physical essence to a physical body. We are all familiar with bodies, but the knowledge of non-physical natures is achieved with great difficulty. Because we are unable to perceive them directly with our minds <note>original: "intuitively"</note>, we are filled with doubt about their nature; and this doubt remains as long as we are ruled by our imagination <note>In this context, the faculty of the mind that visualizes physical shapes and spatial dimensions</note>.

Thus, you should say: If that which exists in space is outside of itself, or has departed from itself, through...

<summary>Porphyry explains that non-physical (incorporeal) essences exist within the physical world without being divided by space, remaining whole in every part of a physical body. He warns that we often fail to understand these spiritual truths because we mistakenly apply the laws of physical space and "imagination" to things that are indivisible.</summary>

<keywords>incorporeal, intelligible nature, impartible, magnitude, spatial interval, Neoplatonism, Porphyry, philosophy of mind, indivisibility</keywords>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of how the non-physical (intellectual) world interacts with the physical world of size and matter. It argues that while physical things are divided into parts, the intellectual essence remains whole and unified even when it is present within those divided parts.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>

<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

Having proceeded into physical size <note>original: "bulk"</note>, that which is intellectual <note>original: "intelligible"</note> does not exist in a specific place. It remains within itself because it has not transitioned into physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> extension. Therefore, if the former (the physical) is an image, the latter (the intellectual) is the original pattern <note>original: "archetype"</note>. 

The physical world derives its existence through the intellectual world, but the intellectual world exists independently, in and through itself. For every physical image is a reflection <note>original: "image"</note> of the higher mind <note>original: "intellect"</note>. It is also necessary, when remembering the unique qualities of both of these, that we should not be surprised at the difference <note>original: "discrepance"</note> that occurs when they come together <note>original: "congress"</note>—if, indeed, "coming together" is even the right word to use in this case. We are not looking at the meeting of physical bodies, but the meeting of things that are entirely distinct from each other in their fundamental nature <note>original: "hypostasis," referring to the essential reality or substance of a thing</note>. 

Because of this, their coming together is different from anything normally observed in things that share the same basic nature. Therefore, this relationship is not a physical blending <note>original: "temperament"</note>, or a mixture, or a joining <note>original: "conjunction"</note>, or a side-by-side placement <note>original: "apposition"</note>. Instead, it exists in a way that is different from all of these. It appears in every interaction between things of the same substance <note>original: "consubstantial natures"</note>, however that may happen, but it goes beyond everything that can be understood by the senses. 

Consequently, an intellectual essence <note>original: "intelligible essence"</note> is entirely present, without any physical distance <note>original: "interval"</note>, within every part of a physical object—even if those parts were infinite in number. It is not present by being divided into pieces, giving one piece to one part and another to another. Nor, when it is multiplied, does it give itself away in many parts to a crowd of things. Instead, it is fully present within the parts of that which has physical size <note>original: "extended into bulk"</note>, within each individual thing, and throughout the entire mass, in an indivisible <note>original: "impartibly"</note> way, without becoming many, but remaining as a single unit <note>original: "numerically one"</note>. 

However, those physical things whose power is scattered into different parts can only experience it in a divided <note>original: "partibly"</note> and distributed way. Because of a flaw in their own nature, these physical things often imitate <note>original: "counterfeit"</note> an intellectual essence. This leads to doubts about the true nature of that essence, as it seems to have changed from its own nature into the nature of the physical things.

36. Truly-existing being is neither large nor small,

<summary>Porphyry explains that the intellectual (intelligible) world does not occupy space and interacts with the physical world without being divided, remaining a single, unified whole even when present in many physical parts. He cautions that this "meeting" of the physical and non-physical is unique and cannot be compared to physical mixing or blending.</summary>

<keywords>intelligible, corporeal extension, archetype, intellect, hypostasis, consubstantial, essence, impartibly, Neoplatonism, Porphyry</keywords>

--- Page 248 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that "True Being"—the non-physical, intellectual reality—is not restricted by physical dimensions. He explains how this reality can be present in both large and small objects simultaneously because it does not possess physical size itself.</meta>

<page-num>225</page-num>

<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

For greatness and smallness <note>original: "magnitude and parvitude"</note> are properly the characteristics of physical size <note>original: "bulk"</note>. But true reality <note>original: "true being"</note> transcends both greatness and smallness; it is above both the largest and the smallest things. It is numerically one and the same, even though it is found to be shared <note>original: "participated"</note> simultaneously by everything that is greatest and everything that is smallest. 

Therefore, you must not think of it as something that is physically "greatest," for then you would wonder how something so large could be present in the smallest objects without being shrunk or compressed. Nor should you think of it as something "smallest," because you would then wonder how something so tiny could be present in the largest masses without being multiplied, increased, or having more added to it. 

Instead, by understanding that the greatest physical size receives that which transcends even the greatest volume, and the smallest size receives that which transcends even the smallest <gloss>e</gloss>, you will be able to conceive how the same thing, remaining as it is within itself, can be seen simultaneously in any given size, and in infinite multitudes and physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> masses. By its very nature, it is present throughout the vast size of the world without being divided into parts <note>original: "impartibly"</note> and without having any physical size itself. 

It also precedes <note>original: "antecedes"</note> the physical volume of the world and encompasses every part of it within its own indivisible nature. Conversely, the world—through its multitude of parts—is present in many different ways to this true reality as far as it is able. Yet the world cannot fully contain <note>original: "comprehend"</note> this reality, neither with its entire physical mass nor its entire power. Instead, the world encounters it in every part as something infinite and unsurpassable—not only in this regard but also in others.

---

<gloss>e</gloss> In the original Greek, this reads: "But taking at once that which transcends the greatest bulk into the greatest, and that which transcends the least into the least, etc." <note>original: "αλλα το εκβεβηκος του μεγιστου ογκου, εις το μεγιστον, και του ελαχιστου εις το ελαχιστον, αμα λαβων, κ.τ.λ."</note> Holstenius <note>Lucas Holstenius (1596–1661), a German humanist and geographer</note> most erroneously translates this as: "But that which exceeds the greatest mass by the greatest interval, and the smallest by the smallest, taking them together, etc." <note>original Latin: "Verum id quod maximam molem intervallo maximo, et minimam minimo excedit simul sumens, &c."</note> This is because a truly non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note> nature, such as the one Porphyry is discussing here, has nothing to do with physical space or distance <note>original: "interval"</note>. Therefore, it does not surpass the greatest or least physical mass by any distance, but is instead present in a transcendent way within both the greatest and the smallest sizes.

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s explanation of how "True Being"—the non-physical, intellectual reality—interacts with the physical world. He argues that spiritual power is the opposite of physical size: while physical objects gain size by spreading out and losing strength, spiritual reality gains power by remaining unified and concentrated.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>

<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE</header>

...because true reality <note>original: "truly-existing being"</note> is entirely free from all physical extension.

37. That which is larger in physical mass <note>original: "bulk"</note> possesses less power when compared, not with similar physical things, but with things of a different kind or essence. For physical mass is, in a sense, a thing's departure from its own center and a division of its power into tiny fragments. Therefore, whatever excels in power is completely separate from physical mass. For power that turns inward <note>i.e., concentrates on its own nature</note> is filled with itself; by strengthening itself, it gains its true power. Because of this, a physical body that expands into mass through a loss <note>original: "diminution"</note> of power is as distant from true non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note> being as true reality is from being depleted by physical size. True reality remains in the greatness of its own power precisely because it is free from physical mass. 

Just as true reality, when compared to a physical mass, lacks size and mass; so too a physical object, when compared to true reality, is weak <note>original: "imbecile"</note> and powerless. For that which is "greatest" in terms of power is exempt from all physical mass. Thus, although the world exists everywhere and—as the saying goes—encounters true reality which is also everywhere, the world cannot grasp or contain the vastness of that power. 

The world encounters true reality, which is not present in separate parts <note>original: "partibly"</note>, but is present without physical size and without any specific limits. Therefore, the presence of true reality in the world is not "local" (restricted to a specific place) but occurs through assimilation—insofar as it is possible for a body to become like something non-physical, and for that non-physical nature to be observed within a body that has become like it. 

Consequently, a non-physical nature is *not* present in a body to the degree that matter cannot become like a perfectly immaterial nature; yet it *is* present to the degree that a physical essence can become like a non-physical one. However, this interaction does not happen through physical "reception" (as if one were a container for the other); if it did, both would be degraded <note>original: "corrupted"</note>. For the material object, in physically receiving an immaterial nature, would be destroyed;

<summary>Porphyry argues that spiritual power is found in concentration rather than physical size, explaining that the non-physical "True Being" is present in the world through likeness rather than occupying a physical location.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, incorporeal, bulk vs power, assimilation, immaterial nature, true being, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's discussion of the relationship between non-physical reality ("True Being") and the physical world. He argues that while they interact through "assimilation," they remain fundamentally different in nature: one is unified and powerful, while the other is divided and weak.</meta>

<page-num>227</page-num>

<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

...through being changed into it; and the non-physical <note>original: "immaterial"</note> nature would become physical matter. Interactions <note>original: "Assimilations"</note>, therefore, and the sharing of powers—as well as the lack of power—move between these things which are so different in essence from each other. The physical world, therefore, is very far from possessing the power of true reality <note>original: "real being"</note>; and true reality is very remote from the weakness <note>original: "imbecility"</note> of a physical nature. But that which exists between these two, making things similar and being made similar itself, joins the two extremes to each other. This becomes the cause of confusion regarding those extremes, because it makes the one look like the other through this process of similarity.

38. True Reality <note>original: "Truly-existing being"</note> is said to be many things, but not by existing in different places, nor by the measurements of physical mass <note>original: "bulk"</note>, nor by a piling up of parts <note>original: "coacervation"</note>, nor by the boundaries or graspings <gloss>f</gloss> of parts that can be divided. Instead, it is "many" through a difference that is non-physical, without physical mass, and without a mere numerical plurality; its diversity is divided according to its own variety. Hence, it is also "one"—not as a single body is one, nor as if it were in one place, nor as one mass, nor as a single thing made of many parts. It is diverse precisely because it is one, and its difference is both divided and united at the same time. For its difference is not acquired from the outside, nor is it added on <note>original: "adscititious"</note>, nor obtained through participating in something else; rather, it is many things from within itself. For, while remaining one, it acts with every possible energy, because through its own sameness, it creates all difference. This is not like what we see in physical bodies, where we distinguish one thing from another. On the contrary, in bodies, unity exists within difference because diversity is their primary state <note>original: "precedaneous existence"</note>, while any unity they contain is derived from the outside and added on. But in True Reality, unity and sameness come first; difference is only generated because this unity is active. *Therefore, true reality is multiplied while remaining indivisible <note>original: "impartibility"</note>; but a physical body is made into a unity only through a collection of parts and physical mass.* The

---

<margin>
f For *distinctions* <note>original Greek: διαληψεσιν</note> here, I read *comprehensions* <note>original Greek: καταληψεσιν</note>, and the scholar Lucas Holstenius also uses "comprehensions" <note>original Latin: *comprehensionibus*</note> in this passage.
</margin>

<summary>Porphyry explains that True Reality (the non-physical realm) possesses an inherent, active diversity that does not rely on physical parts or space. He contrasts this with the physical world, where things are naturally divided and only achieve unity through external means.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, True Being, immaterial essence, unity and diversity, Holstenius, physical mass vs. spiritual power</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of point 38, discussing the distinction between the unified nature of true reality and the divided nature of physical existence, before moving into point 39 regarding the nature of the soul.</meta>

<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES</header>

The former is established within itself, existing in itself according to unity; but the latter is never self-contained, because it receives its essential nature <note>original: "hypostasis"</note> through an extension of existence. The former, therefore, is a single, pure activity <note>original: "all-energetic one"</note>; but the latter is a unified plurality <note>original: "multitude"</note>. Hence, it is necessary to investigate how the former is both one and diverse, and how the latter is both a plurality and a unity. We must not apply the unique characteristics of the one to those which belong to the other.

39. It is not proper to think that the plurality of souls was created because of the plurality of bodies; instead, it is necessary to admit that before bodies existed, there were many souls, and one "Whole Soul" which is the cause of the many. The single, whole soul does not prevent many souls from existing within it; nor do the many souls divide the one soul among themselves. For they are distinct from, but not cut off <note>original: "abscinded"</note> from the soul that exists as a whole; nor do they break this whole soul into small pieces for themselves. They are also present with each other without being mixed up, nor do they create the whole soul by being piled together <note>original: "coacervation"</note>. For they are not separated from each other by any physical borders; nor, again, are they confused with each other. This is just as many areas of knowledge <note>original: "sciences"</note> are not mixed up within a single soul that possesses them. For these areas of knowledge do not exist in the soul like physical objects, as if they had a different nature <note>original: "essence"</note> from the soul; rather, they are specific activities <note>original: "energies"</note> of the soul.

For the nature of the soul possesses a boundless power. Everything that occurs within it is also soul; and all souls are, in a certain way, one. Again, the soul which exists as a whole is different from all the rest. For just as bodies—even if divided infinitely—do not end in that which is non-physical <note>original: "incorporeal"</note>, but only result in pieces of different sizes, so too the soul, being a living structure <note>original: "vital form"</note>, can be understood to consist of forms without end <note>original: "ad infinitum"</note>. For it possesses distinct characteristics, and the whole of it exists both with and without these. For, if there is in the soul that which is, as it were, a part divided from the...

<summary>Porphyry argues that souls are not divided like physical matter; instead, many individual souls can exist within a single "Whole Soul" without being confused or physically separated, much like different areas of knowledge exist within one mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s unity and its relationship with the body, specifically how the soul maintains its integrity even when associated with physical matter.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>

<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

...rest of the parts; yet, even while there is difference, the sameness remains. In physical bodies, difference predominates over sameness. Even so, when a non-physical element joins with a body, it does not break the body’s union; instead, all the parts remain essentially united and are only divided by qualities and other physical forms. If this is true for bodies, what should we think about a specific non-physical life? In such a life, sameness is more prevalent than difference; nothing foreign to its form is added to it, and the very union of physical bodies is derived from it. 

The body does not break the soul's internal unity when they become connected, even though the body hinders the soul's activities <note>original: "energies"</note> in many ways. The sameness of the soul produces and reveals all things through itself—through its own specific activity, which extends to infinity. Any part of the soul is capable of accomplishing everything once it is liberated and purified from its connection with bodies, just as any part of a seed possesses the power of the entire seed. 

When a seed is united with matter, it dominates that matter according to the productive principles the seed contains. The whole seed, with its power collected into one, possesses the full extent of its power in every one of its parts. In the same way, in the immaterial soul, even what might be thought of as a "part" possesses the power of the whole soul. 

However, the part of the soul that leans toward <note>original: "verges to"</note> matter is overcome by the physical form it moves toward. Yet it is still naturally suited to associate with immaterial form—even while connected to matter—whenever it withdraws from material nature and turns back toward itself. By leaning toward matter, the soul becomes lacking in all things and suffers a loss of its own inherent power. But when it is raised toward the higher intellect, it is found to possess a fullness <note>original: "plenitude"</note> of all its powers. Therefore, those who first understood this fullness of the soul appropriately described its emptiness as *poverty* and its fullness as *satiety*. <note>Porphyry is likely referencing the myth of Poros (Plenty) and Penia (Poverty) from Plato’s *Symposium*, used here to describe the soul's state when separated from or united with divine intellect.</note>

<summary>Porphyry explains that the soul possesses a universal power in all its parts, similar to a seed, and that its apparent weakness arises only when it "leans" toward matter, losing its inherent fullness.</summary>
<keywords>soul, matter, intellect, poverty, satiety, unity, seed analogy, incorporeal life</keywords>
