<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of the nature of the soul and immaterial reality, specifically how "true being" differs from the physical world by being both a unified whole and present everywhere simultaneously.</meta>

<page-num>230</page-num>
<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE</header>

### SECTION III.

40. The ancients, wishing to show us the unique nature of immaterial <note>original: "incorporeal"</note> being—as far as words can describe it—stated that while it is "one," it is also "all things." By this, they meant that it is not just one specific thing among those we perceive with our senses. However, we might assume this immaterial "one" is simply different from physical objects <note>original: "sensibles"</note> because we do not see this "total unity" (which is all things contained in one) within the physical world. Because this unity *is* all things, the ancients added that it is *one as purely one*. They did this so we might understand that what constitutes "all things" in true reality is something simple <note>original: "uncompounded"</note>, helping us move away from the idea of a mere collection <term>original: "coacervation"; a heap or accumulation of parts</term>. 

Likewise, when they say it is everywhere, they add that it is nowhere. When they claim it is in all things, they add that it is not contained within any specific thing. In the same way, when they say it is in all things—and in every divisible nature capable of receiving it—they add that it is "a whole within a whole." In short, they explain it to us through opposite characteristics, using both at the same time. They do this so we can purge from our minds the false ideas derived from physical bodies, which hide the true, knowable qualities of real being.

41. Once you have accepted the idea of an eternal essence—infinite in its own power—and begin to intellectually grasp an underlying reality <term>original: "hypostasis"</term> that is tireless, untamable, and never-failing, but rather rising above the most pure and 

---

^8 In the original Greek, the phrase is "according to something of those known by sense" <note>original: "καθό τι τῶν κατ' αἴσθησιν συνηγνωσμένων"</note>; but it appears to me to be necessary, after "according to," to insert the words "is not." For immaterial being is not like any one thing known by the senses, because no physical thing is simultaneously "one" and "all things." Holstenius <note>Lucas Holstenius (1596–1661), a noted scholar and geographer</note> did not see the need for this correction, as is clear from his translation of the passage.

<summary>Porphyry explains that ancient philosophers used paradoxical language—such as saying a thing is both everywhere and nowhere—to describe immaterial reality without relying on physical metaphors. He argues that "true being" is a simple unity that contains all things, unlike the physical world where parts are merely heaped together.</summary>

...genuine life, and full from itself; and which is likewise established in itself, satisfied with, and seeking nothing but itself: — to this essence, if you add an existence in a specific location <term>original: "subsistence in place"</term>, or a relation to a certain thing, at the same time that you [appear to] diminish it—by ascribing to it a dependency on place, or a relative condition of being—you do not [in reality] diminish this essence. Instead, you separate yourself from the perception of it by accepting your own imagination <note>original: "phantasy"</note> as a veil that covers your theoretical understanding <note>original: "conjectural apprehension"</note> of it. For you cannot move beyond, or stop, or make more perfect, or effect even the least change in a thing of this kind, because it is impossible for it to be in the slightest degree deficient. Indeed, it is much more never-failing than any perpetually flowing fountain could ever be imagined to be. 

If, however, you are unable to keep pace with it and to become aligned with the universal intellect <note>original: "intelligible all"</note>, you should not investigate anything pertaining to real being; or, if you do, you will deviate from the path that leads to it and will look toward something else. But if you investigate nothing else—being established in yourself and your own essence—you will be aligned with the intelligible universe and will not cling to anything lesser than it <note>original: "posterior to it"</note>. 

Therefore, you should not say, "I am of a great magnitude." For by letting go of this idea of physical size, you will become universal—though you were already universal before this. However, when something else was present with you alongside the universal, you became "less" by that addition; this is because the addition was not made from truly-existing being. For you cannot add anything to true being. When, therefore, anything is added from non-being <note>the realm of the physical and transitory which lacks true, eternal existence</note>, a place is opened for Poverty <note>original: "Poverty" or *Penia*; in Neoplatonism, this refers to a state of lack or deficiency inherent in the material world</note> to become your associate, accompanied by a lack of all things. Hence, by dismissing non-being, you will then become sufficient to yourself<sup>h</sup>. For he will not properly return to his true self who does not dismiss those things...

<note><sup>h</sup> Immediately after this, something is missing in the original (as is evident from the asterisks in the text), which, as it appears to me, no logical guess can appropriately supply.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that true, eternal essence is self-sufficient and perfect; we fail to perceive it when we try to limit it to a physical location or define it by material "greatness." By stripping away the "non-being" of the material world, the soul returns to its universal, self-sufficient state.</summary>

<page-num>232</page-num>

...of a more degraded and humble nature, and who believes himself to be something naturally insignificant, rather than what he truly is. In doing so, he simultaneously distances himself from both his own nature and from true reality <note>original: "truly-existing being"</note>. However, when someone is present with that which is within themselves, they are also present with true being, which is everywhere. But when you withdraw from yourself, you also move away from real being. It is of such great importance for a person to be present with what is within them—that is, with their rational mind <note>original: "rational part"</note>—and to be distant from what is external to them.

However, if true being is present with us, while "non-being" <term>In Neoplatonism, "non-being" often refers to the material world or things that lack the permanent, unchanging reality of the divine</term> is absent, and if real being does not exist in conjunction with things of a foreign nature, it does not "arrive" in order to be present. Instead, we are the ones who depart from it when it seems not to be present. And why should this be considered surprising? Even you, when present, are not "absent" from yourself; yet you are not truly present with yourself, even though you are there. You are both present with and absent from yourself when you observe external things and neglect to behold yourself. 

Therefore, if you are present in body but not truly present with yourself—and for this reason are ignorant of yourself—you will discover things that are remote from your essence more easily than you discover yourself, even though you are naturally present with yourself. Why, then, should you wonder if the divine—which is not present to you—is remote from you, when you have become remote from yourself? 

The more you are truly present with yourself (even though true being is already present and inseparably joined to you), the more you will be present with real being. It is so essentially united to you that it is as impossible for it to be severed <note>original: "divulged"</note> from you as it is for you to be separated from yourself. Thus, it is universally possible to know what is present with real being and what is absent from it, even though it is everywhere present and, at the same time, nowhere. For those who are able to proceed...

<summary>Porphyry explains that "true being" (spiritual reality) is always present within us, but we lose sight of it by focusing on external things and thinking of ourselves as merely physical or insignificant beings.</summary>

<page-num>233</page-num>

->### PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.<-

Those who proceed into their own essence intellectually and obtain a knowledge of it will—through that knowledge and the science accompanying it—be able to recover or regain themselves. This happens through the union of the knower with the thing being known. For those who are present with themselves, truly-existing being will also be present. But for those who abandon their own true being for the sake of external things, true being will be absent, just as they are absent from themselves.

If, however, we are naturally suited to be established in this same essence—to be wealthy from within ourselves rather than descending into what we are not—then by descending, we become "in want" of ourselves. We thus associate with Poverty, even though Porus <note>original: "Porus [or Plenty]"</note> is present. <margin>i</margin> And if we are cut off from real being (from which we are not actually separated by physical place, essence, or anything else), it is only through our turning toward "non-being" <term>In Neoplatonism, "non-being" refers to the material world, which lacks the eternal, unchanging reality of the divine realm.</term>. As a just punishment for abandoning true being, we suffer a departure from ourselves and an ignorance of our own nature. 

Yet, by giving proper attention to our essence, we recover ourselves and become united to divinity. It is, therefore, rightly said that the soul is confined in the body as if in a prison, and is detained there in chains like a fugitive slave. <margin>k</margin> We should, however, earnestly endeavor to be liberated from these bonds. For by turning toward these sensory objects, we desert ourselves, even though we are of a divine origin and are—as Empedocles says—

> Heaven’s exiles, straying from the orb of light.

***

<margin>i</margin> In the original Greek, the text reads: *and through these things again to be with poverty, even though he [it] is present* <note>original: και δια τουτων παλιν τη πενια συνειναι, καιπερ παροντος αυτου</note>. However, instead of "of him" <note>original: αυτου</note>, I read it as "of Porus" <note>original: πορου</note>. It appears to me that Porphyry is here alluding to what is said by Diotima in Plato’s *Symposium* <note>original: "Banquet of Plato"</note> concerning the parents of Love: that they are *Poverty* and *Porus*, or *Plenty*.

<margin>k</margin> See the *Phaedo* of Plato. However, something is missing from the original text here, as is evident not only from the asterisks but from the lack of connection in the words themselves.

<summary>Porphyry argues that the soul regains its divine nature by turning away from the material world and inward toward its own essence. He utilizes Platonic and Pythagorean imagery, describing the soul as a divine exile currently imprisoned within the physical body.</summary>

<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

So every corrupt life is full of slavery; for this reason, it is without God and is unjust, because the spirit within it is full of impiety and, consequently, injustice. Thus, it is rightly said that justice consists in the performance of that which is the proper work of the one who performs it <note>original: "performance of that which is the province of him who performs it." This is a reference to Plato’s definition of justice in the *Republic*: each part of the soul or society doing its own specific job.</note>. The image of true justice also consists in giving to each of those with whom we live exactly what they deserve.

42. Consider something that exists only within another thing (that is, something different from itself) and does not have its own independent essence. If it were to turn inward to know itself by pulling away from that which sustains it, it would be destroyed by that knowledge because it would be separating itself from its own essence. However, if something is able to know itself without the subject in which it exists, and can withdraw from that subject without being destroyed, it cannot be defined by that from which it is capable of turning away. It can know itself through its own independent activities <note>original: "energies," from the Greek *energeia*, meaning the active operation or functioning of a thing.</note> without being ruined. 

Therefore, if sight and every other sensory power can neither perceive itself nor sustain itself when separated from the body; but the intellect <term>In Neoplatonism, the "intellect" (or *Nous*) is the highest part of the soul, capable of direct, non-physical understanding of eternal truths.</term> perceives most clearly, turns inward toward itself, and is not destroyed specifically when it separates from the body—it is evident that the senses gain their power to act through the body, but the intellect possesses its activities and its essence within itself, not in the body.

43. Some non-physical <note>original: "Incorporeal"</note> things are correctly named and understood based on a lack of a body. For example, according to the ancient philosophers, matter, the form found in matter, and also physical natures and powers are understood by mentally stripping away the body. Place, time, and the boundaries of things are understood in the same way. All such things are called non-physical because of a lack of physical substance. There are likewise other things which are called non-physical in an imprecise sense, not just because they lack...

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's exploration of the intellect's independence from the physical body. <header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

body, but, in short, because they are not naturally designed to produce a physical body <note>original: "generate body." In Neoplatonic thought, higher principles "generate" lower ones. Porphyry is explaining that the highest things are so far removed from matter that they do not directly create physical form.</note>. Consequently, those things described in the first sense exist within bodies; but those of the second sense are perfectly separated from bodies, and from those incorporeal natures which exist alongside bodies. For physical bodies exist in a specific place, and their boundaries are contained within the body itself. But the intellect, and intellectual reason, exist neither in a place nor in a body. They do not immediately give existence to bodies, nor do they exist together with bodies, or with those incorporeal things that are defined merely by the absence of a body. 

Therefore, even if we were to imagine an incorporeal vacuum <term>vacuum</term> <note>original: "vacuum." In ancient physics, this refers to a "void" or empty space. Porphyry argues that the intellect is not "in" space at all, even an empty one.</note> existing, it would still be impossible for the intellect to be inside that vacuum. For while a vacuum may be able to receive a physical body, it is impossible for it to receive the intellect or provide a "place" for its activity. However, since the category of "incorporeal nature" appears to be twofold, the <term>followers of Zeno</term> <note>The Stoics, who believed that everything that exists is ultimately a form of matter.</note> do not acknowledge the first kind at all, but only accept the second. Perceiving that the first kind is not like the second, they reject it entirely; however, they ought instead to realize that it belongs to a different class of existence, rather than imagining that it does not exist simply because it is not like the material version.

44. Intellect and the "intelligible" <term>intelligible</term> <note>original: "intelligible," or *noeton* in Greek. This refers to things that can only be understood by the mind, like mathematical truths or divine forms, as opposed to "sensible" things perceived by the eyes or ears.</note> are one thing, while the senses and that which is perceived by the senses are another. The intelligible is joined with the intellect, just as the sensible is joined with the senses. However, the senses cannot grasp themselves through their own power * * *. But the intelligible, which—

---

¹ That is, they are not designed to be the immediate causes of a body, because they are perfectly separated from it. The original Greek is: <note>original: ἤδη δὲ ἦν ἀλλὰ καταχρηστικῶς λεγόμενα ἀσώματα, οὐ κατὰ στέρησιν σώματος, κατὰ δὲ ὅλως μὴ πεφυκέναι γεννᾶν σῶμα.</note> "Indeed, there were things called incorporeal in a loose sense, not because they lacked a body, but because they were not at all designed by nature to produce a body." Holstenius <note>Lucas Holstenius (1596–1661), a German humanist and geographer who translated Porphyry.</note>, not understanding what Porphyry is saying here, translates the Greek words as: <note>original: "sed quod nullum omnino corpus generare possunt."</note> "but because they can generate no body at all." For Porphyry, as is evident from what follows immediately, is speaking here of natures which are perfectly separated from bodies. Therefore, they are not naturally designed to be the immediate creators of them—not because they lack the ability, but because their power is so vastly superior <note>original: "transcendency of power."</note>.

<summary>Porphyry distinguishes between different types of incorporeal things, arguing that the intellect exists entirely outside of space and physical bodies, contrary to Stoic belief. He explains that the intellect's separation from matter is a result of its superior power, not a lack of ability.</summary>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s argument that the intellect is a non-physical reality. He is moving from the idea that the intellect is not in a physical "place" to explaining how the intellect perceives itself and the objects of thought.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>

<header>AUXILIARIES TO THE</header>

[The soul] is joined with the intellect, and the intellect—which is joined with the **intelligible** <note>original: "the intelligible." In Neoplatonism, this refers to the realm of pure ideas or truths that can only be known by the mind, not the physical senses.</note>—in no way falls under the perception of the senses. The intellect, however, can be understood by the intellect. But if the intellect is the object of its own understanding, then the intellect will be its own intelligible object. If, therefore, the intellect is an intellectual object rather than a physical one, it will be "intelligible." But if it is understandable to the intellect and not to the senses, it will also be "intelligent" <note>original: "intelligent." Here, this means the capacity to think or the act of perceiving intellectually.</note>. 

The same thing, therefore, will be both the "thinker" (that which intellectually perceives) and the "thought" (that which is intellectually perceived). This is true of the whole intellect in relation to the whole; it is not like a person who rubs and a person who is rubbed <note>Porphyry is contrasting physical touch, which requires two separate parts or bodies, with the mind, which is a single, unified whole.</note>. The intellect, therefore, does not think with one part while being thought of by another part. This is because it is **indivisible** <term>impartible</term>, and the whole is an object of thought to the whole. 

It is likewise entirely intellect, having nothing within itself that can be imagined as lacking intelligence. Hence, it does not think with one part of itself while the other part remains idle. <note>original: διὸ οὐχὶ τόδε μὲν ἑαυτοῦ νοεῖ, τόδε δὲ οὐ νοεῖ. Translated: "Therefore it does not think of one part of itself, while not thinking of another."</note> <sup>m</sup> For if it did not think, it would be unintelligent. Furthermore, it does not move from one thought to another. For when it leaves one thought behind, it would no longer be perceiving that thing. But if there is no transition or "movement" in its thoughts, it must perceive all things at once. If, therefore, it understands all things at once—not thinking of this thing now and another thing later—it understands all things instantaneously and eternally. * * * * <sup>n</sup>

Consequently, if all things are perceived by it instantaneously, its perceptions have nothing to do with the past or the future—

---

<sup>m</sup> In the original Greek: *Therefore it does not think of one part of itself, while not thinking of another,* <note>original: διὸ οὐχὶ τόδε μὲν ἑαυτοῦ νοεῖ, τόδε δὲ οὐ νοεῖ</note> which Holstenius erroneously translates as, "*And so it does not indeed understand one part of itself, but another part it does not understand.*" <note>original Latin: "Ideoque non quidem unam sui partem intelligit, alteram vero non intelligit."</note> Porphyry is not speaking here of the intellect looking at its own parts, but of its nature being entirely active and "intellective." This is clear from what follows immediately.

<sup>n</sup> The asterisks in the original indicate that some text is missing. Nevertheless, what follows is clearly connected to what came before.

<summary>Porphyry explains that the intellect is a unified, indivisible whole that perceives itself and all truths simultaneously rather than through sequential or physical processes.</summary>
<keywords>intellect, intelligible, self-perception, indivisibility, simultaneity, Porphyry, Neoplatonism, Holstenius</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of how the Intellect (the divine mind) differs from the Soul. The previous page established that the Intellect is indivisible and perceives itself as a whole. This page moves into the distinction between the timelessness of the Intellect and the time-bound nature of the Soul.</meta>

<page-num>237</page-num>

<header>PERCEPTION OF INTELLIGIBLE NATURES.</header>

...the future, but exist in an indivisible, timeless *now*. Therefore, everything that happens at once—both in terms of many things existing together and in terms of time—is present within the intellect. Consequently, all things exist within the intellect as a single unity and in one state, without gaps and without time. 

If this is the case, there is nothing wandering or transitional <note>original: "discursive or transitive." In philosophy, "discursive" reasoning means moving step-by-step from one idea to the next, like a logical argument, whereas the Intellect perceives all truth instantly.</note> in its thoughts; consequently, they are without motion. They are therefore activities <term>energies</term> that occur as one, existing in one, without growth, change, or any transition. If, however, the many exist as a unity, and this activity is gathered together all at once without time, then a nature of this kind must necessarily always exist in an [intelligible] unity. This state is eternity. Therefore, eternity is present within the intellect. 

However, that nature which does not perceive through a single, unified act, but instead through transition and movement—leaving one idea to grasp another, dividing its focus and proceeding step-by-step—is the soul. This nature exists in conjunction with time. For with this kind of motion, the future and the past exist together. But the soul, in changing its thoughts, passes from one thing to another. It is not that the previous thoughts leave and new ones take their place; rather, there is a transition of the former thoughts while they remain in the soul, and the latter arrive as if from somewhere else. 

They do not, in reality, arrive from another place. Instead, they appear to do so because of the soul's own movement, and because the soul’s "eye" is directed to observe the different forms <note>In Neoplatonism, "Forms" are the eternal blueprints or ideas of all things.</note> it contains, which act as parts of its whole essence. For the soul resembles a fountain that does not flow outward, but instead scatters its streams in a circle back into itself. Therefore, time exists alongside the motion of the soul, while eternity exists alongside the intellect's remaining permanent within itself <note>original: "See the fourth book of my translation of Proclus’s commentary on the *Timaeus* of Plato, in which the nature of time and eternity is most admirably [explained]."</note>. It is not, however, divided...

<summary>Porphyry distinguishes between the Intellect, which exists in a timeless "now" (eternity), and the Soul, which moves step-by-step through thoughts (time).</summary>
<keywords>intellect, soul, eternity, time, discursive thought, Timaeus, Proclus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s discussion on the relationship between time and eternity. He explains how the soul's movement creates the experience of time, which serves as a moving image of the Intellect's eternal, unified state.</meta>

<page-num>238</page-num>

<header>AUXILIARIES, ETC.</header>

...from the intellect in the same way that time is derived from the soul; because in the intellect, the essences that exist together as one are unified. But that which is constantly in motion creates a false impression of eternity, because the immeasurable extent of its motion produces a concept of eternity. Furthermore, that which remains [in one state] is mistakenly thought to be the same as that which is [constantly] in motion. 

For that which is constantly in motion unfolds its own "now" in the same way it unfolds its time, multiplying it according to a progression through time. Therefore, some have understood that time should be observed in states of rest just as much as in motion. They have also thought that eternity, as we have said, is simply infinite time—just as if each of these concepts gave its own properties to the other. In this view, time (which is always moving) casts a faint image <note>original: "adumbrating"</note> of eternity through its own never-ending nature and the consistency of its motion; meanwhile, eternity (being established in a consistent activity <term>energy</term>) <note>original: "energy." Here, "energy" refers to the Greek *energeia*, meaning the active, lived reality or "actualization" of a being.</note> becomes similar to time because of its own permanence.

In the world of sensible things <note>Things perceived by the senses rather than the mind.</note>, however, the time of one thing is distinct from that of another. For instance, there is one time for the sun, and another for the moon; one time for the morning star <note>The planet Venus.</note>, and another for each of the planets. Because of this, each planet has a different year. 

The "Great Year," which encompasses all these different times, reaches its highest point <note>original: "terminates as in a summit"</note> in the motion of the Soul of the Universe. It is by imitating this universal soul that the celestial spheres are moved. However, because the motion of this universal soul is of a different nature than that of the planets, its time is also different from theirs. The time of the planets exists with intervals and is distinguished from the universal soul by their specific movements and transitions through space.

---

...unfolded. See also my translation of Plotinus, *On Eternity and Time*. In these works, what both of these divine men have said about eternity, and what the former has said about time, contains, as it appears to me, the ultimate limit <note>original: *ne plus ultra*</note> of philosophical investigation on these most difficult subjects.

# APPENDIX.

->ON<-

## THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.

---

In my *History of the Restoration of the Platonic Theology* (see Volume 2 of my work, *Proclus on Euclid*) and in a note accompanying my translation of Porphyry's treatise, *On the Cave of the Nymphs*, I tried to reveal the hidden meaning of the wanderings of Ulysses as told by Homer. I based this on hints provided by Porphyry and a work by an anonymous Greek writer titled *On the Wanderings of Ulysses* <note>original: *De Ulyxis Erroribus*</note>. However, because I have continuously studied the philosophy of Plato for over forty years, I now understand that philosophy much better than I did then. Since thirty-five years have passed since that earlier work, I will try once more to explain these wanderings, discarding some of my previous ideas and keeping others.

First, it is important to note that Ulysses is not ranked among the highest heroic figures. In other words, he was not one of those heroes who descend into the mortal realm at specific times. Such souls descend not just because of the necessity that causes all "partial souls" <term>In Neoplatonism, "partial souls" refers to individual human souls that can become separated from the divine whole and descend into physical bodies</term>—like our own—to descend periodically, but also to help others and lead them back to their original state of perfection. Therefore, he was not nearly as elevated a hero as Hercules, Pythagoras, Socrates, or Plato; for they greatly benefited—

<sig>R</sig>
<meta>Catchword: benefited</meta>

<summary>In this appendix, Thomas Taylor announces a revised Platonic interpretation of Ulysses' travels, distinguishing Ulysses as an ordinary soul rather than a divine "descending" hero like Socrates or Hercules.</summary>
<keywords>Ulysses, Homer, Platonic Theology, Porphyry, Cave of the Nymphs, Thomas Taylor, partial souls, reincarnation, Greek mythology</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of Ulysses' spiritual status. On the previous page, the author distinguished Ulysses from "divine" heroes like Hercules or Socrates, noting that while divine heroes descend to help others, Ulysses focused on his own return to a state of perfection.</meta>

<page-num>242</page-num>
->ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.<-

...benefited others; but he only benefited himself. For all his companions perished before his arrival at Ithaca. Thus, he was able to save himself, but not others. “For this reason,” says Olympiodorus <note>A 6th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote influential commentaries on Plato's works.</note> in his manuscript notes <note>original: MS. Scholia</note> on the *Gorgias* of Plato, “it is said that Ulysses wandered on the sea by the will of Neptune <note>The Roman name for the Greek god Poseidon.</note>. This signifies that the life of Ulysses was neither earthly <note>original: terrestrial</note>, nor yet heavenly <note>original: celestial</note>, but between these. Since, therefore, Neptune is the lord of the middle natures, it is said that Ulysses wandered through the will of Neptune because he held a Neptunian realm <note>original: allotment</note>. In the same way, theologians speak of the sons of Jupiter <note>Zeus</note>, Neptune, and Pluto <note>Hades</note>, regarding the realm assigned to each. For we say that he who has a divine and heavenly way of life <note>original: polity</note> is the son of Jupiter; he who has an earthly way of life is the son of Pluto; and he is the son of Neptune whose way of life or realm is between these.”² Hence Ulysses, because of his Neptunian realm, was a man who ranked among the middle class of characters—those who rise above the majority of mankind.

Next, in order to understand accurately the hidden <note>original: recondite</note> meaning of the wanderings of Ulysses, it is necessary to know what the most divine and theological poet Homer indicates by the Trojan war in the *Iliad*. For Homer, by combining fiction with historical facts, has delivered to us some very hidden, mystic, and valuable information in those two admirable poems, the *Iliad* and *Odyssey*. Because of this hidden information, ancient writers used a dramatic <note>original: tragical</note> way of speaking to say he was blind. This was because, as—

---

² For this reason, they say that Ulysses wandered the sea by the will of Neptune; for they signify that the Odyssean life was neither earthly, nor yet truly heavenly, but in the middle. Since, therefore, Neptune is the lord of the middle realm, they say Ulysses [wandered] by the will of Neptune because he had the allotment of Neptune. Thus, they say some are sons of Jupiter, some of Neptune, and some of Pluto, according to the allotments of each. For we call the one who has a divine and heavenly state a son of Jupiter; the one with an earthly state, a son of Pluto; and the one in between, a son of Neptune. <note>original: Δια τοι τουτο, και τον Οδυσσεα λεγουσι κατα θαλατταν πλανασθαι βουλη του Ποσειδωνος· σημαινουσι γαρ την Οδυσσειον ζωην, οτι ουδε χθονια ην, αλλ’ουδε μην ετι ουρανια, αλλα μεση· επει ουν ο Ποσειδων του μεταξυ κυριος εστι, δια τουτο και τον Οδυσσεα φασι βουλη Ποσειδωνος [supple πλανασθαι] επειδη τον κληρον του Ποσειδωνος ειχεν· ουτω γουν και τους μεν φασι Διος υιους, τους δε Ποσειδωνος, τους δε Πλουτωνος, προς τους κληρους εκαστου· τον μεν γαρ εχοντα θειαν και ουρανιαν πολιτειαν Διος φαμεν υιον, τον δε χθονιαν, Πλουτωνος, τον δε την μεταξυ Ποσειδωνος.</note>

<summary>This page explains that Ulysses represents a "middle" spiritual state between the earthly and the divine, governed by Neptune. It also suggests that Homer's poetry contains hidden mystical truths, and his legendary blindness refers to his focus on inner, spiritual realities.</summary>
<keywords>Ulysses, Neptune, Olympiodorus, Homer, Neoplatonism, Jupiter, Pluto, allegory, Iliad, Odyssey</keywords>

<page-num>243</page-num>

# ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.

<margin>In Plato’s *Republic* <note>original: Polit.</note>, page 398.</margin>
Proclus observes that he <note>Referring to Homer.</note> separated himself from physical <note>original: sensible</note> beauty and extended the intellect of his soul to invisible and true harmony. He was said, therefore, to be blind because that spiritual <note>original: intellectual</note> beauty to which he raised himself cannot be perceived by physical eyes. In the same way, it is said in tragedies that Orpheus was *torn apart* <note>original: lacerated</note> in many different ways because the men of that age only *partially* shared in his mystical teachings. The *main part* <note>original: principal part</note> of his doctrine, however, was received by the Lesbians; for this reason, his *head*, after being separated from his body, is said to have been carried to the island of Lesbos. Consequently, the Platonic philosopher Hermeas—following this view of the hidden meaning of the *Iliad*—beautifully explains the Trojan War in his commentaries <note>original: Scholia</note> on Plato's *Phaedrus* as follows:

<margin>Instead of "rational souls" (*analogoi psychai*), in this place, it is necessary to read "irrational souls" (*alogoi psychai*).</margin>
> "By *Ilion* <note>The city of Troy.</note>, we must understand the created and material realm, which is named after *mud and matter* <note>original: παρα την ιλυν και την υλην (*para ten ilun kai ten ulen*)</note>, and in which there is war and rebellion. But the Trojans represent material forms and all the forms of life that exist around physical bodies. For this reason, the Trojans are called *native* <note>original: ιθαγενεις (*ithageneis*)</note>. This is because all the lives that exist around bodies, including irrational souls, are devoted and attentive to their own matter. On the contrary, the Greeks are rational souls coming from Greece—that is, from the spiritual <note>original: intelligible</note> realm—into matter. Thus, the Greeks are called *foreigners* <note>original: επηλυδες (*epelydes*)</note> and they defeat the Trojans because they belong to a superior order. But they fight with each other over the image of Helen, just as the poet says regarding the phantom of Aeneas:

<margin>Iliad, Book 5, line 451.</margin>
->Around the phantom, Greeks and Trojans fight.<-

Helen signifies spiritual beauty; she is a certain *vessel* <note>original: ελενη τις ουσα (*helene tis ousa*)</note> that attracts the intellect to itself. An outflow <note>original: efflux</note>, therefore, of this spiritual beauty is granted to matter..."

***

<summary>This page provides a Neoplatonic allegory of the Trojan War, casting the Greeks as rational souls from the spiritual realm and the Trojans as material forms native to the physical world, fighting over the mere image of divine beauty.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Orpheus, Hermeas, Trojan War, Ilion, matter, rational souls, irrational souls, Helen, intelligible beauty, allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic commentary on Homer’s epics, specifically exploring the symbolic meaning of the Trojan War as a struggle for beauty and the soul's eventual return to its divine origin.</meta>

<page-num>244</page-num>

<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>

...through Venus <note>The goddess of love and beauty, representing the attractive force of the physical world.</note>; and about this outflowing <note>original: efflux</note> of beauty, the Greeks fight with the Trojans [that is, rational lives fight with irrational lives $^e$]. And those, indeed, who oppose and defeat matter return to the world of pure intellect <term>intelligible world: the realm of divine ideas and truth, perceivable by the mind rather than the senses</term>, which is their true country. But those who do not—as is the case with the majority of people—are bound to matter. 

Therefore, just as the prophet in the tenth book of the *Republic* <note>original: Republic, referring to the Myth of Er in Plato's work.</note> announces to souls before their descent how they may return to their original happiness according to cycles of a thousand and ten thousand years, so too Calchas <note>The Greek seer during the Trojan War.</note> predicts to the Greeks that they will return in ten years—the number ten being the symbol of a perfect period or cycle. And as in the lives of souls, some are raised up through philosophy, others through the art of love, and others through the disciplines of leadership and war; so it is with the Greeks: some act rightly through wisdom <note>original: prudence</note>, while others act through war or love, and their return journey is different according to their different pursuits.

The first clearly mythical adventure of Ulysses <note>Odysseus</note> is that of the Lotophagi <note>The Lotus-eaters.</note>, which Homer beautifully narrates, and which Alexander Pope very elegantly translates as follows:

> The trees around them all their fruit produce,
> Lotos the name, and sweet <note>original: dulcet</note> is the juice $^f$!
> (From this called Lotophagi) which, whoever tastes,
> Riots without satisfaction in the sweet feasts,
> Nor intends to seek another home, nor any other care,
> But leaves his house, his country, and his friends.

---

$^e$ Consistent with this, Proclus—in his commentary on Plato’s *Republic* <note>original: Plat. Polit.</note>, page 398—says, "that all the beauty existing within the realm of physical birth <term>generation: the material world characterized by change and decay</term> [or the regions of the senses], resulting from the creation of things, is represented by Helen. There is a perpetual battle of souls over this beauty, until those who are more intellectual defeat the more irrational forms of life and return to the place from where they originally came." For the beauty that exists in the realms of physical matter is an outflowing of divine, intellectual beauty.

$^f$ In Pope's version, this second line is: "Lotos the name, divine, nectarious juice!" which I have altered as above, as it is more consistent with the original Greek.

<margin>Book 9, line 94, etc. <note>original: "Lib. ix. l. 94, &c."</note></margin>
The three we sent from off the enchanting ground
We dragged reluctant, and by force we bound:
The rest in haste forsook the pleasing shore,
Or, the charm tasted, had returned no more.

Plato, in the eighth book of his *Republic*, has admirably unfolded to us what the *lotos* <note>The lotus fruit in the Odyssey causes the eater to lose all desire to return home.</note> secretly indicates—namely, that it signifies "false and arrogant reasonings and opinions." Daily experience shows that nothing is more enchanting and delicious than these to those who have made no solid progress in virtue. Like some of the companions of Ulysses, such people are fascinated by erroneous ideas, abandon <note>original: "consign"</note> their true country and true kindred to oblivion, and desire to live forever lost in the intoxication of deceptive delight.

<margin>See Censorinus, *On the Birthday* [original: *De Die Natali*], chapter 3.</margin>
<margin>This is evident from the following passage in the Commentary of Proclus, on the *First Alcibiades* of Plato: "For souls that live in a state of restoration <note>original: ἀποκαταστατικαις (apokatastatikais)</note>, the dæmon is the same both above and here; but for more imperfect souls, the dæmon according to essence is one, and the dæmon according to the pre-chosen life <note>original: προβλεφημενον βιον</note> is another." p. 37, Creuzer edition. For a detailed account of the essential dæmon and the various orders and offices of dæmons, see the notes accompanying my translation of the *First Alcibiades*, *Phaedo*, and *Gorgias* of Plato.</margin>
The next adventure of Ulysses is that of the Cyclops, whom he blinded <note>original: "deprived of sight"</note> and provoked with insults. But according to Porphyry, in the excellent treatise mentioned above, this Cyclops represents none other than the natal dæmon <term>natal dæmon: a guardian spirit or guiding force assigned to a person at birth, governing their earthly fate</term> of Ulysses—the dæmon to whose protecting power he became subject as soon as he was born. 

In order, however, to perfectly understand the hidden <note>original: "arcane"</note> meaning of this fable, it is necessary to observe that according to the ancient theology, those souls that will quickly return to their original happiness in the intelligible world <term>intelligible world: the divine realm of pure intellect and truth, as opposed to the physical world of the senses</term> do not have an essential dæmon <term>essential dæmon: the highest, unchanging aspect of a soul's guiding spirit that remains linked to its divine essence</term> that is different from the dæmon presiding over their birth; for in their case, they are one and the same. But the case is different for more imperfect souls, as the natal dæmon in them is distinct from the essential dæmon. As Ulysses, therefore,

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic symbolism of the Lotus-eaters as "false reasoning" and introduces the Cyclops as a symbol for the "natal dæmon" that governs a soul's earthly birth.</summary>
<keywords>Ulysses, Lotus-eaters, Cyclops, natal dæmon, essential dæmon, Plato, Porphyry, Proclus, Neoplatonism, Odyssey symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of Ulysses' journey, focusing on his encounter with the Cyclops as a symbol of the soul's struggle against its lower, physical nature and the "natal dæmon" that governs birth and the senses.</meta>

<page-num>246</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES</header>

Ulysses does not rank among the most perfect heroic characters. He was not one who, in this present life, is immediately ascending to his kindred star—or, in Platonic language, to the "paternal port," the soul's true paradise of rest. Instead, he was a man who, before reaching that state, had many laborious wanderings to complete and many difficulties and dangers of no common magnitude to endure; therefore, his *natal* <note>The "natal dæmon" is the guardian spirit assigned to a person's physical life and birth.</note> dæmon was not the same as his *essential* <note>The "essential dæmon" represents the soul's true, divine nature or its highest potential.</note> dæmon. 

As he is departing from a life of the senses <note>original: "sensible life"</note> toward an intellectual life—though he does so circuitously and slowly—he is represented as blinding and irritating his *natal* dæmon. For someone who blinds the "eye of sense" and extinguishes its light, after their will has previously and deeply consented to using it, must expect punishment for the deed. Such punishment is ultimately necessary for their own peculiar good and for the general order of the universe. Indeed, the troubles and misfortunes resulting from such undertakings not only help to appease the anger of those who cause them, but also purify and benefit the people who are the subjects of their revenge. 

According to Greek theology, therefore, anyone in this present life who is on the road of virtue and eagerly searching for wisdom, and who perceives a great resemblance between their own destiny and that of Ulysses, may safely conclude one of two things: either here, or in a prior state of existence, they voluntarily submitted to the power of their *natal* dæmon and have now deprived him of sight—or, in other words, they have abandoned a life of the senses. It implies they were once profoundly delighted with the nature of matter and are now revoking the "confessions" <note>An "abrogating of confessions" suggests a soul taking back its previous commitment or attachment to physical, material existence.</note> or commitments they once made to it. 

This, too, is hinted at in the beautiful story of Cupid and Psyche by Apuleius. In that story, the terrestrial Venus sends Mercury with a book in which her name is written to arrest Psyche as a fugitive from her mistress. This entire story relates to the descent of the soul into this earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> body, and its wanderings and punishments until it returns to its true country and original happiness<sup>k</sup>.

---

<sup>k</sup> See the note (p. 90) accompanying my translation of the *Metamorphosis of Apuleius* <note>A famous Latin novel, also known as *The Golden Ass*, containing the allegory of Cupid and Psyche.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic allegory of Ulysses blinding the Cyclops, interpreting it as the soul's rejection of the physical senses and its subsequent purification through suffering. It draws a parallel between Ulysses' journey and the myth of Cupid and Psyche as metaphors for the soul's descent into matter and its eventual return to a divine state.</summary>

<keywords>Ulysses, Neoplatonism, natal dæmon, essential dæmon, allegory of the soul, Greek theology, Apuleius, Cupid and Psyche, purification, material world</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Odyssey, moving from the encounter with the Cyclops (representing the lower senses) to the episodes of Aeolus, the Laestrygonians, and Circe, which symbolize the soul's struggle to maintain reason and rise above material desires.</meta>

<page-num>247</page-num>

-># ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.<-

In the next fable, which is that of Aeolus, the poet appears to me to signify that divine providence which is of an elevating and guardian nature. When this influence is properly received by those it guides, it enables them to pass safely over the stormy sea of life to their native land. However, when this influence is neglected through the "sleep of reason," that negligence is followed by a temporary destruction of hope. This providence of the gods is not only one, but *all-various*, which Homer appears to indicate by the name Aeolus; the word *aiolos* <note>original: αιολος; meaning various, shifting, or manifold</note> signifies various and manifold. 

Because Ulysses' progress in the virtues is still imperfect at this point—extending no further than the *ethical and political* virtues, which are but shadows <note>original: "adumbrations"</note> of the *true virtues*, the purifying <note>original: "cathartic"</note> and contemplative <note>original: "theoretic"</note>¹—he is said to have fallen asleep. He was thereby disappointed in his wishes, as his soul was not in a truly vigilant state at that time, having not yet lifted its eye toward "real being" and away from the objects of the senses, which resemble the delusions of dreams.

By the adventure of the Laestrygonians <note>original: "Lestrigons"</note>, which follows next, Homer represents Ulysses fleeing from gluttony <note>original: "voracity"</note> and fierce, savage manners. This flight is absolutely necessary as a preparation for attaining the higher virtues.

In the next adventure, which contains the beautiful allegory of Circe, we shall find some deep secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> of philosophy, quite apart from its connection to Ulysses. By the Aeaean isle, where the palace of Circe was located, the "region of sorrow and lamentation" is signified, as is evident from the name of the island itself <note>The name Aeaea is likely derived from the Greek cry of grief, *aiai*</note>. And by Circe, we must understand the "Goddess of Sense." For as Porphyry says in Stobaeus, p. 141:

"Homer calls the period and revolution of reincarnation <note>original: "regeneration"</note> in a circle 'Circe,' the daughter of the Sun, who perpetually connects and combines all physical decay <note>original: "corruption"</note> with new birth <note>original: "genera-"</note>

---

¹ For an accurate account of the levels <note>original: "gradation"</note> of the virtues, see Porphyry’s *Aids to the Perception of Intelligible Natures* <note>original: "Auxiliaries to Intelligibles"</note>, p. 217.

<summary>This page provides a Neoplatonic allegorical interpretation of Aeolus, the Laestrygonians, and Circe, framing Ulysses' journey as the soul's progression from basic civic virtues toward higher spiritual purification.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic analysis of the Odyssey, specifically focusing on the allegory of Circe. It transitions from the physical dangers of the Laestrygonians to the spiritual and psychological dangers of the material world and sensory pleasure.</meta>

...tion, and the cycle of birth <note>original: "generation"</note> and decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>." This is asserted even more explicitly by Proclus in his *Commentaries* <note>original: "Scholia"</note> on Plato’s *Cratylus*. He says, "Circe is that divine power which weaves all the life contained in the four elements, while simultaneously harmonizing the entire physical world beneath the moon <note>original: "sublunary world"</note> through her song. The shuttle with which she weaves is described by theologians as golden because her essence is intellectual, pure, non-physical, and untouched by the cycle of birth; all of this is symbolized by the shuttle being golden. Her work consists of *separating* <margin>The shuttle is a symbol of the power to separate different elements.</margin> stable, eternal things from those that are in constant motion, according to the variety of the divine order." Proclus also informs us "that Circe ranks among the divinities who preside over the process of birth, or the realms of the senses." 

Homer, too, with great appropriateness, represents Circe—who rules over the realms of physical birth—as being served by Nymphs born from fountains. According to Hermias in his commentary on Plato’s *Phaedrus*, Nymphs are goddesses who preside over the soul's rebirth <note>original: "regeneration"</note> and are the attendants of Bacchus <note>also known as Dionysus</note>, the son of Semele. For this reason, they are associated with water; that is, they enter into and rule over the world of physical existence. This Dionysius, or Bacchus, provides the renewal of every nature that can be perceived by the senses.

From this, we may observe that the Isle of Aeaea <note>original: "Æean isle"</note>, or this realm of the senses, is appropriately called the home of trouble and grieving. In this region, the companions of Ulysses are changed into beasts by the Goddess's spells because they are very flawed characters; that is, they descend into shameful and irrational habits and behaviors. 

Ulysses, however, is one who is returning—though slowly—to the true perfection of his nature. By the assistance of Mercury <note>the god Hermes, representing Reason</note>, he is saved from destruction. Thus, the intellect, awakened by its own steady strength <note>original: "impassive power"</note> and armed with both "prudent anger" and the plant *moly*—which represents temperance <note>self-control</note> and the ability to reject the temptations of pleasure—wages war against...

<summary>This page provides a Neoplatonic interpretation of Circe as a divine force governing the physical world and the cycle of rebirth. It explains that while weak souls (Ulysses' companions) are degraded by sensory pleasures into a beast-like state, the wise individual uses reason and temperance to resist these impulses.</summary>

sensory delight and prevents the effects of its power to transform the soul. Ulysses also, though he was not yet able to lead his companions back to their native land—which represents the soul’s true home <note>original: "paternal port of the soul"</note>—nevertheless saves them from being transformed into an irrational state of existence through the enchantments of the senses.

After this comes the allegory regarding Ulysses's descent into *Hades*. This symbolically signifies <note>original: "occultly signifies"</note> that he was still living a life governed by his senses rather than by his intellect. Because he had not yet overcome the pull of a physical, earthly life, he was shrouded in darkness. Ancient wise men universally believed that "Hades" actually begins during our current life on earth. They taught that sensory perception is merely the activity of a sleeping soul—a perception of dreamlike delusions—as I have proven extensively in my treatise *On the Mysteries*. 

The hidden meaning of what Ulysses saw in Hades is as beautiful as it is profound, as shown by the following extract from the manuscript commentary by Olympiodorus on Plato’s *Gorgias*: 

"Ulysses," he says, "descending into Hades, saw Sisyphus, Tityus, and Tantalus, among others. He saw Tityus lying on the earth with a vulture devouring his liver. The liver signifies that Tityus lived entirely according to the <term>appetitive</term> <note>original: "epithymetic"</note> part of his nature (the part of the soul that is the source of physical desires). Through this, he possessed a kind of internal 'wisdom,' but the earth symbolizes that his wisdom was limited to a purely physical or terrestrial condition. 

Sisyphus, however, lived under the control of ambition and anger. He was occupied with continually rolling a stone up a hill, only for it to perpetually roll back down. The stone’s descent implies his failure to govern himself, while the act of rolling it represents the hard, stubborn <note>original: "refractory"</note>, and repetitive nature of his life. 

Lastly, he saw Tantalus stretched out beside a lake. There was a tree before him with branches full of fruit that he wanted to gather, but it vanished from his sight. This indicates that he lived..."

...under the dominion of the imagination <note>original: "phantasy"</note>. Tantalus hanging over the lake and trying in vain to drink represents the elusive, fluid, and rapidly-fading condition of such a life."

We must now, however, observe Ulysses passing from the world of the senses to the world of the imagination. During this voyage, he is attacked by various temptations of great power and destructive effect. We will see him emerge victorious from some of these and fall victim to others, yet he continues to struggle against the intrusion of them all. Among the first of these temptations is the enchanting melody of the Sirens:

->Whose song is death, and makes destruction please.<-

The hidden meaning of the Sirens is beautifully explained by Proclus in his commentary on Plato’s *Cratylus* as follows:

> "The divine Plato knew that there are three kinds of Sirens: the *celestial* Sirens, who are under the government of Jupiter <note>The Roman name for Zeus</note>; those *responsible for physical birth* <note>original: "effective of generation"</note>, who are under the government of Neptune <note>The Roman name for Poseidon</note>; and those who are *purifying* <note>original: "cathartic"</note>, who are under the government of Pluto. It is common to all of these to draw all things toward their ruling Gods through a harmonious motion. Therefore, when the soul is in the heavens, the Sirens desire to unite it to the divine life that flourishes there. However, it is necessary for souls living in the world of physical birth <note>original: "generation"</note> to sail past them, just as the Homeric Ulysses did, so that they are not lured away by the physical world, of which the sea is a symbol. And when souls are in Hades, the Sirens desire to unite them to Pluto through intellectual concepts. Thus, Plato knew that in the kingdom of Hades there are Gods, <term>daemons</term> <note>original: "dæmons"; here meaning intermediary spirits or divine beings, not evil demons</note>, and souls who dance, as it were, around Pluto, allured by the Sirens who dwell there."

Ulysses, therefore, is now proceeding toward a life governed by the imagination. This state is superior to a life consisting entirely of sensory activities <note>original: "sensitive energies"</note>. Consequently, he abandons those alluring and deceptive pleasures of the senses which charm the soul with flattering and honey-sweet <note>original: "mellifluous"</note> spells. To do this, he fortifies himself with divine principles <note>original: "reasons"</note> and activities, as if with...

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic allegory of the Sirens, explaining them as divine forces that attract the soul to different realms (heavenly, physical, or the underworld) and Ulysses's need to resist their sensory allure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Odyssey, moving from the Sirens' temptation to the allegorical dangers of Scylla and Charybdis.</meta>

beeswax, which represents the impulses of desire and the sense organs. By doing this, every passage of access is barred so that the Sirens may sing their song of ecstasy in vain, never succeeding in ruining the soul with their enchanting melody. Ulysses also restrains physical impulses with the restraints of morality. In this way, he employs the senses without yielding to their forceful invasions; he experiences delight without surrendering the rule of reason to the Sirens' fascinating control.

Having escaped the dangers of the Sirens, Ulysses passes on to the rocks of *Scylla* and *Charybdis*, which have a terrifying appearance and irresistible force. By these two rocks, the poet seems to signify the passions of anger and desire, along with their accompanying effects, which squeeze human life from both sides. Every person must experience these who proceeds, like *Ulysses*, in a methodical way toward a state of higher consciousness <note>original: "intellectual state of existence"</note>. Some of these passions are like *Scylla*: they possess a haughty malice that is fraudulent, yet hidden and obscure, because they are concealed deep within the soul. Revenge and other similar passions are like this. Within these hidden recesses a demon <note>original: "dæmon"; in this context, a spirit or supernatural force that influences human behavior</note>, the ruler of such passions, resides.

The Chaldean oracles <note>A set of mystical texts from the 2nd century AD, highly regarded by Neoplatonists</note> assert that terrestrial demons dwell in the soul when it is full of irrational emotions. This demon may also be rightly called a dreadful and enraged dog, who partly reveals his own malice and partly hides it in impenetrable darkness. Therefore, he is capable of producing mischief in two ways: he hurts privately through wicked schemes, and he openly seizes the soul upon the high rock of fury, tearing it with the triple evil of deadly teeth—namely, the abandonment of duty, the hatred of humanity, and self-conceit. Indeed, a demon of this kind will be perpetually watchful, trying to destroy a person all at once.

<note>original: "And this is the meaning of the Chaldaic oracle, — Σου αγγειον θηρες χθονος οικησουσιν. i. e. 'The wild beasts of the earth shall inhabit thy vessel.'"</note>

This is the meaning of the Chaldean oracle: 
->*The wild beasts of the earth shall inhabit your vessel.* <note>original Greek: "Σου αγγειον θηρες χθονος οικησουσιν"</note><-

As *Psellus* <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine philosopher and scholar</note> rightly observes, the "vessel" signifies the combined physical and spiritual makeup of the soul, and the "wild beasts of the earth" represent terrestrial demons.

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of Ulysses's journey, specifically focusing on the choice between the dangers of Scylla and Charybdis and the symbolic meaning of the wild fig-tree.</meta>

<page-num>252</page-num>

at one time the whole, and at another time a part of the soul of a person who is struggling, like Ulysses, against passion and yielding reluctantly to its invasions.

But the other affections which pertain to desire are of a more bodily <note>original: "corporeal"</note> nature, and are more obviously depraved. A wild fig-tree—that is, the will—grows on the top of this rock. It is wild because of its free nature, but sweet when its fruit is enjoyed. Beneath it, often throughout the day, the violent urges of the physical body tend to absorb and destroy the man, agitating inflamed desire up and down, so that great destruction is caused to both soul and body by their mutual agreement. 

But it is highly important that a rock of this last kind should be anxiously avoided by one who, like Ulysses, is laboring to return to his true country and friends. Hence, if necessity requires, he will rather expose himself to the other danger; for there, only the energy of thought and the soul's simple motions must be exerted, and it is easy to recover the original state <note>original: "pristine habit"</note> of the soul. 

In short, by this allegory of the two rocks, the poet seems to represent the dangers that spontaneously arise from the irascible <note>The "irascible" part of the soul, in Platonic philosophy, is the seat of anger, courage, and spirit.</note> part of the soul, as well as those which result from deliberation and are of a physical nature. Both of these must be endured—or at least one of them—as a necessary consequence. For it is impossible that neither of them should be experienced by one who is passing over the stormy ocean of a life of the senses <note>original: "sensible life"</note>.

After this follows the allegory of the Trinacrian isle <note>An ancient name for Sicily, so called for its three corners.</note>, which contains the herds sacred to the God of Day <note>Helios, the Sun god.</note>. These herds were violated by the companions of Ulysses, but not without the destruction of those who committed this impiety, and the most dreadful danger to Ulysses himself. By the result of this fable, the poet clearly shows that punishment follows the sacrilegious and the perjured. He teaches us that we should perpetually reverence divinity with the greatest sanctity of mind, and be cautious never to commit any act in divine matters that is contrary to piety.

behavior <note>original: "manners"</note> and purity of thought. But Homer, by attributing feeling <note>original: "sense"</note> to the meat and hides of the slaughtered cattle, clearly demonstrates that every base deed universally reveals the wickedness of its creator. However, he shows that perjury and sacrilege are followed by the most obvious signs of guilt and the most terrifying marks of coming punishment and unavoidable destruction. We can also see here that Ulysses’s will was far from agreeing to this unholy <note>original: "impious"</note> act. Though his passions eventually overwhelmed his reason, it only happened after he had given frequent warnings and worked hard to prevent it. This is so true that his guilt was the result of being caught off guard, rather than a planned choice; Homer suggests this by recording that Ulysses was asleep when his companions committed the crime.

In the next story, we find Ulysses driven by the south wind toward the rocks of Scylla and Charybdis. He found safety in the latter by clinging to the fig tree growing on its peak until the whirlpool vomited back <note>original: "refunded"</note> the mast he had been riding after the storm. But the hidden meaning of the allegory seems to be this: Ulysses, who has not yet moved past a life defined by the senses, is driven by the heat of passion (represented by the southern winds) into the terrible whirlpool of irrational <note>original: "insane"</note> desires. These desires frequently boil over, stirring up storms of corrupt emotions that ruin any soul vulnerable <note>original: "obnoxious"</note> to their waves. However, perceiving the danger when the base storms begin to rise and the whirlpools of corruption roar, he takes hold of the steering-oar <note>original: "helm"</note> of self-control <note>original: "temperance"</note> and binds himself firmly to the solid structure of his remaining virtue. The waves of desire are indeed extremely violent; but before he is forcibly pulled down by the fury of his passions into the depths of corruption, he holds fast to his unwilling spirit <note>original: "unconsenting will"</note>, which sits, as it were, on the high peak of earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> desire. For this will, like the wild fig tree, provides the best refuge for the soul

...struggling with the waves of low-minded disturbances <note>original: "base perturbations"</note>. In this way, he recovers the wholeness <note>original: "integrity"</note> he had lost and afterward swims safely over the waves of temptation. He remains ever watchful and diligent while sailing through this turbulent river of the flesh, exposed to the stormy winds of intense passion and ruinous vice. Because he is aware of these dangers and anxious that the loss caused by unworthy emotions might harm him, he will escape being torn apart by the teeth of Anger. Even though Anger might bark fiercely and terribly near the territory of Desire—trying, like Scylla, to snatch him up onto her high rock—he remains safe. For those who, like Ulysses, are unintentionally disturbed by the waves of Desire, they suffer no harm from the corrupt rock of Wrath. Instead, recognizing the danger of their current situation, they trade the false confidence produced by rage for a modest caution and an anxious hope.

So far, we have followed Ulysses on his voyage across the turbulent and dangerous ocean of the senses. We have seen him struggling against the storms of temptation and risking death in the stormy waves of vice. Now, we must follow him into the region of the imagination and observe his progress from the enchanted island until he recovers the long-lost kingdom <note>original: "empire"</note> of his soul. 

It is evident, I believe, that the poet secretly represents the <term>phantasy</term> <note>The "phantasy" refers to the faculty of the mind that creates, stores, and manipulates mental images; in modern terms, the imagination.</note> or imagination through the character of Calypso, as shown by the description of her home. She is described as living in a cave lit by a large fire. This cave is surrounded by a thick forest, is watered by four springs, and is located on an island far from any inhabited place, surrounded by the vast ocean. All these details match the nature of the imagination, as I believe the following observations will demonstrate. 

First, the primary and proper vehicle of the imagination—or what the Platonic philosophers call the *imaginative spirit* <note>original: "pneuma phantastikon"—the subtle, "airy" body that Neoplatonists believed acted as a bridge between the physical body and the rational soul.</note>—is refined and celestial <note>original: "attenuated and ethereal"</note>, and is therefore naturally full of light. Next, the island is said to be surrounded by a thick forest, which...

<summary>This page interprets Ulysses's struggle between the personified dangers of Anger (Scylla) and Desire before identifying Calypso's island as an allegory for the "phantasy" or imaginative faculty of the soul.</summary>

<page-num>255</page-num>

# ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.

...evidently corresponds to a material nature, or this body, which the <term>phantasy</term> <note>The "phantasy" or imagination was seen by Neoplatonists as a "garment" or "vehicle" that the soul wears.</note> clothes. This is because "wood" <note>original Greek: *hyle*</note> is also a word for "matter." Furthermore, the four fountains that water the cave hiddenly represent the four <term>gnostic</term> <note>From the Greek word for "knowledge"; here referring to the soul's powers of perception and understanding.</note> powers of the soul: *intellect, the step-by-step process of reasoning <note>original: "discursive energy of reason"</note>, opinion, and physical sense*. The imagination communicates with all of these. It receives images like a mirror from every one of them and retains the images it receives from the senses even when the objects that produced them are no longer there. For this reason, the imagination, or the phantasy, is named from the Greek phrase "the permanency of appearances" <note>original Greek: *ton phanenton stasis*</note>. Finally, the island is said to be surrounded by the ocean; this perfectly matches the nature of the physical body, which is always in a state of flux <note>original: "flowing"</note> without ever finding a moment of rest. This concludes the explanation of the secret connection between the cave and island and the realm of the imagination.

The poet’s names for the Goddess, Calypso, and the island, Ogygia, also clearly support this interpretation. "Calypso" is derived from the Greek word "to cover as with a veil" <note>original Greek: *kalypto*</note>, and "Ogygia" comes from the word for "ancient" <note>original Greek: *ogygios*</note>. Since the "imaginative spirit" is the primary vehicle of the rational soul—which the soul received from the planetary spheres as it descended into the earthly <note>original: "sublunary"</note> regions—it is very appropriate to say it "covers" the soul like a fine garment or veil. It is also rightly called "ancient" because it is considered the soul's very first vessel.

In this realm of the imagination, then, Ulysses is shown as an unwilling captive. He is constantly grieving his absence from his true country <note>His true country represents the spiritual or intellectual realm.</note> and longing to leave the enchanting embrace of the Goddess. The poet beautifully describes his situation:

> But sad Ulysses, sitting by himself apart,
> Poured out the great sorrows of his aching heart;

> All on the lonely shore he sat to weep,
> And rolled his eyes around the restless deep;
> Toward his loved coast he rolled his eyes in vain,
> Until, dimmed with rising grief, they streamed again <note>original: "° Odyss. lib. v. 82, &c. The translation by Pope." Odyssey, book 5, line 82, etc.</note>.

His return, however, is finally achieved through Mercury <note>The Roman name for Hermes.</note>, or reason, who persuades the Goddess to agree to his dismissal. Therefore, after she gives her consent, Ulysses is very appropriately said to have sat himself on the throne where Mercury had sat. This is because reason resumes its proper place when the power of reasoning is about to abandon the deceptive and soul-detaining charms of the imagination. 

But Homer seems to me to suggest something wonderful when he represents Ulysses, upon his departure from Calypso, sailing by night and contemplating the order and light of the stars in the following beautiful lines:

> And now, rejoicing in the prosperous gales,
> With beating heart Ulysses spread his sails;
> Placed at the helm he sat, and marked the skies,
> Nor closed in sleep his ever-watchful eyes.
> There he viewed the Pleiads, and the northern team <note>original: "northern team"; referring to the constellation of the Big Dipper or Ursa Major.</note>,
> And great Orion's more radiant beam;
> To which, around the axle of the sky,
> The Bear, turning, points its golden eye;
> Who shines exalted on the heavenly plain,
> Nor dips its blazing forehead in the sea <note>original: "ᵖ Ibid. lib. v. 269, &c." Odyssey, book 5, line 269, etc. The word "main" in the original refers to the open ocean.</note>.

For what the poet says here of Ulysses is perfectly consistent with what is said by Plato in the seventh book of his *Republic*. Plato describes the person who is to be led out of the cave (the physical world) into the light of day—that is, from a life of the senses to an intellectual life. Plato says: "*that he will more easily see what the heavens contain, and the heavens themselves, by looking at the light of the stars and the moon at night, than by looking at the sun and the light of the sun during the day.*" As Proclus <note>A Greek Neoplatonist philosopher (412–485 AD) who wrote extensive commentaries on Plato.</note> well observes, this is because...
