<page-num>257</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>
<sig>S</sig>

observes, "Plato refers to the contemplation of intellectual realities <note>original: "intelligibles," meaning the eternal forms or ideas that can only be understood by the mind, not the senses</note>, of which the stars and their light are imitations, since all of them share in the form of the sun, in the same way that intellectual realities are defined by the nature of *the good*. These, then, such a person must contemplate, so that he may understand their essence and those highest points of their nature, by which they are god-like outgrowths <note>original: "deiform processions"</note> from the unspeakable <note>original: "ineffable"</note> source of all things." Ulysses, therefore, who is hurrying toward a life of pure intellect, contemplates these shining objects with watchful eyes, rejoicing in the light and help they provide him while sailing over the dark ocean of a life governed by the senses.

But as he is now seriously committed to leaving behind the world of the senses, he must unavoidably be pursued by the anger of Neptune—the lord of the physical world <note>original: "generation"</note> and of a life of the senses. Ulysses has forsaken Neptune's service, and he has blinded Neptune's offspring <note>Referring to the Cyclops Polyphemus</note> through trickery and provoked him with insults. Because of this, in the middle of these delightful contemplations, he is almost overwhelmed by the waves of misfortune, stirred up by the rage of his relentless enemy. He is, however, enabled to survive the terrible storm through divine help, or Leucothea. For, receiving from the goddess the immortal headband <note>original: "fillet"</note> of true courage <note>original: "fortitude"</note> and binding it under his chest (the proper seat of courage), he faces the waves of adversity and bravely moves through the stormy ocean of life. It must, however, be carefully noted that the poet is not actually attributing a human emotion <note>original: "passion"</note> to a divine nature when he speaks of the anger of Neptune. In speaking this way, he—as well as other theologians—only intended to show our own inability to receive the god's helpful influence.

Ulysses, therefore, having with much difficulty escaped the dangers arising from the wrath of Neptune, finally lands on the island of Phaeacia, where he is hospitably received and honorably sent on his way. Now, as it is proper that someone who, like Ulysses, leaves behind the delusions of the imagination should immediately turn toward the higher intellectual light of the soul's rational power,

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic allegory of Ulysses's sea voyage, where Neptune represents the distractions of the physical world and the "immortal fillet" represents the spiritual courage needed to reach the realm of reason (Phaeacia).</summary>
<keywords>Ulysses, Neptune, Leucothea, Phaeacia, Neoplatonism, allegory, intellectual life, sensible life, fortitude</keywords>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Odyssey, specifically analyzing Ulysses's arrival in Phaeacia as a symbol of the soul reaching the realm of rational thought (dianoia) after escaping the storms of the material world.</meta>

<page-num>258</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>

The land of Phaeacia should correspond to our intellectual part, and particularly to that portion of it which is called *dianoia* in Greek <term>dianoia: the capacity for discursive reasoning or scientific thought, which derives its principles from the higher, intuitive intellect</term>. And the following observations will, I am persuaded, abundantly prove that it has this correspondence. 

In the first place, then, this island is represented by the poet <note>Homer</note> as enjoying a perpetual spring, which plainly indicates that it is not a physical location on earth. Indeed, the critical commentators have been so fully convinced of this that they acknowledge Homer describes Phaeacia as one of the Islands of the Blessed <note>original: "Fortunate Islands"</note>; but they have not attempted to understand his purpose in such a description. If, however, we consider the perfect liberty, unfading variety, and endless delight which our intellectual part affords, we shall find that it is truly the "Island of the Blessed" for the soul. In this state, by exercising the contemplative virtues <note>original: "theoretic virtues," referring to the pursuit of wisdom through study and meditation</note>, it is possible for a man, even in the present life, to obtain genuine happiness—though not in the same perfection as when he is liberated from the body. 

With respect to the Islands of the Blessed, their hidden meaning is beautifully explained by Olympiodorus in his manuscript commentary on the *Gorgias* of Plato:

> "It is necessary to know that islands rise above the sea, being higher than the water. Therefore, a condition of being which transcends this physical life and the world of change <note>original: "generation," the realm of birth and death</note> is called the 'islands of the blessed'; these are the same as the Elysian fields. For this reason, Hercules is reported to have accomplished his last labor in the western regions <note>original: "Hesperian regions"</note>. This signifies that after he had conquered a dark and earthly life, he lived thereafter in open day—that is, in truth and resplendent light." <note>original Greek: Δει δε ειδεναι οτι αι νησοι υπερκυπτουσι της θαλασσης ανωτερω ουσαι, την ουν πολιτειαν την υπερκυψασαν του βιου και της γενησεως, μακαρων νησους καλουσι· ταυτον δε εστι και το ηλυσιον πεδιον. δια τοι τουτο και ο Ηρακλης τελευταιον αθλον, εν τοις εσπεριοις μερεσιν εποιησατο, αντι κατηγωνισατο τον σκοτεινον και χθονιον βιον, και λοιπον εν ημερα, ο εστιν εν αληθεια και φωτι εζη</note>

<summary>The author explains that Phaeacia represents the "dianoia" or reasoning mind, a state of intellectual happiness comparable to the "Islands of the Blessed" where the soul finds relief from physical existence.</summary>
<keywords>Phaeacia, dianoia, Islands of the Blessed, Olympiodorus, Hercules, Elysian fields, intellectual life, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Odyssey, focusing on the palace of Alcinous as a symbol of the "rational energy" or the illuminated state of the human mind.</meta>

<page-num>259</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>

<margin>q *Odyssey*, book vii. 84, etc. The translation by Alexander Pope.</margin>
In the next place, the poet <note>Homer</note>, by his description of the palace of Alcinous, the king of this island, admirably indicates the pure and splendid light of the activity of reason. For he says of it:

> The front appeared with radiant splendors gay,
> Bright as the lamp of night <note>the moon</note>, or orb of day <note>the sun</note>.
> The walls were massy brass: the cornice high
> Blue metals crowned in colors of the sky.
> Rich plates of gold the folding doors encase;
> The pillars silver on a brazen base.
> Silver the lintels deep projecting over,
> And gold the ringlets that command the door.
> Two rows of stately dogs on either hand,
> In sculptured gold, and labored silver, stand.
> These Vulcan formed intelligent to wait
> Immortal guardians at Alcinous' gate <gloss>q</gloss>.

And he represents it as no less internally luminous by night:

> Refulgent <note>shining brightly</note> pedestals the walls surround,
> Which boys of gold with flaming torches crowned;
> The polished ore, reflecting every ray,
> Blazed on the banquets with a double day.

<margin>r In Euclid's *Elements*, book i. p. 14.</margin>
Indeed Homer, by his description of the outside of this palace, sufficiently indicates its agreement with the planet Mercury, the deity of which presides over the rational energy. For this God, in the language of Proclus <gloss>r</gloss>, “unfolds into light intellectual gifts, fills all things with divine *reasons* [*that is, forms and productive principles*], elevates souls to intellect, awakens them as from a profound sleep, converts them through investigation to themselves, and by a certain obstetric art <note>The "obstetric art" refers to the Socratic method of "midwifery," where the teacher helps the student give birth to the knowledge already latent in their soul.</note> and invention of pure intellect, brings them to a blessed life.” According to astronomers, likewise, the planet Mercury is resplendent with the colors of all the other planets. Thus Baptista Porta in *Cœlestis Physiognomonia*, page 88: “You will see in it the pale yellow of Saturn, the fire of Mars, the brightness of Jupiter...” <note>original Latin: *Videbis in eo Saturni luridum, Martis ignem, Jovis candidum*</note>

<summary>This page interprets the palace of Alcinous as a symbol for the "rational energy" of the soul, comparing its brilliance and construction to the attributes of the god and planet Mercury.</summary>
<keywords>Alcinous, Phaeacia, Homer, Mercury, Proclus, Neoplatonism, symbolism of light, rational energy, architectural symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of Homer’s Odyssey, specifically identifying the island of Phaeacia with the faculty of human reason and the planet Mercury.</meta>

<page-num>260</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>

"You may perceive in this planet <note>the planet Mercury</note> the pale color of Saturn, the fire of Mars, the whiteness of Jupiter, and the yellow of Venus; and likewise the brilliance and joy of each. Because of this, it does not have a unique form of its own, but takes on the form of those with which it is associated; this causes astrologers to differ when they describe its color." <note>original: *Veneris flavum, necnon utriusque nitor, hilaritasque, et ob id non peculiaris formæ, sed eorum formam capit, cum quibus associatur, ob id in describendo ejus colore astrologi differunt.*</note>

However, I think it is indisputably confirmed that the island of Phaeacia represents the domain of reason, based on Homer's account of the ships built by its inhabitants. Concerning these, he says:

> So you shall instantly reach the assigned realm,
> In wondrous self-moving ships, endowed with mind.
> No helm secures their course, no pilot guides them;
> Like intelligent men, they plow the tides,
> Aware of every coast and every bay
> That lies beneath the sun’s all-seeing ray;
> And veiled in clouds impenetrable to the eye,
> Fearless and rapid through the deep they fly <gloss>s</gloss>.

It would be absurd to suppose that Homer would use such an exaggeration <note>original: "hyperbole"</note> merely to describe the high quality of Phaeacian ships. Therefore, since this description so greatly exceeds the bounds of probability and contradicts the admirable wisdom that Homer consistently displays, it can only be accepted as an allegory, full of hidden meaning and the secret wisdom of antiquity. The poet also adds regarding the Phaeacians:

> These the ruler of the deep <note>Poseidon</note> ordained
> To build proud navies and command the sea;
> On canvas wings to cut through the watery way—
> No bird is lighter, no thought swifter than they.

The last of these lines agrees so remarkably with the preceding explanation that I presume no stronger confirmation—

---

<margin>s *Odyssey*, book 8, lines 556, etc.</margin>

<summary>The author argues that the Phaeacian ships in the Odyssey, which move by thought rather than rudders, are allegories for the swiftness and navigational power of human reason. He links this "rational" realm to the planet Mercury, which assumes the qualities of the planets surrounding it.</summary>
<keywords>Phaeacia, Homer, Odyssey, allegory, Mercury, astrology, ships of Phaeacia, Neoplatonism, reason</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Phaeacians in Homer’s Odyssey, moving from the symbolism of their ships to the internal state of the rational soul and the process of philosophical "recollection."</meta>

<page-num>261</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>

No better illustration can be desired. Nor is the original text less satisfactory:

->Their ships are as swift as a wing or a thought.¹<-
<note>original Greek: Των νεες ωκειαι ωσει πτερον ηε νοημα</note>

That is, "The ships of these men are swift as a wing, or as a conception of *the mind.*" Furthermore, the inhabitants of the palace are represented as spending their days in continual festivity and unceasing mirth, listening to the harmony of the lyre or performing the rhythmic movements of the joyful dance. For to the man who lives under the guidance of reason—to the good man—every day is a festival, as the philosopher Diogenes used to say. Hence, such a person is constantly employed in "tuning the lyre" of recollection, moving in harmonious revolutions around an intelligible essence <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, "intelligible essence" refers to the higher realm of pure, eternal reality that can only be grasped by the intellect, rather than the senses.</note>, and enjoying the never-satiating and deifying banquet of the intellect.

And here we may observe how much the behavior of Ulysses at the palace of Alcinous confirms the preceding explanation. It accords with his character as a man passing in a regular manner from the delusions of the senses to the realities of intellectual enjoyment. Because he has now "turned inward" toward himself and is seated in the "palace of reason," it is highly appropriate that he should call to mind his past conduct and be pained by the review. It is fitting that he should be awakened to sorrow by the "lyre of reminiscence" <note>reminiscence: the philosophical process (anamnesis) of remembering the divine truths the soul knew before it was born into a physical body.</note> and weep over the follies of his past active life. Hence, when the divine bard Demodocus—inspired by the passion of the Muses—sings the story of the argument between Ulysses and Achilles on his golden lyre, Ulysses is deeply moved by the account. And when the inhabitants of the palace (which represents the powers and energies of the rational soul) were so transported by the song that they demanded its repetition:

->Again Ulysses veiled his pensive head,<-
->Again, unmanned, a shower of sorrow shed.<-

For to the person who is making progress in virtue, the recollection of his former conduct is both pleasing and painful. It is pleasing insofar as it was, in some instances, guided by righteousness, but painful insofar as it was, in others, erroneous.

***

¹ *Odyssey*, book 7, line 33. <note>Modern editions usually cite this as line 36.</note>

<summary>This page interprets the Phaeacian palace as the "palace of reason," where the soul experiences intellectual joy and undergoes the painful but necessary process of reflecting on past moral errors.</summary>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Odyssey, focusing on Ulysses’ arrival at Ithaca as a symbol for the soul's return to its intellectual and rational home.</meta>

<page-num>262</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>

Ulysses is also represented, with the greatest appropriateness, as recounting his past adventures in the palace of Alcinous. Because he is now turning toward the intellectual light of the reasoning power, it is highly necessary that he should review his past conduct, faithfully list the errors of his life, and anxiously seek a return to true character and perfect mental integrity. Furthermore, as he is now on a journey to regain the lost kingdom of his soul through the pure power of reason, he is depicted as falling into such a deep sleep during his voyage that he is unaware of its successful conclusion for some time. By this, the poet indicates that he has been separated from the concerns of the senses and is entirely focused on the activities of the rational soul. 

Nor is it without reason that the poet describes Ithaca as appearing to the sailors' view just as the bright morning star emerges from the darkness of night. For he sings:

> But when the morning star, with early ray,
> Flamed in the front of heaven and promised day;
> Like distant clouds, the sailors descry
> Fair Ithaca’s emerging hills arise.
<note>original: Odyssey, book 13, lines 93, etc.</note>

This is because it is only by the dawning beams of the intellect that the soul's reasoning power can catch a glimpse of its native country and the proper seat of its empire.

Therefore, Ulysses—having now turned toward the activities of the rational soul and being eager to begin the <term>cathartic virtues</term> <note>cathartic virtues: in Neoplatonism, these are "purifying" virtues used to cleanse the soul of its attachment to the physical body and material world</note>—recognizes his native land through the help of Minerva, or Wisdom. He immediately enters into a consultation with the Goddess on how he may effectively banish the various disturbances and excessive desires that still lurk in the <term>penetralia</term> <note>original: penetralia; the innermost, hidden parts of a building or, metaphorically, the soul</note> of his soul. To achieve this, it is necessary that he relinquish all external possessions, discipline every sense, and employ every strategy that might finally destroy these malevolent enemies. For this reason, the clothes of poverty, the wrinkles of old age, and the lack of life's necessities are symbols...

...of disciplined habits, the abandonment of pursuits focused on the senses, and a deep turning toward intellectual goodness. For the eye of the senses must now make way for the purer vision of the rational soul. The strength and energy of our physical nature must give way to the superior power of intellectual effort and the rigorous practice of <term>cathartic virtue</term> <note>cathartic virtue: from the Greek "katharsis" (purification); in Neoplatonic philosophy, these are the virtues that cleanse the soul of its attachment to the material world and the physical body</note>. Homer seems to clearly indicate this in the following beautiful lines:

> Now seated in the olive’s sacred shade,
> The hero and the warrior goddess <note>original: martial maid; referring to Athena/Minerva</note> confer.
> The Goddess of the blue eyes began:
> "Son of Laertes! man of much experience!
> The crowd of suitors demand your first attention,
> To rid the land of that decadent race.
> For three years your house has seen their lawless rule,
> And their arrogant proposals to the matchless queen <note>i.e. Philosophy; for Penelope is an image of this.</note>;
> But she mourns your absence day by day,
> And bleeds within, and silently wastes away;
> Avoiding the wedding hour, she gives
> Fond hopes to all, and deceives everyone with hope." <note>original: Odyssey, book 13, lines 373, etc.</note>

Therefore:

> "It is now appropriate for you to wear a dark disguise,
> And walk in secret, unknown to mortal eyes;
> For this, my hand shall wither every beauty,
> And every elegance of form and face.
> Over your smooth skin I will spread a layer of wrinkles,
> Turn the brown glory of your hair grey <note>original: Turn hoar the auburn honours of thy head</note>,
> Disfigure every limb with coarse clothing,
> And extinguish all the fire in your eyes;
> I will add all the needs and decays of old age,
> To make you a stranger to your own—your son, your wife;
> Every eye shall turn away from the loathsome sight,
> And the blind suitors will mock their own destruction." <note>original: Odyssey, book 13, lines 397, etc. The translation of these and the following passages from the Odyssey is by Alexander Pope.</note>

After this follows the revelation of Ulysses to Telemachus, which is no less philosophically profound than...

...poetically beautiful. For, through the character of Telemachus, we must understand a *true scientific conception of things*; since this is the natural offspring of the rational soul's energy when combined with philosophy. Therefore, Ulysses—while engaged in the great work of spiritual discipline <note>original: mortification; in this context, the ascetic practice of subduing physical desires to purify the soul</note>—recognizes his true child and secretly plans with him the destruction of his treacherous enemies. From this, we can see why it is appropriate that Telemachus is shown searching for his absent father and longing for his return. For the rational soul only connects with a true understanding of things when it withdraws itself from the pleasures of the senses <note>original: sensible delights</note> and reflects on restoring its lost honor and its original authority.

Now, Ulysses appears before us dressed in the clothes of spiritual discipline, hurrying to his long-abandoned palace—or rather, the hidden depths of his own soul—so that he may observe the behavior and plan the destruction of those ruinous passions which are secretly trying to overthrow the rule of his mind. Thus, the poet very fittingly and movingly exclaims:

> And now his city meets the monarch’s eyes,
> Alas! How changed! A man of many sorrows;
> Propped on a staff, a beggar, old and stripped,
> In tattered garments, fluttering in the breeze <note>original: Odyss. lib. xvii. 201, &c. (Odyssey, Book 17, line 201)</note>.

<margin>Odyssey, Book 17, line 201, etc.</margin>

However, since this disguise was taken on only for the purpose of regaining his former purity and rightful authority, he sheds the torn clothing of his discipline as soon as he begins to destroy his hidden <note>original: occult</note> desires; he then regains the proper dignity and strength of his true form. It is for good reason that Penelope, who represents philosophy, provides the weapon by which the hostile mob of passions is destroyed. For what except the arrows of philosophy can uproot the primary forces of impurity and vice? Therefore, as soon as he is equipped with this irresistible...

weapon, he no longer delays the destruction of his treacherous enemies, but:

> Stripped of his rags, he blazed forth like a god.

But Homer depicts Penelope remaining unaware of Ulysses' identity, even after the suitors are destroyed and he is seated on the throne of majesty, anxious to be recognized and impatient to return her chaste and affectionate embrace. For thus the poet describes her:

> Then gliding in state through the marble doors <note>original: "valves"; here meaning folding doors</note>,

> The monarch, enthroned by a high column,

> Anxious to hear his queen break the silence:
> Amazed she sat, unable to speak;
> She rolled her eyes over the whole man in vain,
> Now hoping, now fearing, now recognizing, then doubting again <note>original: Odyss. lib. xxiii. 88, &c. (Odyssey, Book 23, line 88, etc.)</note>.

By this, Homer indicates that Philosophy—because of her long absence from the soul and the foreign manners and habits which the soul had adopted—seems like a stranger to it. Consequently, it is difficult for the soul to recognize the natural union and rightful connection that once existed between them. However, in order to make this discovery easier, Ulysses makes everything pure and harmonious within the hidden depths of his soul; and, by the assistance of Minerva (or Wisdom), he resumes the appearance and dignity which he had formerly displayed.

> Then immediately to the bath (the monarch cries),
> Command the cheerful youth and lively maidens to rise,

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic allegory of the Odyssey. Having defeated the suitors (the passions), Ulysses now undergoes a divine transformation, symbolizing the soul's purification and its reunion with Philosophy (represented by Penelope) and eventually with the divine.</meta>

> Then all descend in state and proud formation,
> and make the palace ring with joyful song;
> while the sweet musician sings of ecstatic melodies,
> and leads the dance to the rhythm of the strings$^d$.

Afterward, Ulysses is described as appearing, through the intervention of Athena <note>original: "Minerva"</note>, *like one of the gods*.

> So Athena <note>original: "Pallas"</note> enhances his heroic form
> with a divine glow, and he moves like a god$^e$.

Indeed, someone who—like Ulysses—has completely destroyed the power of their passions and purified themselves from their corrupting nature through the <term>cathartic virtues</term> <note>From the Greek "katharsis" (purification); in Neoplatonism, these are the virtues that cleanse the soul of bodily attachments.</note>, no longer belongs to the order of mortals but is made like a deity. Now, in order for him to be fully known to Philosophy—that faithful Penelope of the soul—it is only necessary for him to share the secrets of their spiritual <note>original: "mystic"</note> union and recognize the inner chamber of intellectual love. At that point, a perfect memory will return; the anxiety of doubt will be transformed into the joy of certainty and tears of blissful delight.

Thus we have followed Ulysses through his many travels and hardships until, by means of the <term>cathartic virtues</term>, he recovers the lost kingdom of his soul. However, it is necessary that he should next exist and act according to the <term>theoretic</term> <note>From the Greek "theoretikos" (contemplative); these are the highest virtues involving the direct vision of divine truths.</note> or <term>contemplative virtues</term>. The goal of these virtues is a union with the divine, as far as a human can achieve this in the present life. Homer only hints at Ulysses reaching this goal without detailing the specific steps he took to achieve this ultimate happiness. This union is secretly symbolized by Ulysses first seeing, and after—

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Ulysses' reunion with his father, Laertes, representing the soul's final ascent and union with the divine source or "The One."</meta>

<page-num>267</page-num>

...afterwards ardently embracing his father with ecstatic delight. With most admirable appropriateness, Ulysses is also represented as proceeding—in order to achieve this union—by himself *alone* to his father, who is also *alone*.

> Alone and unattended, let me try
> If I still live in the old man’s memory <note>original: Odyssey, book 24, line 215, etc.</note>,

says Ulysses. And afterwards it is said,

> But all alone the white-haired <note>original: "hoary"</note> king he found <note>original: Odyssey, book 24, line 225.</note>.

For a union with the inexpressible <note>original: "ineffable"</note> *one* of the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>From the Greek "demiourgos" (craftsman); in Platonic philosophy, this is the deity who fashions the physical universe.</note>—the true father of the soul—can only be accomplished by the soul returning to its own *unity*. To do this, it must first dismiss and abandon everything foreign to itself. This meeting of the soul with deity is, as Plotinus divinely says, *a flight of the alone to the alone* <note>original Greek: "phyge monou pros monon." These are the concluding words of the last book of his last Ennead.</note>. In this most beautiful expression, I have no doubt he alludes to this spiritual <note>original: "mystic"</note> conclusion of the wanderings of Ulysses in the arms of his father. 

Proclus also, in an equally admirable manner, alludes to this union in his *Commentaries on the Timaeus of Plato* <note>See volume 1, page 254, of Thomas Taylor's translation of that work.</note>. The allusion is found in his comment on the words: “It is difficult, therefore, to discover the maker and father of this universe; and, once found, it is impossible to speak of him to all men.” On this passage Proclus observes: “It is necessary that the soul, becoming an intellectual world and being as much as possible likened <note>original: "assimilated"</note> to the whole <term>intelligible world</term> <note>The realm of pure ideas and forms that can be understood by the mind, as opposed to the physical world of the senses.</note>, should introduce herself to the maker of the universe. From this introduction, she should, in a certain respect, become familiar with him through a continued intel—" <note>The text continues on the next page, likely as "intellection" or "intellectual vision."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s ascent, specifically commenting on a passage from Plato's Timaeus regarding the difficulty of "discovering the Father" (the Creator). It explains that this discovery is not reached through logic or opinion, but through a direct, solitary union between the soul and the divine.</meta>

<page-num>268</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>

...intellectual activity. For uninterrupted focus on any subject calls forth and revives our dormant ideas. Through this familiarity, as we take our place at the door of the Father, it becomes necessary for us to be united with him. For "discovery" consists of this: to meet him, to be united with him, *to associate alone with the alone*, and to see him directly—the soul quickly withdrawing itself from every other activity to focus on him. For, being present with her Father, she then considers scientific discussions to be nothing but words <gloss>k</gloss>, feasts with him on the truth of ultimate reality <note>original: "real being"</note>, and, surrounded by pure splendor, is purely initiated into complete and unchanging visions. 

Such, therefore, is the discovery of the Father. It is not "doxastic" <note>pertaining to "doxa" or mere opinion</note>, for opinion is uncertain and not far removed from the life of irrational animals. Neither is it "scientific" <note>In this context, "scientific" refers to "episteme," or knowledge gained through step-by-step logical reasoning.</note>; for this is logical <note>original: "syllogistic"</note> and complex, and does not come into direct contact with the intellectual essence of the intellectual <term>Demiurge</term>. Instead, this discovery happens through intellectual vision itself—a direct contact with the intelligible and a union with the creator’s mind. 

This may properly be called "difficult," either because it is hard to achieve—presenting itself to souls only after every cycle of life has unfolded—or because it represents the true labor of souls. For after the wandering through the world of change <note>original: "generation"</note>, after purification, and the light of science, intellectual activity and the intellect within us shine forth. These place the soul in the Father as if in a harbor, firmly establishing her in the divine acts of understanding and joining light with light. This light is not like that of mere science, but is more beautiful, more intellectual, and partakes more of the nature of *the One* than science does. *For this is the Father’s harbor, and the discovery of the Father: namely, an unpolluted union with him.*

With great beauty also, and in perfect agreement with the most profound <note>original: "recondite"</note> theology, the father of Ulysses...

---

<gloss>k</gloss> This is because a union with the <term>Demiurge</term> is so much higher than mere scientific perception.

<summary>This page explains that "finding the Father" (the divine source) requires moving beyond logic and opinion into a state of "intellectual vision" or direct union. It allegorically frames this spiritual arrival as the soul reaching a "paternal port" or harbor after its long wanderings.</summary>
<keywords>Demiurge, intellectual vision, Plotinus, Proclus, spiritual union, Neoplatonism, allegory of Ulysses</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Odyssey, moving from the soul's discovery of "the Father" (the Creator) to the specific symbolism of Ulysses’ father, Laertes, whom he finds working in a garden upon his return to Ithaca.</meta>

<page-num>269</page-num>

# ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.

Laertes is represented as coarsely clothed and occupied in gardening:

> But all alone the gray-haired king he found;
> His clothing coarse, but warmly wrapped around;
> His head, that bowed with many a pensive care,
> Protected with a double cap of goatskin hair;
> His boots old, in former service torn,
> But well repaired; and gloves against the thorn.
> In this array the kingly gardener stood,
> And cleared a plant, encumbered with its wood¹.

This simplicity and the coarseness of Laertes’ clothing—viewed as a symbol of the true father of Ulysses—is, in every respect, consistent with the method used by ancient mythologists in their portrayals <note>original: "adumbrations"</note> of the divine. For they represented the transcendence of divine natures through things that are supernatural; a power more divine than all reason through things that are irrational; and a beauty that surpasses everything physical through apparent deformity. 

This clothing of the father of Ulysses is, therefore, in the language of Proclus, indicative "of an essence established in the simplicity of *the One*, and greatly rejoicing (as one of the piously wise says) in an unadorned lack of form <note>original: "privation of form"</note>, and extending it to those who are able to look upon it <note>Original Greek: "τα μεν γαρ εστι θεια... και προτεινοντα τοις εις αυτα βλεπειν δυναμενοις." — Proclus, *Commentary on the Parmenides*, Book 1.</note>." Furthermore, the gardening <note>original: "botanical labours"</note> of Laertes is an image of the providential attention of the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>The divine craftsman or creator of the material world in Neoplatonic thought.</note> to the immediate branches and blossoms of his own divine essence, in which they are ineffably rooted and from which they eternally grow.

Although Ulysses is placed through the contemplative <note>original: "theoretic"</note> virtues in the fatherly harbor <note>the "paternal port" signifies the soul's return to its divine origin</note>—so far as this is possible to be achieved in the present life—yet we must remember, according to the beautiful observation of Por—

---

¹ *Odyssey*, book 24, line 225, etc.
ᵐ "For these things are divine and established in the simplicity of the One, loving exceedingly the unadorned formlessness [original: *amorphian*], as one of the piously wise says, and offering it to those who are able to look upon them." — Proclus, *Commentary on the Parmenides*, Book 1, p. 38. 8vo. Paris, 1821.

<summary>This page interprets the character of Laertes and his simple gardening as a Neoplatonic symbol for the Demiurge (the Creator) and his providential care for the "blossoms" of reality. It explains why ancient myths used "coarse" or "deformed" imagery to represent the transcendent simplicity of the divine.</summary>

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Odyssey, moving from the symbolism of Laertes to the final stage of Ulysses' journey: his liberation from the "sea" of material existence and his return to the soul's true home.</meta>

<page-num>270</page-num>

# ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.

Porphyry says that he is not freed from harassment until he has passed over the raging sea of a material nature; that is, until he has become unaffected <gloss>original: impassive</gloss> by the excitations of the irrational life and is entirely detached from external concerns. For,

> Then heaven decrees in peace to end his days,
> And steal himself from life by slow decays;
> Unknown to pain, in age resign his breath,
> When late stern Neptune points the shaft of death;
> To the dark grave retiring as to rest;
> His people blessing, by his people blessed <gloss>o</gloss>.

I shall only observe further that Plotinus also considered the wanderings of Ulysses as a mythological story <note>original: "fabulous narration"</note> containing a hidden meaning, such as that which we have explained above. This is evident from the following extract from his admirable treatise *On the Beautiful*: "It is here, then, [in order to survey beauty itself] that we may more truly exclaim,

> Haste, let us fly and all our sails expand,
> To gain our dear, our long-lost native land <gloss>p</gloss>.

But by what guiding stars shall we direct our flight, and by what means avoid the magic power of Circe and the detaining charms of Calypso? For this is what the myth of Ulysses obscurely signifies, which portrays him remaining as an unwilling exile, though pleasant sights...

---

<gloss>n</gloss> This impassivity, or perfect subjugation of the passions to reason, which is the *true apathy* <note>The term "apathy" (Greek: *apatheia*) for Stoics and Platonists did not mean a lack of care, but a state of being free from irrational emotions and disturbances.</note> of the Stoics and Platonists, is indicated by Ulysses finding a nation

> "Who never knew salt or heard the billows roar."

<gloss>o</gloss> *Odyssey*, Book 23, line 281, etc. By *the people* in these lines, the inferior parts or powers of the soul are indicated.

<gloss>p</gloss> *Iliad*, Book 2, line 140, and Book 9, line 27.

<summary>This page explains how the end of Ulysses' journey symbolizes the soul's liberation from material passions and its return to its "native land" or divine origin, citing interpretations by Porphyry and Plotinus.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the Neoplatonic interpretation of the Odyssey, specifically Porphyry’s "On the Wanderings of Ulysses." It ends with a quote from Plotinus emphasizing the soul's return to its divine origin.</meta>

<page-num>271</page-num>
<header>ON THE WANDERINGS OF ULYSSES.</header>

...were continually presented to his sight; and everything was offered to invite his stay—everything that can delight the senses and captivate the heart. But our true country, like that of Ulysses, is the place from which we came, and where our father lives <gloss>q</gloss>.

<note>q See my paraphrased translation of this treatise, page 37, etc.</note>
